==================== BOOK PAGE 26 ====================
25
A 
Player-knight is the imaginary persona used to partic -
ipate in  P aladin  game sessions. In this chapter you 
create your first character, an experienced squire from 
Ardennes who is ready to be knighted.
Characters used by the Gamemaster are called “Gamemaster 
characters” or, alternately, “non-player characters” (NPCs). In 
the world of P aladin , the Gamemaster controls everyone, from 
Emperor Charlemagne to the half-wit goose boy, aside from 
your Player-knights.
The Character Sheet
P
layers use the two-page (and ideally two-sided) char -
acter sheets provided at the end of this book to record 
all information pertinent to the play of their imaginary 
P aladin  personae. This information includes such things as the 
characters’ physical qualities, appearance, family data, personal-
ity, equipment, and personal history. Each character requires a 
separate character sheet. After a Player-knight is completed, the 
character sheet is the only piece of paper necessary for play.
The character sheet should be filled out using a pencil. The 
information contained on it will change during play.
If your character dies during the game, give his character 
sheet to the Gamemaster. He or she will keep these together in 
a “cemetery” for later reference (and as a handy tool to use as in-
stant Gamemaster characters, simply changing their names and 
origins as needed).
Character Creation  
in Nine Steps
F
or your first character, you may either choose one of the 
pre-constructed characters found in the back of the book 
and give him a name, or create your own character using 
the rules below. 
Y our initial character is an experienced Frankish squire 
from the duchy of Ardennes, ready to become a knight. All other 
players’ characters should share these characteristics, so that you 
Chapter One:  
Character Creation
may begin play with a coherent group of squires serving the same 
lord, possibly all from the same family.
Many characters who you will meet during play are not from 
this mainstream. They may be of different gender, geographical 
origin, social class, or religion. Though the information given 
in this chapter is primarily destined for first characters, it holds 
for all characters. The Gamemaster should apply changes where 
necessary.
Nine steps must be considered when creating a (starting) 
character: 
1. Personal Data
2. Family
3. Yo ut h
4. Possessions
5. Attributes
6. Personality
7. Skills
8. Knighthood
9. Glorious Stories
Step 1: Personal Data
T
his information includes the character’s name, homeland, 
culture, age, date of birth, and so forth.
Here is the information a player should write down 
on his character sheet for a character created for the year 767: 
Name: 
Age:    Son no.: 1
Blessing:

Culture: Frankish  Lineage: 
Liege lord: Duke Thierry
Father’s class:   Current class: Squire
Name: The first thing you need to do to create a character 
is to pick a Frankish name from the list below, or you may create 
a Frankish sounding name using the Name Generator given in 
Appendix 1.

Chapter One: Character Creation
26
Age: Some men are born lucky, gifted with strength and 
dexterity and the best family. They may qualify for knighthood 
sooner than normal. Some men are unlucky, and will lag behind 
their peers in qualifying for knighthood (see Step 7: Knighthood). 
Y our character will probably be 18–21, but leave this blank until 
the game starts. 
Son Number: Y ou are the eldest son of your family: W rite 
a “1” here. Y ou are probably your father’s favorite, and will be 
given the best arms and equipment he can afford when you are 
knighted. As an alternative, you may roll your son number (see 
Son Number, page 30).
Blessing: When a previous character dies and is canonized 
by the Church, the next character receives a divine blessing (see 
Canonization, page 42).
Homeland: All initial characters are from the duchy of 
Ardennes. Other characters may come from different parts of 
Frankland, or even beyond (see Chapters 14 and 17).
Home: First characters live with their lord at the castle in 
the town of Bastogne.
Culture: Starting characters are raised in the Frankish cul-
ture, which creates a bond between them. The character’s reli -
gion is not noted, as all Frankish characters are automatically 
Christians. Later characters may be from a different culture (see 
Chapter 17: Foreign Cultures).
Lineage: Y our family.
Liege Lord:  Y our first character’s lord is Duke Thierry. 
Listing a lord’s title in this space is sufficient. If a knight has mul-
tiple lords one must be selected as liege, and should be specially 
marked here.
Father’s Class: First characters are the first sons of knights, 
but the type of knight is determined either by Gamemaster fiat 
or a random roll in Step 3, using T able 1–4: Father’s Class.
Current Class: First characters all start off as squires. For 
more advanced character classes, see the section on Ambitions, 
page 183.
Name
Characters should have one name only. Last names or fam-
ily names are not used. Instead the name is modified by the ad -
dition of a nickname, which often is in French (you decide if 
it sounds better in English or French). Some titles are descrip -
tive: le Bref (the short), Courtnez (short nose); some tell where 
the character is from: le Danois (the Dane); and some refer to a 
character trait or a story: Martel (the hammer), le Restoré (re-
stored to his ancestral rights). It is often best to wait until your 
character earns a nickname for his actions.
Male Frankish Names: Adalhart, Archembaud, Aymeri, 
Aymon, Baldwin (Baudouin), Bego(n), Bevis (Bueves), Bernier, 
B(r)eton, Carloman, Charles, Doolin, Doon, Drogo(n), 
Enguerand, Ernaut, Eustac(h)e, Floovent, Fulco (Foulque), 
Fromond(in), Ganelon, Gerard (Girart), Godfrid (Gaufrey), 
Geoffroy, Gontran, Grimaud, Grifo, Guerri, Gilbert (Guibert), 
Guielin, Guy(on), Hardrad (Hardré), Hugo, Isoré, Lewis 
(Louis), Mainet, Maugis, Naymon, Oliver, Otto (Eudes), Pepin 
(or Pippin), Rainfroi, Renier, Richard, Roland (Rhuodland), 
Thierry (Theuderic), T urold, T urpin, Vivien, W alter (Gauthier), 
W arin (Garin), W elf, William (Guillaume), W orad.
Female Frankish Names: Ada (-laïde), Aelis, Aude, 
Avissa, Beatrice, Bellisent, Bertha, Bertrada, Clarissa, Dhuoda, 
Ermengard, Fastrada, Gisela, Hiltrude, Imma, Leandre, 
Passerose, Rolinda, Rosamund, Plectrude, Swanhilde.
Step 2: Family
E
ach Player-knight is part of a family, and it is encouraged 
that all players come from the same family. Therefore, 
before entering play the players should cooperatively in-
vent a family for their characters. See, for instance, the family 
trees for the various great houses in Appendix 3. Y our characters 
may even come from an offshoot of one of these great houses, 
though the benefits you’ll get from being related to them will be 
offset by the enemies you’ll earn!
The Player-knights may be cousins, all about the same age. 
The players should create their family tree, agreeing on a com -
mon ancestor (possibly their grandfather) and their place in the 
family. Their family should include at least one count or duke. A 
character that rolls “Lord” as Father’s Class in Step 3 is the son of 
such an exalted noble. 
Immediate Family Members
First characters, starting in a campaign beginning in 767, 
may use the procedure from Chapter 2: The Past , to randomly 
generate a heroic background for their grandfather and father, 
who were both knights. Y our father was born in the year 724. 
If multiple players share the same ancestor, they all get the same 
benefits from his adventures. 
For example, here is Adalhart’s family:

Family Characteristic
Every noble family is known to be naturally gifted in one 
area of competence. Figure it now, from the table below. Note 
the bonus value on the character sheet.
Different lines of the same great family may have different 
Family Characteristics, so each player should roll independently 
of the others, unless they share a father or grandfather. The Male 
Characteristic is transferred through the male line, given to all 
children of all men of this line. Y our brothers and sons all have 
this characteristic. 
Female characters use the Female table to determine their 
family characteristic. The Female Characteristic is passed along 
the female line, from mother to daughter.
The added value can violate the rules established for limita-
tions; raising a skill over 15 during Step 6: Skills.

Family Saint
Every family benefits from the benevolence of a special 
saint. This confers a certain bonus to all members of the family, 
men and women alike. Roll up the family saint and note the bo-
nus on the character sheet.
Most great families share the same saint, though it is cer -
tainly possible that one branch of the family has a different saint. 
Players may all choose the same saint, or choose their own saint 
separately.

Directed Traits and Passions
If the group creates a family for their knights, they may in -
clude family traits and passions, which represent prejudices the 
entire family holds (whether from direct experience, or hearsay). 
Fathers may also pass down directed traits or passions to their 
sons — especially Hate for particular types of enemy, such as 
Saxons or Saracens. 
Family Motto and Battle Cry
A self-respecting noble family has its own family motto (of-
ten in Latin) and a specific battle cry. Examples of mottoes are 
Amore non timore (“Out of love, not out of fear”), or “Y ou only 
have one death — sell it dearly!, ” which is the motto of the House 
of Ardennes. Charlemagne himself creates the Carolingian mot-
to: Renovatio Imperii (“Restoring the empire”).
A family’s battle cry may refer to the motto, but not nec -
essarily so. The famous battle cry of the Carolingian House is 
“Monjoie!, ” which is a reference to Charlemagne’s holy sword 
Joyeuse.

He girt about him his sword Joyeuse, which had no peer
And whose color changes thirty times a day.
W e could speak for a long time of the lance
With which our Lord was wounded on the cross.
Charlemagne has its point, thanks be to God,
Which he has had set in his golden pommel.
Through this honor and excellence
The name Joyeuse was given to the sword.
Frankish barons should not forget it; 
From it they derive their battle cry “Monjoie. ”
— The Song of Roland , CLXXXIII
In battle, knights and vassals fighting under their lord’s ban-
ner may use their lord’s battle cry, or their own if Inspired by 
Love [family], for example. Likewise, they may shout “Monjoie” 
if they prepare to risk their lives for the king, or call on God or a 
saint if they are religiously Inspired.
Player-knights from the same House should all have the 
same motto and battle cry.
Mustering Kin and Retinue
An important factor in the game is how many knights are 
in your family, from your Grandfather down. Players may share 
kin if they are closely related. Y ou can call on your kin in a cri -
sis (such as a feud with another noble family) to defend your 
interests and honor. These knights are either Old (older than 50 
years old), Middle-aged (31–49), or Y oung (under 30). 
Family Knights: These are your relatives who are knights, 
whether uncles, brothers, or other relations. Y ou can use the 
family you generated for your knight, or determine the numbers 
randomly by rolling: 
 n 1d6–5 Old Knights (use the Old Knight stats on page 373)
 n 1d6–2 Middle-Aged Knights (use the Ordinary 
Knight stats on page 373)
 n 1d6+1 Y oung Knights (use the Y oung Knight stats on 
page 373)
V assals: Starting characters have no vassals; they are not 
lords commanding other knights. If a character acquires vassals 
during the campaign, these knights are obliged to come to battle 
when summoned.
Other Lineage Men: Non-knights from the family can be 
useful in a fight. They are never well armed, and are generally 
unskilled at fighting, but at least they won’t run away immedi -
ately upon being attacked by the enemy. A character can call on 
3d6+5 men.
Levy: None for starting characters. When a Player-knight 
has one or more manors, the number of men is equal to the 
Standing [commoners] score multiplied by 5, calculated for each 
manor individually. The levy calls forth every able-bodied man 
of a knight’s personal holding. The total population of a manor 
is around 420 people, but they do not all show up.

Family Character Sheet
Included in the back of the book is a Character sheet for the 
players’ extended family. Fill it out just as you would a character 
sheet for a knight, but all players should have a hand in creating 
the family. 
Family Name: Create a name for your family. it may be the 
name of their Home county or family seat (Mayence, Clermont), 
or the name of an ancestor (Arnulfings). 
Motto: The family’s motto.
Battle Cry: The family battle cry. 
Ancestor: The name of the man who founded the family. 
While he would have had siblings of his own, they are not nec -
essarily acknowledged, though their descendants might count as 
allies (or enemies!). 
Home County: Where the family comes from, and the 
base of their power. While the family may hold other counties or 
even duchies, this one is their ancestral home. 
Notable Members: Feel free to add some names (and 
possibly history) here to give your family a base of great deeds. 
Figure that counts, dukes, bishops and abbots can be considered 
“Notable.” Once the order of paladins is created, paladins should 
be added to the list. Y our Player-knights should work towards 
entering the list of notable members. 
Patron Saint: As rolled above. Some branches of the family 
can have different patron saints — this is perfectly alright. 
Family Characteristics: As rolled above. It is possible for 
different branches of the family to have different Characteristics; 
perhaps one brother was a renowned as a hunter, another for his 
eloquence, and a third for his way with women. 
Allies: Friends and distant relatives of the main branch of 
the family. Y ou may roll Love [Family] –10 to call on their aid. 
Enemies: No great family can exist without making ene -
mies. The enemies should be other great families (such as the 
Mayence clan), not individuals. The players should come up 
with why these families are enemies — they may have feuded 
over land or a death; or their ancestors may have been rivals for 
the same lady. Where family pride is involved, no slight is too 
small! A passion of Hate [Family Enemy] for each enemy family 
should be generated by rolling 2d6+3. 
Honor: The family Honor is generally the lowest Honor of 
any of its members. It can be generated by rolling 2d6+3. Deeds 
done by the Player-knights may reflect on family Honor — for 
good or ill! Gaining offices will increase family Honor, while 
vile behavior will reduce it. The family Honor should usually 
not increase or decrease by more than one for any particular act 
(though the execution of a high-ranked traitor, like Ganelon, 
can have a greater effect). 
Standing [Charlemagne], Standing [Church], Standing 
[Commoners]: These standings are normally used when the 
character meets someone for the first time, before they have a 
chance to know them better. So a knight visiting a bishop for 
the first time will be affected by the family Church standing, un-
til the bishop has time to evaluate the knight as an individual. 
Generate these standings by rolling 2d6. 
Directed T raits, Passions: The family may have a particu-
lar directed trait based on their collective past. A common pas -
sions is Hate toward a particular enemy — Saxons, Bretons or 
Moors. See the sections on  Directed T raits (page 69) and Other 
Common Passions (page 76).

Note that the character will either inherit the Glory in the table 
below, or 1/10
th of the Glory earned by his father in Chapter 2: 
The Past, but not both.
When the time comes for an existing character’s son to be 
created, you should find the closest equivalent in the tables be -
low to determine what training he gains from his father.

Son Number
Poor knights, landed knights with only one manor, officers, 
and the like probably can only afford to knight their eldest son. 
In this case son number should either simply be “1, ” or roll 1d3 
to determine son number.
Knights banneret and lords are usually able to knight all 
their children. T o determine your character’s birth order, roll 
1d6 and write in that number. 
Daughters who become knights roll Standing [family] to 
determine their “son” number: critical: “son number” = 1, suc -
cess: 1d3+1, failure: 1d3+3, fumble: = 6.
Finally, see how your father fares. If you use Chapter 2: The 
Past to generate your father’s career, skip this step. Otherwise, 
roll to see what how your father is doing:

20 Father has been missing for 2d6 years.

Cultural Modifiers
Illustrious race of the Franks, instituted by God himself, cou-
rageous in war, in peace constant. They are of noble stature, 
exceptionally beautiful, daring, swift and hardy, generous, 
faithful Christians free of all heresy. Long live Christ, who 
loves the Franks! 
— Prolog of the Frankish Salic Law
The modifiers below apply to all Frankish characters. Note 
them on your character sheet.
 n +1d3 Energetic, +1d3 Generous, +1d3 V alorous.
 n +1 Honor, +1 Love [God].
 n +1 to all six Christian religious traits (Chaste, 
Forgiving, Merciful, Modest, T emperate, Trusting).
Homeland Modifiers
Depending on their homeland, the character may have 
bonuses in skills, traits and passions. Player-knights who grew 
up outside Frankland have cultural modifiers only, none for 
homeland.
For all Ardennes knights, these are +1d3 to each of Hunting, 
T emperate, Modest, and Suspicious. See Chapter 14: Frankland 
for homeland modifiers for other parts of Charlemagne’s empire. 
Step 4: Attributes
T
he physical characteristics of any P aladin  character 
are quantified by five crucial statistics called Attributes. 
V arious other statistics are derived from this group. 
Size (SIZ): This reflects the bulk and height of your 
character. Bigger characters are superior in combat because 
bigger men do more damage, can absorb more damage, and 
because SIZ is used to calculate Knockdown, as explained in 
Chapter 7: Combat .
Dexterity (DEX): A character’s quickness, balance, and 
agility are all measured by Dexterity. It influences the character’s 
Movement Rate.
Strength (STR): This reflects your character’s ability to lift 
and carry weight. It influences combat and movement rate.
Constitution (CON): Health is quantified by the 
Constitution score. A CON value less than 6 indicates a sickly 
character, while a value greater than 15 indicates robust health. 
For combat, a Major W ound is one which immediately affects 
the character, knocking him unconscious, and is equal to CON.
Appearance (APP): The physical beauty of a character is 
reflected by his APP. A value over 15 indicates a handsome or 
beautiful person, while APP below 6 is extremely ugly and repul-
sive. This statistic comes notably into play when seducing ladies.
Base Attribute Values
T o determine the starting values for Attributes, simply 
roll 2d6+3 for each attribute. For damsels and other ladies, 
use the “W omen” column; female knights may roll on the 
“Men” column, as it is assumed they are tougher and stronger 
than other ladies.
Add an additional 5 points to your attributes, with a maxi-
mum of +3 to any single attribute. 
Add any cultural modifier bonuses from Step 3.
t able  1–8: b ase  a ttributes

Men W omen (optional)

Derived Statistics
Some statistics are derived from those determined above. 
They are not definite and may be subject to change during sev -
eral character generation steps. W ait until character creation is 
over before filling in the values. 
Damage (SIZ+STR)/6: A character’s Damage value re -
veals his potential to do harm to his foe. The value acquired 
from the equation indicates the number of six-sided dice that 
the player rolls when his character successfully hits something or 
someone with a sword or other weapon.
Healing Rate (CON+STR)/10: The Healing Rate of a 
character indicates the number of hit points which can be re -
gained during a week of game time by the character, as long as 
that character is resting quietly. Refer to the  Injury and Health 
section of Chapter 7.
Movement Rate (STR+DEX)/10: This value indicates 
the relative speed your character walks while in armor. The 
Step 4: Attributes
==================== BOOK PAGE 33 ====================
Chapter One: Character Creation
32
Movement Rate number affects daily overland rates and forced 
march (see Movement in Chapter 6).
T otal Hit Points (CON+SIZ): T otal Hit Points are used 
in combat to determine how much damage a character may sus-
tain before going unconscious or being killed.
Unconscious (T otal Hit Points/4): This value is a 
threshold below which a character falls unconscious. Every 
time any character’s current hit points falls below this value, he 
drops to the ground, senseless and out of play. In a bad fight, 
this may save his life.
Distinctive Features
APP measures relative appearance, determining whether a 
character is handsome, beautiful, or ugly. Higher APP statistics 
indicate a more handsome character, while numbers below 5 in-
dicate true ugliness.
Frankish knights don’t wear beards, but they do have broad 
mustaches. T o further personalize your character, you may roll 
up or choose some physical features in the lists below. They al -
low for objective differences between characters with the same 
subjective value.

Step 5: Personality
T
he stories about Charlemagne and his peers are full of 
personality and passion. The game emphasizes those 
points. T raits and passions are methods of quantifying 
your character’s inner self. They record both reputation and pro-
pensity. They help you run your character in a consistent manner 
and may change according to his actual play activity. Standings 
are a measure of what opinions others hold about your character.
A value of 16 or more indicates great interest and activity, 
perhaps bordering on the fanatical. This sort of behavior is very 
obvious to everyone, and significant in game terms.
Once the values of your traits, passions and standings are 
determined in this step, they no longer affect one another. For 
example, Standing [Lord] does not automatically increase if you 
later raise your V alorous. 
Traits
The personality traits used in P aladin  consist of twelve 
opposed pairs of virtues and vices. Every virtue has its oppos -
ing vice. For starting characters, the total on the two sides of the 
slash must equal 20.
T o determine the initial base scores for the left hand traits 
(virtues), roll 2d6+3 and write down the number on the left side 
of the slash. Add any modifiers from previous steps. 
At the end of character generation, complete the trait pairs 
by subtracting the values of each trait from 20 and filling in the 
resulting number in the vice opposite that trait. Note that a pos-
itive modifier on the right-hand trait is the same as a negative 
modifier on the left-hand trait. 
Chivalrous Traits
Chivalry is a code of honor much encouraged by the king. 
It elevates a knight above the mere Loyalty felt by a knight to 
his lord. The Chivalrous traits are: Energetic, Generous, Just, 
Merciful, Modest, V alorous
Religious Traits
The Franks are Christians, which emphasizes the following 
traits: Chaste, Forgiving, Merciful, Modest, T emperate, T rusting. 
T ogether, these are known as the Christian Religious traits. 
Romantic Traits
Romance, as exemplified by fin’amor, is an ideal that raises 
all women above the knight, and one above all. His amor inspires 
him to the highest deeds. The Romantic traits are: Forgiving, 
Generous, Honest, Just, Prudent, T rusting.
Directed Traits
Directed traits are explained on page 69. The family created 
by the players may have generated directed traits, and a character 
may take one directed trait of his father’s, at the same value as 
his father. 
Character Freedom and Control
Having a trait over 16 affects the player’s actual control over 
his character. Y our character’s actions may be determined by the 
trait, and may be contrary to your wishes as a player. For exam -
ple, a character with an Honest trait of 16 tells the truth in most 
situations, even those where honesty might be inadvisable. The 
game system assures this (see Chapter 3 for more information).
==================== BOOK PAGE 34 ====================
33
The purpose of P aladin  is to illustrate the splendor and 
glamour of the legendary realm, and to explore the many possi -
ble solutions to typical Carolingian problems. Therefore, we rec-
ommend that no evil Player-knights (with high scores in many 
right-hand vices) be generated, as they can ruin the game. 
A significant portion of the game, in fact, is an exploration 
of good and bad. The Frankish Christians have a difficult set of 
values to uphold, some of which are in direct conflict with their 
profession, whereas pagan enemies always have the choice of fall-
ing back on their dark side and justifying their actions that way.
Passions
Passions are strong emotions within the individual. They 
include Love, Hate, Amor, and Honor, among others. Beginning 
characters all begin with four standard passions: Honor, Love 
[Charlemagne], Love [family], and Love [God]. 
t able  1–10: Passio Ns  b ase  v alue  
Passion Base V alue
Honor  (Generous+Just+V alorous)/3
Love [Charlemagne] 2d6+3
Love [family] 1d6+10 – son number
Love [God] lowest Religious trait
Honor: Honor is the measure of a character’s credibili -
ty, trustworthiness, respectability, personal dignity and pride. 
This primordial passion describes knightly loyalty and obedi -
ence to one’s lord(s), culture, homeland and to the universal 
tradition of hospitality. Honor is the knight’s special passion. 
All knights must have Honor (in other words, have a value 
greater than 0, and preferably over 5), and if they later lose 
their Honor they lose their knighthood. Without Honor the 
feudal system cannot exist.
Love [Charlemagne]: As the supreme ruler of the Frankish 
people and the incarnation of the divine power in the temporal 
world, King Charlemagne is admired by all Player-knights. The 
loyalty and dedication to their king may grant Player-knights di-
vine assistance (see Prayers & Miracles in Chapter 9).
Love [family]: This natural emotion is common to man -
kind in any age. T o most people, their family are the only people 
they can really trust and rely on when it matters most. The travel 
restrictions of the Carolingian era emphasize family closeness. 
It applies not only to the knight’s immediate family, but to the 
grand extended family. 
Love [God]: All Player-knights are Christians. They are 
more or less devout or spiritually interested in their religion, 
but all share the love of their Creator. Their Love of God allows 
Player-knights to benefit from the prayers of other Christians 
(see Prayers & Miracles in Chapter 9).
Other passions may be allowed at the beginning by the 
Gamemaster, and characters will acquire passions as the result of 
game play. If the Family History is used, you may have generated 
additional passions, such as Hate [Family Enemy]. 
Standings
The generous Roland is loved by his vassals: 
The Franks love him so much they will not fail him.
So much gold and silver does he bestow on them.
Mules and war-horses, silks and arms.
— The Song of Roland , XXX
A standing quantifies the general opinion and esteem that a 
group of people has of your character. It represents their willing-
ness to support and defend you.

Step 6: Skills
S
kills in P aladin  define those activities which characters 
commonly perform during game play. The numerical 
value of these skills reflects the chance of successfully 
completing that action during the game. Higher numbers mean 
better abilities. As a result of different talents and personal expe-
rience, not all characters have equal ability in these skills.
Base Skill Values
A character’s beginning skill values are shaped by his envi -
ronment, especially his culture and his father’s social class. 
Roll up the scores indicated on T ables 1–12 and 1–13  and 
note them on your character sheet. Add all the modifiers gener-
ated from the Father’s Class table, Page T raining table, Cultural 
and Homeland modifiers. No skill can be raised over 15; excess 
points in that skill are simply lost. 
Training
Now you may spend the skill points that you received from 
the Father’s Class table. Y ou may not spend skill points to raise a 
skill with a value of 0, nor can you raise any skill over 15.
Family Characteristics
Finally, add the skill bonuses for Family Characteristic and 
Family Saint (if applicable). These bonuses may raise skills of 0, 
and they can raise skills over the threshold of 15, but only to a max-
imum of 20.

Step 7: Knighthood
Y
our character is now 15 years old, and must still probably 
spend some years as a squire. It’s normal for a character to 
be knighted in his 18th year. By this time, he should have 
gained the reputation, connections, and minimum skills needed 
for knighthood. 
As a squire, your character is learning the competencies of 
a true knight. He must have the following scores to qualify for 
knighthood: 
 n Traits: V alorous 13
 n Passions: Honor 10
 n Standings: Standing [lord] 10
 n General skills:  First Aid 10, Horsemanship 10, two 
courtly skills 10
 n Combat skills: Battle 10, two melee weapon skills 13 
(Phase 1 and later: Lance 10)
For each year beyond 15 that you spend as a squire, you 
may choose two different benefits listed below to upgrade your 
character: 
 n 1 attribute: +1 (up to maximum value of 20)
 n 1 trait, passion or standing:  +1 (up to maximum 
value of 15)
 n 4 skills:  +1 common skill, +1 courtly skill, +1 com -
bat skill, and +1 in another freely chosen skill (no 
common skills of value 0; and only up to maximum 
value of 15)
These squire year benefits are similar to, but slightly differ-
ent than, the Winter Phase system of the game. Do not confuse 
them! Age your squire one year at a time, keeping track of how 
many years he spends as a squire. 
As soon as a character qualifies for knighthood, he should 
be knighted and the squire aging procedure stop. Intentionally 
putting off your knighthood to gain better statistics is both un -
realistic and wrong. 
Check to see if a character qualifies for the Chivalrous, 
Religious and/or Romantic bonus (see Ideals in Chapter 11). He 
gets some significant advantages for qualifying! T o qualify, the 
knight must have a combined 90 or more points in the traits list-
ed, as well as 16 or more in the associated passion:

Age (Year Born)
Once you have qualified for knighthood, you can determine 
the age and year of birth of your character (ask your Gamemaster 
the year that his campaign is currently set, if necessary).
Initial Glory
Now calculate your Player-knight’s initial Glory. If you use 
the Family Events in Chapter 2, your starting Glory is 1/10th your 
father’s glory. Otherwise, your starting Glory is determined by 
T able 1–4: Father’s Class. 
Add any Glory from Page T raining, and all cumulative 
scores over 15 (a score of 19 in First Aid, for example, would 
count as 16+17+18+19 = 70 initial Glory). 
The Knighting Ceremony
Beginning knights receive a full 1,000 Glory points when 
knighted, are entitled to use the title Sir (or Dame, if female) be-
fore their name, and qualify for their own personal coat of arms.
Several men are often knighted at the same time. In that 
case, the order in which they are called forth depends upon the 
precedence of their fathers (See Chapter 4: Reputation: Glory 
and Standings for matters of precedence).
The ceremony is generally done with full panoply. This 
means the lord sits upon his high seat or throne, banners are 
displayed, courtiers are present in their best clothes, and heralds 
make announcements. Knighting can also occur without all the 
pomp and ceremony, for instance when a squire is hastily knight-
ed on a battlefield.
For first characters, their liege is the Duke of Ardennes and 
their king is Pepin the Short. Characters who get knighted in the 
years between 768 and 771 may swear to King Carloman instead 
of King Charlemagne, depending on the territory their lord is 
living in. The Ardennes is in King Charlemagne’s territory. 
Ceremonial Flourishes
In addition to the standard knighthood ceremony, there are 
sometimes additional tasks that may be added by regional cus -
tom or by the desires of the lord.
t He  v igil
Religious lords may use a more elaborate ceremony. 
T ypically, the squire spends the night before the ceremony in 
a vigil of prayer and contemplation. The sword, armor, coat of 
arms, and spurs that he will wear as a knight are placed upon 
the altar to receive a blessing from God or the knight’s favorite 
saint. A priest or bishop bestows blessings, and the knight makes 
further oaths to support the church.
The candidate is dressed in special clothing which symbol -
ized his future as a knight: a white overtunic symbolizes purity; 
black tunic, hose, and shoes denote death; a red cloak indicates 
blood (both that which may be shed and that which runs in a 
nobleman’s veins); and a white belt denotes the chastity of a 
good Christian man.
In such a ceremony the player should make rolls for 
Energetic (to see if he stays awake) and Love [God] (to see if he 
prays). There is no Glory award for the Vigil. 
t He  l eaP
Many lands follow an informal ritual held immediately 
after the knighting ceremony is concluded. The new knight, 
armed and armored, dashes outside and leaps into the saddle. 
If he makes it into the saddle without touching the stirrups, the 
knight is praised and honored, but if he misses, then everyone 
laughs. No other meaning is attached to the rite.
Some lords follow this custom. T o see if your character is 
successful, roll 1d20. If the number rolled is equal to or less than 
your character’s DEX, he is successful and earns an additional 
10 points of Glory.
Knightly Privileges
Coat of Arms
A coat of arms is the design worn by a knight on his shield. 
This design serves to identify the knight and his family when 
he wears full armor and is not otherwise recognizable. The skill 
that allows the identification of a coat of arms is called Heraldry. 
Heralds spend all their lives mastering this skill.
A knight is entitled to bear his own, unique, coat of arms. 
If your character does not have a ready-made coat of arms and 
you cannot make one up on the spot, your character may ap -
ply for provisional arms and ride for a year with a blank shield. 
Additionally, a squire may reserve his design ahead of time by 
checking with the Royal Herald (the Gamemaster). 
Draw the coat of arms you want or trace a design from the 
nearby pages. One page of Charges (pictorial elements) and one 
page of Ordinaries (geometrical background designs) are given. 
Y ou can combine those. The background part of a coat of arms, 
upon which a Charge is set, is called a Field. 
Label each section with the desired color, or color it in with 
pencils. Normally a coat of arms includes a color and a metal. 
Colors available are Gules (red), V ert or Sinople (green), Azure 
(blue), Purpure (purple), and Sable (black). Metals are Argent 
(silver or white) and Or (gold or yellow). “Furs” are also possible. 
For the sake of readability, the general rule is to not place a color 
on a color, or a metal on a metal.
Squire
Once your character gets knighted he needs his own squire, 
usually a young man from his own or his lord’s family. They are 
assigned by the character’s lord or an officer, and the knight is ex-
pected to train the squire in the duties and skills of knighthood. 
Starting squires are always age 15. Usually a squire’s success at

s quire  r oll
The squire’s age is used as a statistic, rolled any time the 
squire must perform under stress. For instance, if a knight needs 
a new lance in a tournament’s grand melee, roll the squire’s age 
on 1d20. If successful, the knight gets the lance; if not, wait until 
the next melee round and try again. The age roll can be used in 
other instances, such as to see if the knight’s armor is polished 
properly when the king visits to inspect the castle.
d uties  aNd  b eNefits
A squire does whatever his knight tells him to do. T ypical 
chores include tending horses, sharpening weapons, polish -
ing armor, helping don armor, carrying messages, preparing 
and handing over fresh weapons, providing the knight with a 
horse, taking charge of prisoners on a battlefield, riding errands, 
standing guard, waiting on the knight at a banquet, rescuing the 
knight on a battlefield, and fighting when so ordered.
Squires receive goods, training, board and protection 
from their knights. Goods include their clothing, horses, ar -
mor, weapons and everything required for sustenance. Unless 
the knight is staying in a lady’s quarters, the squire can expect 
lodgings comparable to the knight’s. The knight must work and 
fight to protect his squire, and may not squander his squire’s life 
needlessly. This is a subjective judgment made by the knight, and 
a matter of trust for the squire. Many squires chafe at being held 
back, preferring the chance to fight the enemy and gain the ex -
perience. Others appreciate the protection. One way or another, 
players should always remember that a squire is a noble’s son in 
their knight’s charge. This is a serious responsibility, so if a squire 
gets seriously injured or even killed, the knight will have some 
explaining to do. Squires are trained in the use of the sword, but 
are not allowed to use one in combat until they are knighted. 
Squires, as the lowest class of noble and gentlemen-in-train-
ing, are ransomed for £6 if captured. 
Step 8: Possessions
Starting Outfit
A
ll knightly outfits given include standard travel and war 
gear. Characters who are not the first son degrade their 
starting outfit by 1.
Player-knights created after the retirement or death of a 
previous character may inherit the equipment of that predeces -
sor instead of using T able 1–14.
Some of the entries contain references to coin. This extra 
money can be used to purchase goods from the Price List, found 
in Chapter 12: W ealth and T reasure. 
Birth Gift
Apart from his starting outfit, every character inherits 
something personal from his ancestors. The number of rolls on 
the table below depend on Father’s Class.
Step 8: Possessions
T able 1–15 gives various colorful items to beginning char -
acters. Make up the where and why of this, if necessary. For heir-
looms, you can make up a story of how it came into the family’s 
possession. It might be from the mother’s side of the family, a gift 
from a lord, a war trophy, and so on.
Equipment
The simplest way to divide a knight’s cargo among several 
steeds is to classify it into one of the types below, and write in the 
number for the appropriate steed to locate it (the character does 
not normally carry these items himself ).
Personal Gear: Includes money, documents, your best set 
of clothing, favorite secondary weapons, and so on. Usually a 
small bundle, very portable. It is generally carried on the knight’s 
riding horse. Note any special items here as well.
T ravel Gear: This includes a tent, blankets, tent stakes, 
cooking and eating materials, horse-tending equipment, cold 
weather and rain cloaks, and so on. This can be carried on a horse 
which is also being ridden, and is about a quarter of a horse-load 
per person, without much food or water. If your character has 
something special in his travel gear, such as a mirror or a wig, 
write this in the small space provided.
W ar Gear: This includes things for extended military 
camping, such as more warm socks, rope, paint to fix up 
shields, whetstones, pieces of armor, keg of sand for polishing 
mail, spare weapons, a couple bottles of wine, and anything

Female Knights
I know of no path safer than that along which my sword  
directs me.
— Marfisa, in Orlando Furioso , XX
Y
our campaign may have room for female knights. A 
woman may choose to take up the profession of arms 
for several reasons. For instance, a military emergency 
might make great and unusual demands for soldiers (like in the 
example below); a kinsman may need rescue without any other 
family member or champion available; she may be motivated by 
her own independence rather than the desire to follow custom.
The valiant Lady Orable is willing to fight: 
“I shall remain in Orange, the great,
With the ladies, there are so many here.
Each one will have a Moorish coat of mail
And on her head a green shining helmet,
At her side she will gird a good sword,
At her neck a shield, a sharp spear in her hand.
 (...) We shall climb up on these walls in the front
And defend them well, if the Saracens attack.
I shall be armed like any warrior. ” 
— Aliscans , LV
Female knights are called “Dame” or “Sir” (their prefer -
ence),so Bradamant should be addressed as “Dame Bradamant.” 
Note that “Lady Bradamant” means something different al -
together; “Lady” is the honorific given to the wives of nobles. 
Female squires are simply called “Squire, ” just like men. 
T o create female characters, two possibilities exist. Either 
you generate a female character exactly the same way as if she 
were a male, or you take into account the specific entries for 
women in the tables relating to her family, attributes and skills.
Acceptance
While female knights like Bradamant are accepted as the 
equals of men by Charlemagne and his paladins, reactions from 
others vary. Old-fashioned knights generally think that women 
belong in the kitchen and nursery, and any deviance from this is 
met with scorn, contempt, and hostility. Others accept women 
knights only if they can prove themselves and do not get too up-
pity about it.
Bradamant refuses to marry a man of less valor than herself: 
“Permit no husband to be bestowed upon me until he has 
demonstrated greater prowess at arms than I possess. I must 
make trial of whoever would have me, either with lance or 
with sword: let the first man to defeat me win me; let the 
defeated seek other brides. ” 
— Orlando Furioso , XLIV
which might not be replaced while on campaign. This is about 
one horse-load per knight and squire team, and includes the 
T ravel Gear for each person. These possessions are usually kept 
in a trunk in the lord’s hall. When a knight goes on campaign 
with his lord, the lord lends the knight a horse to carry this 
gear. If adventuring on his own, a knight must find, borrow, 
buy, or otherwise obtain a horse if he wishes to both carry this 
gear and allow his squire to ride.
Possessions: Knights often accumulate gold, silver mon -
ey, extra weapons, additional mounts, sumptuous goods, and so 
forth, which they do not carry everywhere they go. It stays back 
at their manor or in their lord’s hall. 
Step 9: Glorious Stories
Glory
T
his space is provided to display your character’s accumu-
lated Glory. When your character is knighted, he gains a 
massive 1,000 Glory points. If your Gamemaster practic-
es the custom of The Leap (see page 35), then your Glory may be 
increased by 10 more.
Stories
If you have followed instructions this far you now have a 
character ready to play. The character is a squire, 18 or so years 
old, possessing the connections, skills, and reputation necessary 
to become a knight immediately. Each player should now take 
some time to reflect on his character and prepare a little presen-
tation, possibly with a little history and a psychological interpre-
tation of his or her personality.
“This is Sir Adalhart, a household squire of Duke Thierry 
d’ Ardennes. He is 22 years old, dresses very well, and has pale 
skin and a deep voice. He is notably [scores of 16+ values]. 
He has the Glory of an ordinary knight. He is proud of the 
fact that he is the oldest of the four brothers who make up 
the leaders of his family clan. His father, the famous knight 
known as Berenger du Faucon, died heroically some years 
ago at the battle of [name]. ” 
Player-Knight History
As a player, you are encouraged to record the game history 
and significant facts of your character. Y ou do not have to write 
things down, but you should. Y ou lose much of the long-term 
impact of playing this game if you don’t. P aladin  an epic, 47-
year campaign, with knights staking their lives for Glory. Their 
play-lives are worth the minor effort of a written synopsis for 
each of their adventures.
After experiencing several adventures with a number of 
characters, you will begin to sense the history as your characters 
grow into the story.
==================== BOOK PAGE 42 ====================
41
In order to avoid the problem of acceptance, some female 
knights operate disguised as men. A woman disguised as a 
knight has many problems retaining her secret. When a wom -
an masquerading as a man has revealed her true gender, anoth -
er problem often presents itself: a lord to knight her. Reluctant 
lords may never accept the situation, in which case she must find 
another lord. Alternatively, a lord may set some difficult quest to 
be completed before he grants his permission.
Death and Retirement
Roland feels that his death is near; 
Through his ears his brains are seeping.
— The Song of Roland , CLXVIII
T
here are two ways in which a Player-knight can end his 
career: he dies, or he retires. A character may leave active 
play at any time — preferably with his lord’s permission 
— and retire as a hermit or monk.
A more elegant way of retiring is when a Player-knight is 
promoted to a position of great power, like count, lay bishop, or 
Royal officer. When such a character has become too powerful, 
the Gamemaster himself may require a character to quit active 
play and turn him into an un-played or Gamemaster character.
A Second Character
Every player should have a second character ready to play. 
Sometimes the primary character is incapacitated, unable to 
continue the adventure. In such a case the second character 
comes into play, being found nearby or riding up from the rear 
in time to join the expedition and avenge his kinsman’s injury. 
An alternative to playing another knight is to play the squire of 
another player’s knight. 
Because the game is family oriented, players should make 
this second character part of the same family. The coat of arms 
and history will be similar, and the new character will know 
most of the things that the first one knew. 
Sometimes the secondary character becomes more inter -
esting than the initial one, and Gamemasters ought to allow a 
changeover. After all, the point is to have fun.
Salvation
The heavens themselves mourn for Roland’s death: 
In Frankland there is a most terrible storm,
A tempest with thunder and strong winds,
Rain and hail in great quantity.
Lightning strikes again and again
And the whole earth, in truth, begins to quake.
From Saint Michael’s Mount to Seinz,
From Besançon to the port of Wissant,
There is no house whose walls do not collapse.
At high noon a great darkness gathers; 
There is a light only when the sky is rent.
No one could see it without a feeling of dread.
— The Song of Roland , CX
When a knight character dies or definitively retires, the 
player makes a special die roll against a score called Salvation, 
to see if the character is worthy of going to Heaven immediate -
ly (even if he is not yet actually dead), or must spend time in 
Purgatory cleansing his soul. This score depends on his status:

Apart from indicating whether a character is allowed en -

benefits for the player’s next character:

previous character, with a maximum value equal to 
the Salvation score.

Hell and Damnation
The knightly way of life puts souls in peril. Almost inevi -
tably, a knight will violate the commandments from the Bible, 
especially the one which says not to kill. Knights accept this, it 
is the price of their prerogatives. Many make generous donations 
to the Church on their deathbed as a way of doing penance for 
their sins. In the game, though, this has little beneficial effect on 
their Salvation. On the other hand, a character going to Hell has 
no further negative implications.

New Characters
W
hen creating a new character, a player should chose a 
member from his previous character’s, or his compan-
ion’s, family, if any. He continues the lineage and fully 
experiences the dynastic aspect of the game.
Alternatively, and with the Gamemaster’s approval, a player 
may create a completely new character from another homeland 
and possibly even from another culture. In this case, he can either 
play a Frank who, for some reason, was raised amidst another 
people, like Vivien of Monbranc, or the other Vivien (William 
Shortnose’s nephew) who was exchanged with the Moors to lib-
erate his imprisoned father. Or else he may be a foreigner raised 
in Frankland, like Ogier the Dane or the Gascon prince Sancho 
Lupus, who were initially both hostages at the Frankish court 
before becoming paladins.
Character From the Same  
or Companion’s Family
Apart from possible Salvation or Canonization benefits, a 
newly created character in the same family as his predecessor in-
herits several things from the previous character. 
Companions swear to hold all things, even their families, in 
common. A new character may inherit goods and bonuses from 
the companion of his previous character. A player may choose 
whether to use the character’s father’s, or father’s companion’s 
values when creating his character. 
Family
Family Features: The player may choose which family (fa-
ther’s or companion’s) to have been brought up in. He gains all 
the benefits (and disadvantages) of that family. This decision 
may affect Cultural and Homeland modifiers, below. 
Youth
Father’s Class: The father’s class at the time of his death, 
or at the knighting of his son, determines the training the new 
character receives. 
Cultural Modifier: It’s assumed that the character is 
brought up in a Frankish home, but a companion may have come 
from another culture.
Homeland Modifier: Use the homeland of the household 
in which he grew up. 
Attributes
APP: Beauty reflects nobility and worth. Newly generated 
characters may modify their initial score by adding a bonus equal 
to the previous character’s Glory/1,000 (max. +10).
V alor: T he Franks consider V alor to be hereditary. 
Therefore, a new character may add a V alorous bonus equal to 
the previous character’s V alorous –15. 
Knighthood
Initial Glory: A new Player-knight starts play with Glory 
equal to 1/10th that of the previous character.
Possessions
Equipment: The deceased character may pass down special 
equipment, treasure, and goods to the new character, in addition 
to the results of the T able 1–15: Frankish Birth Gifts. The char-
acter starts with the same equipment as the previous character 
was using.
Manors: The new character may inherit allodial manors or 
manors that were granted to his father. Inheriting manors may 
only be done with the Gamemaster’s approval — the lord or the 
family may have a different candidate for the manor.

Chapter Two: The Past
T
he information given in the chronology below contains a 
history of the Franks, as well as a procedure to generate a 
family history for your first character. It is, in itself, a way 
to familiarize yourself with the Frankish past in general.
Historical events are in Bold. 
Many summarized events are taken from the chansons 
de geste . Chansons are identified in the entries below in Bold 
Italics. T o allow for a fluid diachronic reading, if the entry is a 
continuation from a previous year, it is listed as [←previous year]. 
If it is continued in a succeeding year, it is marked [→next year]. 
If the story is picked up in another Chanson, the listing will be 
marked [←previous year, Name] or [→next year, Name] The end 
of a story is marked with a n.
In between are the notes and tables which enable starting 
players to roll up a short family history for their grandfather and 
father.
Your Family History
Your Grandfather
T
he family history below starts in 723, the year before your 
father was born. Y our grandfather was a wealthy warrior 
in the service of Charles Martel, the Mayor of the Palace 
of the weak King Thierry IV. Y our grandfather was a knight of 
notable fame and starts his career at 2,500 Glory.
Many raids and battles generate a certain amount of Hate 
against particular nations. If an ancestor dies in battle against a 
certain enemy, his direct descendant gains an extra 1d6 of Hate 
[Enemy]. At the end of his life your grandfather passes on his 
Hates to his son, your father, if their value exceeds 10. Likewise, 
your father passes on his Hates that exceed 10 to you. 
Your Father
Y our father (born in 724) was also a notable knight, living 
in the Ardennes and serving under King Pepin. His starting 
Glory is 2,500 plus 1/10th of your grandfather’s total Glory.
Charles Martel
King Thierry IV (Theuderic) “reigned” from 721 to his death 
in 737, and he was not succeeded by anyone until Chilperic III 
(the last Merovingian king) in 743, who was deposed in 751, 
when Pepin the Short was finally anointed King of the Franks. 
All the while, the real power was in the hands of Charles 
Martel, the Mayor of the Palace and Duke of the Franks. As 
such, he was in command of the royal army.
Charles Martel was a tough and talented military com -
mander, who celebrated many victories against the Saxons, 
Moors and other enemies. He is most famous for defeating 
the Moors at the Battle of Poitiers (or Battle of T ours) in 732.
As the de facto ruler of the Franks, he laid out the basis for 
the concept of knighthood, and forged a solid alliance with 
the Pope.
Pepin the Short
Son of Charles Martel, he succeeded his father as Mayor of 
the Palace, but later he deposed King Chilperic III and was 
anointed King of the Franks in 751.
During his political and military career, he had many 
problems with his brother Carloman and half-brother Grifo. 
As a king, Pepin waged many offensive wars in which he 
forced the submission of the southern duchies of Burgundy, 
Aquitaine, Provence and Septimania.
King Pepin confirmed the pre-eminence of the Frankish 
people in Western Europe. Inside his realm he promoted feu -
dalism, vassalage and the use of knights as heavy cavalry.
What exactly happened during Pepin’s marriage with 
Bertrada Broadfoot and how their son Charlemagne was born 
is known to only a few people, and this episode of history is 
subject to the wildest rumors and speculations.

Phase –2: Feudal Anarchy
T
he years 700–722 are included as a background; there are 
no consequences for your family. 
a NNo  700
Doon de Mayence: Guy of Mayence, Lord of Montblois on 
the Rhine, accidentally kills a hermit while hunting. As penance, 
he takes the holy man’s place. When Guy fails to come home his 
seneschal, Archibald, proposes to marry Marguerie, Guy’s “wid-
ow, ” but she refuses. Archibald then accuses her of adultery and 
the murder of her husband. She is imprisoned together with Sir 
Baldwin, who wanted to defend his lady’s honor.
Archibald orders a knight to go out into the forest and kill 
Guy’s three sons. The man slays two out of three, but is himself 
stabbed to death by young Doon. The young squire wanders 
around the Ardennes Forest and finally finds his father, living 
as a hermit. He tells his father what happened. When Guy takes 
up his weapons to avenge the death of his sons, an angel strikes 
him with blindness. Guy and Doon live in the forest for a few 
years. [→705] 
a NNo  701
Floris and Blancheflour: Felix, the pagan King of Asturias, 
conducts a slave raid and captures a band of Christian pilgrims, 
among whom is a beautiful, pregnant young widow. This wom-
an becomes the personal servant of the queen, who is also preg -
nant. Both women give birth on the same day: the pagan lady 
has a boy, Floris, while the Christian lady has a girl, Blancheflour. 
Both children are raised together at Felix’ court, where they 
study diligently. [→717] 
a NNo  705
Doon de Mayence: [←700] One day, Doon kills an insolent 
knight carrying a letter from Archibald. He reads it and learns 
about his mother’s distress. Doon rides back to Montblois to 
defend her.
On his way he is welcomed at his uncle’s castle, where he 
kills a robber knight in a joust. The knight’s sons want to avenge 
their father and the next day they attack Doon in their own cas-
tle, but Doon escapes unharmed.
When Doon finally arrives at Montblois, he demands a 
duel against Archibald to defend his mother’s honor. Archibald 
names Droart as his champion. Doon defeats Droart in a single 
combat, but the champion and his master Archibald flee to the 
city of Mayence.
Doon pursues them but is taken prisoner. In prison, he 
meets the loyal Sir Baldwin. W audry, the jail warden, takes 
Doon’s side and frees the prisoners. T ogether the three men sur-
prise Archibald and his men and slay them all.

Phase –1: Charles Martel
Customs
F
eudalism: Charles Martel introduces feudalism as a 
system of land ownership in which small land holders 
pledge fealty to an overlord in return for protection. 
Locally, the great magnates rule by “Might Makes Right” and 
don’t hesitate to seize their neighbor’s land by force if the oc -
casion presents itself. 
Knighthood: If a man can equip himself with armor, weap-
ons, and a horse, he can ride with other heavy cavalry under the 
command of Charles Martel or his officers. This is the beginning 
of knighthood — those closest to the Mayor are recognized and 
given honors. 
Heraldry: Knights may use whatever design they wish on 
their shields. Most “heraldry” just a plain color, or a simple de -
sign (crosses are favored) on the shield. 
Wo m en :  They are the property of the family patriarchs, 
who marry them at will to advance the interests of their House.
Fashion: Nobles wear the traditional Frankish linen shirt, 
a knotted or pinned cloak, trousers, thick stockings and san -
dals. Elegance is expressed by superficial Roman influences like 
brooches and sashes. Men have long hair, but shave and have nei-
ther mustache nor beard.
Warfare
Armor: Ring mail body armor and an open conical helmet 
with a nasal protection (8 points) is the standard for knights. 
Round shields (6 points) are made of leather-covered wood with 
an iron boss in the center.
W eapons: Knights wield a spear and an iron sword, and 
perhaps an axe, mace or hammer.
Horses: Chargers are rare and expensive. Poor knights 
fight on rouncies or sometimes coursers. Horses are unarmored.
Castles: W ooden stockades, perhaps surrounded by a ditch, 
are prevalent. A few cities have preserved their Roman walls, but 
stone castles are unknown. 
Levy T roops: Free men present themselves at their lord’s 
army mostly as lightly armored cavalry, spearmen, or unarmored 
archers.
a NNo  714
Frankland: Birth of Pepin the Short, son of Charles Martel.
a NNo  717
Floris and Blancheflour: [←701] King Felix is worried about 
his son, Floris, who is obviously in love with the Christian slave 
girl Blancheflour, so he decides to send his son to Andalusia. This 
separation is painful for both Floris and Blancheflour. King Felix 
intercepts his son’s love letters and becomes convinced that the 
girl has bewitched his son, so he sells her to Saracen merchants. 
She is taken to Babylon and is locked up in a tower serving in the

Gaufrey: [←706, Doon de Mayence] Garin of Monglane asks 
his friend Doon for help in his war with Gloriant the Saxon. 
Garin’s sons, Gerard, Milo, and Renier, have already been taken 
prisoner. Doon and his sons leave for Monglane. When they ar-
rive, Garin conducts a sortie, but he is taken prisoner together with 
==================== BOOK PAGE 50 ====================
49
his vassal Robastre. Godfrid and his brothers attack the Saxons in 
the rear. Some Saxons flee to their ships, where they meet Doon 
in battle. The Count of Mayence liberates the squires, but their 
father Garin is taken away. All alone, Doon pursues the Saxons, 
who easily capture him. Doon’s and Garin’s sons finally settle in 
V auclere to make a plan to rescue their fathers.
T aken to the Barbel T ower, Doon and Garin receive unex-
pected help from the Saxon princess Fleurdepine. She is in love 
with a Frankish knight called Berart of Mondidier, who happens 
to be Doon’s nephew. Before leaving with Gloriant and his ally 
Machabray (Fleurdepine’s father), the young princess orders the 
construction of a secret tunnel between the prison and her tower.
Meanwhile, the Franks arrive at Grailmont Castle, held by the 
Saxon Lord Guitant who is at war with his rival, Quinart. Robastre 
pretends to be Grailmont’s dead lord (who is still out fighting for his 
lord, Gloriant). Robastre is brought into the castle, which he cap-
tures at night. All pagan warriors are converted or killed. The lady 
of the castle, Fauquette, is baptized and married to Grifo. Guitant 
returns back home, refuses baptism and is slain by Godfrid.
Quinart lays siege to Grailmont. The Franks ride out to do 
battle, kill Quinart and convert the remaining Saxons. Robastre 
is wounded, but healed by one of Fauquette’s magic potions. T o 
celebrate their victory, Garin marries Clarisse, and Gerard mar -
ries Avissa (aunts of Duke Naymo).
Godfrid leads the Franks to the south. Grifo leaves the 
army, pretending to be ill, and travels to the Frankish court. He 
obtains the title of count and the permission to build a castle 
at T royes, which he names Hautefeuille. He tells lies about his 
brothers, claiming their victories against the Saxons as his own.
Gloriant, Machabray and his daughter arrive at the Saxon 
stronghold of Amandon, where they are welcomed by Lord 
T urfanie. Fleurdepine is allowed to return to the Barbel T ower, 
together with Gloriant’s nephew Malprin. Berart of Mondidier 
and some other Frankish knights are sent into Saxony to help 
Garin and Doon. They arrive at the Barbel T ower to do battle 
with the Saxons, but they are defeated. In the Saxon prison, they 
meet Doon and Garin. [→730]

Auberi de Bourgogne: [←730] Aubery unfairly suspects his 
wife of infidelity, but later realizes her innocence. While he is pur-

until he is rescued by his nephew, Gaselin. Aubery betroths his 
step-daughter Senehaut to Gaselin and pardons his enemies.
The powerful Sir Lambert of Oridon lusts after Senehaut 
and plots to kidnap her. He locks Aubery in a tower with two 
young maidens and later accuses him of raping them, hoping 
to blackmail him. Fearing death, Aubery submits to Lambert’s 
wishes and is forced to trick Senehaut into going to a monastery 
where Lambert is waiting for her. Once he has kept his word, 
Aubery promises to come and rescue his furious step-daughter.
Sir Lambert marries Senehaut against her will, but she de -
lays consummating the marriage and is rescued by Aubery and 
Gaselin. Aubery receives troops from Charles Martel and besieges 
Oridon, but the villain escapes the siege through an underground 
tunnel and arranges for Charles to reconcile him with Aubery. 
The Mayor of the Palace forces Aubery to forgive Lambert and 
grants the traitor safe passage through Frankland. [→732]

Berthe aux grands pieds: [←720, Floris and Blancheflour] 
Y oung princess Bertrada, daughter of King Floris of Asturias and 
Queen Blancheflour, is taken to Paris to be married to Charles 
Martel’s second son, Pepin the Short. But Margiste, the lady-in-wait-
ing who takes care of the young princess, notices that Bertrada and 
her own daughter Aliste resemble each other as if they were twins, so 
she substitutes Aliste for Bertrada the day after the marriage.
Margiste orders Tibert, a forester, to take the real Bertrada out 
to the woods and kill her, but the princess is saved by a sergeant called 
Morant, who lets her escape into the Forest of Le Mans. There she 
is taken in by the swineherd Simon and his wife Constance. [→736] 
Huon d’ Auvergne: Huon II of Auvergne is falsely accused 
of having seduced a Merovingian princess. As a punishment, 
Charles Martel sends him to Hell to demand tribute in the name 
of the Franks. In reality, the king just wants an opportunity to 
seduce Huon’s wife. [→738] 
Girart de Roussillon: T o thank Charles Martel for his aid 
in defending Rome, the Emperor of Byzantium wishes to marry 
his daughters Bertha and Elissent to Charles and his powerful 
vassal Gerard of Roussillon, Duke of Burgundy. When the girls 
arrive in Frankland, Charles prefers the younger and prettier 
Elissent to Bertha, and arranges an exchange with Gerard. In 
return, Gerard’s fief of Burgundy becomes a free allod. Princess 
Elissent gives Gerard a ring as a token of her sincere affection.
Charles is jealous of Gerard’s independence and he finds a 
pretext to attack him. He takes Roussillon by treason and defies 
Gerard to do battle, but God, by a divine intervention, orders 
the lords to make peace, which they do. [→736] 
Daurel and Beton: [←734] One day, as Duke Bevis is hunting 
a great boar in the Ardennes Forest, his companion Guy stabs him. 
The dying duke forgives his jealous friend and tells him how to con-
ceal his crime, asking him in return to raise Beton as his son. When 
the duke’s body is found, Ermengard accuses Guy but nobody be-
lieves her. Guy bribes Charles Martel and obtains Ermengard’s hand.
The suspicious Ermengard sends the baby Beton to Poitiers, 
to be raised anonymously by a gentle woman named Aiceline. 
When her new husband asks her where the boy is, Ermengard 
refuses to tell him, even after he hits her in the face.
Beton is betrayed by a fisherman. Guy locks up Ermengard 
and prepares to go and kill Beton. When Sir Daurel hears of Guy’s 
plans, the loyal minstrel-vassal immediately rides to Poitiers and 
substitutes his own son for Beton, exclaiming: “My son shall die; 
my liege shall be saved!” Lord Guy arrives and smashes the baby’s 
head against a pillar. Daurel buries his son’s body and takes little 
Beton across the sea. His wife dies of grief. [→736]

Bordelais finally capitulate. A fragile peace is concluded. [→739] 
Orson de Beauvais : Counts Orson and Hugo are made 
companions to seal the peace in Burgundy. Orson marries 
Aceline, the daughter of the exiled Duke Huon of Auvergne, 
who gives him a son, Milo. Hugo is Milo’s godfather.
The problem is that Hugo is also in love with Aceline. 
Therefore, he stages an angelic vision in which he tells Orson 
to leave on a pilgrimage with his companion. Before they go, 
Aceline gives her husband a golden ring. On the road, Hugo 
steals the ring and sells his companion as a slave. [→740] 
Lion de Bourges : [←737] Herpin retires as a monk near

save her lord, who then wishes to marry “him” to his daughter 
Floirie. When she reveals her true identity to avoid marriage, the 
wali himself desires her hand, so she flees into the city disguised 
as a beggar. [→754] 
Frankland: At Thierry IV’s death, the Frankish throne is 
left vacant.
Rome: St. Boniface visits Rome, where he receives a papal 
mission to convert the Saxons.

Phase 0: King Pepin (742–767)
Customs
F
eudalism: During King Pepin’s phase it functions only 
on a lower level. The king represents a formal central au-
thority. Locally, the great magnates still rule by “Might 
Makes Right.”
T ruce of God: This set of rules, created by the Church to 
limit the violence against the innocent and weak, is ignored by 
most nobles. In theory there should be no acts of war during holy 
days and Sundays, and church property should be protected.
Knighthood: As an extension of early feudalism, Pepin cre-
ates a separate caste of noble fighters. These knights follow the 
Code of Honor: unswerving loyalty to one’s lord, the willingness 
to face one’s enemies and deal out death on command.
Heraldry: Counts have an individual coat of arms and have 
their own battle standard with symbolic insignias. The knights 
in their retinues wear their colors, but they do not have an indi-
vidual coat of arms unless they belong to a very powerful family.
T ournaments: T ournaments are purely informal challenges 
or training contests, occasionally organized at a lord’s court.
Wo m en :  They are the property of the family patriarchs, 
who marry them at will to advance the interests of their House.
Chivalry: Despite King Pepin’s best efforts to civilize his 
knights, the chivalric ideals are known in theory but remain 
largely unapplied in practice. Minstrels and troubadours may 
refer to chivalry in song, and knights treat their fellows-at-arms 
with the respect due to a nobleman, but almost everybody agrees 
it would be exaggerated to extend similar courtesy to the Saxons 
or Moors or, for that matter, even to a Frankish enemy.
Romance: This novel set of ideas has slowly evolved 
from the Asturian literature about King Floris and Queen 
Blancheflour. For the moment it remains confined to the realm 
of poetry.
Phase 0: King Pepin
==================== BOOK PAGE 55 ====================
Chapter Two: The Past
54
Fashion: Nobles wear the traditional Frankish linen shirt, 
a knotted or pinned cloak, trousers, thick stockings and san -
dals. Elegance is expressed by superficial Roman influences like 
brooches and sashes. Men have long hair, but shave and have nei-
ther mustache nor beard.
Warfare
Armor: Ring mail body armor and an open conical helmet 
with a nasal protection (8 points) is the standard for knights. 
Round shields (6 points) are made of leather-covered wood with 
an iron boss in the center.
W eapons: Knights wield a spear and an iron sword, with an 
axe, mace, or a hammer.
Horses: Chargers are rare and expensive. Poor knights 
fight on rouncies, or sometimes coursers.
Horse armor is limited to simple trappers (4 points).
Castles: Motte-and-bailey castles are prevalent. A few cities 
have preserved their Roman walls, but stone castles are almost 
unknown. The most frequent defensive works are wooden for -
tresses and single stone towers.
Levy T roops: Free men present themselves at their lord’s 
army mostly as lightly armored cavalry, spearmen, or unarmored 
archers.

==================== BOOK PAGE 58 ====================
57
the sacred kings of the Franks, but whether this includes Prince 
Charlemagne is a matter of debate, since Pepin and Bertrada 
were not officially married when the boy was born. Some nobles 
are skeptical about Charlemagne’s royal birth, doubting if Pepin 
is indeed his father.
Mainet: T o usurp the throne and to avenge their mother 
Aliste, the bastard princes Heudri and Rainfroi (with the help 
of some discontent nobles) plan to poison their half-brother, 
Charlemagne. Luckily, Charlemagne’s friend, David, discov -
ers the wicked plot. Charlemagne and David decide to leave 
Frankland, where they are no longer safe. Before leaving, David 
takes the sword Joyeuse from the royal treasury and hands it 
to Charlemagne. The next day, the two evil princes imprison 
Milo of Aiglent (Aliste’s eldest son, their and Charlemagne’s 
half-brother), who aided Charlemagne to escape.
Charlemagne and his friends meet an Asturian guide 
named Macabrin who leads them to T oledo, where they are en-
gaged as mercenaries by Emir Galafre. T o avoid being recognized 
as Pepin’s son, Charlemagne takes on the name of Mainet, for 
he remembers how his father recently killed Galafre’s relative, 
Justamont. David calls himself Esmeray. [→752] 
Frankland, Aquitaine: Grifo rebels and, after a few skir -
mishes, flees to the court of Duke W aifer of Aquitaine.
Lombardy: The Lombards take the city of Ravenna from 
the Byzantines.

Mainet : [←751] The emir of Cordoba is at war with the 
rebellious wali of Pamplona, Caimant. Morant of T urfier 
leads Emir Galafre’s Syrian and Frankish mercenary troops. 
Despite his inferior equipment, squire Mainet distinguish -
es himself and kills Caimant in battle. Back in T oledo, he 
is praised by all for his valor. Galafre’s daughter, princess 
Galienna, falls in love with Mainet, but he politely refuses 
her advances. The emir knights Mainet and equips him, but 
the Frank refuses the sword he’s given, because he already has 
a better one ( Joyeuse).
Galienna refuses to marry Prince Braimant, to whom her 
father had promised her. When Galafre breaks the engagement 
in favor of Mainet, Braimant is furious and declares war on the 
emir. Another battle is fought. Again Mainet kills Galafre’s ene-
my and takes an enormous booty. The Syrian mercenaries are so 
impressed with Mainet’s valor and skill, that they demand to be 
baptized by chaplain Solin. These new converts all join Mainet’s 
mercenary company.
Galafre’s son, Marsile, is jealous and fears that Mainet will 
take his place as the future emir of Spain. He plans to ambush 
the Frank on his way back to Galafre’s court. Meanwhile, in the 
marvelous tent looted from Braimant, Galienna finds a magic 
mirror in which she sees Mainet’s life: how he was born in the 
forest as Charlemagne, and how his half-brothers tried to poison 
him. She also sees the ambush her jealous brother Marsile is pre-
paring. She warns the Franks, who ride out to help Mainet. They 
arrive just in time to defeat Marsile. Since it has now become 
impossible for them to return to T oledo, they decide to go back 
home. [→753] 
Septimania: The Saracens raid Septimania.
Frankland: Birth of Carloman II, brother of Charlemagne.

Aymeri de Narbonne: [←709, Enfances Garin de Monglane] 
After a long and bloody siege, Count Aymeri liberates the city of 
Narbonne from Saracen oppression.
Aymeri sends a marriage proposal to princess Ermengard of 
Pavia. His envoys intimidate her father with their excessive gen-
erosity. When Aymeri himself travels to Lombardy, the Saracens 
take advantage of his absence to lay siege to Narbonne again, but 
with Gerard of Roussillon’s help, Aymeri returns and once more 
defeats the Saracens, and then marries Ermengard. [→775, Les 
Narbonnais] 
Lion de Bourges : [←738] Rome is besieged by Saracens, 
who kill the abbot of Herpin’s monastery. Herpin takes up arms 
and defeats the invaders. He is betrayed, however, by the jealous 
Count Gaudiffer of Rome, who sells him to Moorish slave trad-
ers from T oledo.
Meanwhile, Baldwin generously raises Lion as a knight and 
allows him to participate in a tournament at Monlusant in Sicily. 
When Lion hears Baldwin is not his real father, he sets out to 
find his real parents. Shortly after, he is ambushed by brigands 
who kill a traveling companion. Lion gives the dead knight a 
proper Christian burial.
On the road, Lion meets Ganor, his father’s former squire, 
and decides to go to the Sicilian tournament. He teams up with 
Raymond of V auvenice and wins the hand of Princess Florentine, 
thanks to the assistance of a powerful and mysterious White 
Knight (who is in fact the ghost of the dead knight he recently 
buried). [→755] 
Frankland: The Pope, grateful for King Pepin’s support, 
travels to Paris where he once more anoints Pepin, this time 
along with his two reconciled sons, Charlemagne and Carloman. 
The Pope gives King Pepin the sacred standard Oriflamme and 
declares the royal Frank, “Vir nobilis, filius regnatoris, patricius 
Romanorum” (Noble Man, Son of kings, Patrician of Rome).
Frisia: Saint Boniface is slaughtered while trying to convert 
the Frisians.

siege of Pavia. King Aistulf dies after falling from his horse.
King Pepin presents the lands he won to the Pope (the 
“Donation of Pepin”), these lands become the Papal States. 
Emperor Constantine V of Byzantium sends rich gifts to en -
dorse the Byzantine claim to these Papal States, which King 
Pepin refuses.
