==================== BOOK PAGE 66 ====================
65
Chapter Three: Personality: 
Traits and Passions 
C
harlemagne’s epics are stories of personality and action 
on a grand scale. Everyone whose name is remembered 
is known for both great deeds and great feelings. No 
great character is a mere two-dimensional cutout whose history 
consists solely of high scores and lists of events. Everyone feels 
strongly about something.
Personality in P aladin  is depicted through traits and pas -
sions. They provide guidelines for playing characters who may 
be quite different from the player.
Why Do We Need  
Personality Values?
T
raits and passions are used to quantify character behav -
ior. They are used to make the player’s roleplaying task 
easier by providing guidelines for how the character acts, 
has acted, and is likely to continue to act. Numerical values are 
assigned to each trait and passion, and rolls are made using the 
values to determine behavior.
Consistency
In cases of distinctive behavior, your character may have to 
struggle to maintain his behavior, especially in the face of con -
flicting desires. The chaste knight is able to maintain his chastity, 
the drunkard remains a drunkard, and the courageous will be 
courageous — sometimes even against his better judgment.
Even after a long hiatus from play, you’ll be able to remem-
ber how a given character acts just by glancing at his traits and 
passions.
Entertainment Value
The interplay of character personalities provides a great 
deal of the fun in P aladin . With traits and passions, each char-
acter’s personality is very different. A character may chivalrous 
like Roland, wise like Oliver, or a sinister knight with a treach -
erous side, like Ganelon. Each character has a well-defined and 
colorful basis for roleplaying and for action within the game.
T raits and passions are entertaining because they can lead 
to amusing results. In addition, some successful trait or passion 
rolls result in the character becoming inspired, with temporary 
benefits to die rolls. Fumbled passion rolls might even result in 
madness.
Glory
Any dramatic pattern of behavior gains Glory. Characters 
with interesting personalities generally gain more Glory than 
those without. However, behavior in accord with society’s ideals 
gains more Glory than idiosyncrasies.
Authenticity
P aladin  deals with what is indisputably the greatest of all 
medieval stories—the epic story of Charlemagne and his pala -
dins. The game takes place in a world of knights and ladies, in an 
age of feudalism and chivalry, where democracy is seditious and 
unequal authoritarianism the norm.
Players control the actions of characters that may be, quite 
understandably, unlike themselves, yet they are expected to act 
appropriately to their station. Acts such as incidental murder, 
stealing horses, participating in ambushes, or unmerciful behav-
ior are not necessarily villainous; for death, slaughter, and misery 
are normal parts of a knight’s life.
How, then, can you know what is appropriate and correct? 
T raits and passions are the primary indicators that you use to de-
termine “correctness” in the P aladin  setting. If your character 
gains the “proper” traits and passions, then he is doing just exact-
ly those things that constitute correct behavior in Carolingian 
society.
If you wish to run a character who defies Carolingian tra -
ditions, you can and may, but your character will probably lose 
out in various passion values for acting outside the norms of 
society. The Gamemaster must decide whether a loss is war -
ranted and how much the loss should be, based on the specific 
circumstances.
==================== BOOK PAGE 67 ====================
Chapter Three: Personality: Traits and Passions
66
Using Traits and Passions
T
raits and passions are important to game play, being the 
primary component of a character’s reputation, along 
with standings and Glory — the general indicator of 
success in the game. T raits and passions measure such things as 
Honor or Honesty, while Glory measures raw prestige and pow-
er and standings determine how others react towards you. The 
two systems are inextricably linked, though, for traits and pas -
sions may gain you standings and Glory.
Heroic Traits or Passions
The threshold value for gaining Glory and reputation from 
a trait or passion is 16. If your knight has a V alorous personality 
trait of 15 he is brave, but not particularly noted for courage. 
Nor is a priest with a T emperate of 14 particularly frugal, or a 
passionate Love of 11 worth great notice. But a knight whose 
V alorous is 16 is considered heroic, and he whose Love [God] is 
18 is nearly a saint, while a lover with an Amor of 20 is famous 
throughout the realm for the depth of his passion.
Trait and Passion Rolls
During play, character behavior is often challenged by 
the Gamemaster. T emptations are paraded forth, moral crises 
heaped up, and critical judgments and actions must be made.
Since traits and passions define the character’s personality, 
they must be consulted whenever the Gamemaster determines 
that a critical decision is at hand. In crises, individuals act ac -
cording to character, not deliberate choice. Custom and train -
ing triumph over intellect. Players may not want their charac -
ters to do something dictated by a die roll, but free choice is 
not always possible.
Ordinary Traits and Passions
T raits and passions with values of 15 or less do not have to 
be rolled against if the player wishes to use his personal will to 
determine an action, although rolling is obviously the most im -
partial way to determine action.
Characters who consistently act a certain way will eventual-
ly have traits or passions valued at 16 or higher, due to the rules 
below, at which time they’ll have to make the required rolls.
Notable Traits and Passions 
T raits and passions greater than 15 are noted by society and 
gain Glory, and such traits or passions must be tested with a die 
roll when character behavior is challenged in a crisis. Basically, if 
you get Glory for a trait or passion, rolls based on the value must 
be made when required by the Gamemaster.
This does not mean that trait rolls must be used whenever 
the character makes any decision in the game. Even characters 
with famous characteristics are allowed free choice of behavior 
except when the plot demands otherwise. The Gamemaster 
should request trait rolls only when a trait is tested in an 
important situation. In general, trait rolls simulate situations in 
which a crisis forces the character to act unconsciously.
Modifiers 
T rait or passion values may be modified based on the situ-
ation in order to keep the game realistic. Players are responsible 
for reminding the Gamemaster when a situation might call for a 
trait or passion modifier. The Gamemaster determines whether 
such modifiers are valid.
Changes in Value
V alues for personality scores rise and fall during the game. 
Experience checks for traits and passions are assigned by the 
Gamemaster in a slightly different fashion from checks for 
skills. Players who are reluctant to roll should not be forced to. 
However, if they consistently have their characters act against 
their established personalities, the Gamemaster should have 
them check the trait or passion they actually exhibited, in order 
to simulate the possibility of a change in the character’s psychol-
ogy and reputation.
For example, the player of a knight with a Generous value 
of 13 loots and pillages his foes at every turn and constantly talks 
about the amount of money (or lack thereof ) his character has. 
This attitude and behavior is not in keeping with the Generous 
trait, so the Gamemaster is perfectly within his rights to tell the 
character to check his Selfish trait the next time he expresses the 
desire to loot an enemy.
Passions are particularly vulnerable to reduction. When a 
character acts against a passion, no check is assigned; instead the 
Gamemaster simply instructs the player to reduce the value of 
the passion by one point immediately. Any failed passion roll 
causes the character to lose a point.
Acting consistently, according to the role of your charac -
ter, will prevent compulsory checks and reductions from being 
necessary. 
The traits and passions system should not to be used to turn 
the Player-knights into puppets. Most of the time characters just 
do whatever the player wishes them to do, collecting checks 
along the way. However, if your character has a reputation, it’s 
only fair that he maintains it or loses it. The system ensures that 
this occurs.
Evil or Undesirable Behavior
It is not in the spirit of the game for Player-knights to 
become evil knights. However, minor character flaws such as 
Cruelty or Laziness can be very amusing in moderation, and 
give the Gamemaster more opportunities to create interesting 
situations. 
With one or two undesirable traits or inferior passion val -
ues, characters can have weaknesses other than those revealed 
only in mortal combat, giving the Gamemaster the possibility of 
creating non-lethal challenges for characters. Given the dangers 
involved in combat, this is a useful opportunity.
==================== BOOK PAGE 68 ====================
67
Traits
T
raits are dualistic personality factors. A trait and its oppo-
site both exist in every individual. They define a person’s 
feelings and tendencies.
P aladin  has twelve pairs of personality traits which are im-
portant. Characters certainly display other traits, but those listed 
are the ones critical to the Carolingian literature of the game. 
Some traits are essential to a certain kind of behavior, described 
in Chapter 11 in the section on Ideals.
T raits are presented in opposed pairs. The total value of each 
trait pair must always equal 20 when the character is created, and 
in most cases this will never change: When one trait increases, 
the opposite one decreases by the same amount. Normally, no 
trait may ever be higher than 19 or lower than 1, except through 
experience or by the use of bonus points gained from increased 
Glory (see T raits Over 19, below).
T raits should be written on the character sheet as whole 
numbers, each to one side of the slash (/) mark.
Traits Over 19
Any trait with a value between 5 and 15 is considered nor -
mal, while those less than 5 and greater than 15 are excessive and 
deserve to be noticeable, even famous. For instance, a man whose 
Modest trait is 15 and whose Proud trait is 5 does not have ei -
ther trait in a notable proportion. Some extraordinary characters 
have a trait of 20, 25, or perhaps even more! They are known 
through all the land for their unrelenting, fanatical behavior.
Scores above 19 are always the result of increases made 
during the Winter Phase (or by Gamemaster fiat). Such charac-
ters always have a value of 0 for the opposite trait. Rolls against 
these traits are handled as with any other roll having a value of 20 
or higher—the score is treated as 20, and any amount in excess 
of 20 is treated as a modifier to the roll itself (see V alues Greater 
Than 20 in Chapter 6).
Trait Descriptions
This section provides details and explanations for the 
twelve sets of paired traits, in many cases including examples of 
famous literary characters who exemplify one trait or the other.
Chaste/Lustful
If, because of the persuasion of the devil, fornication or 
some other spur of the flesh should drive your heart to 
frenzy, set chastity against it. Flee fornication and keep 
your mind away from any prostitute. How great is the 
strength and the enduring splendor of chastity, which 
makes a mortal man like a fellow citizen of the angels. 
For learned authors do not refuse sacred marriage to the 
union of the flesh, but they try to root out from among us 
libidinous and wrongful fornication.
— Dhuoda , 4.6
T o be Chaste is to be monogamous, or faithful to one’s sex-
ual mores. It does not always require virginity — a man being 
faithful to his wife is being Chaste. A Chaste person is modest 
and decorous in behavior. A fanatically Chaste person is celibate 
and probably virginal.
Lustful describes sexual desire, and implies activity, often with-
out personal commitment between the persons involved. Excessive 
promiscuity is called lechery, wantonness, and bawdiness.
Famous Characters: Prince Louis is respected for his 
Chaste behavior.
Energetic/Lazy
A vigorous person is Energetic. It includes the natural abil-
ity to get up and go to work, and to apply oneself to the tasks at 
hand. The comparable Christian virtue is Industry. Persons who 
are Energetic are called vigorous, robust, and industrious.
Laziness includes all slothful activity, such as loafing and 
general sedentary behavior. At the furthest end of the spectrum 
lies indolence.
Famous Characters: Archbishop T urpin is often admired 
for his Energetic nature.
Forgiving/Vengeful
“If, because of the plague of rancor, your heart seethes in 
wrath, control it as best as you can.
Peace holds back anger
Quarreling fears peace
Secure peace rests far and wide
Friendly concord flies towards heaven. ” 
— Dhuoda , 4.7
T o be Forgiving means that a character can take insult with-
out injury, and that he is unlikely to seek revenge for injuries in-
tended or done to him. Extremely Forgiving people are called 
“meek, ” in the Biblical sense.
V engeful indicates a character’s propensity to seek revenge 
— perhaps petty, but possibly sweeping and grandiose — for 
wrongs done or imagined done to him. This trait includes 
spitefulness.
Famous Characters: Count Ganelon is often noted for his 
V engeful streak. 
Generous/Selfish
Although three virtues are written about in books, one 
of them — that is, charity — is singular, the highest of 
them. […] If you encounter a poor man, offer him as 
much help as you can, not only in words but also in deeds. 
I direct you likewise to offer generous hospitality to pil -
grims, widows and orphans, children and indigents and 
to be quick to lift your hand to help those you can see 
are in need. May no one attribute greed — which is the 
serving of idols — to you. Give so that you may receive.
— Dhuoda , 2.2; 4.8
Traits
==================== BOOK PAGE 69 ====================
Chapter Three: Personality: Traits and Passions
68
T o be Generous is the impulse, learning, or desire to share. 
It includes the largesse of the feudal lords, and the Christian vir-
tue of charity. Extremely Generous persons are called unselfish, 
magnanimous, and big-hearted.
Selfishness is the desire to possess, keep, and further ac -
cumulate things for yourself. Greed is usually a component of 
Selfishness. Possession could be of material property, with the 
character being known as a miser or hoarder, or of credit, like 
hogging attention (Glory). V ery Selfish persons are labeled both 
stingy and self-seeking.
Note: In some cases, jealousy can be included under the 
Selfish trait. When someone acts jealous, he may get a check for 
Selfish.
Famous Characters: King Charlemagne and Sir Roland 
are well-known for their generosity.
Honest/Deceitful
Deceit is normally held in low esteem, pointing as it does 
to an evil disposition; there are, nonetheless, countless 
circumstances when it has reaped obvious benefits and 
deflected all manner of harm and ill report and mortal 
perils. For our conversation is not always with friends in 
this earthly life, dogged as it is by envy, and compounded 
of shadow far more than of light. 
— Orlando Furioso , IV.1
T o be Honest is to deal truthfully in matters of importance 
or triviality, no matter what the consequences. Persons of ex -
treme Honesty are said to have integrity and to be trustworthy, 
scrupulous, and reliable.
It is important to note that Honesty is only expected to -
wards friends, family and generally to those who trust you and 
whom you trust. Deceiving enemies is not considered Deceitful, 
but simply clever. Consider William Shortnose, for example, 
when he enters Orange and the Gloriete tower disguised as a 
Moor, or when he uses the trick of the hand-carts in the con -
quest of Nîmes.
A Deceitful person is generally likely to distort truths, or to 
fabricate untruths, for his own ends (or perhaps on behalf of oth-
ers) or, in some cases, simply for the sake of doing so. Chronically 
Deceitful people are called liars, frauds, and false-hearted.
Famous Characters: Sir Rainouart is noted, among some 
more obvious things, for his Honesty.
Just/Arbitrary
Love justice, so that you may be recognized as just in 
legal matters. Whatever is passed over in lesser persons 
is demanded of those who are greater. Each iniquity or 
injustice returns to him who committed it. It is so among 
kings and dukes, and also among bishops and other prel-
ates who live badly or vainly and who perish not only for 
their own injustices but also cause others to fall headlong 
by allowing their wrongdoings.
— Dhuoda , 4.8
Just means that a character is capable of telling what is right 
and wrong, and is desirous of passing due judgment on that in -
formation. A very Just person is called fair and impartial.
Arbitrary means that the character has no concern for what 
is right or wrong, and uses other information in his decision 
making. V ery Arbitrary people are labeled unjust, unfair, wrong-
ful, and probably biased and partial.
Famous Characters: Duke Naymo of Bavaria is perhaps 
most famous for his extraordinary sense of justice.
Merciful/Cruel
Be merciful. Reveal your mercy and your gentleness 
whenever you take part in the resolution of legal matters. 
For after judgment is given, that excellent mercy always 
finds its place.
— Dhuoda , 4.8
Merciful means that a character is willing to extend unusual 
sympathy, pity or aid to others. This includes sparing an enemy, 
giving money to the poor, helping the weak, and any other act 
that is not expected of one’s rank and station. A very Merciful 
person is called compassionate.
Cruel indicates a disregard for the feelings and needs of 
others, or lack of sympathy. Extreme values indicate the charac-
ter even enjoys others’ discomforts and troubles.
Famous Characters: Sir Pinabel the Gascon is the most 
Cruel Frankish knight around.
Modest/Proud
Fear pride and flee it, and apply profound, honest humil-
ity in all situations to cure this sickness of plague-ridden 
mortality. The humble, truthful maker of the human 
race gives his grace to the humble.
— Dhuoda , 4.3
T o be Modest means that your character is quiet and does 
not seek excessive attention in the recitation of his deeds. He is 
glad to bask in the mere doing of his deeds, rather than repeated-
ly hearing them extolled. V ery Modest people are called humble 
and reserved, perhaps even shy.
Proud means that a character gets pleasure from hearing 
and/or boasting of his deeds. Excessive pride implies arrogance, 
and likely a boastful nature. Pride that is quick to take offense 
and slow to forgive leads to self-perpetuating and often horri -
fying violence, like the atrocious feud described in the story of 
Raoul de Cambrai .
Famous Characters: Sir Ogier the Dane is notorious for 
his great Pride.
Prudent/Reckless
Count William is a man of strong action,
But he is wise, for he knows how to flee,
And when one should change one’s way and detour.
“It is true valor, ” he says, “to protect oneself; 
==================== BOOK PAGE 70 ====================
69
It’s a bad joust that sees many men die.
When one sees that there can be no advance
And that his force will not get him through,
If he remains, you may hold him for a fool
When, for one blow, he would gather a hundred. ” 
— Aliscans , XXI
Prudent means that a character gives thought to what he 
does before he acts. Such a character is called cautious, and exces-
sively Prudent people are called shrewd, circumspect or thought-
ful — or “slow to act.” 
Reckless means that the character acts before he thinks, 
without concern for anything but the immediate consequences. 
Almost any time someone gets a check for acting according to a 
passion they will have acted rashly. An extremely Reckless char-
acter is called careless, or a hothead.
Famous Characters: Sir Roland, Sir Vivien and Sir Huon 
are decidedly rash, while Sir William and Sir Oliver are noted 
for being Prudent and wise.
Temperate/Indulgent
T emperate means that a character takes only what he needs 
of food, drink and other temporal needs. He is frugal and ab -
stains from excess. Extreme T emperance indicates asceticism, 
perhaps even self-mortification.
Indulgent means that a character takes pleasure in food and 
drink, both in quality and quantity. Extremes of this indicate 
gluttony and drunkenness.
Famous Characters: Sir Maugis is the best known 
T emperate knight.
Trusting/Suspicious
T o be T rusting is to believe information without any in -
clination to suspect its verity. An excessively T rusting person is 
gullible and credulous, perhaps even a dupe.
Suspicious indicates that a person is an unbeliever and 
unlikely to believe what he hears unless proof is offered. An ex -
tremely Suspicious person is called a skeptic or a doubter. In some 
cases, Jealously can be included under the trait of Suspicious. 
Jealousy cannot exist without Suspicion. When someone acts 
jealous, they may get a check for their Suspicious.
Famous Characters: Sir Astolf of England is admirably 
T rusting, while Prince Charlot is noted for his Suspicious and 
skeptical behavior.
Valorous/Cowardly
Courage will conquer everything
But wisdom has to be its guide.
— Orlando Innamorato , 3.7.XVI
T o be V alorous means you are brave and courageous, willing 
to place yourself in danger for the sake of victory, your friends, 
or for the simple love of battle. Normal knights are doughty, 
stalwart, and valiant. Extremely heroic individuals are called 
fearless and intrepid. V alor may be exhibited not only in combat, 
but whenever risks to one’s health or wellbeing are involved.
Cowardly means to be fearful and afraid enough to affect 
your actions. Someone who is extremely faint-hearted is labeled 
as a poltroon, dastard, craven, caitiff, or recreant.
Famous Characters: All paladins are well known as the 
most V alorous knights in Frankland and beyond.
Directed Traits
A character may have strong feelings about someone or 
something which modify one or more of their traits. These are 
called directed traits. Directed traits represent strong feelings or 
beliefs, but not strong enough to drive your character mad, as a 
passion might (see Passions, page 72).
In situations where the directed trait might reasonably alter 
your character’s actions or perceptions, he applies its numeric 
value as a modifier on appropriate rolls.
 n Weakness for blondes (adds to Lustful rolls where 
blondes are involved)
 n Mistrust Sir So-and-so (adds to Suspicious rolls where 
Sir So-and-so is involved)
 n Mistrust residents of Narbonne (adds to Suspicious 
rolls where residents of Narbonne are involved)
 n Forgiving of crying women (adds to Forgiving rolls 
where crying women are involved)
 n Unjust towards Mayence clan (adds to Arbitrary rolls 
where the Mayence clan are involved)
 n Loves mead (adds to Indulgent rolls where mead is 
involved)
 n Fears boars (adds to Cowardly rolls where boars are 
involved)
Any number of other directed traits are possible; as you can 
see, some can be quite specific and some seemingly inane—but 
remember that truth is always stranger than fiction.
Specific directed traits are entered in the blanks beneath 
the “T raits” heading of your character sheet. W rite in the specific 
trait which is modified, its object, and the value of the modifier.
Example of Directed Trait: Sir Radehelm has a directed 
trait of Suspicious [Gascons]+3. While on an investigative mis -
sion for his lord he is invited to a hunt by the Gascon lord he is 
visiting. The Gamemaster asks everyone to make a Suspicious roll. 
Radehelm must add his Suspicious [Gascons] value of +3 as a mod-
ifier to his normal Suspicious trait.
Acquiring a Directed Trait
Directed traits can be inherited from your parents, assigned 
by the Gamemaster, or taken voluntarily by the player.
V oluntary directed traits usually have a value of 
+2d6, or whatever value both player and Gamemaster 
agree upon. Gamemasters can assign a directed trait to a 
Traits
==================== BOOK PAGE 71 ====================
Chapter Three: Personality: Traits and Passions
70
character who consistently displays specific prejudices; the value 
of a Gamemaster-imposed directed trait is usually not more than 
+5. When a character dies, his son may take any one directed 
trait at the same value as his father’s when he is knighted. 
Using Traits
T raits define characteristic behavior. When the opportuni-
ty arises to behave one way or another, they can either be used as 
casual guidelines, or rolls may be imposed by the Gamemaster.
Most of the time you simply state what you want your char-
acter to do and he does it, possibly receiving an experience check 
in the process. Sometimes, though, in a non-thinking situation, 
behavior takes precedence over conscious intent. Most of us has 
experienced doing something without thinking, and a trait roll 
duplicates that kind of situation. Modifiers may be used to un -
derscore the dictates of a situation.
t able  3–1: s taNdard  t rait  r esults  
Tr a i t  R o l l Effect
Critical Success An experience check is normally gained, and the 
character acts strongly in accordance with the trait.
Success The character feels the trait enough to act in accor-
dance with it. The player may decide what action 
ensues within that limitation. An experience check 
is gained on the rolled trait if an appropriate and 
significant action follows.
Failure Roll against the opposed trait. Success on this sec -
ond roll means the character acts in accordance 
with that second trait. Failure indicates the play -
er may choose freely how the character acts. No 
checks are given.
Fumble The opposite trait gains a check, and the character im-
mediately acts in accordance with the checked trait.
Interpreting Trait Rolls
Each result of a trait roll has special implications, and high 
or low trait values may influence interpretation. 
Critical Success: A critical success in a trait roll indicates 
that the character must act in the manner described by the trait. 
The action needs not be totally outrageous and extreme, but is 
visible and overt enough to be noticed by others, and to make 
the character feel that he has revealed strong emotions or even 
compromised his beliefs or his integrity in some way.
Y oung King Charlemagne criticals a Proud roll when his wife 
jokes that she finds the Byzantine Emperor Hugo more ele -
gant than her husband: 
“Lady, have you ever seen a man more handsome,
Who wore his sword and crown with more dignity?
Many more cities shall I conquer with my lance!”
The queen, a scatterbrain, answered without thinking: 
“Sweet Emperor, you are over-confident!
I know of one who is of more noble bearing
When he carries his crown amidst his knights; 
And it suits him even better when he puts his on his 
head!”
When the king hears this, he becomes red with anger; 
He puts a sour face, for his men have heard what she said.
“My Queen, please tell me where I can find such a king,
For I wish to wear my crown sitting next to him.
Your friends and courtiers will be present,
And I shall call for all the knights of my court.
If the Franks agree with you, I will humble myself; 
But if you spoke falsely, you will pay for it dearly: 
With my naked steel sword I shall make your head roll!”
— Charlemagne’s Voyage , I
Success: Success in a trait roll indicates that the knight felt, 
and was moved by, the feelings expressed in the trait; if he made 
a Merciful roll he feels that he should grant mercy in this in -
stance. However, the player may choose to have the character act 
in the opposite manner. The penalty for disobeying one’s charac-
ter’s feelings is a check in the opposite trait.
Failure: Use of a trait is not the same as striking with a weap-
on or using a skill. The traits quantify a character’s likelihood to 
act in one of two opposed ways. It is not enough to know that a 
character does not feel merciful; the player must know whether 
the character acts cruelly. The traits define the chances of either.
Failure at a single die roll is not enough to force a character 
to act in the opposite way. The player must check to see if chance 
and statistics force his character to act. Success at either trait has 
determined that the character’s innate behavior has overcome his 
free will. Failure to succeed at both allows the character free will. 
Example of a Failed Trait Roll: Sir Adalhart has accompa-
nied a distressed damsel to her inheritance, which has been seized 
by a wicked uncle. The uncle refuses to return the land and gladly 
agrees to duel for it. The fight is fierce, but at last Adalhart’s enemy 
collapses and begs for mercy. The damsel urges Adalhart to kill her 
foe despite Adalhart’s desire to be merciful. The Gamemaster asks 
for a roll of Adalhart’s Merciful.
Adalhart receives a failed Merciful of 18. Now his player must 
attempt a Cruel trait roll, and that result is also a failure, at 12. 
The choice is up to Adalhart’s player. Given the fact that Adalhart 
hopes to marry this harsh but beautiful woman, the player chooses 
death for the unhappy uncle. The damsel is given revenge, and a 
marriage is planned, but no experience checks are given since both 
rolls failed, even though a very significant event took place.
Fumble: A fumble at a trait roll is the same as a critical in 
the opposed trait: the opposite trait is checked, and the charac -
ter acts in the opposite manner to the original trait.
Example of a Fumbled Trait: Sir Girart (Reckless 14) is 
being chased by some enemy knights. Riding through the hilly for -
est, he encounters a wide chasm — possibly too far for his horse to 
jump. The Gamemaster asks for a Reckless roll, to see if Girart can 
attempt to jump the chasm. Girart’s player rolls a 20 — a fumble. 
==================== BOOK PAGE 72 ====================
71
Instead of taking the chasm at the gallop, he reins in and goes the 
long way around the chasm. Girart must check his Prudent trait. 
Opportunities to Avoid Trait Rolls
Players often become aware of impending personality trait 
rolls, and should take action as needed to keep the game going 
without arguments. They can have their characters avoid conflict, 
but not after the Gamemaster initiates the challenge process.
Example of Trait Roll Avoidance: While lost in the woods 
some months after his marriage, Sir Adalhart finds a brightly-lit tow-
er amid the trees. He asks for refuge and is granted it from the beau-
tiful female host. The player is suspicious, and states that over dinner 
Adalhart tries to find out more about his hostess. His Recognize and 
Faerie Lore skill rolls are successful and Adalhart abruptly realizes he 
is supping with Morgan le Fay, the Lustful faerie queen. 
Fearful for Adalhart’s life and virility, the player announces 
that Adalhart will attempt to avoid all lustful relations with the 
woman, and requests a modifier to Chaste. The Gamemaster grum-
bles, but because Adalhart was forewarned he cannot be imposed 
upon by Morgan’s lascivious beauty. The Gamemaster rules that 
Adalhart’s Chaste trait is modified by the addition of his new Love 
[wife] passion value, raising Chaste above 20 and making it possible 
to overcome Morgan’s advances. Had Adalhart not recognized his 
hostess, then he might not have tried to avoid the affair at all.
Casual Use of Traits
A player often does not know what his character would 
do under specific, non-crucial circumstances. This might be 
because the player never thought about it, doesn’t care, or the 
character has mid-range traits.
In such circumstances the player can simply use the appro -
priate trait(s) as a guideline, or roll 1d20 to determine his be -
havior; if the number rolled is equal or less than the number in 
the left trait then the character follows that trait. Otherwise the 
character uses the right-hand trait. A modifier may be applied if 
the situation warrants one.
Characters don’t automatically receive experience checks 
for actions imposed on themselves this way. The Gamemaster 
might reward characters with a check in this situation, but it is 
still always the Gamemaster’s decision to give out checks.
Example of Casual Trait Use: Sir Adalhart is one among 
seventeen knights riding to hunt a fabled White Deer. A mysterious 
huntress comes from the woods and speaks privately with each man 
while he is resting. On Adalhart’s turn she quietly asks him, “ Are 
you seeking the White Deer?”
A simple question, but coming from such a mysterious charac-
ter it seems fraught with danger. Neither player nor Gamemaster 
knows whether Adalhart’s reply would be truthful or not.
His player rolls 1d20 for his Honest trait and gets an 18, which 
is over the value. Therefore Adalhart replies, “No, not exactly... ” 
She replies, “T ake the left fork on the road, then, ” and moves on.
Qualifying for a Moral Test
Carolingian adventure is full of magical and moral tests. A 
magical shield may be wielded only by a chaste knight, an en -
chanted horn may only be blown by a knight from a certain lin-
eage, and so on.
Some of these tests use absolute trait values. For example, 
only those characters with an Honest trait of 15 or more may 
wield a specific sword or pass through an enchanted doorway. In 
other cases, the character must simply receive a successful unop-
posed 1d20 roll on a trait to determine success. Success gains the 
reward, while failure indicates that the consequences of failing 
the test ensue.
Conflicting Emotions
In some cases, a character may be torn between two warring 
traits (that is, not an opposed trait pair like T rusting/Suspicious, 
but entirely different virtues, such as Energetic and T rusting).
By making an opposed roll between two unrelated traits, 
you may play your character’s emotions off against each other, 
emulating the deep introspection of someone tortured by inter-
nal doubts. Y ou or your Gamemaster may set opposed tests of 
conflicting emotions, requiring you to make several separate un-
opposed trait rolls, with varying results depending on which of 
them was successful and which failed.
Whichever traits are successful (if unopposed) or win (if op-
posed) receive experience checks if approved by the Gamemaster.
Traits
==================== BOOK PAGE 73 ====================
Chapter Three: Personality: Traits and Passions
72
Example of Conflicting Emotion: Sir Adalhart, while wan-
dering through the forest, has been ambushed and attacked by a fe-
rocious knight with a black shield. By dint of much effort Adalhart 
has defeated his foe, who lies helpless on the ground before him and 
cries for mercy. Just as Adalhart is about to spare him a maiden 
rushes from the woods and cries, “Good Sir Knight, I call on you to 
slay this villain. For he has slain his wife, my sister, and all of my 
other sisters as well. As you revere God, do not spare him!”
Adalhart is caught between conflicting emotions. Justice 
demands that he kill this villainous knight, but Mercy calls on 
him to spare his defeated foe. Neither trait has an unusual value 
in Adalhart’s case. So Adalhart gets an opposed resolution roll 
matching his Justice versus his Merciful. His Justice roll is a 4, his 
Merciful a 7, so his mercy wins and Adalhart spares the caitiff. He 
didn’t have the stomach to cold-bloodedly slaughter a helpless man. 
The damsel curses Adalhart for a false knight and leaves. The de -
feated knight swears eternal gratitude to Adalhart for his wisdom 
and charity. 
The Gamemaster decides that the deed deserves a check on 
Merciful.
Personality Challenges and Disputes
Personality challenges between individuals may be de -
termined by opposed resolution rolls of traits. The challenger 
matches his personality against his rival’s.
Example of a Personality Challenge: Sir T urold states that 
Sir Adalhart is too slothful to amount to much. Adalhart challeng-
es T urold to an endurance contest of wearing arms and armor day 
and night until one of them falls asleep. Players of both characters 
attempt Energetic rolls, and both succeed. However, Adalhart’s roll 
of 9 is higher than T urold’s roll of 2. When T urold falls asleep after 
two days and two nights Adalhart is declared the winner. Adalhart 
wins the challenge and gets the experience check (and promptly takes 
a nap).
Because traits have opposites, results are more complex 
when someone fails during opposed trait resolution than is the 
case with simple opposed skill rolls. Whenever a character re -
ceives a failed personality trait roll during a personality dispute, 
his player must attempt to roll the opposite trait. If that roll 
succeeds, then the character acts accordingly, even though this 
means that behavior is not as intended.
The challenged party in a personality dispute who fails 
both trait rolls has free choice to do as they please. They have 
managed to control their inclinations.
Example of a Personality Dispute: Sir Radehelm and some 
of his men are visiting their newly-conquered lands. They are eat -
ing dinner with a converted Saxon lord, Sir Aethelfrith.
With a loud voice Sir Aethelfrith engages the visitors with 
boasts of his courageous martial exploits. After a pause for breath, the 
Saxon asks Sir Radehelm what he has done to compare with such 
bravery. Sir Radehelm hates such boors and so he tries to be Modest 
in response to his host’s Pride. A personality dispute occurs, and an 
opposed resolution is performed. Because the Saxon is so overbearing, 
Sir Radehelm’s player requests and receives a +5 modifier to Modest.
Sir Aethelfrith receives a successful roll of 6 for his Proud trait, 
while Radehelm receives a roll of 18, failing to be Modest. The 
Saxon chuckles over Radehelm’s hesitation.
Since Sir Radehelm failed to be Modest, he attempts to out-
boast his opponent after all. Radehelm’s player rolls a second time, 
this time with no modifier. The player rolls a 9 for Radehelm’s 
Proud trait, a successful result greater than the Saxon’s roll of 6. 
Radehelm speaks harshly of his recent victory in battle over a Saxon 
army, and wins the personality dispute. 
Radehelm gets an experience check for such Proud behavior. 
Although he wanted to remain Modest, his natural personality won 
out over both his initial desire and the influence of the situation.
If Radehelm had received a successful Modest trait roll that 
was inferior to the Saxon’s Proud trait roll he would not have had 
a chance to receive a Proud roll. His humility would not have been 
enough to put down the boastful Saxon, and silence would have 
fallen over Radehelm’s side of the dinner table.
Experience Checks for Traits
One key task of the Gamemaster is to decide when an ac -
tion performed by the player deserves an experience check. The 
action need not have been preplanned as a test or challenge, nor 
need it be the result of a trait or passion roll. 
For instance, if a player decides that his knight will kill a 
peasant who insulted him, the Gamemaster may give that knight 
an experience check for both his Proud and Cruel traits, though 
no resolution rolls took place.
Passions
Nevermore shall I feel joy, without the pain for you.
— The Song of Roland , CCVIII
T
he heroes in the epics regularly faint when they hear bad 
news, or boil with rage and frustration in the face of di -
saster. The Carolingian tales are full of intense emotion, 
much of it uncontrolled. Beautiful women drive men to incredi-
ble and outrageous actions to prove their love. Family feuds turn 
otherwise sane men into wild avengers. A king falls crying to the 
ground when he discovers the body of a fallen paladin.
Passions are strong personal emotions. They include reli -
gion, love, hate, amor, loyalty, envy, and anything else that the 
Gamemaster admits into the game. They provide a method of 
measuring a character’s inner self. They help the character follow 
the morals of his age, and let him benefit from being a notable 
example of proper behavior.
The most universal emotions of Player-knights have been 
isolated for game use. Expect to encounter these in your game. 
These typify the types of feeling felt most intensely by P aladin  
characters. Some of these are so common they appear on the 
character sheet, while blanks are provided for further passions.
==================== BOOK PAGE 74 ====================
73
The Characteristics of Passion
Several characteristics mark a passionate person. Some are 
admirable, others less so. Players must make an effort to play 
their passions actively, and to act in accordance to them.
Passionate people may perform with superhuman effort 
and a greater likelihood of success. This is accomplished through 
the Inspiration rules. Passionate people are volatile. Their feel -
ings may change instantly due to success or failure. Passionate 
people are extreme, and likely to be found in any of several states 
of mind which are not found among dispassionate folk. These 
states include: Inspiration, Introspection, Melancholy, Shock, 
and even Madness. All have effects in game terms.
Initial Passions
Four passions (Honor, Love [Charlemagne], Love [Family] 
and Love [God]) are obligatory because everyone has them, or 
is expected to. They are the unwritten laws of Frankish society.
Honor
Honor is the basis for a feudal society, and it is the passion 
that sets knights apart from commoners. It is a combination of 
dignity, integrity, and credibility. It is required from a knight for, 
among other things, it dictates hospitality to those who visit his 
home, and loyalty to his lord(s).
l oyalty
Serve your lord not only so that you please him in obvi -
ous ways, but also as one clear-headed in matters of both 
body and soul. Be steadfastly and completely loyal to 
him in all things. May the madness of treachery never, 
not once, make you offer an angry insult. May it never 
give rise in your heart to the idea of being disloyal to your 
lord. T ake pleasure in fighting on your lord’s behalf; bear 
patiently the yoke that governs a servant and be faithful 
to your lord — whatever sort of a lord he may be.
— Dhuoda , 3.4–3.8
In the epics, Loyalty is clearly part of one’s Honor: a disloyal 
vassal is honorless. It is impossible for a disloyal vassal to have 
honor, or for an honorable vassal to be disloyal. 
A knight’s initial lord is the one who knights him, and for 
whom he harbors a special passion. A knight may later acquire 
other lands, and therefore other lords as well.
Feudal loyalty is the prime virtue of the medieval world—
without it the feudal system could not exist. Most knights be -
lieve in “King before God, ” no matter what the priests tell them. 
Showing obedience is correct behavior, and disobedience to a 
lord is shocking to all true knights.
HosPitality
We decree that throughout our whole realm no one shall 
dare to deny hospitality to the rich, or to the poor, or to 
pilgrims. If anyone shall wish to do further kindness to 
them, he shall know that his best reward will be from 
God, who said Himself: And who so shall receive one such 
little child in my name, receiveth me. And again: I was a 
stranger and ye took me in. 
— Capitulary , anno 802
If you have fraternal compassion for your neighbors, if you 
offer hospitality, and if you are the steadfast helper of the 
poor and the afflicted, you will have the spirit of piety. 
— Dhuoda , 4.4
W elcoming strangers is a both a duty of Honor and a 
Christian virtue.
Again, you cannot be both honorable and inhospitable at 
the same time. Receiving guests according to both your and their 
status is a matter of Honor, not a thing in itself. Note that many 
evil knights have high Honor and follow the code of knighthood 
(though not chivalry) and refrain from attacking their guests.
For knights, the sense of hospitality extends to visiting en -
emies and hostages held captive in their manor or castle. Enemy 
ambassadors often demand the exchange of hostages as guaran -
tees before delivering their unwelcome message. Hostages must 
be given when preparing a judicial combat.
I have heard that your father has given you as a hostage 
to the lord king. I hope that you acquit yourself of this 
worthy duty with perfect good will.
— Dhuoda , “Prologue”
An extremely honorable knight feels honor-bound to correct 
others’ behavior, perhaps even to seek out and destroy those who 
break the rules of hospitality. If a Player-knight rises to defend 
someone else’s hospitality, he gets a check in Honor (no check is 
given to someone defending their own home, which is expected of 
them, and is not extraordinary enough to warrant it).
On the other hand, only honorless thieves disregard hospi-
tality to the point of stealing without compunction.
In the literature, the murder of a cook is an indirect attack 
against the lord of the place, as a retaliation for his poor hospi -
tality and lack of Honor.
The laws of hospitality have to be respected not only at cas -
tles, but even in a poor man’s cottage, as the anonymous King 
Charlemagne himself is reminded: 
The Coilyear gudlie in feir tuke him be the hand
And put him befoir him, as ressoun had bene; 
Quhen thay come to the dure, the King begouth to stand,
To put the Coilyear in befoir maid him to mene. (pass)
He said, “Thow art uncourtes; that sall I warrand!”
He tyt the King by the nek, twa part in tene, (angrily)
“Gif thow at bidding sud be boun or obeysand,
And gif thow courtasie couth, thow has forget it clene!
Now is anis, ” said the Coilyear, “kynd aucht to creip,
Sen ellis thow art unknawin,
To mak me lord of my awin; 
Passions

Sa mot I thrive, I am thrawin;   (angry)
Begin we to threip. ” (quarrel)
(...)
“Thocht that I simpill be,
Do as I bid the; 
The hous is myne, pardie,
And all that is heir. ” 
(...)
In the name of Sanct July,
That all that wantis harbery,
Suld have gestning.
— The T ale of Ralph Collier
Perso Nal  Ho Nor
Personal Honor is a combination of personal dignity, in -
tegrity, and pride. Personal Honor can be a slippery issue. Ta b l e  
3–2:  Dishonorable Acts lists things which everyone agrees are 
dishonorable actions for a knight. Performing these deeds di -
minishes Honor. This is the code of knighthood which knights 
have agreed to. However, beyond that list disagreement arises 
about what is or is not honorable, because Honor is personal 
rather than social. The term “Personal Honor” is carefully used 
to separate Honor from other sworn or innate social obligations, 
including issues or behavior covered by other passions. It is not 
possible to have one’s sense of honor abused if someone insults 
your family — Love [family] covers that. Likewise, someone in -
sulting your lover should incite the Amor passion, not Honor.
But Honor can still cover many other things. In fact, it can 
include almost anything that a character chooses to include. 
Someone with an extremely high Honor may be offended by 
anything that anyone says that he does not like. Rationality may 
not be relevant.
Honor is tied into the traits more deeply than other pas -
sions usually are. The obligation of sharing one’s home and food 
with others, for instance, comes more easily to Generous char -
acters. The “integrity” component of Honor is closely linked 
to the trait of Honest, and “pride” is obviously linked with the 
Proud trait. A knight with a high trait of Proud might be re -
quired to make a roll by the Gamemaster and, upon losing, the 
player might then invoke the Honor passion (rather than the 
Modest trait) to help him through the event.
d isH o Nor
A dishonorable character suffers considerably in his soci -
ety. He loses the trust of those about him, and in committing 
dishonorable acts probably incurs various punishments ranging 
from fines, banishment, or forfeiture, to a blood feud with the 
family of those he has wronged. These social troubles are further 
reflected by rules concerning this passion.
Whenever a character’s Honor is reduced to 5 or lower, he 
has proved himself unfit to bear the title of knight and serve a 
lord. His lord must either outlaw him or degrade him (i.e. strip 
him of knighthood). T o fail to do so places the lord’s own status 
in jeopardy because he would be failing to uphold his own gov -
ernance. Honor can eventually be regained from this grim point 
in a character’s career.
If a character’s Honor reaches 0 the player must remove him 
from active play. Recovery from such a low state is not possible. 
If the player wishes to see him in the campaign, he must turn the 
character sheet over to the Gamemaster, who can play him as a 
Gamemaster character if he wishes.
Love [Charlemagne] 
It is fitting for you and all those who fight under his royal 
power to serve him to good ends, faithfully, with all your 
might. For as we believe, God chose him and established 
him in royal power, granting him glory.
— Dhuoda , 3.8
Charlemagne is the leader of all Franks and God’s personal 
representative on earth. God helps only Charlemagne and those 
who are loyal to him; therefore, only the use of this passion al -
lows players knights to actively pray for divine aid (see Magic for 
Player-Knights in Chapter 9).
During his reign, many vassals rebel or oppose Charlemagne, 
but in the end they all demand forgiveness and try to make 
peace. The paladins Renaud, Ogier and Huon have more than 
one quarrel with their king, but they, like everybody else, know 
neither their body nor their soul can have peace without recon -
ciliation with Charlemagne.

Huon asks the Pope to bless him before he goes to the Holy 
Land. The Pope agrees on one condition: 
“Dear Huon, ” the Pope replies, “I will not absolve you 
before you will have abandoned all hatred. Therefore 
I command you to immediately and completely forgive 
Charlemagne and those who plotted against you; if you 
do not, I cannot hear your confession nor absolve you. ” 
— Huon of Bordeaux , XVIII
Even more paradoxically, many knights who were neglected or 
unjustly punished by Charlemagne remain fanatically faithful to him.
Despite being a victim of Charlemagne’s harsh punishment, 
a banished count declares his unwavering love and loyalty to 
his king: 
He spoke: “May God forbid!
None can bring me to harm the king.
Even though by wicked counsel
He took my land and banished me,
All my life will I be as good
A friend of his as I can be.
This night I will not do him harm,
For he is my lawful lord.
Did I all but honor him dutifully
Ashamed in God’s eyes would I be.
None should give such evil advice. ” 
— Karel ende Elegast
Love [family] 
You should fear, love, and be faithful to your lord and 
father. If the time comes that God finds someone worthy 
to have children of his own, he will not wish them to be 
rebellious or proud or full of greed, but humble and quiet 
and full of obedience, so that he rejoices to see them. He 
who was a son before, small and obedient to his father, 
may then be fortunate in his own fatherhood. My son, I 
caution you to render first to you whose son you are spe -
cial, faithful, steadfast loyalty as long as you shall live.
— Dhuoda , 3.1
T o the Frankish mind, the bonds of blood promote a uni -
fied, orderly and peaceful society. For, according to Dhuoda, if 
everyone obeys his father, and if the father obeys his lord, and 
if both attend to priestly advice, the many antagonistic forces 
of the world will be locked into a neatly hierarchical and fully 
peaceful configuration.
Love of family is a natural emotion common to mankind 
in any age. The difficulties of travel of the medieval era were se -
vere, and reinforced family closeness. Serfs almost never traveled 
more than a day’s walk from their birthplace. Noblewomen were 
fortunate to travel across the country once a year. T urning to 
one’s kin for help was the universal answer to any problem.
The less fortunate younger sons, sent from the hearth, were 
more likely to find fault with their kin, and so expressed less love 
and loyalty to them. All sons get a Love [family] value equal to 
1d6+10–son number. Female generate a Love [family] value of 
1d6+4.
When Love [family] is applied to distant relatives, like indi-
rect cousins and so on, a –5 modifier may be appropriate.
When he finds out that Huon and Gerard are his relatives, 
the Abbot of Cluny assures them of his deepest sympathy: 
Young gentlemen, you are my friends, because your fa -
ther Sevin was my cousin; I swear on my soul that who -
ever would harm you will find me on his path. So have no 
fear and ride by my side; in Paris I am a member of the 
court of justice: your enemies are bound to be condemned 
to a cruel death, as I will always speak in your favor. I 
give you the keys to my treasure chest, you may take as 
much as you like: marten skins or silken shirts, take what 
pleases you from the treasure of Cluny. 
— Huon of Bordeaux , VII
Love [God] 
Seek Him and pray to Him in short, pure, direct speech. 
Offer your prayer not only in church but wherever cir -
cumstances take you. Pray with your mouth, cry out with 
your heart, ask in your deeds that God comes to your aid 
always, day and night, in every hour and every moment.
— Dhuoda , 2.3
The Franks are a Christian nation, and Charlemagne takes 
it as one of his sacred missions to spread the T rue Faith across 
Europe, by fire and sword, if necessary. As Charlemagne is the 
worldly arm of God, in P aladin  the love for Charlemagne and 
the love for God are closely connected. Still, most knights find 
it easier to follow the battle commands of their king than to un-
derstand the spiritual message of the Church.
Religion plays a large part in the lives of knights. They live 
close to the realm of death and spiritual life provides some com-
fort and guidance for them. Religious attitudes are determined 
by the value of the Love [God] passion: 
Fanaticism: Religious standings are placed before anything 
else, including loyalty to one’s family, lord, or anything else which 
interferes with dedication to the religious life (or dedication to 
the Church). Fanatic knights have a Love [God] score of 16 or 
more. Archbishop T urpin is a good example of such a knight.
Interest: Most people have been brought up on Christian 
ideals and accept them without thinking one way or the oth -
er. They regularly attend church functions, believe what they 
are told, and define the core of the mainstream belief. Most 
Carolingian nobles see Christian devotion as central to their life. 
Their Love [God] ranges from 11 to 15.
Indifference: Some knights of Charlemagne’s era show 
monumental indifference towards their religion, just as most 
Passions
==================== BOOK PAGE 77 ====================
Chapter Three: Personality: Traits and Passions
76
people do today and probably always have. They are subject to 
its invisible cultural influence, but don’t really care. They might 
attend mass regularly, or as needed, but think it’s probably a 
waste of time. Their Love [God] ranges from 6 to 10.
Hostility: Some knights hate the church and plunder it 
with glee. Reasons can vary widely, and a few examples of these 
types appear in literature, like Raoul de Cambrai . Their 
Love [God] is lower than 6. If a Player-knight’s Love [God] 
score drops to 5, he is automatically excommunicated and can no 
longer benefit from prayers and other types of Christian magic.
As explained in the section on Magic for Player-Knights in 
Chapter 9 , pious Player-knight may benefit from the force of 
prayers from other Christians.
Inspiration from the Love [God] passion may only be 
gained if a religious issue is at stake. Participation in a Crusade 
declared by an archbishop or the Pope is rewarded by an imme -
diate gain of 1 point in Love [God].
The Pope proclaims a holy war: 
“By striking blows with blades of steel unchecked
Your sins will be absolved and your souls blessed; 
There is no doubt of this — you have my pledge; 
Rise up at once sweet Jesus to avenge!
You will be saved — or may I go to hell!”
— Song of Aspremont , I
T o the Christian mind, such a holy war is an act of penance 
in itself, no matter how bloody and murderous it may be. Killing 
pagans in battle is not a sin; quite the contrary — but only if it is 
done for the love of God.
“While I’ve good arms and a good horse beneath me
Why would I not strike mighty blows for Jesus
And pay to God the debt of all believers?
Body and soul I’ll give him here quite freely,
Prepared to die for Him as He for me did. ” 
— Song of Aspremont , IV
Other Common Passions
The most common passions of player characters are de -
scribed in this section. Expect to encounter these in your game. 
Other passions are certainly possible.
Amor [person] 
The conventions of fin’amor, or Courtly Love , detailed 
in Chapter 13: Frankish Society , insist upon the utter devotion 
of the knight to his lady. According to the laws of Romance, a 
knight may have only one Amor passion at any given time. The 
Amor passion must remain chaste (as opposed to being con -
summated by sexual relations) — if the amor relationship is no 
longer totally chaste, then the relationship becomes one of Love 
[amor], as described below. 
The name of the Amor should be kept secret. The 
Gamemaster must be informed as to the identity of the lady, but 
the other players need not be told. Between each other, courte -
ous lovers use code names. When the Amor’s name is no longer 
a secret, it should be written into the brackets.
An Amor [person] value starts at 1d6+6. The following 
modifiers are common for this passion: 
t able  3–3: a Mor  Modifiers
Circumstance Modifier (max +10): 
The Amor has significant Glory +1 per 1,000 Glory 
for a W oman, +1 per 
5,000 for a Man 
The Amor is a stunning beauty +1 per APP point 
over 15
The Amor saved the knight’s life +5
The knight saved the Amor from a dire fate +5
The Amor is from an enemy family or 
culture
–1
Commonly, would-be Romantic knights travel about seek-
ing one to be their amor. In such a case the knight may make a 
roll for their starting passion whenever he meets or hears of an 
eligible maiden or lady, and ignore any rolls that are below 16 if 
they wish. However, any roll of 16 or greater is a true passion, 
and cannot be discarded. Once the character decides on an amor 
(whether by rolling 16 or more, or simply deciding on an appro-
priate object of his affections), he may not change it — if you 
are going to play the game of Love, you must obey it’s strictures! 
The only exception to this rule is if your Amor dies. A knight 
may seek another love — after a suitable period of mourning, 
of course. 
The Gamemaster may generate a Potential Amor for the ob-
ject of the knight’s affections. If the lady’s Amor is under 10, she 
will flirt with him, but never intend anything more. An Amor 
of 10–15 means that she is intrigued, and may be swayed by the 
knight’s efforts. An Amor of 16+ means she may toy with the 
knight, even be cruel to him (within the boundaries of fin’amor), 
but never has any intention other than to progress through the 
stages of fin’amor with him.
A lady may have several knights vying for her affections, 
and need not generate an Amor for any of them. In fact, the 
Gamemaster should never reveal the value of a lady’s Amor — a 
knight should not know if his affections are shared or not. The 
female side of the romance is in control, and is definitely “play -
ing the game.” She is gratified by the attention paid her, but the 
rules of the game ensure that she is supreme in the relationship. 
Amor is usually advanced during the Winter Phase with the 
Romance Solo, or through adventures during play. Only once a 
knight wins the heart of his lady through his heroic efforts will 
her affection turn to Love, and she will consent to attempt the 
essai with him. If the essai is successful, she will generate a Love 
[knight] for him, or convert her current Amor passion to Love. 
==================== BOOK PAGE 78 ====================
77
If a knight is utterly rejected by his lady — for failing the es -
sai, for example, he has “lost the game” — he may never have an -
other amor. And her Amor can turn to Hate in the blink of an eye!
Note that Amor need not be reciprocated, or even rec -
ognized; a knight or lady may develop an Amor for someone 
they only saw briefly at a tournament, or have heard rumors of, 
without ever actually meeting. Such an unreciprocated amor can 
still be used as a source of inspiration, and an infatuated knight 
may even impose tasks from the Lover’s T asks table (page 434) 
on himself to win the heart of his lady. Once the lovers meet, 
generate the other’s Amor as normal, with a +1 bonus for every 
self-imposed task the knight has successfully performed. 
Love
Love outlaws avarice and wrath: 
Love lets the heart confront bold tasks.
— Orlando Innamorato , 2.4.II–/III
Love is an emotional bonding or attraction felt by one in -
dividual for another individual or group. A character may have 
many loves, but only one counts for Glory points each year, nor-
mally the highest.
Love must always be for someone. It is not possible to love 
an abstraction or activity. Thus there is no Love [country] or 
Love [fighting] passion possible.
Count William Shortnose fights for the love of his Lady 
Orable: 
“God, ” says the count, “now I will prove myself !
But, by Him, who holds all in His keeping,
I will not stop, if my head is cut off,
If I must be cut apart while I live,
Without fighting them now in front of her.
For her love, I must endanger myself; 
In order to exalt and lift high God’s law,
I must endure pain and toil with my body. ” 
— Aliscans , XLIX
God is love, and love may inspire knights to heroic great -
ness. In the epics, the presence of love for a lady is in itself al -
most magical. But apart from a source of motivation, it brings 
on some chaotic effects on enamored paladins. Their heart-felt 
emotions for the lady of their dreams tends to lead them away 
from their duties to their king or their family (i.e. to neglect oth-
er passions), and towards the glorious adventures they wish to 
undertake in her name.
Count Roland and his friend Renaud have many adven -
tures as the result of their falling deeply in love.
No strong arm, no audacity,
No blade well-honed, no shield or mail,
No other power can avail,
For in the end Love conquers all.
— Orlando Innamorato , 1.1.II
Love [amor]: The Amor [person] passion, as noted above, 
often becomes a Love [amor]. The term [amor] may stand in for 
a name here, indicating it is a secret affair. Secret or not, a Love 
[amor] indicates that an affair has been culminated in physical 
and carnal commitment.
A character may have either Amor or Love for a person, but 
not both.
The value of the Love [amor] passion is the same as the Amor 
[person] when amor changes to love. No new number is rolled.
As long as the name of the lover is secret, the Gamemaster 
must be told who that person is, but other players need not be 
informed. If the name of the lover is revealed, write the name 
onto the character sheet.
Love [spouse]: Deep feeling and attraction for one’s hus -
band or wife was apparently quite uncommon in the feudal world 
of arranged marriages, but not absent. A significant exception in 
the epics is the love of Count William for his wife Orable.
The starting value for a Love [spouse] passion is 3d6, and 
the modifiers listed above for Amor may be used.
Hate
Hatred motivates many people in P aladin , especially pol-
troons who are driven to dastardly deeds. For instance, Ganelon 
assuredly hates his step-son Roland. Even some of the good guys 
develop a hatred because of their Love [family]. The best in -
stance is Ogier’s unrelenting hatred of Prince Charlot, brought 
about by Charlot’s slaying of Baldwin, Ogier’s beloved son.
Due to the long-lasting and bloody wars against the pagans, 
many knights strongly hate the Saracens, the Saxons or the Danes.
Hate may be for an individual, a people, a kingdom, a reli -
gion, giants or goblins, magicians or monks, a station or class, or 
whatever the Gamemaster agrees to.
Starting Hate values are up to the Gamemaster, but should 
have a value of at least 10. A Hate value should be based on the 
event that provoked the passion.
Fear
Fear is a negative emotion that can be inherited as a family 
curse, but is normally gained only through personal experience 
(that is, through game play). A Fear is an irrational and abso -
lute, mindless state of panic. Only extraordinary adventures can 
instill such terror in knight characters (lesser characters may be 
more vulnerable). Such Fear usually stems from supernatural 
places or creatures. Fear is often wisdom in disguise, since many 
supernatural creatures are immensely powerful and mean only 
harm to humans. Some sample Fears might be for hags, sailing, 
sea monsters, crazed holy men, or standing stones that move.
Note: Fears give no benefits. They are an exception to the 
normal rules. There is no Glory gained, and no Inspiration pos-
sible. A Fear only places the character out of the player’s control 
under specific situations.
The Gamemaster may create an opportunity to overcome a 
Fear. Such a chance probably only comes once, and if the character 
Passions
==================== BOOK PAGE 79 ====================
Chapter Three: Personality: Traits and Passions
78
succeeds in overcoming his Fear, he may gain Glory for it. This Glory 
gift should be about ten times the value of the character’s former Fear.
Gaining a Passion
Passions may be gained during play. Plenty of opportuni -
ties will be given to gain enemies, lovers, and loyalties. Both the 
player and the Gamemaster should agree upon passions. When 
something significant occurs to a character the Gamemaster 
or player may suggest that a passion has been generated. They 
should discuss the matter and then, if an accord is reached, deter-
mine the character’s starting passion value — an entirely subjec -
tive amount agreed upon by both player and Gamemaster.
Using Passions
Invoking a passion is a good way for the Gamemaster to 
add excitement to a scenario. But passion rolls are a risky busi -
ness for players. The results vary, but are likely to be dramatic. 
Normally a passion roll results in a particular state of mind, such 
as Inspiration or Madness. See States of Passion, below.
Benefits and Disadvantages
A knight seeks courage and inspiration from his Lady: 
He quickly had a herald sent
To the fair lady in the town,
Requesting her to mount the walls,
To give him twice the heart to fight.
— Orlando Innamorato , 1.11.IV
Passions define the character’s life intentions. Characters 
who share similar passions have much in common. Passions such 
as Honor provide common ground between strangers and make 
introductions easier.
Of all that passions do, most notable is that they serve to 
inspire their possessors. Rules for the state of Inspiration follow.
Famous passions generate Glory. Each winter, a character 
receives Glory for most high-value passions. Only passions with 
a value of 16 or more generate Glory, and several restrictions and 
exceptions apply.
The Gamemaster may call for a passion roll, possibly with 
a modifier for the particular situation. At other times the player 
may request a roll, with the Gamemaster’s approval. Remember 
that the Gamemaster has final word on the appropriateness of 
attempting to use a passion for Inspiration. Players are warned 
that passion rolls can be extremely risky as well as rewarding; 
passions may subject a knight to several unusual states of mind, 
including Introspection, Melancholy, and Madness.
Use the T able 3–4: Standard Passion Results for all passions 
except prayers using Love [Charlemagne] or Love [God], which 
are rolled on the T able 9–2: Prayer Results (see Magic for Player-
Knights in Chapter 9).
States of Passion
i NsPiratio N
T o be Inspired is the object of passion. Inspiration can turn 
an ordinary character into an extraordinary one. Inspiration is 
the source of the greatness which the apparently superhuman 
paladins exhibit.
The result of this inspiration is a vastly increased chance of 
success in skill use. All skills temporarily gain +5 for a normal suc-
cess; +10 for a critical passion roll. The duration of Inspiration is 
for the length of the task at hand, but never more than a day.
s Hock
Shock occurs when a knight fails to perform a deed that he 
was Inspired to perform. Normally, Shock means that the char -
acter must immediately make one roll on T able 10–1: Aging. 
The Gamemaster determines whether failure occurred.
The Gamemaster may impose Shock on characters (with 
or without their first being inspired) in other appropriate situ -
ations as well. A likely circumstances for imposing such a state 
might be after the knight has just abandoned a lord or a lover to 
grave danger or a dire fate, or betrayed their family.
d isHearteNed
A Disheartened knight suffers a –5 modifier on all further 
rolls made during the situation that brought on his state. Once 
the situation passes, he then becomes Melancholic.

==================== BOOK PAGE 80 ====================
79
MelaNcH oly
Melancholy is a mental disease that strikes without appar -
ent cause. When it manifests the victim is overwhelmed by grief. 
He falls to the ground weeping aloud, lamenting his losses and ill 
luck, crying out from his deep emotional pain. He may fall into 
a deep and silent depression. Melancholy usually lasts for a day.
Melancholy usually comes about as a result of an unsuccess-
ful passion result. The Gamemaster may impose melancholy on 
characters in other appropriate situations as well.
If disturbed by a man, the knight will fly into a violent rage, 
letting his misery explode without regard to the identity of the 
offender. The Melancholic knight is Inspired during this, hav -
ing a +10 to whatever combat skill he uses. After 1d6 rounds 
of combat the rage subsides and the knight becomes aware of 
whatever he has done.
If a woman disturbs the Melancholic knight, he won’t awak-
en from his misery, but can be led around by her and be tended 
as if he were a sick pet, until he awakens.
The knight naturally awakens from his Melancholy on his 
own, but it requires a number of weeks equal to the passion that 
provoked the dismal mental state.
s NaP  o ut  of  i t !
It’s possible to intentionally and abruptly awaken a 
Melancholic knight, or even a Mad one, or the one lost in the 
senseless bliss of love that overcomes amorous knights. It is dan-
gerous, and knights ought to be armored—they are going to be 
attacked without warning, and it is impossible for them to react 
in time.
W e use the term “victim” for whomever is in the profound 
state, and “healer” for the person who is attempting to fix the 
other one.
The intent is to shock the victim out of his state with an 
abrupt, mentally or emotionally provocative statement. He will 
fly into a rage, but calm down in 1d6 rounds.
The healer must defeat the victim’s passion that put him 
into his miserable state. The healer may use any passion or trait 
that he has which might penetrate the victim’s feelings, even if it 
is to provoke and anger him. V aguely appropriate passions may 
incur a penalty to the passion score (–5 or –10), depending on 
the Gamemaster ruling.
Attempt an opposed resolution between the victim’s pas -
sion that brought on the state, and the healer’s passion or trait.
 n If the Healer wins, then the condition is ended and 
the victim snaps out of it.
 n If the Victim wins, then he flies into a rage and at -
tacks the Healer.
 n If it is a tie result, then nothing happens.
 n If the Healer’s roll fails, it is the same as a failed pas -
sion roll: he loses 1 point of the passion or trait, and 
becomes Melancholy. If he fumbles, he loses 1 point 
and goes Mad.
 n If the Victim fumbles, he becomes entirely Mad and 
dashes away, throwing off his armor, tearing off his 
clothing, and the Gamemaster puts his character into 
that special file labeled “Not Dead Y et. ” 
A gentle friend helps Sir Huon to overcome his Melancholy 
by opposing Huon’s Love [family] with his own Love [God]: 
All day long Huon laments and loudly regrets his home -
land; he cries out for his beloved mother, the fair duchess, 
but his companions try to calm him down: 
“You weep too much and your face is so full of sadness; 
please stop, for the Virgin’s sake!”
“Sirs, by God almighty, I have such sorrow that I shall 
soon go mad — because I was unjustly chased from my 
lands I am obliged to go adventuring like this. ” 
There was a knight from the Ardennes; he turns to Huon 
and speaks to him gently: 
“Sir, by the blessed Virgin, it happened to me to lose Niv-
elles; the duke, my liege, chased me from my lands be -
cause I had killed one of his vassals. Now, hear what I 
became of: the duke died, his son asked my friends to in -
vite me back to his court, and he restituted my fief. Dear 
sir, the same fate could be yours; you are a paladin from 
a very noble and respected house, so you may one day re-
trieve what you think is now lost.
Don’t give in to sorrow, you who are the paragon of youth, 
but humbly ask God, who created the world, to guide you 
amidst our enemies; have faith in St. Mary who bore Je -
sus, for those she aids have nothing to fear. ” 
— Huon of Bordeaux , XIX
MadNess
A character may be driven Mad by his passions. This 
Madness may occur at once, or once the relevant action is over 
(at the Gamemaster’s discretion). Once Madness sets in, he must 
immediately give the character sheet to the Gamemaster, who 
describes what ensues, based on what the other player characters 
know or can perceive. 
Normally, Mad characters run away immediately. For the 
duration of their Madness they’ll attempt to avoid the scene of 
their disastrous experience at all cost. 
During the Winter Phase, a madman may attempt the 
Madness Solo Scenario (page 431) to see if his Madness ends.
When Roland discovers that Angelica prefers another knight 
to himself, he goes crazy
Weary and heart-stricken, he dropped onto the grass and 
gazed mutely up at the sky. Thus he remained, without 
food or sleep while the sun three times rose and set. His 
bitter agony grew until it drove him out of his mind.
On the fourth day, worked into a great frenzy, he stripped 
off his armor and chain-mail. (...)
Now began the great madness. (...) He fell into a frenzy so 
violent that his every sense was darkened. (...) At one jerk

he rooted up a tall pine, after which he tore up several 
more as if they were so many celery-sticks. (...)
And some shepherds were attracted by the noise, whether 
by their stars, or by some wicked misdeed of theirs. When
