When he recovers, the madman will have undergone un -
usual, unknown tests that can result in changes to some statistics 
at the Gamemaster’s option. Players should simply accept these 
changes, which may not always be negative. The character will 
be unaware of what has been experienced, but may search for the 
lost knowledge, and may eventually determine what happened 
to him during his period of madness. 
i NtrosPectio N
How Brunello stole the horse Frontino as King Sacripant was 
lost in Introspection
Brunello said, 
“Who’s this, asleep upright, and with so fine a steed?” 
I’ll make him keep alert next time!”
And saying this, he seized a stump,
quickly untied the horse’s girth, 
and set the saddle on the trunk.
Sacripant never notices.
The thief flees, leaving him unhorsed.
— Orlando Innamorato,  2.5.XXXIX–XL
Knights and ladies in love periodically fall into a dreamlike 
daze while contemplating the virtues of their fair amor. They get 
a distant look in their eyes and totally ignore everything going 
on around them. This can happen anytime they are not actively 
doing something important. It isn’t uncommon to see a passion-
ate knight riding his horse blindly down the road in a rainstorm 
while viewing with his mind’s eye the countless virtues of the 
pale hand of his lady fair.
The Gamemaster may call for an Amor or Love [amor] passion 
roll once per game day from a given character with either of those 
passions. A critical success indicates that the character becomes 
Introspective, but has none of the other effects typical of a passion 
roll. For 4d6 minutes, the character enters an almost hypnotic state; 
during this time, he or she cannot make any Awareness, Heraldry, 
Recognize, or other perception or knowledge-type skill rolls. 
While lost in Introspection, the knight is Inspired normally 
(+5 to skills), but only to defend themselves against anyone who 
attacks them.
Group Inspiration
Roland addresses his comrades and tries to inspire them with 
a noble speech: 
“It is our duty to be here for our king: 
For his lord a vassal must suffer hardships
And endure great heat and great cold; 
And he must lose both hair and hide.
Now let each man take care to strike great blows,
So that no one can sing a shameful song about us.
The pagans are wrong and the Christians are right.
No dishonorable tale will ever be told about me. ” 
— Song of Roland , LXXIX
A knight may try to inspire his comrades (a group of 
at least three knights) by making a Group Inspiration roll 
against a passion they all share. He rolls the die once, and 
that number is compared to all the knights’ passion scores 
individually. The next step is to determine the average group 
result, which is applied to all group members, independently 
of the individual results.
Example of Group Inspiration: Adalhart tries to inspire 
his friends, Radehelm and T urold, using Honor in a contest 
at court. Adalhart’s player rolls the die and obtains a 17. For 
Adalhart, this is a critical success, for Radehelm it indicates a 
normal success, whereas T urold obtains a normal failure. The 
mean result is a normal success, and this result applies to all 
three knights.
Frivolous Passion Rolls
Players often request passion rolls in inappropriate or bor -
derline circumstances, hoping for Inspiration. If a player insists 
on rolling his passion, is Inspired, but then fails to win his goal 
(defeat an enemy, win a lady’s heart, capture the stag, whatever), 
then the character becomes Shocked, due to grief and frustration.
This helps to keep players from abusing the power of 
passions.
Mandatory Passion Rolls
Glorious passions place your character at the mercy of the 
Gamemaster and the scenario. As noted in Notable T raits and 
Passions (page 66) , values of 16 or higher require mandatory 
rolls whenever the Gamemaster chooses.
Lowering a Passion
Once gained, a passion can be lowered or replaced (see 
Amor, page 76). 
Reducing a passion willingly is normally something per -
formed during the Winter Phase (see Chapter 10 ). It is as -
sumed that the character spends time loudly disclaiming his 
==================== BOOK PAGE 82 ====================
81
former passion in public, and acting in every way contrary to 
the passion. A Player-knight may lower its value by 3 points 
during the Winter Phase.
A passion can eventually — one Winter Phase at a time — 
be eliminated (i.e., reduced to a value of 0) in this way.
A character is almost certain, at some time, to receive a 
failed passion roll in time of a crisis. This failure may cause an 
immediate loss of 1 point in the associated passion. Always ask 
the Gamemaster before you subtract the point, however (some 
circumstances may not warrant the reduction).
Oaths
William Shortnose takes a solemn vow: 
“By the faith that I owe to my love,
I shall eat no more bread made out of flour,
No salted meat, I shall drink no more wine,
Until I have seen how Orange is set.
And I must see the tower of marble,
And lady Orable, the gracious queen.
Love of her has me so in its power,
That I could not describe or conceive it.
If I can’t have her soon, I shall lose my life. ” 
— Conquest of Orange, X
An oath is a promise made under the witness of God; but 
in game terms, oaths are handled using the Honor passion, not 
Love [God]. An oath is the most sacred form of promise, and 
cannot be broken except with the most serious consequences. T o 
Christians, oath-breaking is lying to God, with its subsequent 
threat of eternal damnation in Hell. 
Just as, or more, important are the more immediate so -
cial consequences. Normal people shun oath-breakers. A man’s 
sworn word is one of the few possession that he has after all ma-
terial goods are taken away; it measures his soul and personality. 
A breaker of oaths has a shriveled and tiny soul, is not to be trust-
ed, and forfeits the rights that he had as a member of society. 
Since all of society is based upon oaths and keeping one’s word, 
anyone who fails in this fails to uphold society and, therefore, 
cannot be part of it. 
Oaths can be taken literally or figuratively. Most com -
mon people look to the spirit of the oath to be fulfilled, but 
intellectuals sometimes allow only the letter to be fulfilled. 
Such misunderstandings are the cause of much friction be -
tween classes.
Player-knights can take an oath to gain Honor. They can 
have only one active oath at a time.
Positive oaths: When a Player-knight takes an important 
and extraordinary vow to do something, the player must make 
an appropriate passion roll. Points of Honor are at stake, de -
pending on the result (critical: 3; success: 2; failure: 1; fumble: 0).
T aking a voluntary pilgrimage counts as a vow, as does 
participation in a crusade. Swearing to convert and marry a 
pagan princess, or to liberate one’s lord from a pagan prison, 
also qualify.
Once a character fulfills his oath, he gains the staked Honor 
points. On the other hand, if he fails the points are subtracted 
from his score.
Negative oaths: A Player-knight may make a vow to not, 
or never, do something. In the case of an appropriate negative 
vow, a passion roll must be made just like for a positive oath. 
The character immediately gains the Honor points, but should 
he ever break his vow, he will somehow die.
Sir Vivien, for example, was known for his reckless vow to 
never retreat against the Saracens. At the battle of Aliscans, he 
unwittingly recoiled and later died a hero’s death on the field.
Passion versus Trait
A player may temporarily reinforce certain traits by in -
voking an appropriate passion. In these cases, a player may roll 
against his passion score instead of the trait value.
The honorable Sir Landri succeeds in remaining Chaste us -
ing his Honor when a lovely Byzantine princess slips naked 
into his bed: 
“Do with me as you please, none shall speak ill of it; 
If I die of love, I am sure my soul shall be saved. ” 
“Lady, ” replies Landri, “what are you saying?
Surely you have awakened love in me, presenting yourself 
thus,
But I know all too well that your father is my lord; 
You’d better go back to your own bed, my dear friend. ” 
— Doon of La Roche, v.2725–2730

Chapter Four: Reputation: 
Glory and Standing
K
eeping track of character reputation is an important 
function. Characters with similar Glory may have very 
different reputations, some good, some bad, some sim -
ply colorful. Standings represent the viewpoint of society. They 
quantify how particular persons or groups feel about a Player-
knight, which allows players to more or less predict their reac -
tions, and ask them for help.
What Is Glory?
T
he fundamental object of the game is to gain Glory. 
Knights receive Glory for doing the things that knights 
do, whether it is according to the ancient warrior virtues 
or the high ideals of chivalry and fin’amor. These acts include 
notable actions and behavior, and attaining ambitions. 
Glory is the chief mode of reward in the game. The 
Gamemaster always awards Glory. Players are free to ask for 
Glory at anytime during the game, but the Gamemaster is in 
charge of who gets Glory, when they get it, and how much they 
receive. This chapter contains guidelines for the Gamemaster, 
not exact rules that must be followed slavishly.
Glory measures a character’s fame, success, confidence, im-
portance, influence, and status. Characters don’t know numbers, 
only reputation. A person’s Glory is expressed in minstrel’s songs, 
court gossip, peasant chatter, the prayers of holy men, enemies’ 
curses, traders’ tales, and nobles’ praises. People are aware of each 
other’s Glory rating because they all participate in the same society 
and hear the same things about each other. But while characters 
don’t know their exact number, everyone is extremely conscious of 
their status relative to others. The Glory value is a quantification 
of your character’s position in comparison to others. 
Glory does not measure the quality of a character’s rep -
utation. Reputation is the purpose of the traits, passions and 
I urge you to strive to act among your peers and friends so that 
your life may hold to a good course, marked with no shame of 
disloyalty to your lords but with eagerness of good action, in a 
proper fashion, worthy of praise and glory.
— Dhuoda , 3.9
standings system. A glorious knight may walk about proudly, 
lording it over other knights, yet be despised for his cowardly or 
treacherous deeds. Count Ganelon is an excellent example of such 
a character. It is important for players to understand that, since 
Glory measures status but not reputation, evil knights who are 
extremely successful attain the same Glory as Chivalrous knights. 
Evil knights lose reputation, not Glory, for their vile deeds.
How is Glory Gained?
Characters get Glory by many different means in P aladin ; 
players may choose entirely different paths to Glory for their 
characters. This promotes roleplaying and ensures that charac -
ters are not all the same, or competing for the same rewards.
Any dramatic event during the game offers a potential 
Glory award. Routine events rarely gain much Glory. Sometimes 
simple participation in a significant event is enough, but often 
successful action is required for Glory to be received.
Almost any action that an ambitious knight performs can gain 
Glory. Examples of events that normally gain Glory include defeat-
ing an enemy in personal combat, getting married, being made a 
lord, successfully exhibiting a skill at court, spending a great deal of 
money, and finding the solution to an eerie magical riddle.
Glory can even be gained from events or circumstances 
that occur in the background, outside of game sessions, with -
out the character’s direct attention or interaction. Ownership 
of land and castles gains the owner Glory every year, as do no -
toriety and reputation.
Because society is concerned with personal behavior as well 
as combat and adventure, high passions and faithful service to 
Chivalrous, Romantic, or Religious ideals can gain much Glory. 
In P aladin , behavior and reputation are quantified using vari -
ous character attributes and codes of behavior.
Glory, once gained, cannot be lost.
==================== BOOK PAGE 85 ====================
Chapter Four: Reputation: Glory and Standing
84
Who Can Gain Glory?
Any character, whether peasant or king, may gain Glory. 
Both characters would gain the same amount of Glory for the 
same action. However, knights and higher-ranking members of 
the noble class are very jealous of their prerogatives. They alone 
do the deeds of knights. Squires, sergeants, and other characters 
below the status of knight may frequently be denied the oppor -
tunity to gain Glory.
Sir Bertrand asks his illustrious uncle William if he may 
ignore the usual Glory precedence: 
“Uncle, ” he says, “we suffer too much shame.
All battles and combats you quickly claim.
Beside your valiant deeds, ours are in vain.
This combat, sire, I would undertake: 
grant me this honor, uncle, by your grace. ” 
— The Coronation of Louis , LIX
Most courtly situations in which Glory can be gained are 
off-limits to characters of less than knightly status. For example, 
feasts and tournaments are held for knights and their ladies only. 
Squires are expected to serve, not dance with the ladies or show 
off their skills before the court.
On adventures, sergeants or squires must be under the or -
ders of a knight; either a Player-knight or a Gamemaster knight. 
As the premier warriors, knights are expected to do most of the 
fighting, while their squires render assistance by fetching new 
lances and horses, providing first aid, and fighting off foot sol -
diers and other rabble. Squires and sergeants are expected to 
fight when the combat is unchivalrous or when the knights ask 
for help. But in many cases only the knights fight, and they get 
all the Glory. During combat, any sergeant or squire who rudely 
shoulders aside knights in order to grab Glory may be declared 
outlaw, or simply killed immediately. In non-combat situations, 
a presumptuous sergeant or squire will simply be removed from 
the room, or placed in a dungeon if he resists. Gamemasters 
must enforce these points consistently to maintain the authen -
ticity of the game.
Ranges of Glory
Once your character begins to accumulate Glory, you may 
ask: “how much is a lot?” The nearby T able 4–1: Glory Ranking 
answers this question, and defines the range of Glory for all play-
er and Gamemaster characters.
The high end is left open; awesome heroes such as Roland 
are far superior in Glory to almost all other knights in Frankland, 
with good reason. It is unlikely that Player-knights will attain 
Glory similar to that of King Charlemagne or Count Roland.
Players should set themselves realistic goals, and not ex -
pect the Gamemaster to let their characters become famous 
after only a few adventures. Only characters who have been 
through scores of adventures can hope to attain the status of 
Extraordinary Knight. A very active and very successful knight 
errant should collect around 100–200 Glory per adventure, 
and perhaps another 100 at the end of the year for maintaining 
ideals and passions.
t able  4–1: g lory  r aNkiNg  
Glory Class of Knight
1–999 Non-Knight (squire, damsel)
1,000–1,999 Ordinary Knight
2,000–3,999 Notable Knight
4,000–7,999 Famous Knight
8,000 or more Extraordinary knight
Glory Awards
E
xactly how much Glory should a character be awarded 
for performing significant actions and participation in 
great events? In the section below are guidelines, but not 
absolute rules. P aladin  is a social game, where Glory represents 
good opinions, not something palpable like gold. So to a great 
extent Glory is dependent on the reaction of the Gamemaster 
and the other players to your roleplaying.
If you impress everyone with your character’s actions, 
chances are that you will receive more Glory. If your character’s 
actions and speeches during the game are dull, or if you rely on a 
powerful friend to gain attention and success, you should expect 
to receive less than normal Glory.
Group Glory Awards
Glory is often attained by a lone individual, but not always. 
Sometimes the Player-knights must cooperate to achieve some 
task or goal. The Gamemaster divides the Glory award among 
several characters whenever it is clear that more than one indi -
vidual was responsible for a success. For example, if five char -
acters cooperate to defeat a group of bandits, each of the five 
would receive some of the credit.
The Gamemaster always determines the division of Glory. 
Usually Glory is divided equally among all the participants, with 
an extra portion awarded to any deserving individual for hero -
ic behavior. But the Gamemaster may choose to award most of 
the Glory to one of the participants, with the rest receiving very 
small awards. The wise Gamemaster confers with the players on 
this, but his decision is final.
If a player wishes, a character may assign his portion of 
the Glory to someone else. The Gamemaster should award a 
Generous and/or Modest check to the character for such an 
action.
How Much Glory Is Enough?
T o determine the appropriate Glory award Gamemasters 
need to take several things into account.
First, determine the appropriate category for the event: 
minimum, ordinary, heroic, or extraordinary. Situations may 
==================== BOOK PAGE 86 ====================
85
even occur in which important events take place but zero Glory 
is gained. This is because the action taken, though successful, 
was dishonorable.
T able 4–2: Basic Glory Awards table shows the four basic, 
non-zero categories, while T able 4–3: Sample Glory shows many 
examples (many of which do not fit the four categories exactly).
The Gamemaster should use the examples given in this chapter as 
guidelines for awarding Glory in the special situations that arise.
Many successes in the game are not worth Glory, and those 
that are usually gain a character only ordinary Glory: 10 points. 
But the high points in a character’s career, like the ceremony of 
knighthood, are accompanied by the maximum Glory award 
of 1,000 points. An occasion from which more than 1,000 
points can be gained will be unique and deadly (the Battle of 
Roncevaux, for instance).
Gamemasters will be tempted to award excessive Glory 
during moments of excitement and drama. Keep to the guide -
lines printed here: once a bad precedent is set, players expect 
that much Glory in subsequent games, and Glory inflation can 
ruin a campaign very quickly.
t able  4–2: b asic  g lory  a wards
Category Glory Gained
Mundane 0
Minimum 1
Ordinary 10
Heroic 100
Extraordinary 1,000
Mundane: Some actions are beneath notice in the game, 
and do not accrue Glory. For instance, a knight does not gain 
Glory simply for drawing his sword from his scabbard, no matter 
how impressive the flourish.
Occasionally an act deserves 0 Glory, but certain oblique 
effects of the action might result in great Glory. A knight may 
get no Glory for beheading a hated family enemy at his lord’s 
court (he dishonored both himself and his lord), but he might 
get Glory for achieving such a dramatic and violent success with 
his Sword skill (killing his foe instantly), and rolling criticals on 
his Love [Family] and Hate [Enemy] passions.
Minimum: The minimum Glory that a character can gain 
from a significant event is 1 point. Killing a snake might be 
worth 1 Glory point if circumstances are appropriate. In most 
cases, though, if an event is worth Glory at all, the character 
should gain more than 1 point. 
If the Gamemaster is dividing Glory among a group, and 
each individual would receive less than 1 point by a strict divi -
sion of the Glory, then each individual gains 1 point. 
Ordinary: The common amount of Glory that should be 
awarded for a significant action or event is 10 points. Most nor-
mal actions during the game that are worth praise should receive 
10 Glory. These might include victory over a bandit, a successful 
speech, or an act of courage. 
As a default, the Gamemaster should hand out 10 Glory 
whenever he or she is unsure of how much to award, or whenever 
an argument over Glory seems likely. Gamemasters may reward 
excellent roleplayers with 10 Glory (per action) as a reward for 
their fine play-acting and believable choices.
Heroic: 100 points of Glory is the proper award for most 
heroic acts or important events. A heroic event is an order of 
magnitude more significant than an ordinary event. Some hero-
ic events and actions are worth as much as 250 or more points 
(see T able 4–3, below, for several examples). But even 100 points 
is a lot for a single action or event, and 100 should be the highest 
amount awarded in most cases where no sample is given.
t able  4–3: s a MPle  g lory
100 Glory is heroic. Y ou get 100 Glory for: 
 n Killing a panther
 n Being the champion of a small tournament
 n A critical success in a large battle
 n Behaving in accordance with all the Religious virtues 
for 1 year.
250 Glory is very high. Y ou get 250 Glory for: 
 n Killing a dangerous monster (a fiend, magical bear, 
Faerie sorceress)
 n Being the champion of a large tournament
 n Fighting successfully through 8 rounds of a medium 
battle
300–500 Glory is exceptional. Y ou get exceptional Glory for: 
 n Killing a fearsome monster (griffin, werewolf, wyrm)
 n T en successful rounds of a large battle (400)
 n Becoming a count (350)
Extraordinary: For an action or event that signifies a once-
in-a-lifetime opportunity, 100 Glory is clearly not enough. Such 
extraordinary actions or events gain 1,000 points, an order of 
magnitude over that gained from heroic actions. Adventures 
yielding opportunities for extraordinary Glory are extremely 
rare; only something incredible: an awesomely heroic and suc -
cessful action, or a unique magical or sacred ritual qualifies as 
extraordinary. Perhaps one adventure in twenty, at best, might 
offer this opportunity. Such adventures must involve great risk 
or demand great roleplaying from characters.
For example, defeating an extraordinary monster like a 
huge fire-breathing dragon (one with statistics tougher than 
those shown in Chapter 18: Opponents and Creatures ) might 
gain 1,000 points, should the Gamemaster feel it appropriate. 
But such creatures are rare indeed, and should easily kill most 
knights foolish enough to go against them single-handed. 
Adventures involving tasks crucial to the realm, such as 
championing Charlemagne against a Saracen giant in a judicial 
combat, might yield extraordinary Glory if completed success -
fully. However, only extraordinary knights who are known to 
Glory Awards
==================== BOOK PAGE 87 ====================
Chapter Four: Reputation: Glory and Standing
86
King Charlemagne personally will even be considered for par -
ticipation in such important events — these sort of adventures 
are the domain of the paladins. Until they attain great fame and 
excellent reputation, Player-knights have to accustom them -
selves to tasks of lesser significance. The fact that it usually takes 
a group of knights to achieve such great things means that few 
knights will gain a full 1,000 Glory for successfully completing 
any adventure.
A few extremely heroic non-adventuring acts gain extraor -
dinary Glory, and each of them can occur only once in a knight’s 
life. They include: 
 n Being knighted
 n Becoming a Paladin
 n Marrying a woman of 1000+ Glory (this being the 
one exception to events that might garner Glory more 
than once in a knight’s life)
 n Dying heroically in battle
Extra Glory
Sometimes the nature or the circumstances of a deed 
warrants a bonus to the Glory given. Gamemasters and play -
ers both should be alert for unusual situations, desperate suc -
cesses, exciting solutions and dramatic incidents that might 
warrant a bonus.
Some reasons to award extra Glory might include: 
 n Success against bad odds (worse than 3:1): add 100 
per knight.
 n An extraordinary witness to the event: add 25 for a 
greater noble (count, duke), 50 for a paladin or your 
own lord, 150 for King Charlemagne.
 n Helping a famous hero (for instance, a paladin): add 
100.
 n Extraordinary flourish and show: add 10.
 n Made the Gamemaster laugh with pleasure: add 10.
Areas of Glory Gain
T
he following section lists the primary kinds 
of actions or behaviors from which Glory 
may be gained. Specific Glory awards are 
given in each of these categories; again, these should 
be used as a guideline, not an ironclad set of rules. 
However, the Gamemaster should be wary 
of adding other things to the lists, especially if the 
items added do not clearly fit into one of the follow-
ing categories, for not everything a character does 
should be worth Glory.
Use the guidelines below as the starting point 
for Glory awarded. In a few cases there is a limit on 
the maximum Glory to be obtained from a source. 
They are suggested amounts, and the Gamemaster 
may have his own standard, but it probably follows 
these in ratio.
Inherited Glory 
Every character inherits Glory from his father, 
determined when the character is knighted or his 
father dies, whichever comes first. 
Beginning characters either get the Glory list -
ed in the T able 1–4: Father’s Class, or 1/10 th the 
Glory generated by the event tables in Chapter 2: 
The Past, not both. New characters who are sons of 
Player-knights gain 1/10th their father’s Glory.
Characters gain additional Glory from Ta b l e  
1–7: Page T raining. 
==================== BOOK PAGE 88 ====================
87
Glory from Individual Combat
Knights are warriors first and foremost, and their fame is 
most greatly increased by combat. Combat may be against ban -
dits, monsters, soldiers, or other worthy knights. The basic ratio-
nale for combat Glory is simple: the more fearsome or import -
ant the foe, the greater the Glory.
If the loser had an unusual advantage in the combat, such 
as a large damage value, a magic shield, or inspiration from a 
passion, then more Glory for the winner is appropriate, as the 
risk was greater. Creatures follow the same rationale as human 
opponents: a creature with an unusual power or attribute yields 
the victor extra Glory.
Many sample creatures, and the Glory gained for de -
feating them, are listed in Chapter 18: Opponents and 
Creatures . The Gamemaster must determine the amount of 
extra Glory if the creature has different statistics or capabil -
ities from the standard.
Situations of extreme danger or importance, or those com-
bats that take place in a famous noble’s court or tournament 
field, may gain more Glory, with the extra amount determined 
by the Gamemaster.
Mortal combat with another knight is the supreme chal -
lenge, and great Glory may thereby accrue. However, combat 
between knights in P aladin  is often initiated “for love” (of 
fighting). Such combat is normally performed with one or more 
customary restrictions that reduce the chance of maiming or 
fatal injury. Combat is resolved by some minor event, such as 
knockdown, rather than by the surrender or death of the loser. 
Glory for such safe, formalized combat is calculated normally, 
but the victor receives only 1/10th the normal award.
Consider “defeat” to mean that the enemy surrendered or 
was captured, incapacitated, or killed. If the enemy’s plans were 
foiled but he escaped, no Glory is gained for the defeat (but see 
Successful Quests or T asks, page 88).
The formulae below offer suggested combat Glory.

Marriage Glory
Marriage gains both participants Glory, and usually ben -
efits the woman more than the man. When a Frankish knight 
marries a Frankish woman, each partner acquires Glory equal to 
their partner’s Glory, up to a maximum of 1,000. 
For characters marrying converted pagans, the Glory award 
is equal to the convert’s (Glory/100) x their Honor. This is also 
capped at 1,000. 
Thereafter each character collects Glory individually, not 
mutually.
Death of a spouse allows remarriage, and the subsequent 
Glory gains for the marriage are as for previous marriages.
Spouse Glory
Frankish Glory of the spouse, with a maximum of 1,000
Formerly pagan (Spouse’s Glory/100) x Spouse’s Honor,  
maximum 1,000
Glory From Non-Combat Actions
Although combat is the premier area of Glory gain, the 
Gamemaster should award Glory for any chivalrous, dramatic, 
or prestigious actions of note during the game, such as an act of 
great generosity or courage, or a remarkable performance with a 
harp or lute during a feast. Usually the action must entail a suc -
cessful die roll, but not always.
Skills, T raits and Passions: If the Gamemaster feels it 
is appropriate, a character who succeeds in a statistic, skill, 
trait, or passion roll during courtly or public circumstances 
may gain Ordinary Glory (10 pts); on a critical, the award is 
doubled (20 pts.).
A non-combat action of great significance to an adventure 
may gain double Glory or even more, at the Gamemaster’s op -
tion. In a royal court, for example, a success might gain five times 
Glory. However, five is the maximum multiplier, and should 
rarely be used except in extremely dramatic or complicated sit -
uations (just awarding 100 points for a heroic action is much 
simpler and faster).
Areas of Glory Gain
==================== BOOK PAGE 89 ====================
Chapter Four: Reputation: Glory and Standing
88
Glory may be gained from heroic or becoming actions 
even without a roll. For example, a player may state that 
his character performs a remarkably generous act. As long 
as this is consistent with the character’s normal behavior 
and reputation, no roll is needed. Without a roll, award 
Ordinary Glory.
No Glory is immediately gained simply for having high 
traits, passions, or skills. Statistics above 15 do gain Glory during 
the Winter Phase, however (see Chapter 10).
Successful Quests or Tasks 
This Glory is gained when a task is successfully completed. 
A scenario or adventure may require several tasks to be complet-
ed as part of the process.
Award T ask Glory whenever a task is important to a sce -
nario and offers some challenge. Rolls are not mandatory, and 
no enemies need be killed. The Glory awarded may supplement 
incidental Glory gained for defeating enemies, participation in 
important events, etc. If a knight is specifically ordered by his 
lord to slay a famous ogre, he gains Glory for both defeating the 
enemy and for completing the task.
Assuming cooperation and similar contributions, each par-
ticipant receives an equal share of the Glory. The Gamemaster 
should reduce or eliminate the share of any character who held 
the others back or was uninvolved.

sage safely through a very dangerous place.)
100

Avar raiders.)
250
Extraordinary (Rescue the queen from death.) 1,000
Example of T ask Glory: Sir Adalhart goes on a hazard -
ous magical adventure, in which someone must suffer being bit -
ten by a panther before the group can pass through the gate that 
the sinister beast guards. Every knight on the adventure attempts 
to make a V alorous roll, but either the players are unlucky, or 
their characters are daunted by the panther’s cruel appearance, 
and only Sir Adalhart succeeds. He steps forward, presenting his 
naked arm to the slavering jaws, and receives 100 Glory for his 
heroic and selfless action.
Glory From Participation in Events
On that day there were ten and eight bishops,
ten and eight consecrated archbishops,
and mass was sung by the holy pope.
On that day mass was magnificent.
Our lands have never again seen such brilliance,
it glorified all who were then present.
— The Coronation of Louis , V–VI
Great events such as tournaments, battles, and religious or 
feudal ceremonies are at the heart of the game. Simply partici -
pating in such exciting events gains characters a Glory award, of 
a magnitude based on the importance of the event.
Example of Participation Glory: Sir Adalhart and Lord 
Thierry enter a chapel, where a priest garbed all in white miraculous-
ly heals Adalhart of the terrible wound inflicted by the panther. Both 
knights gain 10 Glory simply for being involved in a wondrous event.
Tournaments 
Knights attend tournaments for the primary purpose of 
gaining Glory. A knight gains normal Glory for every fight 
during the joust, grand melee or challenge if the tournament is 
à outrance (with sharp weapons), or 1/10 the normal combat 
Glory if the tournament is à plaisance (with rebated weapons). 
He gains this Glory no matter what his status once the tourna -
ment is concluded (i.e. whether he won the joust, was eliminated 
after two rounds, or suffered a disabling wound).
Knights who win the elimination joust receive additional 
Glory. Furthermore, one knight is chosen as the T ournament 
Champion and receives a handsome prize as well as Glory. The 
tournament champion and the winner of the joust may be the 
same person, or not. Finally, the tournament host receives Glory 
for sponsoring the tournament.
Tournament Sizes
The smallest size of tournament is a local tournament, in 
which only 100 knights or so participate. 50 Glory is gained by 
the Melee Champion and Champion of the Joust. The same 
amount is gained by the sponsor. Typical participants gain or-
dinary Glory (10 points).
Most tournaments are regional tournaments, typical -
ly sponsored by a count, abbot, bishop or duke; pitting his 
knights against a neighbor’s knights. V olunteers are wel -
comed, and may join the team of their choice. Between 500–
1000 knights compete in these tournaments. Participants typ-
ically get 10–20 points of Glory. Sponsors, Melee and Joust 
Champions of these tournaments are awarded 100 Glory.
The only regal tournament is King Charlemagne’s an -
nual Pentecostal T ournament (from 775 on). 2,000 knights 
typically compete. Sponsors and the Champions of the Melee 
and Joust get 200 Glory points each. Participants typically get 
20–30 for their Glory.
==================== BOOK PAGE 90 ====================
89
Battle 
Glory awards for participation in battle depend on how well 
the character and the army fought. Glory is gained on a per-round 
basis, and is derived from the size of the battle, with adjustments 
for various factors. See the battle rules in Chapter 8: Mass Combat. 
How well your character did with his weapon skill roll 
during the round makes a big difference; and being on the field, 
but disengaged, is worth far less in Glory. Participation in a vic -
tory is far more glorious than surviving a humiliating defeat. If 
the odds in battle were against your side, more Glory is gained.

Honors and Titles 
Receiving a high rank or honor such as the being made a 
count is classified as participation Glory because the event does 
not require any successful die rolls or actions other than accep -
tance (note that qualification to receive such honors may require 
many successful actions, however).
All these Glory awards are one-time awards only; If a char -
acter regains a title after having lost it for some reason, or is given 
another honor of the same rank, no additional Glory is gained.
A heroic death, the last great honor a knight can achieve, is 
listed here.

Miracles and Conversion 
A knight who prays for and receives a miracle through his 
Love [Charlemagne] passion is deserving of Glory. Note that 
this does not include knights who benefit from the prayers of 
others through his Love [God] passion (see Chapter 9: Magic  
for more on Miracles).
A knight who personally converts a pagan is also deserving 
of Glory. The Glory award is equal to the knight’s Eloquence, 
Religion and Honor (the abilities used to convert his foe), plus 
twice the converted pagan’s Honor.

Passive Glory
Glory can be gained without any action during the game tak-
ing place. The Glory award is collected during the Winter Phase 
of the game each year the knight maintains the requirements. 
Quality of Maintenance 
This Glory is received annually. The amount of Glory 
equals the amount of extra money spent on the knight’s mainte-
nance (see Chapter 10: The Winter Phase). 
Maintenance Average Annual Glory
Ordinary None
Rich 10
Superlative 15
Conspicuous Consumption 
Luxury spending without utility or direct benefit is “con -
spicuous.” Each £1 spent on fancy goods (or other frivolities like 
specialist personnel) gets 1 Glory. A gift of £120 would gain the 
giver 120 Glory.
Holdings, Land and Castle 
The Glory for land equals the standard income for it, with 
each £1 = 1 Glory. Castles grant Glory of 1 DV = 1 Glory. 
Maximum Glory for both of these combined is 100 points per 
year.

Large castle 51
V ery strong castle 66
Possessing Enchanted Items or Horses 
Some particular items or famous horses bring the owner an-
nual Glory just for possessing them. Items usually have a colorful 
history attached to them and attract the covetous, or rivals or 
knights claiming “their” heirloom.

Notable Statistics
A statistic (attribute, skill, trait, passion, or attitude) with 
a value greater than 15 is notable, and gains you Glory every 
year equal to its value. As the Statistic increases, so too does the 
Passive Glory gained from it!
Ideals 
Meeting the requirements of a Chivalrous, Religious or 
Romantic knight, or a Gentlewoman earns 100 Glory (for each 
ideal) during each Winter Phase.
Example of Passive Glory: Sir T urold’s gotten hold of an en-
chanted sword, which gives him 5 Glory per year. He is wealthy, 
holding 3 manors, for which he gains 18 points. In total, Sir T urold 
gains 5+18 =23 passive Glory this winter.
Rewards of Glory
A 
character of 1,000 or more Glory has attained heroic 
stature, and gains special benefits and advantages there-
by. The obvious benefit is enhanced status, which can 
aid the character in many ways. The second, more exciting ad -
vantage is the enhancement to personal confidence that great 
Glory fosters. In game terms this gain in confidence is simulated 
by magical increases in the character’s statistics.
The rewards of Glory accrue at the end of the year, after any 
adventures are over or on hold, and after all Glory gained during 
the year has been calculated, in the latter part of the Winter 
Phase (see Chapter 10).
Bonus Points
Characters with 1,000 or more Glory derive measurable 
benefits from their status: For each 1,000 Glory points, a char -
acter may add 1 bonus point to any one trait, passion, statistic, 
or skill during the Winter Phase of the game. Bonus points allow 
characters to bend or break certain game rules, and can confer 
heroic attributes on characters with already high abilities. This 
bending of the rules occurs in two ways: 
First, statistics can be increased using bonus points, even af-
ter age 30. More importantly, statistics can be increased higher 
than natural maximums, so skills and traits can advance beyond 
20. Thus you occasionally hear of extraordinary knights with 
statistic values of 25 or even greater.
Second, bonus points automatically increase the attribute, 
without the character having to make a successful experience 
check roll (again, see Chapter 10). 
Allocating Bonus Points
Although Glory is recorded as it is earned during a scenar -
io, the effects do not accrue until winter. Any resultant bonus 
points are gained and must be allocated during the Winter 
Phase. Glory takes effect on a year-by-year basis. A bonus point 
cannot be saved over to next year’s Winter Phase, but must be 
spent during the winter immediately following the adventure in 
which it was gained.
Players are advised to spend the Winter Phase training a few 
skills or attributes that they have earmarked for heroic values. 
Once the skill or attribute is brought up to its normal maximum, 
a bonus point from Glory increases it to the heroic level, and 
then more can take it beyond.
==================== BOOK PAGE 92 ====================
91
Example of Bonus Point Allocation: Sir Adalhart has been 
developing his Constitution every year while a squire. It reaches a 
value of 20 on the year he is knighted, which is the maximum nor-
mally possible. Adalhart’s Glory has increased 1210 points from 
adventures this year, gaining him a bonus point. Adalhart uses it to 
increase the value of his CON to a heroic 21.
Status
The following rules give the standard ways in which in -
creased status and prestige from Glory translate into game terms. 
Both players and Gamemaster should be alert for other ways to 
bring Glory into the story. Glory should be a source of pride, and 
can be an excellent basis for roleplaying.
Increased Recognition 
Glorious individuals are well known in the lands of 
Charlemagne. Each 1,000 Glory adds 1 point to a knight’s 
chance of being identified using Recognize or Heraldry skills 
(see Chapter 5: Skills).
Example of Increased Recognition from Glory: Count 
Isoré is taking part in a tournament at which the evil Lady Alice, 
wife of an enemy, has secretly arrived. Alice has a Recognize skill of 
15. Sir Isoré has 4,500 Glory, so Alice gains a +5 modifier, and is 
guaranteed to recognize her husband’s enemy if she spots him in the 
crowd or at the tourney.
Precedence 
When characters are in a social situation among chivalrous 
folk, the character with the highest Glory takes precedence over 
others of equal title. Titles of rank still maintain their prece -
dence, so a king or other lord won’t defer to an ordinary knight, 
no matter what the knight’s Glory. In situations where feats 
of arms may be called for, it is the privilege of the greatest to 
make the first attempt. In social situations, “precedence” usual -
ly means that the person of lower rank goes first. For example, 
when entering a feast, the lower-ranked individuals go first and 
the highest-ranked person is last.
Example of Precedence: Four knights are preparing to enter 
a mysterious ruined castle. The laws of courtesy and chivalry accord 
the knight of highest Glory the right to enter first. If there is only one 
enemy to defeat inside the castle, he gets the chance to fight it first, 
and gain all the Glory thereby.
Skill Modifiers
Glory indicates status and importance, not just notoriety. 
In formal situations where characters interact, such as the courts 
of nobles, great status may enhance the character’s capabilities to 
influence or impress others. Normally the character must have 
a respectable reputation to do so, though; evil knights with no 
Honor, even if very powerful and influential, aren’t usually in -
vited to court.
Only non-combat skills such as Singing or Eloquence can 
be enhanced by Glory in this way. The rationale is that charac -
ters of great Glory are much more likely to receive attention and 
praise, and less likely to be judged harshly if their performance 
is mediocre.
The character gains a modifier of up to +1 per 1,000 points 
of Glory to the skill being used, with a maximum of +10. Thus, 
a character with 8,200 Glory could gain a maximum modifier of 
+8 to a courtly skill in this way. A player can request such a bo -
nus to courtly skills, but the Gamemaster is always the arbiter of 
how much or how little of a bonus the character actually gains.
Keeping Track of Glory
A character’s Glory may increase many times during his ca-
reer. The player should keep careful track of each major award. 
Part of the fun in the game is looking back with pride on the ad-
ventures, tournaments, and battles your character was involved 
in. Each adventure qualifies, even if little Glory was gained, and 
certain events within an adventure, such as a battle or tourna -
ment, should be recorded separately. Special ceremonies, such as 
marriage, or the gaining of paladin status, should definitely be 
recorded separately. These events are provided with a space for 
the date achieved.
Example of Recording Glory: In game year 769, his second 
year in play, Sir Adalhart participates in an adventure in which 
he and his comrades successfully vanquish a powerful group of fa -
erie knights in a castle. The Gamemaster’s title for the adventure 
is “The Dark T ower. ” During the adventure Sir Adalhart slays a 
giant in an incident unconnected with the main story.
At the end of the adventure, during which Adalhart earned 
45 miscellaneous points, the Gamemaster announces that all par -
ticipants gain 100 adventure Glory for their clever and success -
ful solution to the adventure, which he feels was heroic in overall 
significance and drama. The Gamemaster awards Adalhart 250 
Glory for single-handedly defeating the giant (a remarkable feat). 
Sir Adalhart’s player adds two entries on the back of the character 
sheet: 
Ye a r Important Event New To t a l
769 “The Dark T ower” 45+100 1355
769 Slew a giant single-handed 250 1605
In later years, Sir Adalhart will be able to look back with plea-
sure at this record of his success and courage. When someone asks, 
“What deeds of fame have you performed?” the player can check the 
back of the character sheet and answer accurately.
Standings
The duchess of Bordeaux gives her two sons sensible advice: 
My children, you’ll be going to court and in God’s holy 
name I demand you: do not listen to flatterers, keep good 
company, go to church often, honor and love the clergy, 
don’t hesitate to donate your goods to the poor, be cour -
teous, generous and hospitable, for in that manner you 
shall be loved and praised. 
— Huon of Bordeaux , VII
Standings
==================== BOOK PAGE 93 ====================
Chapter Four: Reputation: Glory and Standing
92
J
ust as a Player-knight has opinions and feelings about 
other people, they appreciate him in a certain way as well. 
A standing represents the person’s willingness to help or 
even to grant a favor.
Base Standing Values
V alues of less than 5 indicate animosity or even scorn or 
overt hatred, while values of 16 or more represent solid friend -
ship and loyalty. A value of 20 or more suggests that people glad-
ly risk their life for your character.
The standings of persons who don’t fit in this table, espe -
cially foreigners, are typically determined by the Player-knight’s 
Glory/1,000 (or by his APP, for noble ladies).
Changing Standings
When a Player-knight does something for others that is 
not part of his duty to them, their standing towards him may 
be raised. Inversely, not helping those who hold you in esteem 
diminishes their standing.
Most standing scores are group estimates, so personal favors 
are not taken into account, apart from where King Charlemagne 
and the Player-knight’s liege are affected.
Raising Standings by Bestowing Gifts
Most standings can be increased by gifts. All standings in -
crease by 1 for every donation of £10, except Charlemagne, for 
whom gifts of £100 are needed (over and above any regular oblig-
atory gifts). Lesser gifts have a proportional chance to increase the 
king’s standing, so a small offering worth £5 has a 1 in 20 chance to 
gain one point. These gifts also gain Glory as normal.
Standing Rolls
Standings come into play on a number of occasions. Most 
often, this is when a Player-knight actively asks someone for 
help, or when he needs some sort of favor. The Gamemaster may 
make passive standing rolls in situations where he has to judge 
non-player character’s reactions to player actions. Here is a list of 
typical uses to give a general idea of the possibilities: 
Standing [Charlemagne] 
Every time King Charlemagne must make a decision with 
regards to the Player-knight, roll his standing. This may be to 
grant the character a boon, promote him to a high office at 
court, in decisions of justice, or to settle feuds, for example.
A Player-knight whom Charlemagne strongly dislikes (i.e. a 
standing value of less than 6) will not be made a vassal or inherit 
lands, and won’t be promoted (see Ambitions in Chapter 11). A 
value of 0 means that the king will degrade the knight (i.e. re -
move his knighthood, making him a commoner), at which point 
he should normally be taken out of play.
Standing [lord] 
A vassal needs his lord’s permission to marry, to build a 
castle, or to engage mercenary troops. A knight whose horse is 
killed or lost in battle may try an Standing [lord] roll to get a 
new steed from him.
An unhappy lord whose standing is lower than 6 will ask for 
extra annual services and double the normal amount of gifts. If 
his standing drops to 0, the lord breaks the feudal contract: he 
dismisses the knight and claims back any fiefs he gave the knight.
Standing [family] 
Knights often call on their relatives to support their side 
in court decisions, or for more serious matters such as when he 
needs them as hostages in a judicial duel. Roll Standing [family] 
when a knight demands his kin to support him in a feud.
A hostile family, whose standing has value of 5 or lower, no 
longer considers the Player-knight to be their kin; either sending 
him reproachful messages, or completely ignoring him to the 
best of their ability. If their standing drops to 0, they publicly 
denounce him as a usurping bastard.
Standing Starting score Notes
Charlemagne Lowest Chivalrous trait Note that from 767 to 771, Charlemagne is the lawful King of the Franks in only half of Frankland. If 
you create a character from the other half of the kingdom, you may use his elder brother’s standing for 
Carloman as well.
Lord V alorous Unless you hold an allod (free land), you are a vassal of a lord to whom you owe allegiance. By exten -
sion, this standing is used for the lord’s entourage.
Family Honor Y our extended family includes your brothers and sisters, your wife and children, plus your uncles, 
aunts, cousins, nephews, nieces and people who married into your family.
Retinue Generous All persons who directly depend on you. This includes your squire, and possibly your household 
knights, vassals, manorial officers and other specialist personnel, if any.
Church Love (God) All members of the Church such as your lord’s household priest, your bishop, nearby abbots, hermits, 
missionaries, and local monks or nuns.
Commoners Merciful All anonymous non-nobles such as peasants, artisans, or merchants living in villages, towns or cities in 
your homeland and beyond. However numerous the people in this category, they are the least import-
ant to the life of a knight.
==================== BOOK PAGE 94 ====================
93
Standing [retinue] 
Players tend to demand a lot of their retinue, but their loyal-
ty to him is often limited, especially when it puts them in danger.
A retinue with a low standing (less than 6) becomes corrupt 
and disobedient. If it drops to 0, they’ll simply leave the Player-
knight’s service without notice, if they don’t sell him out to his 
enemies!
Standing [Church] 
The life of a knight differs much from that of a monk. The 
Church keeps a close watch over knights and admonishes them 
for unchristian or evil behavior. The Church’s standing is rele -
vant when a knight demands a monk to tend to his wounds, to 
pray for his success in an adventure, to lend him money, or even 
the use of a relic (such rolls are at –5, at least), for example.
If the Church’s standing drops below 6, the bishop will ask 
you for an annual donation to the Church in order to cleanse 
your soul, or may pronounce an interdict — temporarily for -
bid the character from participating in Church rituals. In ex -
treme cases, he may ask you to perform a pilgrimage (see The 
Pilgrimage 
Solo Scenario in Chapter 19). When the Church’s 
stadning reaches 0, the character is excommunicated and is tak -
en out of play.
Standing [commoners] 
When a player vassal calls on the men on his land to join 
him at the May Field, the player uses his standing score multi -
plied by 5 to see how many free men per manor show up (the 
remainder find excuses not to come).
If the standing drops below 6, the peasants generally con -
ceal their hatred from their overlord who, after all, has the right 
to imprison or kill them. It manifests as reluctance at first, and 
if it worsens, by the peasants running away, turning to banditry 
(standard fief income is halved) or, if the standing drops to 0, 
even outright rebellion (no fief income at all!).
