95
Chapter Five: Skills
S
kills express the social and physical activities popular in 
the Carolingian mythos. No knight is expected to master 
all, or even most, of the possible skills.
Skill Categories
Skills are divided into two broad categories: ordinary skills 
(further divided into “common skills” and “courtly skills, ” and 
normally referred to as simply “skills”), which include a wide 
range of different practical proficiencies and areas of knowledge; 
and combat skills, the crucial military skills that are the tradition-
al area of expertise for knights, divided into T actics, Melee skills, 
and Missile skills. The two broad categories are listed in two sep-
arate areas on the knight’s character sheet, and are described in 
separate sections below.
The following table gives an idea of what a skill score 
represents: 
t able  5–1: s kill  l evels
Score Skill Level Title
0–5 Low; only very basic skills Novice
6–10 Normal; basic mastery Apprentice
11–15 High; professional use Journeyman
1– 20 Excellent; full expertise Master
21–25 Heroic; unfailing Hero
26+ Perfect; superhuman Legend
Success or Failure
Success or failure with skills is resolved using normal reso -
lution rolls (see Chapter 6: General Mechanics). Some skills are 
used only in unopposed resolution, while others are normally 
used in opposition against other characters, in contests or chal -
lenges. Modifiers may be applied to any of these rolls.
A failed skill roll does not automatically mean the Player-
knight failed to perform a certain task. It may just indicate that 
it was done successfully, but without the intended result.
Example of a Failed Skill Roll:  Sir Adalhart is trying to 
impress the ladies at court with his dancing skill. The player rolls 
the die and fails. This simply means that Sir Adalhart’s dance did 
not provoke any positive comments from the ladies, but not that he 
somehow failed to dance correctly.
The results of a critical success or fumble with a general or 
courtly skill are given in the individual skill descriptions below 
when significant. Otherwise the Gamemaster describes the re -
sults, based on the situation. 
The Combat Skills section gives the results of criticals and 
fumbles for combat skills.
Glory from Skills
Success with any skill may gain the character ordinary Glory, 
normally 10 points, at the Gamemaster’s option. The usual re -
quirement is that the success must have contributed to the Player-
knights’ goals in the scenario. A critical success is worth double the 
ordinary award: 20 Glory. Successes in the court of a king double 
the basic skill awards. A heroic use of a skill, perhaps to save a char-
acter’s life, might gain 100 Glory. This should be rare.
Combat skills are a special case, and may permit great Glory 
to be gained under appropriate circumstances. However, Glory 
for success in combat is based upon victory, not particular skill 
rolls. After all, killing a giant should gain Glory even if the char-
acter used cunning rather than any particular skill. Note that 
combats “for love” or victories gained by using missile weapons 
gain only 10% of normal Glory.
Player Knowledge
Sometimes a player may know something related to one 
of Paladin’s  skills, but, due to the values assigned his charac-
ter, is unable to make a successful die roll. In such cases, the 
Gamemaster should accept the fact that the player, and thus 
the character, knows the fact being requested: Remember, 
there is no “Intelligence” attribute in this game.
In fact, in such cases, the Gamemaster might even wish 
to reward the player’s knowledge by awarding an experience 
check to the character. (Y ou might be disinclined to use this 
option, because of the unfair advantage it would introduce, if 
one of the players is a scholar of medieval history….)
==================== BOOK PAGE 97 ====================
Chapter Five: Skills
96
Finally, characters may gain Glory for a success with a skill 
roll if it reveals information crucial to a scenario. Usually, howev-
er, little Glory is to be gained from knowledge — Glory is gained 
from doing, not thinking.
Skills and Honor
The Honor passion reflects your character’s code of behav-
ior and reputation as a knight. Fumbled or failed skill rolls do 
not affect your character’s Honor value; Honor cannot be lost 
simply because of a bad die roll. The penalty for a fumbled skill 
roll is the social embarrassment suffered, which the Gamemaster 
must keep to an appropriate level. Honor is lost only due to char-
acter actions.
Certain situations might occur, however, in which a 
Gamemaster feels justified in subtracting Honor because a skill 
was used unwisely or unjustly. For example, causing harm or hu-
miliation to another character through failure in a skill might in-
cur the loss of 1 point of Honor (or, in particularly severe cases, 
more) — especially if another character with a higher value in 
that skill was available to attempt the task, yet the character who 
failed insisted on taking responsibility for the task anyway. The 
Gamemaster is always the final arbiter in such difficult situations.
Improving Skills
Character advancement in P aladin  is measured in part by 
the increase of your character’s skills.
Skills in P aladin  can be increased in many ways. A char -
acter may raise a skill value by training with a teacher, by dili -
gent practice on his own, by first-hand experience in the field, 
by means of a major increase in self-confidence and reputation, 
or by magic.
All these improvements occur during the Winter Phase 
(Chapter 10). Experience is dealt with during Step 7, Experience 
Check Rolls, of the Winter Phase. The training of skills is handled 
in Step 8, T raining and Practice. The effects of self-confidence 
and heroic reputation are simulated using the Glory system.
The Gamemaster must handle any magical skill increases 
that might occur during his scenarios (there should be a prereq-
uisite action, a risk of unexpected side effects, or a corresponding 
penalty involved in any magical increases of a skill).
Part of the fun in the game is seeing your character increase 
in skill. Most players focus on a half-dozen or fewer skills that 
they increase through all the methods listed above, over several 
decades of game time. T raining and practice can help raise skills 
to 15. Thereafter, only experience checks and Glory can be used 
to raise each skill into the realm of heroic mastery.
Common Skills
Awareness
Use this skill when a knight is listening for a sound, trying to 
spot a concealed Saxon or discern some other hidden danger, or 
otherwise draw some sensory information from his surroundings.
This skill measures the knight’s awareness of all activity in 
his surroundings, using his the five senses and the mysterious 
“sixth sense” to recognize that something dangerous or unusual 
is about to occur. 
W earing a helmet does not give negative modifiers. Instead, 
taking it off gives the knight a +5 bonus (see Encumbrance, page 
118). Fast movement, on the other hand, may have a negative 
influence on a knight’s Awareness (see Movement, page 111).
A critical success in Awareness might reveal extra informa-
tion. A failure should reveal no information, while a fumbled 
Awareness should reveal incorrect information. 
Chirurgery
This ancient practice of healing and care includes much 
useful knowledge, such as herbal medicine and bone setting. 
It also includes folk knowledge, simple prayers, and generous 
heaps of misinformation, so its use is fraught with uncertainty. 
Chirurgery does little to heal the patient directly, but is a 
process that keeps the patient alive so that the natural healing 
processes of the body may take effect.
Chirurgery is a most important skill for women to have, 
and many holy people know it. However, it is not the duty of a 
knight to learn the skill, and many knights feel uncomfortable 
performing a skill associated with women.
Badly wounded, ill, or debilitated characters often require 
application of the Chirurgery skill. If your character needs 
Chirurgery, for whatever reason, the Gamemaster will tell you 
to check the box on the character sheet marked “Chirurgery 
Needed. ” 
Glory should always be gained for successful use of the 
Chirurgery skill. The amount gained can be increased in pro -
portion to the Glory or rank of the character being treated if 
the Gamemaster feels this is appropriate. More Glory should be 
gained if a life was saved thereby.
See Injury and Health  in Chapter 7 for more information 
on the complicated processes of chirurgery and healing. 
Chirurgery is pronounced “KY-rir-jur-y; ” In modern times 
the word has evolved in spelling, pronunciation, and usage to 
become “surgery.”
Faerie Lore
Faerie Lore quantifies how much a knight knows about 
the mysterious ways of the faerie and their world, as well as en -
chanted creatures in general. It is used to identify a type of faerie 
or monster that was sighted, to recognize a faerie encounter as 
such, or to aid communication with them.
A Faerie Lore skill success reveals magical information, 
which the Gamemaster should present in an entertaining way. 
However, this information may be sheer superstition, or incor -
rect in detail. A failure or fumble gives erroneous information. A 
critical success always reveals some important fact, which might 
be secretly communicated to the player by the Gamemaster.
==================== BOOK PAGE 98 ====================
97
First Aid
First Aid provides immediate medical assistance to wounds. 
It is actually more reliable than the medieval remedies used lat -
er in treatment (Chirurgery). Successful First Aid reduces the 
chance of infection, and returns 1d3 hit points to the injured 
character. Critical success returns 1d3+3 hit points, while a fum-
ble removes a further 1d3 hit points and requires Chirurgery. 
Characters cannot apply First Aid to themselves.
First Aid, like Chirurgery, is an important and complicated 
subject. See the Injury and Health section in Chapter 7 for more 
information.
Glory should usually be gained for successful use of the 
First Aid skill. The amount gained should be increased in pro -
portion to the Glory and rank of the character being treated. If a 
life is saved, more Glory should be gained.
Folk Lore
Folk Lore is the knowledge skill of peasant beliefs and way 
of life. It stems from familiarity with the land that has been 
gained over thousands of years of experience. Folk Lore includes 
information on many subjects, from such ordinary things as the 
lore of pigs or local landmarks, to the household herbal cures of 
old women. Folk Lore includes thousands of tiny facts useful to 
daily living, such when to plant, how to tell if the coming win -
ter will be hard, and how to cheat the tax collector, as well as a 
certain amount of nonsense, such as how to rid oneself of warts, 
charms to kill rats, and songs to make the plants grow.
Folk Lore is used in play when a knight observes peasants at 
work to determine what they are doing, or when trying to eval -
uate how they feel. It may be used to gain a benefit in communi-
cating with peasants. 
A successful Folk Lore roll shows that the knight communi-
cated his friendliness and knowledge of the folk ways, presumably 
making the peasant more agreeable and less afraid. Failure reveals 
the knight to be a typical upper-class oppressor. Fumble indicates 
a major gaffe that offends, and possibly enrages, the peasant.
Glory is not normally gained through Folklore. What 
Glory is there in dealing with commoners and peasant matters?
Horsemanship
Count Oliver fumbles a Horsemanship roll: 
Count Oliver, the wise and strong of limb: 
By a rough ditch at the foot of a hill
His lively horse treads awkwardly and trips,
And Oliver falls off beside the ditch.
— Girart de Vienne , XCVII
Horsemanship is the ability to perform actions and maneu-
vers while mounted on a moving horse. Horsemanship is a major 
aristocratic skill. Note that noble women ride astride.
Activities include fighting, jumping obstacles, and gal -
loping. Pages are taught to mount a horse from the flanks and 
rear without help, and to use a shield while riding and fighting. 
Other feats include riding without hands for one melee round, 
recovering an item from ground while riding, jumping off and 
on horses in full armor, even with an unsheathed sword!
In most normal uses success indicates that the horse did 
what it was expected to do. Failure indicates it did not. Critical 
success shows it went faster, jumped farther, or whatever is ap -
propriate for greater than normal effort. A fumble indicates that 
the horse tripped, the rider fell off, the saddle girths broke, or 
that the ride has otherwise ended.
Common Skills
==================== BOOK PAGE 99 ====================
Chapter Five: Skills
98
Hunting
The Hunting skill includes the entire array of tasks performed 
during the noble sport of the chase, except for weapon skills. Hunting 
includes knowing what the different blasts on the horn mean, and 
knowing whether an animal is a “beast of chase, venery, or vermin.” It 
includes care of hounds, understanding of their methods of hunting 
and what their cries mean; tracking the spoor and identifying beasts 
from it; knowing the best way to quickly kill each animal; and the 
skill of cutting it up properly afterwards so everyone, from hounds 
and dog boys to the sponsor, gets their correct share.
The young men bring up thin spears with sharp iron 
points
And linen nets fastened with square mesh,
Leading the excited dogs with leashes round their necks,
The hounds, their mouth ravening, and keen for the prey.
— Anonymous poem
In a hunt, most activity consists of making successful 
Horsemanship rolls with various modifiers, rolls for your horses’ 
statistics, and for your own Hunting skill. Y ou fall behind or pull 
ahead, possibly get lost, and perhaps get in on the kill if you are 
skillful or lucky.
Cornered animals may be fought hand-to-tooth, using nor-
mal melee rules. Remember that it is courteous to allow the most 
highly ranked person to attack first, if possible. Multiple attackers 
generally work together only against monsters, not game animals.
Hunting skills are used to test general woodland and wild 
land knowledge. Skilled hunters are experts in animal lore, ani -
mal handling, foraging, and tracking. For example, certain kinds 
of horse droppings are a sure indication of knightly presence in 
the vicinity, more than traces of hooves. Hunting is used when 
trying to find your way through woods, wastes, or unfamiliar ter-
ritory. A modifier may be added if following an established trail.
Special: Dogs are important for a successful hunt, and a –5 
modifier should be given to the Hunting skill if prey is being 
followed without dogs.
Industry
Knights never engage in Industry, and are in danger of losing 
their high rank if they do. Industry is the work of creating things 
with one’s hands. For noblewomen this is most often expressed 
in the arts of spinning, weaving, and sewing; creating fashionable 
clothing for both men and women, tapestries to hang upon walls, 
ornate church vestments, and even simple table linens.
Industry can be used by churchmen who make fine books, 
or peasant artisans such as coopers or weapon-smiths. It is a very 
common peasant skill. 
The quality of work produced by Industry is based on the 
number rolled for success. A critical success creates a work of 
great craftsmanship or artistry.
Glory can be gained by non-knights from the use of this skill, 
if a particularly beautiful item is made and presented to someone 
in public. Usually this should be only ordinary Glory (10 points).
Intrigue
This skill allows one to know what is going on at court. 
Everyone has access to gossip, but hard work and clever conversa-
tion help to pry out the real facts. The Intrigue skill is needed to 
know who to ask, when to ask them, what to say to them to learn 
of a secret, and how to sift truth from lies. Personal contacts cul-
tured through long, often secret, relationships are another key and 
may provide modifiers, as successful standing rolls do.
Intrigue is not used to poison people in secret, foment re -
bellion, or to assassinate rivals. Such dark practices must be role-
played, not left to random die rolls.
Many modifiers may be applied to an Intrigue roll. For in -
stance, trying to find out secrets in a castle whose entire staff has 
been briefed on a plan, and who agree with its intent, probably 
gets a negative modifier. A sympathetic resident, perhaps because 
both he and the characters are from the same homeland, might 
give a positive modifier. A normal castle or court situation, with 
nothing unusual going on, gives no modifiers.
Success with Intrigue indicates you learn something true 
and probably useful. A critical success indicates you learn a spe -
cial and important fact known only to you and the direct partic-
ipants (perhaps a fact overheard at a critical juncture, or seen by 
accident, or discovered in a lost note). Failure indicates nothing 
new was learned, fumble means that a close contact lies, or is 
thought to have lied, to you. The Gamemaster can exploit this 
uncertainty at his leisure.
Recognize
A little while later, when a certain youth who was a rel -
ative of the king sang the Alleluia on a particular feast, 
the emperor said to that same bishop, “That cleric of 
yours sang very well indeed. ” On account of his stupid -
ity, he took the words as a joke, and not knowing that 
the singer was a relative of the emperor, replied, “Yes, yes, 
every farmer can roar like that when he is out in the field 
working with his oxen. ” After this ill-considered response, 
the emperor turned on him, glared like lightning, and 
threw the astonished man to the ground.
— Notker , 1.19
Not everyone is adept at putting names to faces, or faces to 
coats of arms. When everyone mingles at court there may be hun-
dreds of nobles and knights, along with thousands of servants and 
commoners. In such a crowd you may see someone, perhaps even 
be introduced, and forget him almost immediately as one among 
many. Likewise, after viewing several hundred jousters, you might 
find that all but the best blur into one indistinct figure. Knights 
find the skill useful to recognize particular opponents or relatives 
on the battle field, to spot members of the same family, or to iden-
tify foreign hostages delivered by conquered foes. 
Recognize is the skill of remembering who is who, from 
what family, etc. It consists of putting together clues: a face, 
jousting style, distinctive feature, or other characteristics that 
identify a person. Y ou must have previous reason to recall it at 
==================== BOOK PAGE 100 ====================
99
all; if you’ve never heard about or seen someone before, even a 
critical success will not help — it is impossible to use this skill to 
learn something about a complete stranger. On the other hand, 
the Gamemaster may tell you to make a Recognize roll when you 
don’t think of it, and may assign a positive modifier to your skill 
if an acquaintance is nearby but unrecognized.
A successful Recognize roll indicates that you remember 
a character, and recall what you know about him. A critical 
success means that you can recognize the person even if he is 
in disguise. A failed roll indicates that you don’t remember this 
person. A fumble indicates that you think you recognize him, 
but the Gamemaster can give you false (or true information if 
that might be misleading); a character who fumbles a Recognize 
roll is always unsure about his identification.
The Gamemaster may decide to assign a bonus of 
Glory/1,000 to the Recognize roll for notable characters. 
Religion
This is a knowledge skill that quantifies how much is known 
concerning the beliefs, rites, sacred calendar, and religious prac -
tices of Christianity. It indicates a person’s ability to follow a 
ceremony and do what is appropriate to his station within a sa -
cred context. A person with this knowledge is an initiate — an 
informed worshiper. 
A high Religion skill does not necessarily indicate belief or 
sincerity, nor the fervency of one’s faith: that is measured by the 
Love [God] passion. This skill simply allows one to know the 
forms, ceremonies, and ideologies of a religion, as would be ac -
quired by attending normal worship. 
All Franks know the main tenets of Christian dogma and 
Church organization, acquired by attending normal worship. 
Specific information about Byzantine Christianity, local pagan 
worship, or the religions of heathen foreigners such as Moors 
and Persians, is less accessible and for these at least a –5 penalty 
to Religion should be applied. 
Stewardship
This ability to plan, administer, and oversee the keeping of 
a farm or similar holding is not normally required for knights. 
It is an important skill for women and peasants, used to main -
tain and improve the income generated from land. On a knight’s 
holding the knight’s wife or steward usually knows this skill.
Stewardship includes the knowledge of different mer -
chants, fairs, and markets, as well as the understanding of fluctu-
ating prices of trading goods.
Swimming
Swimming is used to move successfully through water. The 
roll is normally unmodified, but in stormy or flooded conditions 
there may be negative modifiers involved. 
A successful Swimming roll indicates that the character re-
mained above water and traveled his base Movement Rate in the 
desired direction. A critical success indicates that he did so at 
double speed. A failure indicates that he didn’t make significant 
progress this round. A fumble means the knight begins drown -
ing (see Suffocation on page 134).
A Swimming roll can be used to remove armor while under-
water, if stated to the Gamemaster at the beginning of the round. 
A successful roll removes 2 points of armor. During this time, 
the character suffers drowning damage.
Special: Swimming does not use the standard encumbrance 
rules for armor worn. Instead, a character’s Swimming skill is re-
duced by 1 point per point of armor worn; thus, a character with 
a Swimming value of 10 would have a modified skill of 6 while 
wearing leather armor (4 armor points).
Normal encumbrance—a light or heavy load—imposes the 
usual penalties (that is, –5 and –10 respectively) in addition to 
any penalties from armor. However, a character’s gear, other than 
armor, can be dropped freely.
Courtly Skills
Courtesy
Courtesy is a knowledge skill defining a knight’s knowledge of 
court manners. Court manners of all types are included, from eti-
quette to precedence to table manners, modes of speech, protocol, 
vocabulary, style, and forms of address. Included are all types of de-
corum and manners appropriate to a lord’s court, around superiors, 
women, and disliked or disfavored people. All characters understand 
precedence and knows the basic forms of address for court. Courtesy 
is inappropriate to use with commoners (use Folk Lore instead).
Courtesy does not include the art of the formal dance, 
heraldry, or the forms of the tournament. These areas of exper -
tise are so complicated that they are treated as separate skills 
(Dancing, Heraldry and Gaming, respectively).
Successful Courtesy means that the knight performed correct-
ly in the formal manner appropriate to the relative situation, making 
a suitable impression upon his audience. A critical success indicates 
great elegance and style in the performance. A failure means minor 
incorrect behavior, while a fumble indicates that a silly or even of-
fensive act was performed, with attendant humiliation. Haughty or 
cruel lords may become insulted and angry at characters who fum-
ble their Courtesy roll in court, with dramatic consequences.
With the Gamemaster’s approval, characters of high Glory 
receive a positive modifier to Courtesy equal to Glory/1000.
Dancing
This skill measures the character’s ability to move gracefully 
to music, and his knowledge of the many styles of formal danc -
ing done at court. This elegant style of dancing depends primar-
ily on experience and knowledge of forms, rather than agility.
A critical success indicates superb grace and verve, while a 
success indicates accuracy to the forms of the dance being per -
formed. Failure shows lack of style or even error, while a fumble 
means that the character went the wrong way, probably bump -
ing into other dancers. The Gamemaster may even rule that the 
fumbler tripped and fell over his own feet. A fumble in Dance is 
a humiliating experience.
Courtly Skills
==================== BOOK PAGE 101 ====================
Chapter Five: Skills
100
Glory can be gained from Dance if the dancer(s) are the 
center of attention.
With the Gamemaster’s approval, characters of high Glory 
receive a positive modifier equal to Glory/1000.
Eloquence
The art of speaking or writing fluently, with poetic grace 
and charismatic delivery, is useful in any situation which requires 
a character to speak or write, especially in public. An eloquent 
character can be said to subdue the paltry force of acts with the 
irresistible power of words.
The role of poetry at court is to praise and celebrate the 
lord, to educate him and the audience, to criticize those who 
abuse their power, and to mock the unworthy.
Eloquence comes in handy as a persuasive force: 
The artful woman, who could have outwitted a fox, em -
broidered her reproaches so astutely that she quite turned 
the tables on Grifo: she had him see in her lover not sim -
ply her kinsman, but in very flesh and blood a second 
father to her. She could weave her yarn to such effect that 
Luke and John seemed less truthful by comparison. 
— Orlando Furioso , XVI.13
A success indicates the text is florid and well-delivered. A 
critical success indicates the listeners or readers are genuinely 
moved. Failure shows that it is boring. A fumble indicates that 
the speaker or author makes a fool of himself.
A particular Frankish use of Eloquence is gabbing. Gabs are 
boasts of virile prowess, which are not to be taken too seriously. 
They are mostly a form of entertainment and verbal jousting. 
Here are examples of the gabs that Charlemagne and his com -
panions make at the Byzantine court in 769: 
 n Charlemagne: “With one single blow of my sword I 
shall cut a rider and his horse in two!”
 n Roland: “So mightily will I sound my horn that the 
gates of the palace will be blown open!” 
 n Oliver: “Would the emperor allow his daughter in my 
room, I would possess her a hundred times in a single 
night! “
With the Gamemaster’s approval, characters of high Glory 
receive a positive modifier to Eloquence equal to Glory/1000.
Falconry
Falcons and hawks can be trained to hunt birds and other 
small prey. Such sport is the pleasure of nobles, who sometimes 
spend considerable money to maintain a first-class mews (the 
building reserved for the maintenance of hunting hawks).
The Falconry skill is used whenever knights and ladies take 
the birds into the fields to hunt. The skill indicates how well the 
character knows the sport, such as when to let the bird go, how 
to call it back, and how to handle it both safely and decorously.
Critical success means the bird caught its prey with a flourish 
and returned with it to the hawker’s feet, and that both falconer and 
bird acted gracefully and stylishly. Success indicates that the bird 
got its prey. Failure shows it missed. A fumble means the hawk flew 
away and was lost, or was somehow fatally injured in its hunt.
Individual birds may be trained by their masters, and a well-
trained bird can actually improve a knight’s Falconry skill with a 
positive modifier.
Gaming
The knights are seated on white silk brocade,
Amusing themselves by playing backgammon
And the wiser and older men play chess,
Whilst the agile young warriors practice fencing.
— The Song of Roland , VIII
The Gaming skill allows the user to perform certain types 
of medieval games effectively and with aplomb, whether in com-
petition or for entertainment. This skill does not include ability 
in physical sports such as wrestling or jousting.
Common types of courtly gaming include forms of gam -
bling, backgammon, draughts, and the noble art of chess.
T wo characters playing a game together make opposed 
Gaming rolls. If a monetary stake is involved, the difference be -
tween the two numbers rolled can be used by the Gamemaster 
to determine the amount of money won (i.e. a difference of 3 
might indicate that 3 denarii were won).
Gaming includes the knowledge of correct tournament 
procedures, customs, and behavior at that spectacle of chivalrous 
entertainment, the tournament. A Gaming skill roll is required 
to find out if the knight registered with the correct person at the 
right time or if he has embarrassed himself by arriving at the last 
minute. A roll could be used to see if the knight understands 
what certain trumpet blasts at a tourney mean; a failure might 
indicate that he did not have his squire place his helmet and sur-
coat out for the helm show, or that he missed an important feast 
or speech. Alternately, a failed roll might mean that the knight 
did not know whom to address, what do upon winning a joust, 
or how to properly ask for, receive, or display a lady’s favor.
Heraldry
A knight is known by his coat of arms. When in armor, ev-
eryone looks the same, except for the coat of arms blazoned on 
the shield, banner, and surcoat.
Members of families commonly display similar arms, such 
as the 2-headed eagle popular among the Aymon clan. Sons 
often design their arms to resemble their fathers’ . Quite by ac -
cident, some coats of arms are similar to one another, making 
perfect identification difficult, such as arms that have horses or 
ships, or have red on top.
Successful use of this skill indicates your character knows 
the members of the group or family for that coat of arms, or 
the several most likely holders of the arms. A critical success in -
dicates the precise person is recognized, as well as the general 
group. Failure means the character has no idea who it is, and a 
fumble means he identifies the coat of arms incorrectly.
==================== BOOK PAGE 102 ====================
101
The Gamemaster may require a Heraldry roll to see if the 
characters properly identify the coat of arms of someone they 
already know. Success means the character identifies the individ-
ual (or at least whose shield he bears).
Special: For every full 1,000 points of Glory a knight has, 
other characters gain a +1 modifier to their Heraldry skill for 
the purpose of identifying the glorious one. if a knight tries to 
recognize the arms of Sir Adalhart, with 1,605 Glory, the skill 
value is raised by 1 point; trying to identify Sir Astolf, with 
4,800 glory, adds 4 to the observer’s Heraldry skill, and no one 
can miss Charlemagne’s arms with a +20 bonus due to the king’s 
enormous Glory.
Languages
All characters from Frankland speak Frankish, as do the 
British, Saxons and Danes (though with heavy accents). In addi-
tion, they may know the following languages at different levels: 
 n Latin at –5.
 n Slav at –10.
 n Greek/Arabic/Hebrew at –15.
This means that only characters with a Languages score of 6 
or more know some rudimentary Latin, since their relative score 
in that language is 6 – 5 = 1.
Only very learned scholars may learn Greek, Arabic or 
Hebrew. Note that some characters may have learned a foreign 
tongue as a child (as a Moorish hostage, for example) and there-
fore ignore the negative modifier for that specific language.
W eird and exotic languages, such as those of the Basques 
and the Huns, can only be learned with special Gamemaster 
approval.
Play Instruments
Graelent, the poet-knight: 
A Breton born and Salomon’s kinsman,
He serves at Charlemagne’s court as his minstrel; 
Brought up by Charlemagne as page-boy since an infant,
He’s slept in Charlemagne’s rooms since he was little; 
No man on earth makes sweeter sound on instrument,
Nor better tells a line of verse or lyric.
— Song of Aspremont , X
Everyone appreciates a good tune to while away the winter 
and after-dinner hours, and some women even prefer men who 
show proficiency with this entertaining skill.
P aladin  music is medieval. It uses a variety of instruments 
which may be unfamiliar to us today, but which were popular in 
the Middle Ages: harp, lute, flute, recorder, double pipes, horn, 
bagpipes, trumpet, drums. The harp and the lute are particular -
ly used by knights at noble courts; the others are non-knightly 
instruments. 
Success indicates a good tune was played, while failure in -
dicates the song was out of tune, off the beat, or in the wrong 
chord. Critical success indicates a rousing tune that evoked an 
emotional response from the audience, while a fumble means 
the character played so badly that everyone laughed at him.
Reading & Writing
Lady Orable writes a message for her lover herself,
Applies her personal seal to it, gives it to her confidant,
her personal chamberlain — no other she trusted.
“Dear friend, may God protect you!
Go to Paris or Orleans on my behalf.
Find William, the noble baron, as quickly as you may,
And give him this silken scarf as a sign of my esteem.
That he may wear it in combat for the love of me.
People shall recognize it among all others.
Bring him this message from me,
May he have it read to him by a clerk,
And believe every word I have written. ” 
— William’s Youth
Reading and writing are treated as one skill, even if in 
Carolingian times they were separate abilities. Many knights 
who had some rudimentary reading ability were unable to write 
words, or even letters.
T o be literate means being able to read and write Latin, the 
old Roman tongue. Few people are well versed in this skill oth -
er than churchmen, some city merchants, and classical scholars. 
A Player-knight can try to read messages in any other language 
he might understand, but the combined use of Languages plus 
Reading & W riting needs two separate rolls.
A successful roll at Reading indicates that a message was 
understood. A critical success means that only a short time was 
needed to read the document. A failure indicates that the text 
remains incomprehensible, while a fumble means that misinter-
pretation occurs.
Romance
Romance is a knowledge skill concerning the practice of 
fin’amor. This custom of the court adores women and holds that 
men are inspired and improved by their emotional commitment 
to women. The Romance skill includes the basic knowledge and 
procedures for this custom. It is a composite skill, including bits 
of Awareness (catching a loving tone or the spark of ire in a la -
dy’s eye) and Intrigue (deciphering the coded messages in a love 
poem). It includes knowledge of the various “lover’s codes, ” such 
as the meaning of various flowers. 
A Romance roll might be required to know how to ap -
proach a woman for the first time; properly avert one’s eyes; 
interpret what she really means when she asks for a deed, and, 
especially, what is the best type of present to buy. For a woman, 
it includes knowing when to refuse an audience, when to accept 
a tryst, how to say no, an understanding of constructive cruelty, 
how to tell whether entertainment is new or customary, and how 
a man lies.
Courtly Skills
==================== BOOK PAGE 103 ====================
Chapter Five: Skills
102
While Romance is normally used by lovers, it can also be 
used against them by an observant third party. Just as the lovers 
use Romance to communicate with each other secretly, so a rival 
or father can use it to intercept those loving glances and read 
between the lines of the song. 
Singing
V ocal music pleases all listeners, whether sung in church, in 
court, or for a lover in a warm summer glade. Professional min -
strels wander from court to court with a repertoire of ballads and 
lays. Noblemen create love poems and romances, and find honor 
in their title of troubadour. The poorer troubadours perform their 
own songs, while the richer ones hire singers, called jongleurs, to 
perform for them. Both men and women sing; both to entertain 
the household on lonely winter nights and to please lovers.
A critical success at Singing indicates a powerful and emo -
tional performance, while a simple success indicates a pleasant ex-
perience. A failure indicates slurred or incorrect words or worse, 
while a fumble indicates something caused social embarrassment.
Tactics Skills
Battle
Battles occur when large bodies of men meet to fight each 
other, whether in true battle or in a tournament melee. Y our 
character’s Battle skill defines his ability in these fights; how 
well he is able to assess and react to situations, to remain within 
support distance of his group, to recognize good and bad situa -
tions and strategies, and to be lucky. 
For more information on uses of the Battle skill, see Chapter 8: 
Mass Combat.
Siege
Siege is used for battles in which troops defend or attack forti-
fied positions. A siege is a long, time-consuming event in which death 
by disease is a likely outcome for the besiegers. The Siege roll is used 
by the commander of the siege to see if he can breach the walls of his 
target, or by the defending commander to prevent such a breach. The 
siege skill can also spot the weaknesses in a structure, allowing the user 
to destroy or strengthen it. Finally, it is used as a simple way to check 
the health of the character when he is involved in a siege. 
For more information on uses of the Siege skill, see Chapter 8: 
Mass Combat.
Melee Weapon Skills
Melee weapon skills include all the weapons listed in their 
respective section: knowing how to use a one-handed sword lets 
you use two handed swords at the same skill rating. 
Axe
This weapon easily shatters or splits open shields.
One-Handed Axe: It does normal rolled damage against 
all targets. It does 2 points of damage to a shield for each ‘6’ 
rolled on the damage dice. A fumble indicates the weapon broke.

==================== BOOK PAGE 104 ====================
103
Two-Handed Axe: It does 1d6 extra damage against all tar-
gets, and does 2 points of damage to a shield for each ‘6’ rolled 
on the damage dice. A fumble indicates the weapon broke.
Bludgeon
Flail: This wicked weapon has many spiked heads mounted 
on the ends of chains, which are in turn attached to a handle. It is 
a one-handed weapon and can be used with a shield. The flail is 
sometimes referred to as a “morning star” because of the spiked 
heads, but see the entry below for the real morning star.
A flail ignores all protection given by shields, wrapping around 
any obstacle to damage its target. The flail does an extra 1d6 damage 
to all opponents wearing chainmail armor. However, the weapon 
is extremely clumsy, and on a roll of 1 (the raw number on 1d20, 
before any increases due to values over 20) it always strikes the user, 
doing full damage. With a fumble, the weapon breaks.
One-Handed Hammer: The military hammer is a blunt 
impact weapon that is particularly useful against plate armor. It 
is normally used one-handed with a shield. It gives an additional 
1d6 damage against suits of partial plate and full plate armor. A 
fumble indicates that the hammer broke.
Two-Handed Hammer: A hammer with a heavier head 
and longer shaft; it does 1d6 extra damage against all targets, and 
an additional 1d6 damage against partial plate armor. A fumble 
indicates that the hammer broke.
One-Handed Mace: This is an one-handed, iron-headed 
club, often embellished with flanged edges or spikes. It does 1d6 
additional damage against chainmail armor. A fumble indicates 
the mace has broken.
Morning Star: This long two-handed weapon ends in a 
clubbed head with spikes. It cannot be used with a shield, and 
does 1d6 extra damage against all targets, and an additional 1d6 
damage against chainmail-armored foes. A fumble means the 
morning star breaks.
W arflail: Essentially a two-handed flail. It does +1d6 ad -
ditional damage against all targets, and wraps around all shields 
and ignores any protection provided by their use. An additional 
1d6 damage is done to chainmail-armored foes. Because it is a 
clumsy weapon, a user always strikes himself for normal damage 
on a roll of 1 (before any increases due to values over 20). On a 
fumble the warflail breaks.
Dagger
This skill includes the use of all one-handed knives, daggers, 
and even short swords —it covers anything from the ancient 
Roman gladius to a common table knife. The tool is usually steel, 
but daggers might be of bronze, iron, or even possibly of stone 
or some other material. This small weapon is ineffective against 
heavily armored knights, but is carried by everyone, including 
women and priests. A knight usually has a dagger sheathed on 
his sword belt, ready for use in close quarters.
Due to its small size, a dagger does one die less damage 
when it strikes. A character who normally does 4d6 damage 
would do 3d6 with a dagger. The minimum damage is 1d6, so 
even a shriveled weak old man can still do damage. A fumble 
indicates the weapon was broken.
Lance
This is a spear used in a horse charge. It is held in the right 
hand and crossed over the horse’s neck, allowing the knight to 
crouch behind his shield and impact his target directly. He must 
move in a straight line for at least one melee round to get the 
lance charge modifier. Lance strikes are done while passing the 
target, who may strike back in opposed resolution. Unless un -
horsed, the lancer must further continue in the straight line for 
at least one full melee round.
A lance attack uses the Damage factor of the horse rather 
than the rider. Average horse Damage values of each size are: 
 n Rouncy = 4d6
 n Charger = 6d6
 n Destrier = 8d6
The lance charge is devastating against opponents using or-
dinary melee weapons. When one combatant uses a lance and 
the other does not have a lance, the lancer receives a +5 modifier 
to his Lance skill.
Fighting with the lance from horseback without the charge 
is the same as using a spear: 
 n Use the character’s Spear skill, not Lance,
 n He does not get the additional +5 for using the charge 
against other weapons.
 n He inflicts his Damage value, not the horse’s.
A lance breaks more easily than some weapons: the lance 
breaks if the attack roll is an odd number. A fumble indicates the 
weapon broke before doing damage.
Lance skill is used to joust, a friendlier and less lethal show 
of a martial skill. See Jousting (page 125) for more information.
A jousting lance is used just like a normal lance. However, a 
jousting lance is a piece of sport equipment, not a weapon, and 
so is designed not to destroy the loser of a joust, just to knock 
him from his horse.
Spear/Pole-arm
One-Handed Spear: This is a long stick with a pointed 
head on the end. It is used one-handed, and it can be used from 
horseback as a lance. It cannot be thrown like a javelin, which is 
much smaller. It does normal damage. A fumble indicates the 
spear broke.
Two-Handed Spear: This is a two-handed heavy spear 
used to strike and stab, but cannot be thrown as a javelin. It ne -
gates the horseman’s normal +5 weapon bonus against footmen. 
It is long enough that it even negates the +5 lance modifier ver -
sus non-lance weapons. A fumble indicates that the spear has 
broken.
Boar Spear: The distressing habit which many wild ani -
mals have of continuing to fight after they should be dead led 
to the invention of the boar spear. This is a normal two-handed 
Melee Weapon Skills

104
spear with a crossbar attached behind the head. When lanced, 
an animal cannot push its way up the spear to fight that last 
round after death. If the boar spear attack roll is the winner, the 
hunter must roll an unopposed STR roll. Success indicates he 
held the dying animal off. Failure indicates he did not quite do 
so, and he is knocked down and takes 1d6 damage from the vi -
olent fall, though the animal still cannot attack him directly. A 
fumble of the strength roll indicates the hunter failed to hold 
the creature off or it shook loose, and it gets its final attack 
against the hunter, unopposed.
Halberd: This is a heavy, two-handed, combination spear 
and axe which can be used to both cut and stab. It cannot be used 
from horseback or with a shield. Its length gains a +5 modifier 
for foot soldiers against horsemen, negating the foot soldier’s 
normal disadvantage. It does +1d6 extra damage against all foes. 
A fumble indicates that the halberd has broken.
Sword
One-Handed Sword: This essential knightly weapon is 
used with a shield. Favorite weapons have names and their own 
histories. A sword is of varying length but always of the best pos-
sible iron or, later, Frankish steel.
It does normal damage and has two advantages over all oth-
er weapons: with the exception of inferior iron swords and those 
produced in foreign lands, swords do not break when the user 
fumbles, but are dropped instead and can be recovered; and if a 
tie occurs in any resolution, a sword always breaks a non-sword.
Two-Handed Sword: It does 1d6 extra damage against all 
targets. On a fumble the great sword is dropped, but not bro -
ken, and can be recovered. On a tied resolution roll, the great 
sword breaks the opponent’s weapon, unless it is also a sword. 
The two-handed sword may not be used with a shield. 
Unarmed (Brawl/Grapple)
Grapple: Characters in the literature often throw down 
their weapons and grapple an opponent in dramatic fashion, 
either when every other weapon is broken, or when stalemate 
has set in between two knights with excellent weapon skills. 
Grapple is the skill used by giants to carry knights or ladies off 
to their castles. 
Brawling: Brawling is an unknightly manner of fighting. 
Common brawling includes wrestling, bashing, gouging, biting, 
kicking, and so on.
Glory should be gained from brawling only if no alterna -
tive form of combat was possible. Frivolously engaging in a brawl 
might even lose a knight 1 point of Honor, particularly if the 
results are disgraceful (a character killed or maimed in front of 
the court, for example).
Missile Weapon Skills
“If we, like common boys, use archer’s bows,
We shall be held in lowest reproach; 
But if we slay with fists or with a pole,
Our courage and our strength would be well shown; 
A curse on him who shoots first at his foe; 
He is a coward, who dares not fight up close!”
— Girart de Vienne , VIII
Knights disdain to use missile weapons in combat except 
for the short-ranged javelin. Only cowards fight from a distance, 
and personal Honor requires men to confront each other body 
to body. Hunting is different, and missile weapons are some -
times used, especially where food gathering is more important 
than sport.
There is no penalty to Honor for using missile weapons, 
but the Glory gained from defeating an opponent or creature is 
1/10th of normal; the same as in combats “for love.” This is the 
penalty whether a thrown rock or a crossbow is used, regardless 
of whether melee combat was part of the victory or not.
In combat, roll the missile weapon skill as an unopposed 
roll. If the target has a shield, he does not get a roll, but the shield 
acts as ‘cover, ’ providing a negative modifier to the attacker’s skill 
equal to the armor value of the shield.
All range weapons can be fired or thrown at full skill up 
to a third of their maximum range. They suffer –5 penalty for 
a distance up to two thirds and –10 for their maximum range. 
Additional modifiers must be applied for small or covered 
targets.
Ty pe Damage Rate of Fire Max. Range
Bow 3d6 1x/round 150 yards
Crossbow, Light 1d6+10 1x/round 150 yards
Medium 1d6+13 1x per 2 rounds 200 yards
Heavy 1d6+16 1x per 4 rounds 250 yards
Javelin normal –1d6 1x/round 30 yards
Sling normal –1d6 1x/round 100 yards
Bow
This is a wooden missile weapon normally used by peasants 
for hunting and by foot soldiers in war. Knights normally do not 
use bows in combat, although they often use them for hunting.
A regular bow does 3d6 damage regardless of the user’s 
Damage statistic, a compound bow does 5d6, and a longbow 
does 6d6. Bows are two-handed weapons, and no shield can be 
used while shooting one. A fumble indicates that the weapon has 
a broken string or has cracked.
==================== BOOK PAGE 106 ====================
105
Crossbow
This is a mechanical missile weapon invented, some say, by 
the devil. The Pope outlaws its use against Christians, but the 
damnable commoners seem not to hear. Knights generally scorn 
the use of crossbows in combat, although some may welcome 
their use in hunting (only!). Light crossbows do 1d6+10 dam -
age and require one melee round to load and shoot. A medium 
crossbow does 1d6+13 damage, and takes one melee round 
to load and another to shoot. A heavy crossbow does 1d6+16 
damage, and requires three melee rounds to load and another 
to shoot. 
Thrown Weapons
Axe: Used by Saxons and other Northmen. This is a regu -
lar one-handed axe, possibly specially balanced for throwing. A 
fumble indicates the weapon broke. 
Javelin: Spears that are intended be thrown at a nearby op-
ponent or game animal are javelins. This is not the same weapon 
as a lance or spear, but is much shorter and lighter. A fumble 
indicates the weapon broke.
Sling: This cheap weapon is much used by commoners. It is 
a simple strip of leather used to propel small stones at a medium 
distance.
Throwing Objects: Success with a thrown weapon indi -
cates that the target was hit, failure that it was not. As always, a 
critical success indicates that maximum damage is done (if such 
was the aim), and a fumble might hit a nearby friend instead of 
the target. The Thrown W eapons skill may be used by a Player-
knight to throw a rope to a drowning person.
The Gamemaster rules on the modifier, if any, that should 
be applied to the task. Throwing a heavy object at a tiny target 
far away might suffer a –10 modifier, while a throw from the top 
of a wall at a huge target below might gain a +10 modifier.

Chapter Six:  
General Mechanics
T
his chapter deals with the fundamental mechanics of the 
game. It is important that the Gamemaster be solidly 
conversant in these rules, but players need not memorize 
them. Gamemasters can use The Adventure of the Jewel introduc-
tory scenario in Chapter 19 to introduce the players to the rules 
of the game while playing.
Die-Roll Resolutions
P
aladin  is a game in which determining a character’s suc-
cesses and failures is important. In fact, several kinds of 
success or failure may result from your rolling of the dice. 
Most things that your character attempts are automatically 
successful. Y ou simply tell the Gamemaster what your charac -
ter is doing, such as, “My knight walks up the aisle towards the 
throne, ” or, “I ride out of the castle to my manor.” These things 
simply occur more or less as you describe them.
However, sometimes your knight attempts things that re -
quire skill, discipline, or talent and which therefore might not 
succeed. During such critical times, the Gamemaster asks players 
to make die rolls for their characters to see whether they are suc-
cessful in what they are trying to do.
Generally, rolls are required whenever a character attempts 
a maneuver or skill in a situation of stress or otherwise acts with-
out deliberate forethought.
Modifiers to the value may apply, temporarily raising or 
lowering the statistic. The value may be lowered to 0, in which 
case failure is certain, or above 19, in which case success is cer -
tain. In either case, though, the die should still be rolled to see 
whether a fumble or critical success occurs (see the sections 
below for more information on fumbles, critical successes, and 
modifiers).
Die Roll Results
A die roll against a statistic has four possible results: success, 
failure, critical, and fumble. 
Success
If the player rolls lower than the modified statistic value 
(see below for modifier rules), he has succeeded at whatever he 
was attempting. It is not a notable success (see Critical, below), 
but it is good enough. No matter the relative value of the statis -
tics involved, a success beats a failure. 
Failure
If the player rolls higher than the specified statistic, he has 
failed at whatever he was attempting. It is not a catastrophic fail-
ure (see Fumble, below), but it is just not good enough. 
Critical
Whenever a character attempts a resolution roll of any type, 
he has a chance for a critical success. A critical success is achieved 
whenever a character rolls a number exactly equal to his modi -
fied statistic value. When this occurs, the character has achieved 
the ultimate success; regardless of the number rolled on the die, 
a critical beats a success, and so guarantees a win or, at least, a tie. 
This is true even if a character with a statistic of 1 rolls a 1. 
In some situations, no special benefit arises from a critical 
success, with no difference between a critical and a normal suc -
cess: In particular, a critical success with an attribute (SIZ, STR, 
DEX, CON, APP) usually gives no special result. In most cases, 
though, unusually favorable results derive from a critical success, 
depending upon whether the roll was for a skill (including com-
bat skills), a trait, or a passion.
==================== BOOK PAGE 109 ====================
Chapter Six: General Mechanics
108
Skills: A critical success on a skill roll has two potential bene-
fits. First, the results are extraordinarily successful; the Gamemaster 
may relate specific game effects beyond those normally associated 
with a success using that skill, at his or her discretion.
In combat, specifically, a critical success with a weapon skill 
indicates that the character inflicts maximum damage (simply 
count each die as a “6, ” plus any modifiers) 
Second, the skill may, with Gamemaster approval, gain an 
experience check (see Experience in this chapter), indicating a 
chance to increase that skill during the Winter Phase.
T raits or Passions: A critical success on a trait or passion 
roll works in a similar way to skills, giving increased benefits and 
an experience check. A critical trait or passion roll usually in -
dicates extreme, perhaps even fanatical behavior in accordance 
with the trait or passion in question.
Fumble
A fumble occurs when a character receives a roll of 20, un -
less his modified statistic value is 20 or more, in which case he 
has no chance to fumble, and a critical success is instead achieved 
on a die roll of 20.
A fumble is a disastrous stroke of bad luck resulting in such 
things as dropping or breaking your weapon during combat, or 
coughing or cursing in the midst of a romantic ballad. Note that, 
in most cases, pure attribute rolls (SIZ, STR, DEX, CON, APP) 
are simply a succeed-or-fail matter, and neither fumbles nor crit-
ical successes should have any special effect for them.
Fumbles can occur frequently in P aladin . This provides 
many opportunities for storytelling to the Gamemaster. In many 
cases, a fumble lets the Gamemaster balance a situation that has 
gotten out of hand, feed the players false information, or simply 
inject some humor and excitement into the story. However, fre-
quent fumbles open the way for unwise Gamemasters to inflict 
excessive and possibly incessant grief on their players.
Most situations not covered by the rules should present a 
possible fumble result. However, Gamemasters who insist that 
every fumble means a major disaster tend to spoil the game for 
everyone.
Gamemasters relate the specifics of a fumbled roll, based on 
the guidelines suggested for the particular skill attempted (see 
Chapter 5: Skills) and the Gamemaster’s discretion.
Unopposed Resolution
Unopposed rolls are made directly against the character’s 
pertinent statistic value. T o determine success or failure, roll 
1d20 and compare the result with the character’s statistic value. 
Example of Unopposed Resolution: During a battle with 
the Saracens, Sir Adalhart tries hard to remember the voice of the 
knight he is fighting, for it sounds strangely familiar. His player 
tries to make a Recognize roll on 1d20, gets a 4, which is lower 
than Adalhart’s Recognize value of 5, and so succeeds. He discovers 
that his opponent is in fact his own brother, abducted as a child by 
Moorish raiders and now fighting in their service.
Opposed Resolution
Opposed rolls are made against the character’s own statistic 
and the opponent’s rolled number. Simple success is not enough 
to defeat the opponent. T o win, the player must both succeed 
with his own roll, and roll higher than the opponent’s player.
An opposed resolution may result in a winner and a loser, a 
tie, or two losers. Both opponents may be losers if they both fail 
their roll against their own statistic.
Example of Opposed Resolution: Adalhart attacks a Saxon 
opponent with his sword. The Saxon has a sword skill of 12 and 
Adalhart 17. In the first round the opponent rolls a 13 and fails; 
Adalhart rolls a 1, a success. Adalhart is the winner of the resolu -
tion and may now roll for damage.
Partial Success: If a character rolls a success relative to his 
own statistic, but loses relative to the opponent’s roll, he gains 
a “partial success.” Some minor benefit is usually gained from 
a partial success. In combat, a character achieving a partial suc -
cess may parry with a shield (see Chapter 7: Combat for more on 
combat resolution), if using one.
Tie: If both characters roll the same (modified) number, and 
are both successful, a tie occurs. A tie means that the situation is 
temporarily unresolved, though time passes while the combatants 
contend with one another. Continue with the resolution during 
the next round, as normal. Ties may have specific results in certain 
special instances; for example, a tie in personal combat means that 
other kinds of weapons are broken by a character using a sword.
Modifiers
The Gamemaster may apply modifiers to any statistic when-
ever they are useful or “realistic.” The Gamemaster should be con-
sistent in applying these to both Gamemaster and Player-knights.
Modifiers are always added to or subtracted from the char-
acter’s statistic values, not to the roll itself. However, values 
modified to 21 or higher increase the die roll (See V alues Greater 
Than 20, below).
t able  6–1: s taNdard  Modifiers
Modifier Situation
+/–5 Standard modifier (used for most situations).
+/–10 Strong modifier (for dramatic situations).
+/–15 Extreme modifier (for spectacular situations).
In certain unique or epic situations, modifiers of+/–20 or 
even more might be awarded or assigned. Such modifiers essen -
tially guarantee success or failure, but the die should be rolled in 
any case to see if a critical success or fumble occurs.
Example of Skill Modifier: Sir Adalhart has been un -
horsed and wounded by his opponent. He tries to grab a riderless 
horse on the battlefield, using his Horsemanship skill of 15. The 
Gamemaster applies a –5 modifier for being wounded, reducing his 
Horsemanship to 10. The player rolls a 9, a success, which means 
Adalhart managed to take the horse by the bridle and mount it.
==================== BOOK PAGE 110 ====================
109
Reflexive Modifiers 
In many cases of opposed resolution, modifiers are reflexive. 
This means that both participants are affected, but in opposite 
directions. For example, in the case of the mounted knight at -
tacking man on foot, the mounted man receives a +5 modifier 
to his weapon skill for being mounted, while the man on foot 
suffers a –5 modifier to his weapon skill for the disadvantage of 
being on foot against a man on horseback.
Note that the disparity produced by these reflexive mod -
ifiers is not the same as a +10 total modifier for the mounted 
character.
Types of Modifiers
Modifiers can be assigned to any statistic. The following are 
the standard types.
s kill  Modifiers  
These are used to adjust a character’s skill in a particular sit-
uation. Modifiers make skills harder to use or, less commonly, 
easier. They should be used whenever the situation calls for it.
Example of Skill Modifier: On a journey through the forest, 
Sir Adalhart actively tries to spot ambushes using his Awareness 
skill of 5. The player states that Adalhart is carefully scanning 
each tree near the trail for robbers before passing underneath. 
The Gamemaster permits Adalhart a +5 modifier to his normal 
Awareness value, temporarily making his skill value a 10, because 
a treetop ambush is indeed the favored tactic of the local bandits.
Adalhart rolls a 10, a critical success. He spots two robbers 
lurking in a tree about 200 yards ahead and quietly leaves the 
trail, bypassing them before they see him. The Gamemaster decides 
that Adalhart receives a check for his Awareness skill. Note that if 
the bandits had been hiding on the ground instead of up in a tree, 
Adalhart would not have received a modifier.
t rait , Passio N aNd  s taNdiNg  Modifiers  
As with skills, these are used to modify traits, passions or 
standings when the current situation is likely to influence a char-
acter’s normal behavior. For example, a character whose family 
had betrayed him to an enemy might receive a –10 modifier to 
his Love (family) passion. 
Modifiers may be requested when a player feels that his 
character or an standing group would act differently from what 
would be determined by a simple die roll. The objective of 
this type of modifier is to make both player and Gamemaster 
comfortable with the trait or passion roll in question. The 
Gamemaster must permit realistic modifiers whenever the play-
ers ask for them: Characters should not be forced to behave ran-
domly at the whim of a die roll if the player is trying to roleplay.
Example of Trait Modifier: Sir Gontran of Boulogne, a 
notoriously lazy knight, is asked to perform a difficult task by a 
beautiful damsel of his lord’s court. Although Gontran is normally 
unlikely to undertake such a task, his desire for the charming young 
woman might make a difference. The player asks for a modifier be-
fore he makes his character’s Energetic roll. The Gamemaster agrees 
and gives Sir Gontran a +5 modifier for this special situation.
a ttribute  Modifiers
Sometimes an attribute roll may be modified. The most 
common use of this is the negative modifier that armor gives to 
DEX rolls in some situations (see Dexterity Rolls and Armor in 
Chapter 7 for more information on DEX rolls and armor worn).
c o Mbat  Modifiers
These modifiers are based on weapon types, the relative po-
sition of combatants, and so on. The standard combat modifier 
is+/–5, as described above. Combat modifiers are discussed at 
greater length in Chapter 7.
Values Greater Than 20
If a character has a statistic value greater than 20, even if it 
is only due to a temporary modifier, then every die roll he makes 
against that value is increased equal to the amount of the value 
over 20. Thus, a knight with a Dexterity of 25 would add 5 to 
the roll every time he makes a DEX check.
T reat any resulting roll of 20 or higher a critical success. 
Thus, a value greater than 20 in a statistic increases the chance of 
a critical success and eliminates the chance of a fumble.
Note that the die roll can never be reduced, only increased. 
A penalty to a statistic is applied to the statistic’s value, not to 
the roll itself. 
Example of V alues Greater than 20: Sir Adalhart fights 
from horseback against a foot soldier. Adalhart’s sword skill is a 
modified 22 (17+5 for attacking an unmounted foe). His die roll 
is increased by +2. Thus if he and the foot soldier both rolled 13 on 
their dice, Adalhart would win because his modified roll equals 15. 
For Adalhart, a roll of 18, 19, or 20 counts as a roll of 20, a critical 
success. He can’t fumble.
Values of 0 or Less
Due to modifiers, a statistic might be temporarily reduced 
to zero or less. In such circumstances the character automatically 
fails, but must still roll since he has an increased chance to fum -
ble. Each point of the skill below zero increases the chance to 
fumble by one point. Fumble is normally a roll of 20. If a modi -
fied skill is –4, then a fumble results when anything from 16 to 
20 is rolled.
Feats
At the Gamemaster’s discretion, a player may attempt a spe-
cial roll to any of his skills, allowing him to gamble all or nothing 
in a desperate situation. 
When attempting a feat, the knight’s skill value is temporar-
ily reduced by half, and the outcomes of the die roll are reduced 
to either a critical success or a fumble — if the roll succeeds, 
count it as a critical success, while if it fails, it fails spectacularly!
Die-Roll Resolutions

Chapter Six: General Mechanics
110
Time Scale
T
ime in P aladin  passes at clearly defined rates, as ex -
plained below.
Campaign Time
Generally, one scenario occurs per game year. A scenario is 
defined as a linked series of adventures, feasts, combats, battles, 
quests, tournaments, or other knightly activities. Usually a sce -
nario lasts no more than one or two seasons of game time.
There are a number of reasons why the one-scenario-per-
year rule is useful: 
1. Much of a knight’s time is not his own. Training, duties 
to one’s lord, and the needs of one’s family take prece-
dence over the pursuit of personal fame. Travel times 
are very slow as well. The period of good weather and 
free time available in a typical year in Frankland only 
suffices for a single scenario, and some years may pass 
without any scenarios occurring at all.
2. Part of the fun of the game is the dynastic segment; 
further, death is more likely in Paladin  than in oth -
er roleplaying games with more magical healing. 
Children are a solution to both these points. If Player-
knights are to have children who grow to adulthood 
during a campaign, game years must pass quickly.
3. Glory is the goal of the game. Since Glory accrues only 
after time has passed and one’s exploits become part 
of heroic legend, time needs to pass briskly. Glory, 
coming as it does from public recognition, garners 
benefits only during the Winter Phase of the game, 
not at the immediate conclusion of glorious events.
4. Characters improve quickly and age slowly as the years 
pass. As each year passes, it is possible to augment 
characters significantly due to Glory, experience, and 
training. This character improvement is based on year-
ly updating, and aging doesn’t necessarily spoil charac-
ters. In fact, the Aging table is designed so that Player-
knights can live (and play significant roles) to about 
seventy years of age — or even older.
5. In order to experience the evolution of Dark-Age 
Frankish society into a socially and technologically 
more advanced medieval society (see Chapter 15: The 
Future), it is necessary that campaign time be mea -
sured in years; for every eleven game years approxi -
mately one century of medieval evolution takes place 
for the Franks. (Foreign cultures and troops do not 
benefit from technological advancements.) 
Winter Phase
Each game year ends with the annual Winter Phase. It is 
a non-playing measure of time used by characters for train -
ing and many other important pursuits. The character sheet is 
updated by the player and various bookkeeping tasks are per -
formed. These changes are figured at the end of the year, during 
winter, when most people retire from the foul weather. See 
Chapter 10 for more information on the Winter Phase. 
Narrative Time
Narrative time is the quick passage of time that moves the 
game along. For instance, most travel time is narrative in nature. 
Other examples, such as those below, are easy to imagine.
 n “Nothing important happens the whole summer. ” 
 n “It is twenty days until you are fully healed again. ” 
 n “It takes you twelve days to get there. ” 
 n “Y ou wait two weeks for the enemy fleet to show up. ” 
 n “Y ou search the woods for a week and nothing 
happens. ” 
 n “The storm lasts for two days. ” 
 n “Y ou have to wait here for an hour. Will you do any -
thing in that time?”
 n “It takes five minutes for you to reach the trees. ” 
 n “The giant will reach you in one minute, so get pre -
pared fast. ” 
In each case, the Gamemaster controls time by stating how 
it passes, in terms of years, months, weeks, days, hours, and/or 
minutes.
Real Time
Real time occurs in scenarios when players speak in charac-
ter, or otherwise vicariously describe the “here and now” of the 
game action from their character’s perspective. As a player, you 
might say, “My knight is walking to stand beside the courtier 
who is speaking; ” or “Y our highness, might I inquire as to the 
identity of the lady I just saw in that window?; ” or “I’ll speak to 
this herald, seeking to gain access to the Duke.” 
Battle Rounds
When a knight is in a battle during a scenario the time pass-
es in battle rounds. Battle rounds are approximately 30 minutes 
in duration; a battle lasts from two to twelve rounds. Pursuit 
rounds are possible, with the same duration as a battle round.
Because of the confused state of affairs in battles (the “fog of 
war” and so forth), only one combat skill use is normally performed 
during a battle round, though many other events may occur.
Melee Rounds
When the game turns into split-second action, especially 
during fighting, it uses melee rounds. The melee round is a brief 
period of time. The exact duration is not measured in seconds, 
but in terms of activities performed. Specifically, one action can 
be performed in a melee round.
==================== BOOK PAGE 112 ====================
111
Movement
M
ovement during the game is one of the most com -
plicated subjects that the Gamemaster has to han -
dle. In P aladin , movement and actions are highly 
abstracted for the purposes of quick resolution and simplicity. 
The Gamemaster must be ready to adjudicate specific situations 
using the following rules as guidelines.
Different rates of movement are calculated according to 
different time scales. Of significance are melee movement and 
travel movement.
Movement Rate
This statistic is used for certain types of movement. It deter-
mines how many yards per melee round an encumbered charac-
ter or creature can move, and it affects forced march travel rates.
For humans, Movement Rate = (STR+DEX)/10.
For animals, Movement Rate varies. Generally, for four-
legged creatures, it is equal to (STR+DEX)/5, but some crea -
tures may have adjustments to this base. 
Movement on foot uses the character’s Movement Rate. If 
the character is mounted, he uses the mount’s Movement Rate 
instead. Normally, the character may move a number of yards 
equal to his Movement Rate as his sole action for one round (see 
Actions in Melee in Chapter 7).
Characters and horses used to bearing heavy loads who find 
themselves unencumbered gain an increase of +2 to their basic 
Movement Rate (see Encumbrance on page 118).
Melee Movement
A character or animal may walk its Movement Rate in yards 
each Melee Round. Running and sprinting allow the character 
to move faster; see Running and Sprinting (page 117). 
Melee combat is the chief situation that calls for melee 
round resolution, but the Gamemaster may use the melee round 
for non-combat events if desired. For example, if two knights are 
racing their horses through a crowded village, melee movement 
may be used to determine who won.
Remember that a character may normally perform only one 
action in a round. Characters fighting one another are consid -
ered to be engaged in combat and may not move or do anything 
else other than fight without suffering a penalty assigned by the 
Gamemaster.
Travel Movement
T ravel movement covers vast distances in a few words. Ta b l e  
6–2: T ravel Distances below offers detailed guidelines for scenar-
io movement. The Gamemaster can use the table to determine 
travel times for specific situations. 
The distances for normal travel, as shown on the table, do 
not vary for individuals but are based on average travel times. 
Forced marching, on the other hand, puts character or horse 
movement to the test, so Forced March movement is affected 
by individual Movement Rates. Given below are travel distances 
along different types of roads and paths. The pace of travel is 
important.
Travel Pace
Given below on the T ravel Distances table are standard rates 
for different types of roads and paths, using one of the four fol -
lowing general paces: 
Cautious: “Cautious” movement is used when travelers are 
taking great care to avoid ambushes, looking for tracks or other 
signs, finding their way when lost, or the like. 
Leisurely: Certain persons or groups may travel only at 
the “Leisurely” pace or slower: most ladies, siege trains, mer -
chants, monks on donkeys, and badly injured characters needing 
Chirurgery, for example. 
Normal: People on horse and afoot move at the same gen -
eral pace for a day’s travel. However, parties on foot cannot move 
at the “Hurried” pace: “Normal” is the best they can do, unless 
they wish to try a forced march (see below). Most often, Player-
knights move at the “Normal” pace.
Hurried: If something important is occurring or there 
is otherwise great need, mounted characters may move at the 
“Hurried” pace without penalty.
Road Terminology
Royal Road: A road protected by the laws of the king. 
Crimes committed against anyone on the Royal Road are treat -
ed as an offense against the king (high treason). Otherwise this 
is like a T rade Road.
T rade Road: A raised dirt or stone road, repaired occasion-
ally. Most often a leftover from the Roman Empire.

Local Road: An undrained, ill-repaired dirt road.
Path: A well-marked path through woods or fields.
