Traveling Unknown Routes
King Charlemagne encounters such bad weather and trav -
el difficulties that his followers get lost and lose the king’s 
track: 
And as that ryall raid ovir the rude mure (king, moor)
Him betyde ane tempest that tyme, hard I tell.
The wind blew out of the eist siflie and sture (strong )
The deip durandlie draif in mony deip dell; (continuously)
Sa feirslie fra the firmament, sa fellounlie it fure,
Thair micht na folk hald na fute on the heich fell. (hill)
In point thay war to parische, thay proudest men and 
pure; 
In thay wickit wedderis thair wist nane to dwell.
Amang thay myrk mountanis sa madlie they mer,
Be it was pryme of the day,
Sa wonder hard fure thay
That ilk ane tuik ane seir way, (each one)
And sperpellit full fer. (dispersed)
— The T ale of Ralph Collier
Often characters enter unfamiliar regions. A successful 
Hunting skill roll (to check the way with passers-by, seek roads, 
and so on) is needed to make progress while traveling in such 
areas.
Critical success allows movement at Normal pace; success, 
a Leisurely pace. On a failure, a character is confused and must 
stop the trip to get his bearings, delaying the journey for at least 
an day or more, depending on Gamemaster preference. A fumble 
leads a character on the wrong direction, and he becomes thor -
oughly lost.
Forced March
Forced marching is a deliberate effort to go farther and fast-
er than normal. Making a forced march requires a CON roll for 
each day of the journey. If the roll is successful, add three times 
the character’s or mount’s Movement Rate as a bonus in miles to 
the appropriate distance given in T able 6–2: T ravel Distances, with 
further adjustments if the Gamemaster deems them necessary. 
A critical success on this CON roll has no additional effect 
beyond that of the standard forced march.
A failed CON roll indicates exhaustion. The character 
or mount stops after moving half the attempted distance and 
must rest for several hours, perhaps for the rest of the day (the 
Gamemaster must determine the exact time spent resting).
W orse, a fumbled CON roll in a forced march indicates 
that a serious injury was taken during the day’s travel. Distance 
traveled and rest needed are the same as for failure, but if a horse 
fumbles the roll, it is lamed. A character fumbling the roll takes 
2d6 damage from heat stroke, a sprained limb, or some other 
travel mishap (see Injury and Health in Chapter 7).
Example of a Forced March: Sir Adalhart decides to make 
a forced march. He takes his best riding horse, a charger named 
Flame. The trip is entirely on a well-known royal road.
Adalhart and his squire (mounted on a rouncy) leave at dawn 
to visit a castle about 50 miles away, so a single day’s forced march 
should easily do it.
Flame has a Movement Rate of 9 and a CON of 13. Adalhart’s 
player successfully makes a CON roll for Flame, so the trip is made 
successfully. The bonus for forced marching gained by this particu-
lar horse is (9x3) = 27 miles. T able 6–2: T ravel Distances shows 
that a day’s movement on a royal road, at a hurried pace, is 30 
miles. 30+27 = 57 miles, so Adalhart covers the 50 miles easily and 
arrives at the castle before sunset.
The squire’s horse also sustains the pace (makes its CON roll), 
but being a much slower horse, gains less of a forced march bonus 
and is gradually left behind as the day progresses. The squire won’t 
arrive until the next day, even if he tries to ride all night: the forced 
march rule puts an absolute limit on what can be achieved in a day.
Experience
O
ne of the most exciting things to occur in roleplaying 
games is the growth and development of characters as 
game time progresses. In P aladin , these changes are 
brought about by Glory, by training, by experience in the field, 
and through the effects of aging. 
Experience is handled using experience checks. The results 
of experience, as well as Glory and training, are noted during the 
Winter Phase.
Gaining Experience
Every skill, trait, or passion that can change as a result of 
experience is marked on the character sheet by having a box (❏) 
near it. This box is checked when the character uses a skill, trait, 
or passion in a significant manner during an adventure. Statistics 
do not change immediately as a result of this experience; all ex -
perience checks are rolled during the Winter Phase.
Sometimes a result grants a “+1” to a value — this is applied 
during the Winter Phase, in addition to an experience check, if 
any (roll for experience first, then apply the +1). Rarely, the +1 
should be applied immediately, but this is for special circum -
stances, such as your knighting ceremony or saving the life of 
Charlemagne. If you acquire two or more +1’s to the same statis-
tic, they are both applied after any experience check roll.
Skills
There are times during play when the Gamemaster tells the 
player to “check” one of his character’s skills. This means that the 
character has successfully used that skill in a time of crisis and 
may learn from the experience. A box may be checked only when 
==================== BOOK PAGE 114 ====================
113
the Gamemaster says the player may do so. The mark reminds 
the player to check the skill during the Winter Phase to see if any 
increase occurred.
Experience checks for skills are intended to be very difficult 
to gain, and are not automatic whenever a success is achieved. 
Gamemasters must explain this point to the players. The ability 
to award or deny an experience check is one of the key powers of 
the Gamemaster.
Note that a squire’s skills may increase in the same manner 
as a Player-knight’s skills.
A check for a skill is possible only if at least one of the fol -
lowing two conditions pertains: 
1. A critical success is achieved. If a character does his best 
possible, learning is likely.
2. A success in a significant situation is achieved. Even a 
dozen successful attacks on a gang of poorly-armed peasant lev -
ies would not be justification for a check to a weapon skill, since 
no real risk or challenge was incurred by the attacker, and noth-
ing important was achieved by their defeat.
Example of Skill Check: Sir Adalhart, in love with a lady, 
announces that he wants to write a love poem to her. Adalhart’s 
player actually writes the poem and reads it to the group. The poem 
is reasonably good and the player showed great effort, bringing 
some real creativity to the game, so the Gamemaster decides that 
Adalhart has succeeded with his Eloquence skill, and states that it 
may be checked. Note that no roll was necessary here, only effort and 
the Gamemaster’s approval.
Traits and Passions
As with skills, there are times during play when the 
Gamemaster tells a player to check one of his character’s traits or 
passions. This means that the character has displayed significant 
behavior in accordance with that statistic. 
This action or behavior does not necessarily have to be ob -
served by other characters or players. 
If the rules for traits or passions request a roll, this means 
that your character might learn something if he acts or feels the 
right way. But his actions are not yet clear, or significant enough. 
A successful roll means he might get the check, too.
Example of Trait/Passion Check: Fickle Sir Adalhart’s new 
lady-love asks if he has ever loved any woman as deeply as he loves 
her. Adalhart answers, “Never has my love been so deep, ” choosing 
not to tell the woman of his recent obsession with her predecessor. 
The Gamemaster feels that Adalhart is being dishonest, given the 
depth of feeling he claimed in the poem he wrote a few weeks earlier, 
and states that Adalhart gets a Deceitful check.
Results Of Experience Checks
When a character has a check next to a skill, trait, or pas -
sion, the player must make an experience roll during the Winter 
Phase. If the roll is successful, the checked value increases by 1 
point. Experience rolls may bring skills over 20, with luck.
See Chapter 10: The Winter Phase for more information.
Experience

==================== BOOK PAGE 116 ====================
115
H
istory, romance, and legend all agree that fighting is a 
knight’s primary work. Therefore, combat is an import -
ant part of P aladin . The usual enemies are other knights, 
honorable opponents and fellow nobles; foreign enemies such 
as invading Saxons or Saracens; personal enemies of the lord; 
beasts of the hunt, such as boars, lions or bears; or monsters such 
as giants or hippogriffs.
But even combat should involve roleplaying. In combat 
situations where love, honor, or another powerful passion is in -
voked, knights may be inspired to greatness and fight with su -
perhuman strength and skill. See Passions in Chapter 3 for rules 
concerning inspiration. 
Much Glory can be gained from combat. Moreover, this 
possibility of winning Glory ensures that combat is directed 
more toward roleplaying than toward basic concerns like expe -
rience and treasure. 
The best games of P aladin  include these emotional and 
intellectual decisions in much of the combat that occurs. This 
is why the combat rules below are relatively simple and flexible. 
Gamemasters are free to add more detail to suit their personal 
preference.
The Melee Round
T
he basic time measure for single or small group combat 
is the melee round. This is a short, elastic unit of time — 
the time required to plan and perform one action in me-
lee. Melee rounds continue in succession until everyone is done 
fighting, whether through incapacitation, surrender, flight, or 
death.
In these rules, the limit of one “action” per round means one 
category of action, not one physical movement, per round. For 
example, combat is not a single blow, it is an exchange of attacks 
and parries and cautious waiting for an opening.
The limit of one action per melee round is a loose one, with 
several apparent exceptions. For example, a character whose 
Knockdown statistic is exceeded must make a balance roll that 
round, which is not considered to be a second category of action. 
Chapter Seven: Combat
Then first they hurled the hurtling spears 
In sharpest showers that shook the shields. 
Then they clashed with their brands, the battle-boards 
bursting, 
And hewed with might the white linden 
Till they shivered the shields with shattering strokes, 
As they wielded their weapons in wild warfare. 
They thrust and lashed and thundered blows 
Till the blood of the twain forth burst in streams.
— The Lay of Hildebrand
The general point of the one-action limit is to keep the 
game moving swiftly. The players and the Gamemaster must 
cooperate in this, or the game soon becomes bogged down in 
pointless minutiae.
P aladin  uses the following personal combat system to de -
termine the winners and losers of a fight. Each time combat is 
joined the following procedure should be followed: 
1. Determination Phase
2. Resolution Phase
3. Winner’s Phase
4. Loser’s Phase
5. Movement Phase
Determination Phase
Fighting does not actually occur during this phase, but is 
declared now.
All combatants state what they intend to do this round, in-
cluding the weapon they are using. T argets and opponents are 
named. The Gamemaster decides whether the players or their 
Gamemaster-run opponents make their statements of intent 
first.
Characters can fight, or do something else, as listed below 
under Actions in Melee. Generally, characters can fight or move, 
not both. 
==================== BOOK PAGE 117 ====================
Chapter Seven: Combat
116
If the Gamemaster permits combat in combination with 
another action, characters attempting such combat suffer 
+5/–5 reflexive modifiers to weapon skills, or other penalties 
as appropriate.
Enemies within 1 yard of each other are automatically 
considered “engaged, ” and need not move to fight. Otherwise, 
movement must take place (during the Movement Phase) before 
combat can be resolved in subsequent rounds.
Resolution Phase
Any non-movement action declared in the previous phase 
is now resolved.
If the character did not intend to move this round, then his 
action now takes place. If a skill or combat skill roll is required 
for the action, it is made now. 
Generally, characters can either fight (during this phase) 
or move (during the Movement Phase), but not both. Lance 
charges are an exception to this rule: Knights both move and 
fight during the charge, and that resolution takes place now. 
If combat occurs, both combatants roll their respective 
modified weapon skills, using opposed resolution (see Die-Roll 
Resolution in Chapter 6). The results leave a winner and a loser, a 
tie, or two losers. In addition, critical successes and fumbles have 
special meaning in combat. 
If one character succeeds and the other fails, proceed to the 
Winner’s Phase.
If both fighters fail their weapon rolls, then both missed. 
The fight is inconclusive this round, and no damage is dealt 
on either side. They can try again (or try something else) next 
round. Proceed to the Movement Phase. 
If both fighters roll the same number and both are success-
ful, then a tie occurs. This is essentially the same as if both had 
failed, but if one combatant’s weapon is a sword and the oth -
er’s is not, the sword breaks the other weapon. Proceed to the 
Movement Phase.
A fumble means one of two things: Either that the fum -
bling fighter dropped his weapon (if it was a sword), or that his 
weapon broke (if it was any other type of weapon). If fighting 
with a two-handed weapon, he is completely disarmed and must 
dodge, rearm, grapple or run away in subsequent rounds. If the 
knight was using a one-handed weapon with a shield, he may still 
use the Defend tactic (see page 126).
A critical success on a weapon roll means that the player 
who rolled the critical deals maximum damage for his weapon 
during the Winner’s Phase — unless both fighters rolled criti -
cals, in which case their attacks are resolved as a tie.
Special: A “natural” roll of 1 on 1d20, before any modifiers, 
is important if a character is using a flail or warflail. 
Example of Combat — Attack: Sir Adalhart, using a 
two-handed axe, is fighting a Saxon Edhilingui (noble). Sir 
Adalhart rolls a 13 — a success. The Saxon rolls a 2 — a success, 
but lower than Sir Adalhart’s roll. Sir Adalhart wins, the Saxon 
loses but gets a Partial Success. 
Winner’s Phase
The winner rolls his damage against the loser. 
The Winner rolls his Damage dice modified by the weapon 
used and totals the results. This is his “rolled damage.” A critical 
success deals maximum damage — count each damage die as “6” 
plus any modifiers. 
If the winner achieved a notable success (a critical, for ex -
ample, or a success against a noteworthy foe or that moves the 
story forward significantly), he may, with Gamemaster approval, 
receive an experience check for the weapon used.
Example of Attack — Rolling Damage: Sir Adalhart rolls 
his Damage: 4d6, plus 1d6 for the two handed axe, for a total of 
5d6. He gets a total of 23 for his rolled damage. 
Loser’s Phase
The loser checks for special results and takes damage.
First, the loser should check for Knockdown immediately, 
based on the damage his opponent rolled (see Knockdown, page 
119).
Next, if the loser is using a shield and made a successful 
weapon roll, he adds his shield’s armor value to his armor for 
this round. 
He now takes damage, subtracting any protection from his 
armor, shield, and magical protection from the rolled damage. 
See Armor (page 117) for more on armor and damage.
Any damage left over after the armor is subtracted is record-
ed under “W ounds” on the character sheet, and this number is 
subtracted from the character’s current hit points. This is the 
actual damage (as opposed to the “Knockdown damage”) taken 
this round. 
Compare the actual damage with the character’s Major 
W ounds statistic to determine whether he suffers any grievous 
effects from his enemy’s blow (see W ound Classifications, page 
130, for more on damage and wounds).
Compare the character’s current hit points with his 
Unconscious statistic to see if the character has taken too much 
punishment and collapsed (see the Injury and Health section on 
page 128  for more information). A character who falls uncon -
scious during combat, whether due to a Major W ound or the ac-
cumulation of many small wounds, may still attempt a DEX roll 
if on horseback or balancing on a wall. Success indicates a gentle 
fall that does little or no damage, at the Gamemaster’s discretion 
(the DEX roll here signifies an involuntary reaction resulting 
from years of training in mounted combat, and has nothing to 
do with the knight’s being aware of his surroundings).
Finally, compare the current Hit Points to the W ounded 
statistic to see if the character suffers penalties in the ensuing 
rounds of combat. 
Example of Combat — T aking Damage: The Saxon is SIZ 
19, CON 18, Hit Points 37, Unconscious 9. The rolled damage 
(23) is greater than his Knockdown, so he must make a DEX roll to 
==================== BOOK PAGE 118 ====================
117
stay on his feet. He succeeds, so is not knocked down. Next, he checks 
his armor (10 point chainmail), plus his shield (6 points) since 
he had a partial success. He subtracts 16 from the rolled damage, 
and records a 7-point wound. This is less than his CON (Major 
W ound),and he has not hit his Unconscious limit, so he is not out of 
the fight yet. However, the wound is enough to lightly wound him, 
giving him a –5 to his actions in the following rounds. 
Movement Phase
Characters who declared movement this round now move 
up to a number of yards equal to their Movement Rate.
Movement is basically only for the purposes of determin -
ing whether one character has approached or catches another. 
Complicated issues such as facing and engaged initiative are not 
normally concerns during movement. When characters move 
within one yard of an enemy, evasion (see below) may be neces -
sary for movement to continue. Once within this distance, com-
bat is possible. A character cannot move and initiate an attack in 
the same round unless the attack is a lance charge.
Characters may wish to move at an increased speed. For 
simplicity’s sake, running or sprinting (cantering or galloping for 
horses) is normally disallowed in close combat. In some cases, 
the Gamemaster may require a DEX roll for characters to in -
crease their speed to a run or a sprint. The Gamemaster must 
determine whether interception or other important events occur 
between characters moving at different rates, always deferring to 
common sense as the final arbiter.
Other kinds of movement-type actions taking place 
during this phase might require skill rolls as determined by the 
Gamemaster. For example, a character wishing to have his horse 
leap an obstacle might be required to make a Horsemanship roll.
Initiative
If it is important to decide who moves first, the character 
with the highest Movement Rate moves first. If two characters 
have the same Movement Rate, the person with the highest 
DEX has the option to take the initiative or let another charac -
ter move first.
Running and Sprinting
Characters often prefer to run or sprint during melee move-
ment. In crowded combat situations, this may be inappropriate. 
The Gamemaster always determines whether movement over 
the base rate is permitted.
Running (cantering): Running doubles the basic 
Movement Rate. The only penalty for running or pressing your 
mount to a canter is a –5 penalty to Awareness. Gamemasters 
should be wary of players who wish to run through a crowd of 
combatants or a battle, though. If sustained for a long period of 
time, running or cantering tires the character or creature (see 
Sprinting for suggested effects).
Sprinting (galloping): Sprinting triples the basic rate 
and gives a –10 penalty to Awareness. If sustained for more 
than a few melee rounds, it may require a DEX or CON roll 
every subsequent round, at the Gamemaster’s option. Failure 
indicates that the character slows down, while a fumble indi -
cates he is exhausted and collapses. Modifiers may be applied if 
the sprint is continued.
Combined Actions
If a character or creature moves only a portion of its 
Movement Rate in a round, the Gamemaster may decide if other 
actions are allowed. Normally the “one action per round” rule 
should be enforced, but note that a lance attack, for example, is 
always made in combination with mounted movement.
See Actions in Melee (below) for more information.
Evasion 
If a character moves within 1 yard of an unengaged enemy 
with the intention of moving on past, or if a character in combat 
wishes to disengage from an opponent, he must attempt to Evade 
(see Evasion, under Special Combat T actics) as part of movement. 
Friendly characters or creatures may move past each other 
freely without becoming entangled, unless their paths converge. 
A surprised enemy, or one engaged in combat with other oppo -
nents, or with his back turned, may be moved past freely with 
Gamemaster approval.
Basic Rules
T
hese rules include the way damage is determined, the 
difference between damage that knocks someone down 
and that which causes actual injury, the actions a knight 
might make in a round, and so forth.
Damage 
The Damage statistic tells you how much harm your char -
acter can do when he strikes with a normal knight’s weapon, such 
as a sword or a battleaxe. Damage is rolled using d6’s: simply roll 
the dice and add up the values. 
Some weapons, such as great-swords or daggers, do 1d6 
more or less damage than the Damage statistic (see Combat Skills 
in Chapter 5 for more information on weapons). Brawling weap-
ons (fists, kicks, chairs) or shields do 2d6 less damage than the 
statistic, to a minimum of 1d6.
All characters use the same rating to determine damage: 
(STR+SIZ/6). Creatures using natural weapons use slight -
ly different formulae that vary depending on size, magic, and 
other factors.
Armor
Armor protects characters from weapon or weapon-like 
damage (but not from other damage, such as falling). When an 
armored character is hit by a weapon or a monster’s natural at -
tacks, the armor value of his armor is subtracted from the total 
damage dealt. The modified result is the amount applied to the 
Basic Rules
==================== BOOK PAGE 119 ====================
Chapter Seven: Combat
118
character’s W ounds value; all other damage is assumed to be ab-
sorbed by the armor.
As described in the Dexterity Rolls  section below, armor 
gives a negative modifier to DEX in many situations. In most 
cases this is simplified as –5 for all leather and –10 for all met -
al armor. However, balance rolls suffer no modifier for armor, 
while armor worn while swimming gives a –1 modifier per point 
of armor protection. The Gamemaster is free to adjust these 
modifiers to suit the logic of unusual situations, such as a fight in 
a muddy field during a rainstorm.
Magical armor is possible but should be very difficult to 
obtain and may have certain limitations, which are up to the 
Gamemaster to enforce.
Animals and monsters have “armor” based on the thickness 
of their skin, muscle and fat, magical nature, and other factors. 
This armor gives no penalty to their DEX, nor can it be removed 
and worn by a knight.
Ordinary warhorses may wear armor in the form of a ca -
parison or trappers, but only chargers and destriers are strong 
enough to wear heavier armor when it becomes available.
Example of Armor Reduction: Sir Adalhart has been struck 
for 17 points while wearing reinforced chainmail worth 12 points 
of protection. This is greater than his knockdown statistic, so he rolls 
DEX, successfully, and remains on his horse. Now armor is con -
sidered. After subtracting the value of the armor, only 5 points of 
damage actually penetrate the armor and injure the knight.
Shields
The shield normally used in P aladin  is known as a “heat-
er”: a medium-sized shield, flat at the top and rounded to a point 
at the bottom. 
The shield may be used with any one-handed weapon, but 
not with two-handed weapons. A shield grants 6 additional points 
of armor reduction to the loser of an opposed combat resolution 
on a partial success (see Die-Roll Resolution in Chapter 6).
If a knight has lost his weapon but still has a shield, he may 
use the Defense option, using his DEX in place of a weapon roll. 
If the Gamemaster allows combined actions, the knight may use 
this defense while re-arming, with a –5/+5 reflexive modifier 
for the round (these modifiers stack with those from the defense 
option).
Finally, a knight who has no other weapon available may use 
his shield to slam his opponent. See Shield Attack, below. 
Dexterity Rolls
Game play presents countless hazards and difficulties 
that challenge your Player-knight’s agility, reactions, or move -
ment-related abilities such as climbing, sneaking, and balanc -
ing. All these situations use a DEX roll, usually unopposed, to 
determine success. The Gamemaster may find other occasions 
to use the DEX roll as well.
Critical Successes and Fumbles: As with most attribute 
rolls, success and failure are usually the only possible results of 
a DEX roll — neither a critical success nor a fumble gives any 
special result.
However, several special uses of the DEX roll, such as 
sneaking, are an exception to this pattern. Furthermore, the 
Gamemaster may allow critical success or failure in a particular 
situation: If storytelling is enhanced, by all means let a critical 
DEX success gain some extra benefit, or a fumble extract a spe -
cial penalty.
Modifiers 
Cumulative modifiers may be applied to DEX rolls. For 
example, a climber carrying a wounded knight on his back and 
attempting to climb up a difficult surface might suffer a –15 
to DEX. Certain feats are impossible and negative modifiers 
should stress this.
Encumbrance
A character or creature may be “encumbered.” Knights and 
horses are usually encumbered during the game as their normal 
state of affairs, and no special rules apply. However, characters 
and horses used to bearing heavy loads who then find them -
selves unencumbered gain +2 to their basic Movement Rate; a 
knight with a Movement Rate of 3 would walk 5 yards when 
unencumbered.
“Encumbered” is defined as carrying a heavy load. Clothing, 
or even some kinds of armor and a sword and shield, aren’t 
enough to constitute a heavy load for a character, but full ar -
mor, weapons, and combat gear do qualify as such. An armored 
knight and heavy saddle are clearly a heavy load for a horse to 
carry, while a small girl riding bareback is not much of a burden 
for most steeds.
As always, the Gamemaster judges specific cases. Note that 
a +1 or +2 bonus is already figured into the Movement Rates for 
characters and creatures that do not normally bear heavy bur -
dens (i.e. giants, bandits, hawks, bears, peasants, and so forth). 
Horses are assumed to be encumbered by the weight of an ar -
mored rider or a heavy pack. So a sumpter horse bearing an emp-
ty pack would have a Movement Rate of 7 (5+2).
Armor V alue Movement Rate
0–4 +2
5–7 +1
8+ +0
Unencumbered knights also fight at +5, and have +5 
Awareness.

T o know how much characters may carry and the effect on 
encumbrance, use this table: 
Whenever a character receives damage equal to twice the 
value of his SIZ attribute or more, he is knocked down automat-
ically, without the chance to recover his balance.
When knocked down, an armored knight may struggle 
back to his feet during the Movement Phase next round (i.e., 
not the one yet to come this round). If the character is attacked 
before getting up, then he and his opponent(s) receive –5/+5 
reflexive modifiers to their weapon rolls. Unless knocked down 
again, he regains his footing at the beginning of the following 
Movement Phase.
Gamemaster characters and even monsters must make 
DEX rolls, just as knights do, when their Knockdown statistic 
is equaled or exceeded. If a knight receives damage in a lance 
charge which exceeds his horse’s Knockdown statistic, the horse 
must make a DEX roll or fall.
Climb
A character may attempt to clamber up a surface using a 
DEX roll. If it is an easy slope, such as a sand dune, the DEX 
roll might be without modification, if a roll is needed at all. A 
–5 modifier might be needed for the character to climb a rough 
stone wall. Encumbered characters who are climbing suffer nor-
mal modifiers to DEX. Bad footing or cold hands may also im -
pose a modifier.
Normally, one DEX roll is made per 30 feet of height or 
fraction thereof. So a 65-foot-tall tower would require three suc-
cessful DEX rolls to climb.
Ropes or convenient vines may add to the chance of success. 
A rope gives a +5 modifier to DEX. A proper ladder gives a +10 
modifier. Siege ladders, which may have suffered from hasty con-
struction or damage, have a modifier of +1d6+4.
Jump
The base height a character can jump up is equal to his 
Movement Rate in feet. The base width is Movement Rate in 
yards. Remember that knights in armor are normally encum -
bered. Without armor, they add +2 to their Movement Rate, 
modifying their jumping distance.

Difficult Footing
DEX rolls should receive a negative modifier for difficult 
or slippery surfaces, when appropriate. For example, a character 
attempting to balance on a narrow wooden beam across a chasm, 
or to climb a wet, slippery wall of polished rock. Usually the neg-
ative modifier should be no more than –10.
Balance
The balance roll is the most common DEX roll — the 
Knockdown check is a balance roll. Balance might be tested 
when a character crosses a narrow bridge, tries to stay afoot on 
the heaving deck of a ship, or staggers about when the earth 
shakes from magic.
Encumbrance, whether from armor or other heavy 
items, is not used to modify a DEX roll for balance unless the 
Gamemaster insists. Difficult footing may impose a negative 
modifier, however.
If the DEX roll is successful, the character remains upright; 
if not, he falls down. A critical balance success gains no special 
advantage unless the situation presents an obvious extra benefit. 
A fumble does no further harm.
Once knocked down, a character needs no roll to get up 
again, even in heavy armor. However, in combat he must fight 
from a disadvantage while clambering back to his feet, as de -
scribed below.
k NockdowN
Whenever a character receives a hit, through combat or 
some other means (such as riding into a tree limb), the rolled 
damage — i.e., the damage dealt before the victim’s armor, if any, 
reduces it — is compared to his SIZ. 
If the damage received is equal to or greater than his SIZ, 
but less than twice that value, then the character has received a 
blow that sends him reeling for balance. He must make a DEX 
roll. If the roll is successful, then the character recovers handily 
and can continue fighting as normal.
If the roll fails, the knight falls down. If he was mounted, he 
takes 1d6 damage from the fall; if he was on a cliff or some other 
high place and fumbles the roll, he plummets over the edge, tak-
ing falling damage as appropriate.

A Successful DEX rolls allows the character to make the 
jump safely. A failure indicates the character decided not to 
jump at the last moment. On a fumble, his jump fails in a spec -
tacular, and possibly disastrous, manner.
If a character wants to jump more than his normal base, roll 
both STR and DEX and if both are a success, the character adds 
one foot or yard, respectively, to height or distance. 
T o know how high a character can reach with his hand 
when jumping, add SIZ/3 feet. On a successful STR roll, he can 
climb onto the obstacle after jumping.
Ju MPiNg  o N Horseback
As for humans, horses can jump their Movement rate in feet 
for height and in yards for horizontal distances. Just as knights 
are normally considered encumbered with armor and equip -
ment, horses usually bear a rider. Unencumbered horses gain +2 
to their Movement Rate.
As for humans, a successful DEX roll indicates a safe jump. 
A failed roll indicates the mount refused to jump, while a fumble 
indicates a potential disaster.
If a character wants his horse to jump more than its nor -
mal maximum, roll Horsemanship and the horse’s DEX. If both 
are a success, the character adds one foot or yard, respectively, to 
height or distance. 
Sneak
Stealth and detection are complicated subjects, and the 
Gamemaster should be ready to adjust or amplify the basic rules 
given here as needed. 
If a character wishes to sneak up on someone, he makes a 
DEX roll. Any character who might reasonably have a chance 
to observe the character makes an opposed Awareness roll (see 
Opposed Resolution in Chapter 6). If both sneaker and observ -
er fail their rolls, the Gamemaster should decide what happens 
based on the circumstances.
Unlike most DEX rolls, a critical success on a sneak roll 
has a distinct effect: It always succeeds unless the opponent also 
achieves a critical, in which case a tie results, meaning that the 
sneaker moves forward, but the victim is alerted that something 
suspicious is going on. Conversely, a fumble while sneaking en -
sures detection.
Standard rules for encumbrance do not apply while sneak -
ing: Instead, a character suffers only a –5 modifier for metal 
armor or a heavy load, and no penalty for sneaking in leather 
armor or with a light load.
Circumstances may provide modifiers to the DEX roll. For 
example, abundant cover or ambient noise might grant a +5 
modifier (or even +10 if the benefit is substantial, such as a loud 
waterfall nearby; or a sudden fire lighting the sky, accompanied 
by faint shouting from a different direction). 
Similarly, if the potential observer is engaged in an activi -
ty rather than devoting his attention to watching and listening 
for intruders, he should suffer a –5 or greater penalty on his 
Awareness roll.
Actions in Melee
M
ost actions normally undertaken in melee are includ-
ed here. Other actions are certainly possible, and the 
Gamemaster should be open-minded about allowing 
players to try new things, as long as the rules they establish mu -
tually are fair and consistent.
The following actions are typical in melee combat, listed 
as taking place either during the Resolution Phase or during the 
Movement Phase.
Resolution Phase
 n Exchange blows with one or more opponents using 
opposed resolution, dividing weapon skill among 
multiple opponents or ignoring them as desired, or 
attempt a combat tactic such as Double Feint.
 n Attack a surprised or helpless enemy, or one ignoring 
your attack, with an unopposed weapon roll.
 n Attempt to dodge rather than fight. See Dodge, page 126.
 n Make a lance charge on horseback (includes moving).
 n Make a squire roll to get help or a new weapon. If more 
than one squire is available, multiple rolls may be made 
as one action. See the Squire Roll definition (page 39).
 n Give commands to followers.
 n Engage in a brief conversation with a comrade.
 n Rearm with a new weapon or a shield.
 n Fire an arrow from a bow or light crossbow and reload. 
 n Fire a medium or heavy crossbow.
 n Scrutinize the surroundings, using Awareness or 
Hunting skill.
Movement Phase 
 n Move up to your full Movement Rate (on foot) or 
your horse’s Movement Rate (while mounted). If the 
Gamemaster permits it, based on circumstances, this 
movement may be at a double-speed run (canter) or 
even a triple-speed sprint (gallop).
 n Mount or dismount.
 n Perform a maneuver on horseback (turn around, leap 
a fence). A Horsemanship roll may be required in dif-
ficult or trying circumstances.
 n Get up from the ground after a fall if not engaged in 
combat, even while wearing heavy armor.
 n Reload a medium crossbow. This effectively allows 
one attack every other melee round.
 n Partially reload a heavy crossbow. Reloading requires 
3 full Movement Phases, allowing one attack every 4 
melee rounds.
==================== BOOK PAGE 122 ====================
121
Combined Actions
Combined actions are not normally allowed, although 
a few borderline or arguable cases exist. For example, fighting 
several opponents is not usually considered multiple actions. 
Overtly combined actions should either not be permitted, or 
should entail a penalty. Gamemaster interpretation will, as al -
ways, be necessary.
A list of example combined actions is provided below for 
those characters who wish to perform them while suffering a 
negative or reflexive modifier.
 n Move and engage in a melee fight. A reflexive +5/–5  
modifier should be applied. If both combatants 
moved, both simply suffer a –5 modifier.
 n Mount or dismount a horse while giving commands. 
A DEX roll might be required, with failure indicated 
that the horse was not mounted, and a fumble indi -
cating a fall with 1d6 damage.
 n Scrutinize the surroundings with an Awareness roll 
while dodging: –5 modifiers to DEX and Awareness 
(in addition to the usual modifiers for dodging) 
should be applied.
 n Arm or rearm while fighting, suffering a reflexive 
modifier of +5/–5.
 n Climb over a wall while fighting, with a height dis -
advantage, suffering a reflexive modifier of –10/+10 
(–5 for combining movement and combat, plus an -
other –5 for the height difference).
Combat Modifiers
C
ertain conditions and situations may affect the skill of 
fighters for better or worse, no matter how skilled or clum-
sy. Modifiers to weapon skills are, as with all modifiers in 
P aladin , applied to the weapon skill value, not the die roll.
Confined Space
Not all weapons work in all places. Most weapons need a 
certain amount of space to be swung. Fighting in confined spac-
es, such as using a 2-handed sword in a peasant’s hovel, or a long 
spear in a dense forest, gives a –5 to weapon skill. This penalty is 
never applied to daggers or unarmed combat.
Cover
A character who is partially protected by a substantial pro -
tective covering (other than armor or a shield) imposes a –5 
modifier to attacker’s weapon rolls. For instance, a knight stand-
ing behind the crenellation on a battlement is much harder to hit 
than one standing in the open, and an archer partly concealed 
behind a tree is a difficult target. 
Cover modifiers usually apply against ranged attacks, but a 
character in melee might, at the Gamemaster’s discretion, gain a 
cover modifier.
Fatigue and Minor Wounds 
In general, combat in P aladin  is over fast enough that 
fatigue and minor wounds do not have a chance to overcome 
adrenaline and valor. However, in the literature knights are 
sometimes too exhausted and wounded to continue their fight, 
and agree to rest for an hour or so before continuing. 
The Major W ound and Unconscious rules insure that char-
acters collapse if they fight on after serious injury, rather than 
continuing until all hit points are lost. Fatigue is not covered, in 
order to keep the game simple and fast-moving.
Characters suffer a –5 penalty when they have taken ¼ their 
Hit Points in damage (other than from a Major W ound), –10 
when they reach ½ their hit Points, and go unconscious when 
they reach ¾ damage. 
Getting To Your Feet
If the character is attacked before getting to his feet after 
falling down, he and his opponent(s) receive –5/+5 reflexive 
modifiers to their weapon rolls. Unless knocked down again, 
he regains his footing at the beginning of the next Movement 
Phase, and may take a move normally at that time.
Height Advantage
Any time a character fights with the advantage of height, 
such as a horseman versus a foot soldier or a knight on a rampart 
attacking a man scaling the wall, a +5/–5 reflexive modifier ap -
plies in favor of the character with the height advantage. When 
opponents are separated by more than a yard (in this case, of 
height), they are too far apart to engage in melee.
Note that this modifier applies when one character has 
fallen to the ground and the other remains standing, or when 
a character fights while backing up a steep castle stairwell while 
fighting foes climbing toward him.
Immobilized
Characters in combat that are grappled, tied up, stuck in 
quicksand, or otherwise unable to move properly, suffer a reflex-
ive combat modifier of +10/–10.
Multiple Actions 
As noted above, characters attempting combat in combi -
nation with another action, such as movement or an Awareness 
roll, incur a reflexive combat modifier of –5/+5, assuming the 
Gamemaster permits such action. The lance charge is the only 
exception.
Combat Modifiers
==================== BOOK PAGE 123 ====================
Chapter Seven: Combat
122
Surprised 
A surprised foe includes those attacked from behind or 
without warning. The attack is always unopposed, with a +5 
combat modifier to attacker. It is dishonorable to attack in this 
manner.
Unencumbered 
As already noted (Encumbrance, page 118), unencumbered 
knights fight at +5, have +2 Movement rate, and +5 Awareness. 
Visibility 
In darkness, fog, smoke, or other similar conditions, char -
acters’ weapon skills are reduced by –5 or even –10, unless they 
make their Awareness roll that round (an exception to the “one 
action per round” rule).
Mounted Combat
H
orses are essential to characters in P aladin , for they give 
many advantages in combat. Several options and rules re-
garding mounted combat are found in this section.
