Lance Charge
A lance charge is the only attack that must  be made 
while moving. The horse must move at least 6 yards in a 
roughly straight line to build up enough speed for lance 
charge damage.
==================== BOOK PAGE 124 ====================
123
If a lance charge is made against anything other than a 
character wielding a great spear or another character making a 
lance charge, the charging knight gets a +5 modifier to his Lance 
skill. Further, because the horse’s weight and momentum is be-
hind the attack, the damage from a lance charge uses the horse’s 
Damage statistic rather than the rider’s, as shown below.
Mount Type Damage
Destrier 8d6
Charger 6d6
Courser 5d6
Rouncy 4d6
Due to the nature of a lance attack, lance damage to a rider 
may affect his horse; if the knight makes his Horsemanship roll 
and the damage is greater than the horse’s Knockdown statistic, 
the horse must also make a DEX test or fall. 
The lance charge is particularly devastating against oppo -
nents on foot using ordinary melee weapons. 
Fighting with a lance while mounted but not charging is 
the same as using a spear: use the character’s Spear skill, not 
Lance; remember that the wielder does not get the usual +5 
modifier for charging, and he inflicts his own Damage value, 
not his mount’s.
Against Unmounted Foes
As noted in Combat Modifiers, a mounted character fight -
ing an enemy who is afoot gains a +5/–5 reflexive modifier to 
his weapon skill unless the footman is armed with a great spear 
or halberd. This modifier stacks with that gained from a lance 
charge (for a total of +10), if applicable.
Fast Melee Movement
A horse moves faster than any man in melee. A very fast 
horse (Movement Rate 10 or more) may gallop 30 yards in a 
round, normally in a straight line. The Gamemaster should 
require a Horsemanship roll if any turns or tricky maneuvers 
are executed. Failure indicates that you stop moving where 
you are, while a fumble means that the rider falls off (with 
at least 1d6 damage for the fall, and possibly another 1d6 for 
the speed). 
Mounted Combat
==================== BOOK PAGE 125 ====================
Chapter Seven: Combat
124
Two-handed Weapons  
on Horseback
T wo-handed weapons may not normally be used from 
horseback. Some archers shoot bows while riding, but suffer a 
–5 penalty, plus the usual modifiers. Grapple may be attempted, 
since it is used in the literature from horseback, but Gamemasters 
should apply common sense to the results.
Destriers and Magical Horses
Destriers are not available in the early phases of the game. 
Even in later phases they are still rare. Most Player-knights can 
only hope to receive such a priceless beast as a reward for valor 
or loyalty from a grateful lord. Alternatively, they may fight and 
defeat the owner of a destrier.
Magical horses are even more rare than destriers, and may 
have unique features. Or they may be simply faster and more 
beautiful than ordinary steeds. As always, the Gamemaster is in 
charge of all things involving magic.
Attack-Trained Horses
Starting at the very end of Phase 3, the Gamemaster may 
allow Player-knights to use horses trained to fight. Destriers and 
chargers are normally the only animals ever so trained, and few 
steeds are for sale with such training. 
The rules for attack-trained horses are found in Chapter 18: 
Opponents and Creatures.
Horse Armor
A caparison is a fancy, full-length cloth covering that dec -
orates and protects a knight’s horse. It usually bears the arms of 
the owner. The basic caparison worn by most warhorses is worth 
2 points of armor. Actual metal armor worn by horses is called 
barding. Heavy caparisons, known as trappers, are more com -
monly available than barding.
Any horse can wear a caparison or trapper. Only chargers 
and destriers can bear armor heavier than a trapper, and only 
destriers may wear plate barding. Barding is an extremely rare 
commodity, primarily because destriers are rare, and demand 
negligible.
Caparisons, trappers, and barding all work exactly as human 
armor does. Even barding rarely provides more than 10 points 
of protection. This includes chain mail, padding, and a quilt -
ed caparison. Armor from caparisons or barding stacks with the 
horse’s usual armor value, so a charger with a caparison has an 
armor value of 5+2 = 7.
Characters may insist on equipping chargers or even lesser 
horses with heavy armor, but Gamemasters should severely pe -
nalize an overburdened mount’s DEX and Movement Rate.
Special Rules  
for Melee Combat
A
ny number of unusual situations and inquiries may arise 
during combat. Answers to some of these questions and 
concerns, for players and Gamemasters alike, can be 
found in this section.
Damage to Armor and Shields 
Five shafts of iron hang from Ogier’s destrier; 
Of his brave shield so little now remains
That never hence could it be used again; 
His helmet’s rear has been rammed out of shape,
And on the left his hauberk’s ripped in twain.
— Song of Aspremont , V
When using a shield in a partial success, each roll of 6 on the 
damage dice indicates that the shield was damaged and it loses 
1 point of its defensive value (note that axes do double shield 
damage).
On a direct hit to the body (i.e., not blocked by a shield), 
each pair of double 6s on the damage dice indicate that the ar -
mor has been damaged and loses 1 point of its defensive value 
(note that a critical hit automatically does all 6s on the damage 
dice!).
Example of Shield and Armor Damage: Sir Adalhart is at-
tacked by a giant brute of a Saxon wielding a 2-handed axe, doing 
6d6 damage. Sir Adalhart gets a partial success, and blocks the axe 
with his shield. The Saxon rolls 2,3,4,6,6,6. The three 6’s cause 6 
points of damage to his shield (axes do double damage to shields), 
destroying it completely.
Even though there are 3 6’s, his armor is not damaged, as he 
managed to block the blow with his shield. 
However, the next round, the Saxon hits again, rolling 
1,3,3,4,6,6. Without a shield to block the blow, his armor is dam-
aged, reduced from 12 points to 11.
Shield and armor points may be repaired in the field (at 
a local smithy) to restore a number of points equal to half the 
original sustained damage. So a 6-point shield which receives 2 
points of damage may be repaired up to a maximum defense val-
ue of 5. Only during the Winter Phase may armor and shields be 
repaired fully. Replacing damaged equipment is usually easier. 
Broken or Dropped Weapons
All weapons except swords and great swords break in 
combat when the fighter fumbles. Swords and great swords are 
dropped instead, and can be recovered in one melee round, even 
if the fighter is on horseback, for the weapon is normally tied to 
the knight’s wrist or belt by a cord.
A sword always breaks a non-sword on a tied roll in combat.
==================== BOOK PAGE 126 ====================
125
W eapons broken in combat cannot be fixed. New ones 
must be borrowed from a friend or captured from an enemy, or 
bought after the battle is over. 
Nonlethal Combat
During a tournament or otherwise “friendly” fight, a knight 
might use rebated (blunted) weapons, or else voluntarily hold 
back the full force of his blows (the latter is often preferred to 
rebating a good weapon). In either case, rebated weapons and 
held blows alike, the knight strikes with force, seeking merely to 
knock his foe to the ground, but without the deadly damage a 
normal attack would confer.
W eapon skills and damage are calculated normally for 
knockdown, using full damage rolled; but a rebated weapon 
reduces actual damage of a pointed or edged weapon by one-
half, while a withheld blow also reduces it by half. Used together, 
actual damage is reduced to one quarter. In all cases, the actual 
damage is calculated as a hit, capable of penetrating armor and 
wounding. A Player-knight must state clearly beforehand his in-
tention to withhold full force.
Combat Against Creatures
In general, combat against creatures follows normal rules. 
For example, a creature who achieves a critical success does max-
imum damage. However, creatures do not suffer broken weap -
ons from fumbles or ties. On the other hand, they may not use 
shields, so gain no benefit from a partial success.
Many creatures have special abilities in combat to reflect 
their unique and magical characteristics. For example, the lion 
is permitted two attacks in a single round, and is given an armor 
value of 10 to reflect his legendary prowess. A boar fights for a 
round after reaching the unconsciousness threshold. A griffin is 
so fearsome that opponents are required to make a V alorous roll 
with a –5 modifier before engaging it in combat.
These rules are specific to the listed creatures, and may 
not be gained by Player-knights. On the other hand, creatures 
should not normally be able use the optional tactics listed in 
this section, though they may dodge as needed. Further rules for 
creatures and monsters are found in Chapter 18: Opponents and 
Creatures.
Jousting 
Jousting lances break very easily. They break any time an 
odd number is rolled, on any successful attack, and on a fumble.
Damage is handled differently from other combat. In a 
joust, the loser is simply knocked from his horse. No damage is 
rolled, and no chance for a DEX roll is allowed. The loser takes 
1d6 from the fall, but no other damage from a success. A crit -
ical success with a jousting lance, however, does normal horse 
damage as if it was a strike with a real lance. Damage in this spe-
cial case is resolved normally (check for knockdown, wounds, 
etc.).Such an accident is seen as unavoidable in the sport, and 
sometimes good knights are killed this way.
A fumbled roll indicates that the jouster did something 
terribly wrong, like striking his foe’s horse, falling without be -
ing struck, or having the saddle girths break, as well as uselessly 
breaking his lance.
Multiple Opponents
More than one opponent may attack a character at a time: 
up to three footmen; two mounted men; or one mounted and 
two on foot. Any number of missile-armed men can shoot at a 
single target.
Defenders may ignore some or all attackers (who get a free 
unopposed attack), or fight against as many attackers as they 
wish, by dividing their weapon skill among them. The player 
decides how many points to allocate to each foe. Each attack is 
treated separately; each skill attempt is rolled separately and each 
loss for the Player-knight means the foe hits him for full damage. 
Likewise, a Player-knight who strikes multiple targets does full 
damage to each of them.
Any +5 mounted bonus is added after dividing the base 
weapon skill. A knight with Sword 16 fighting against two foes 
on foot could divide his skill into 10 and 6, then add +5 to both 
for a result of 15 against one and 11 against the other. A knight 
gains the benefit of his shield on every partial success, no matter 
how many foes he is fighting.
Missiles shot at a target constitute a single attack, from ar -
rows, bolts or rocks.
Example of Fighting Multiple Opponents: Sir Adalhart is 
assaulted by a pair of young Saxon warriors (each with an Axe skill 
of 11), and decides to fight them both. Adalhart’s Sword skill is 
18. He decides to fight the first enemy with a value of 10, and the 
other an 8. 
Against the first warrior, Adalhart gets a 13, failing his Sword 
roll; the Gamemaster rolls a 7 for the Saxon, who strikes Adalhart 
and does damage. Against the second warrior, Adalhart rolls 7, a 
success even considering his effective skill of 8; the enemy warrior 
rolls a 5, so Adalhart beats him. 
Shield Attack
A knight who has no other weapon available may use his 
shield to slam his opponent. For this purpose, treat the slam as a 
brawling attack with –1d6 damage, using the attacker’s Unarmed 
skill (see Unarmed Combat in Special Combat T actics, below). A 
shield used in this manner is destroyed on a tie with a Sword.
Note that a shield attacker cannot use other weapons simul-
taneously, or make two-handed attacks. He may not use other 
tactical options without Gamemaster approval.
Two-Handed Strikes
It is possible to wield any one-handed weapon with two 
hands to cause extra damage, even though it is normally used 
one-handed with a shield. Any shield or item held in the other 
hand must be dropped first.
Special Rules for Melee Combat
==================== BOOK PAGE 127 ====================
Chapter Seven: Combat
126
T wo-handed attacks can be attempted in opposed resolu -
tion. However, because the weapon is not made for this kind of 
use, the attacker gets as –5 modifier and +1d6 damage.
When done unopposed, attack skill is not modified, and he 
gets the extra 1d6 damage. 
Notes on Missile Combat
W
hile bows and other missile weapons are considered 
beneath the dignity of a knight to use in combat, they 
may be on the receiving end of missile attacks.
Shields Versus Missiles
Missile fire at a target using a shield as cover has a penalty 
equal to the shield’s defensive value, typically –6. For targets who 
are not actively using the shield to defend themselves from mis -
siles, the penalty is only –3.
Rapid Fire and Aiming
When firing missiles, characters have the possibility to fire 
normally, use rapid fire, or to take careful aim.
Rapid fire allows a character an extra attack in the same 
round, but both attacks are at half skill value, much like multiple 
attacks with melee weapons. This option cannot be used with 
medium or heavy crossbows, which take additional rounds to 
load. 
Careful aiming means the character spends one full round 
aiming, receiving +5 to his missile skill when he does attack in 
the next round.
Weather Effects on Missiles
When firing an arrow or crossbow bolt, the weather is im -
portant. First of all, when used in rainy weather, bow strings al -
ways break on natural 1 or 2. Use the following skills modifiers 
for inclement weather: 
We a t h er Penalty
Rain/Snow –5
Strong Wind –5
Heavy Storm –15
Gale –20
Special Combat Tactics
T
actics allow more direct player and Gamemaster in -
volvement in the fortunes of combat. These tactics are 
optional, not mandatory. The Gamemaster is in charge 
of whether to permit special combat tactics in any specific situa-
tion. Gamemasters may wish to insist that, once chosen, a tactic 
cannot be changed; the character must fight for as many rounds 
as necessary using whatever tactic was selected.
Defend
Knights may choose to fight a defensive combat. This is es-
pecially useful when a knight is beset by multiple enemies and 
needs to divide his defense, or is weaponless, but it can also be 
used against a single foe.
Many knights use this tactic to avoid injury, resulting in the 
prolonged combats described in the literature. No Honor is lost 
for this option, nor is Glory reduced for victory.
During the Declaration Phase the player declares that his 
knight is going to defend. He gains +10 to his weapon skill for 
purposes of combat that round. The modified skill may be di -
vided among multiple opponents, if desired. He does not deal 
damage if he wins the combat roll (even on a critical). Partial 
successes, failures, fumbles, and ties have normal results in the 
opposed resolution. 
If both fighters opt to Defend, no combat takes place.
Special: If a character using the defense tactic fights a char-
acter using the Uncontrolled Attack tactic (see page 128 ), both 
still gain the +10 modifier from their respective tactics, but com-
bat is otherwise resolved normally: If the defending character 
wins, he deals no damage.
Dodge
A character on foot can attempt to dodge, dancing cra -
zily and throwing himself any which way to avoid a blow. He 
rolls DEX against the opponent’s weapon skill. If facing mul -
tiple enemies, he uses his DEX, unmodified, against each. The 
Gamemaster chooses the order that the foes attack (random de-
termination is recommended).
A winning dodge means the character avoided being struck. 
If hit, damage is calculated normally, including Knockdown. If 
he falls, the character is prone for all subsequent attacks, which 
are unopposed.
Armor and footing may grant modifiers, depending on the 
Gamemaster’s judgment.
Double Feint
The Double Feint tactic confuses the opponent and per -
mits a strike at an unarmored or vulnerable part of the body. 
The Double Feint cannot be used with the spear or lance in 
a mounted charge, or with the great spear or halberd versus cav-
alry charges. The flail and warflail are also unsuitable weapons 
for a Double Feint.
During the Determination Phase the player declares that 
his knight is going to attempt a Double Feint. Before making the 
usual opposed roll, the character must attempt a DEX roll, as 
modified by armor, load carried, and bad footing. The user may 
be mounted, but still suffers the modifier to DEX. See Dexterity 
Rolls, page 118.
==================== BOOK PAGE 128 ====================
127
A successful DEX roll indicates that the opponent’s armor 
is halved. The user has managed to maneuver so that the oppo -
nent’s armpit, groin, or other vulnerable area is hit. If the op -
ponent is a creature, it is hit in its soft underbelly or other less 
armored zone. 
If the DEX roll is a critical success, all armor is ignored. The 
victim is hit in the eye slit of his helmet, or other unarmored 
location.
A failed DEX roll indicates that the tactic fails, and that the 
character does no damage that round (the weapon hits thin air). 
All other rules are still in effect; so the opponent may still lose, 
but the user does no damage due to his failed tactic. 
A fumbled DEX roll indicates that the character broke or 
dropped his weapon.
If the victim of a Double Feint is unarmored, the result of a 
DEX success gives the user a +5 modifier to weapon skill for the 
subsequent attack. A critical success gives a +10 modifier. 
If two characters attempt Double Feints, both roll DEX 
and both may receive the benefits of the tactic in the subsequent 
opposed roll. 
Evasion
Moving out of a melee engagement requires the evasion 
tactic if two characters are fighting afoot or if both are mount -
ed. This is similar to dodging while moving, but less likely to 
succeed since an opposed roll is required. The evading charac -
ter must make an opposed DEX roll (if the combatants are on 
foot) or Horsemanship roll (if they are mounted) opposed to 
the opponent’s modified weapon skill roll. Since both moving 
and dodging is a combined action, this tactic imposes +5/–5 
reflexive modifiers to the attacker and the dodging character, 
respectively.
If the evading character wins the opposed roll, he dodges 
the enemy’s attack and may still move normally this turn. If the 
attacking character wins the roll, he deals damage normally and 
the evading character cannot move this round.
If the characters tie, both are considered to have lost the roll 
for the round: In this case, the evading character fails to move 
but is not hit.
If the evading character fumbles, he falls down; if he was 
on horseback, he takes 1d6 damage from the fall. If multiple 
opponents are involved, this tactic cannot be used at all unless 
the Gamemaster approves. If evasion is allowed against multiple 
foes, the evading character must divide his skill.
Encumbered characters suffer the usual encumbrance mod-
ifiers while trying to evade an enemy.
Grapple
Grappling in combat uses opposed Unarmed rolls (see 
the Unarmed skill in Chapter 5 ), although the defending 
character may use his weapon skill rather than Grapple if he 
has a weapon in hand. The character initiating the grapple 
must drop weapon and shield to tackle his enemy. This is ex -
tremely dangerous against an armed and experienced knight 
with a good Damage statistic, but the reward for success may 
be a quick finish to a fight. 
Armor has no effect on the roll. If both opponents are 
mounted or both are afoot, there is no modifier. If the grap -
pler is mounted and his foe afoot, the grappler has no modifi -
er for being mounted since it’s difficult to bend over and grab. 
Furthermore, the foot man does not have a –5 modifier, since 
the grappler is bending down. If the grappler is afoot and the foe 
mounted, then the normal +5/–5 modifiers apply.
A winning Unarmed combat roll indicates that the grappler 
has seized his opponent in a hold. This occurs whether the de -
fender is using a weapon skill or grapple. A partial success does 
the loser no good in this instance. A held knight automatically 
loses his weapon, though he may retain his shield.
If the grappler loses the resolution against an opponent us-
ing a weapon, he is hit normally. Ties indicate stalemate, even if 
the opponent is using a sword; go on to the next round. This is 
an exception to the rule that a sword breaks any other weapon 
on a tied roll.
When Grappling
Once a knight has held an opponent, he has four options in 
any of the following rounds: 
1. Pin the Opponent: He may attempt to pin the enemy 
with a second opposed Grapple roll. If he wins this 
second roll, the opponent is pinned and immobilized 
(imposing +10/–10 reflexive modifiers if the pinned 
victim still wishes to fight). Assume that a pinned 
character is flat on his back with the winner sitting 
on his chest, or that the winner has both the victim’s 
arms twisted behind his back, or something similar.
In several cases in the epics, a winning grappler un -
laces the pinned character’s helmet and pulls it off, 
creating a situation of great vulnerability for the vic -
tim, who usually surrenders at that point. Giants of -
ten tuck their victims under their arms and take them 
back their cave or castle. 
2. Re-arm with a Dagger: He may rearm with a dagger 
if he is currently unarmed, by winning an opposed 
STR or Grapple roll. This option is available even if 
the Gamemaster does not normally allow combined 
actions (see Combined Actions , page 121 ); however, 
remember that fighting while re-arming incurs the 
usual +5/–5 reflexive modifiers to the respective 
combatants (if both knights attempt to re-arm, these 
modifiers do not apply, since they effectively negate 
one another).
3. Attack by Dagger or Brawling: He may make an op -
posed Unarmed or Dagger roll to deal damage to his 
opponent (see Unarmed Combat, page 128). 
Special Combat Tactics
==================== BOOK PAGE 129 ====================
Chapter Seven: Combat
128
4. Throw the Opponent Down: He may make an op -
posed Grapple roll to throw his foe down. If he wins, 
he releases the grapple to throw his opponent, and the 
foe takes 1d6 damage from the fall and sprawls on the 
ground (if thrown from horseback, he also takes the 
normal 1d6 for the height of the fall, for a total of 
2d6. Further falls are certainly possible, say from the 
parapet of a castle…). 
A thrown knight who remains conscious may regain his 
feet normally, suffering all the usual penalties while doing so (see 
Knockdown, page 119 ) Note that, in some cases, a fall may sig -
nify the end of a fight, such as in some fights “for love” between 
competing knights. 
A fumbled Grapple roll indicates that the fumbler falls 
down (off his horse if mounted), taking falling damage if 
appropriate.
When Grappled
Once he is held, a character is capable of performing only 
one of the following two actions in any subsequent round: 
1. Break Free or Reverse the Grapple: The held knight 
may make a Grapple roll to try to break the attacker’s 
grasp and escape (note that this may not be attempted 
if the victim’s modified Grapple value is 0). If a grap-
pled knight wins the opposed Grapple roll and has no 
weapons or shield in hand, he can choose to reverse 
the hold on his opponent rather than breaking free 
(continue with the grapple normally).
2. Re-arm with a Dagger:  He may rearm with a dag -
ger if he is currently unarmed, by winning an opposed 
STR or Grapple roll. This option is available even if 
the Gamemaster does not normally allow combined 
actions (see Combined Actions, page 121 ); however, 
remember that fighting while re-arming incurs the 
usual +5/–5 reflexive modifiers to the respective 
combatants (if both knights attempt to re-arm, these 
modifiers do not apply, since they effectively negate 
one another).
Unarmed Combat
Damage for fists or kicks is equal to normal Damage –2d6, 
with 1d6 being the lowest possible brawling damage. Brawling 
damage from casual weapons (chairs, candlesticks, rocks, beef 
bones) must be determined by the Gamemaster, but should nev-
er be more than the character’s damage with a dagger (normal 
Damage –1d6).
Uncontrolled Attack
With this option a combatant makes an all-out, screaming, 
enraged, spitting-in-the-wind-crazy attack. 
An Uncontrolled Attacker strikes a mad flurry of blows 
regardless of consequences, driving furiously in a careless rage. 
An Uncontrolled Attack gets a +10 to his normal weapon skill. 
It cannot be used with Lance, or against multiple targets. The 
attacker never gets protection from a shield.
The opponent faced by an Uncontrolled Attack may either: 
 n Make a free attack, unopposed, before the attacker 
strikes; if the attacker is knocked down, his round is 
over. If the attacker is not stopped, he strikes with a 
+10 modifier to his skill. The defender’s shield al -
ways counts in this if he has one; or
 n Defend (see page 126 ), skill versus skill, each with 
their +10 bonus. All combat in this case is calculated 
as if regular combat (remember, ties have no effect).
In a fight where both fighters use the Uncontrolled Attack, 
both strike with +10 simultaneously. 
Injury and Health
Firmly he holds his sharp sword, and when he strikes an 
opponent, the man’s life ends immediately: no need to 
look for a healer, death cures him instantly.
— Huon of Bordeaux , LXI
T
he primary purpose of the knight is to fight, and much of 
the action in the game is personal or military combat that 
results in injury, whether minor or significant.
Knights expect to take injury, and they wear their many 
scars proudly. In the literature, knights often suffer great wounds 
and go through long periods of healing and rest to recover. Such 
events are realistic as well as authentic to the stories. Violence 
in P aladin  should be realistic and terrifying, never casual or 
routine, and the best way to achieve this is to make injuries a 
serious matter.
Knights sometimes languish in foul dungeons or become 
feverish in the literature. Illness, physical deterioration, and oth-
er sources of damage beyond combat are included here, although 
such sordid forms of damage as disease should rarely kill a brave 
knight.
Hit Points
Health is measured primarily through the Hit Points statis-
tic (SIZ+CON). This number represents the character’s capac-
ity to absorb injury. Death is imminent if a character has zero or 
negative hit points. A character with only half his hit points is 
half-dead.
Losing Hit Points
Characters may lose hit points from many different sources. 
Some forms of damage cause wounds, while others act directly 
on hit points. Fatigue alone never causes hit point loss. Damage 
can be partially or completely absorbed by armor, but not in all 
cases (see Sources of Damage, below).
Once injured, characters may lose further hit points 
through illness, excess activity, blood loss, or worse. These 
==================== BOOK PAGE 130 ====================
129
factors are summarized under the Deterioration and Aggravation 
rules below. Deterioration may be prevented by the unsophisti -
cated medicine of the times, known as Chirurgery. Aggravation 
is caused by excess activity while a character is injured or ill, and 
may be averted only by avoiding activity.
Regaining Hit Points
A character’s recovery from injury is a natural bodily 
process, which is quantified as a character’s Healing Rate ( 
(STR+CON)/10). Characters normally recover hit points 
equal to their Healing Rate every week (on Sunday at noon), 
although the Gamemaster may adjust this schedule if necessary.
The First Aid skill lets the injured character regain a few 
hit points immediately, and ensures that bleeding is stopped, 
infection prevented, and so forth. A critical success with the 
Chirurgery skill augments a patient’s Healing Rate (see First Aid 
and Chirurgery later in this section).
Magical healing spells or items, such as the potion some 
characters receive in character generation, may return hit points, 
or otherwise magically improve a character’s health. The de -
tails of healing magic are secrets known only to a few rare 
Gamemaster characters.
In all cases, any hit points regained over the value of the 
T otal Hit Points statistic are lost.
Unconscious
As a character loses hit points he reaches a point at which he 
becomes unconscious. This important threshold is represented 
by the Unconscious statistic, equal to (T otal Hit Points/4). 
Characters with current hit points below the Unconscious 
value are out of the action. At the moment they go below the 
threshold, they slump to the ground, possibly staggering a few 
yards first, or sliding off a horse before collapsing (a DEX roll 
is allowed for mounted characters, with success indicating that 
falling damage is averted). Once below the unconsciousness 
threshold, they are no longer capable of fighting, riding, or even 
standing.
The Gamemaster may wish to let unconscious characters 
have brief periods of lucidity. With Gamemaster approval, they 
may be allowed to speak quietly. For example, they might be ca-
pable of slowly dictating a will, praying for divine guidance, or 
describing an assailant in a weak whisper.
Since they have been injured so severely, they are unhealthy 
and need Chirurgery, and the “Chirurgery Needed” box is 
checked immediately. They continue to require chirurgery un -
til the Gamemaster says otherwise. See the States of Health and 
Recovery from Injury and Illness sections for more information.
Example of Falling Unconscious: A small battle with the 
Saxons ends the year’s scenario for our sample knight, Sir Adalhart. 
After fighting one round of battle successfully, Sir Adalhart is 
caught alone and chased off the field by a band of lightly-armored 
but skillful warriors. The Gamemaster decides to run the combat 
using regular melee rounds.
In a tremendous fight, Sir Adalhart is victorious over two as -
sailants, gaining a Horsemanship check for successfully out-riding 
the remaining enemies. Adalhart takes no Major W ound in the 
fight, due to his heroic CON of 21 (see the W ound Classifications 
section). However, the knight takes many minor injuries, including 
a fall from his horse, for a total of 26 points of damage, leaving him 
with 8 current hit points. 8 is under his Unconscious value of 9. He 
staggers into a wood near the battlefield, and falls unconscious. The 
“Chirurgery Needed” box is checked.
Zero or Negative Hit Points
As a character continues to suffer injury or deteriorate, 
current hit points may become a negative number. The player 
must continue to keep track of the number. A character at zero 
or negative hit points for any reason or combination of reasons 
(disease, drowning, wounds, deterioration) is on the verge of 
death. First Aid or magic may restore and preserve his feeble life 
enough for healing to be possible, but unless healed to positive 
points before midnight of that same day, the character dies. Even 
if he survives, he will need Chirurgery.
Example of Negative Hit Points: After the fight mentioned 
above, Sir Adalhart lies unconscious on the field, with 8 hit points 
remaining. In his delirium he cries out. An enemy foot soldier wan-
ders past and he brutally stabs Adalhart with his spear for 9 more 
points of damage: Adalhart writhes and goes limp. His current hit 
points are now –1. He will die later that evening, at midnight, if 
not given successful First Aid.
Luckily, his squire, although separated from his knight, even-
tually manages to get help (a Squire roll is made successfully), and 
Adalhart receives First Aid for 2 points. Back to 1 point, he survives, 
with many new scars. The squire takes charge of Adalhart’s share of 
the plunder after the battle, which is won by Adalhart’s side.
Recording Injuries
When a character takes damage, use the following steps to 
record the damage and wounds properly.
Step 1: W rite the magnitude of the injury on the lines la -
beled “W ounds” in the “Current Hit Points” box. Record the ac-
tual points of damage (i.e. 4 points, 17 points) that the character 
has just taken as a wound. Bigger wounds are much worse than 
small, so it is important to carefully keep track of the magnitude 
of each wound taken.
Occasionally an injury is received that affects hit points 
without inflicting a visible wound. This is the case, for example, 
with disease, if First Aid is fumbled, or deterioration sets in after 
a previous injury. In these cases go directly to Step 3, without 
recording a wound number or checking the injury magnitude.
Step 2: Check to see if the magnitude of the injury qualifies 
for a Major or Mortal W ound. Check the W ound Classification 
section below.
Step 3: Subtract the magnitude of the injury from current 
hit points. Erase the current hit points number in the smaller, 
inner box in the upper left-hand corner, and put down the new 
total (21, 10, 3, –4).
Injury and Health
==================== BOOK PAGE 131 ====================
Chapter Seven: Combat
130
Step 4: Check to see if current hit points have gone below 
the unconscious threshold. If so, the character sways and passes 
out. A DEX roll is possible if the character is on horseback or 
balancing, to keep from taking damage from the fall.
Wound Classification
When a character is wounded, the magnitude of damage is 
crucial. Three classifications of wounds are recognized for game 
purposes, based on the wound magnitude in comparison to the 
character’s CON (Major W ound) and T otal Hit Points (Mortal 
W ound) statistics. Characters with high SIZ and CON values 
suffer less severely from injuries.
Light Wound
Any wound dealing less damage than the character’s Major 
W ound threshold indicates bruises, cuts, or other minor injuries 
that are painful, but do not in themselves cause any additional 
serious effects. 
A light wound may later fester and worsen if not dealt with 
properly (see the First Aid and Recovery From Injury or Illness  
sections). Aggravation damage may occur if a character fights or 
takes other violent activity after receiving several light wounds, 
at Gamemaster option.
Cumulative light wounds may bring the character penalties, 
or even drop him below his Unconscious threshold. Once the 
character has become unconscious, Chirurgery is needed, and 
deterioration may set in. See the section entitled Recovery from 
Injury or Illness, below.
PeNalties  f or  b eiNg  w ouNded
He has lost so much blood that his sight is blurred; 
Neither near nor far can he see clearly enough
To be able to recognize a living soul.
His companion, when he encountered him,
Receives a blow on his helmet studded with gold and 
gems.
— The Song of Roland , CXLIX
If a character sustains wounds and loses one quarter of his 
T otal Hit Points, he suffers –5 to all subsequent physical actions. 
If he loses more than half his total, he is seriously injured and all 
his skills have a temporary –10 penalty. At three quarters of his 
hit points, he falls unconscious. 
Severe wounds weaken not only the body but also the senses: 
Oliver feels that he is in the grip of death.
Both his eyes roll within his head; 
His hearing and his vision are now completely gone.
— The Song of Roland , CL
W ound penalties apply to all physical activities, including 
Awareness. If the wound penalty causes the Awareness score to 
drop below 1, this indicates that the character is temporarily 
deafened and blinded by his injuries.
Major Wound
Damage from a single injury that is equal to or greater than 
the Major W ound threshold represents serious damage that the 
character cannot just shrug off. Examples might be a concussion, 
crushed or broken bones, heavy bleeding, or damaged inter -
nal organs. There are several negative consequences to Major 
W ounds in game terms: 
1. The “Chirurgery Needed” box must be checked.  
The character requires the attention of an expert 
in order to avoid infection and other dangers re -
sulting from his injury, described as deterioration 
in these rules.
2. The character falls unconscious  unless he makes a 
successful 1d20 roll against his current hit points (for 
example, a character with 10 hit points left would 
need to roll a 10 or less to stay conscious).
Should he avoid unconsciousness, he may foolishly 
wish to continue fighting or performing some simi -
larly active task. The character must make a success -
ful V alorous roll if in combat, possibly with a modi -
fier. Failure indicates the knight cannot summon up 
the courage to continue fighting. He hesitates, and 
may not enter combat unless forced into it. A fum -
bled V alorous roll means the knight flees in fear or 
surrenders. A critical success may gain some benefit, 
at the Gamemaster’s discretion.
3. Roll on the Attributes Lost Table. The player rolls 
on T able 10–2: Attributes Lost  (see Chapter 10: The 
Winter Phase ) immediately to discover the perma -
nent effect of the Major Wound, if any. All derived 
statistics, such as Movement Rate or Unconscious, 
must be refigured immediately if a loss to a relevant 
statistic occurs from this roll. If the T otal Hit Points 
statistic drops, current hit points are not affected.
4. Both aggravation and deterioration may set in.  If 
further action is taken once a Major Wound is suf -
fered, the wound may become worse through action 
or inaction. See the section entitled Recovery from 
Injury or Illness below.
Example of a Major W ound: Hardrad, a huge, fat old 
knight (SIZ 18, CON 9, V alorous 17, Hit Points 27, Unconscious 
7) has become too old and unhealthy to fight safely. Even a 9-point 
wound is a Major W ound for him, given his mediocre constitution.
Hardrad rashly charges into one last fight, and takes 9 points 
of damage through his armor, exactly the minimum for a Major 
W ound. His player checks the Chirurgery Needed box. He then 
rolls against Hardrad’s current hit points of 18 on d20 and easily 
succeeds, indicating that Hardrad stays conscious. The player easily 
succeeds with a V alorous roll —  Hardrad is well known for his 
valor. So Hardrad does not surrender or hesitate to attack. 
Now the player rolls on T able 10–2: Attributes Lost  for 
the Major W ound’s effects, and Hardrad loses a point of DEX. 
==================== BOOK PAGE 132 ====================
131
Checking the character sheet, the player adjusts Hardrad’s statis -
tics to take into account the DEX loss: sadly, Hardrad’s Movement 
Rate goes down a point. At this juncture in combat, Hardrad may 
fight on, with a –5 penalty for his wounds. Aggravation may occur 
over the longer term, however.
Mortal Wound
Damage from a single blow that is equal to or greater than 
the T otal Hit Points statistic is usually mortal, thus the term. 
The character has received a horrible injury; his skull is cracked, 
an artery severed, internal organs punctured, or even a limb 
amputated, if the Gamemaster so rules. The character is inca -
pacitated and totally helpless. However, he may still survive, if 
certain criteria are met. Follow the steps below: 
1. First Aid must be successfully applied  within one 
hour or the character dies, and even then recovery is 
doubtful. First Aid must restore enough lost hit points 
to give the character at least 1 positive hit point. 
Obviously, if the character is at more than –5 hit 
points, the wound is always fatal (see First Aid below). 
Chirurgery cannot help until the character is out of 
immediate danger (i.e., has positive hit points). The 
only remedy is magical healing.
2. If the character is brought back to 1 or more hit 
points he lives, but immediately suffers 3 rolls on the 
T able 10–2: Attributes Lost to determine permanent 
effects. Derived statistics must be refigured immedi -
ately if losses to statistics occur (current hit points are 
not reduced).
3. The “Chirurgery Needed” box is checked.  If the 
character lives beyond the first hour (having been 
brought to positive hit points), he lies unconscious 
and his future is still in grave doubt.
States of Health
A character in P aladin  is either healthy or unhealthy. 
A character may have a reasonable number of hit points and 
still be unhealthy, or he may have only a few hit points above 
Unconscious and still be active. The “Chirurgery Needed” box 
shows the current situation.
Healthy
Healthy individuals are all those who do not require 
Chirurgery: if the box is not checked, the character is fine and 
may function normally, though possibly with a penalty due to 
wounds. The character is considered healthy even if not at full 
hit points: knights are trained to live with minor injuries. The 
injuries of healthy individuals heal weekly at the Healing Rate 
(see Recovery from Injury or Illness below).
Even if an injured character is healthy, excessive strenuous 
activity will cause aggravation, which is handled by incident, not 
by week. See page 132.
Unhealthy
If the “Chirurgery Needed” box is checked, the character is 
unhealthy and needs care. Rest is needed and action is liable to 
worsen his condition. Normally the Chirurgery Needed box is 
checked when: 
 n Losing hit points to below the Unconscious level.
 n Taking a Major Wound.
 n Taking a Mortal Wound (and surviving).
 n Receiving a fumbled First Aid roll.
 n Contracting an illness due to unhealthy condi -
tions or other sources. This might include poisoned 
characters.
Unhealthy characters are incapable of taking care of them -
selves properly and natural deterioration (see page 132 ) may 
set in unless someone else actively tends to their needs. This 
active attention simulated by the Chirurgery roll. Success at 
Chirurgery prevents deterioration.
Once a character is unhealthy and the “Chirurgery Needed” 
box is checked, it remains checked until the Gamemaster ap -
proves its removal, normally after several weeks of rest. A char -
acter requires chirurgery until he has recovered half his total hit 
points, at which time he is still hurt, but Healthy.
First Aid
The First Aid skill can return lost hit points to wounded 
characters, and ensures that bleeding is stopped, infection pre -
vented, and so forth. As emergency battlefield treatment, it is 
useful only immediately or shortly after any kind of wound is 
received. It cannot be applied to wounds more than a day old, 
and its benefits can only be received once per wound. 
If no wound is recorded (possibly due to disease, aggrava -
tion or magic), First Aid cannot be used.
Each use of First Aid for a light wound takes several minutes 
(3d6 melee rounds, or one battle round) if such a calculation 
is required). More serious wounds should take longer to dress, 
but this is up to the Gamemaster to decide. If a failed attempt 
is made, it is too late for further tries. Any subsequent medical 
attention must come from Chirurgery.
Characters that gain successful First Aid for all their 
wounds do not require Chirurgery, unless they have suffered a 
Major or Mortal wound. Note that while many minor wounds 
can be taken care of using First Aid and the knight returned to 
full hit points, a single severe wound leaves him still damaged.
Step 1: Determine which wound is being treated, and its 
magnitude (see the “W ounds” line: the number might be –3, 
–14, or whatever) before making an attempt.
Step 2: Roll normally (modifiers may apply at Gamemaster 
option) and see results, page 132.
Injury and Health

been worsened, and the wound is now infected, 
bleeding further, or otherwise exacerbated. The

Step 3: Adjust current hit points. Excess points gained be -
yond the magnitude of the wound are lost.
Step 4: Regardless of result, erase the number on the

Recovery from Injury or Illness
Recovery from injury or illness is unpredictable. Many sit -
uations cause recovery to be delayed or even retarded, and weak 
characters may require months of convalescence before full re -
covery takes place. It is even possible for unhealthy characters to 
die during convalescence, should they be badly cared for. Even 
Sir Ogier is often out of action due to injury.
Once a character is injured, recovery from injury or illness is 
determined each week of game time.
If injured characters insist on activity instead of resting, ag-
gravation is be applied on a per-action basis, but chirurgery, nat-
ural healing, and deterioration are determined on a weekly basis.
Natural Healing
Natural healing occurs at noon on Sunday (game time), in 
the form of hit points gained equal to the Healing Rate statistic. 
Unhealthy characters who failed to receive successful chirurgery 
during the week suffer deterioration at this time, in addition to 
Healing.
Natural healing occurs weekly, regardless of what form a 
character’s injuries or illness have taken, or whether he is resting 
or active during the week.
Chirurgery
Chirurgery is applied to unhealthy patients only. 
Chirurgery’s purpose is to halt deterioration. Successful appli -
cation means that deterioration does not occur. The successful 
chirurgeon has counteracted the forces that would have caused 
the additional damage from deterioration.
Only one Chirurgery roll per patient may be attempted per 
week. If failure or fumble is the result, the result is not obvious 
until the following Sunday, when natural healing would normal-
ly occur. Note that if more than one character try to use their arts 
on a single patient in a week, all automatically fail.
A character requires chirurgery until he has recovered half 
his total hit points, at which time he is still hurt, but Healthy. 
t able  7–4: c Hirurgery  r esults  
Chirurgery Roll Effect
Critical The patient’s health improves rapidly. The pa -
tient gains double his normal Healing Rate this 
week, in addition to avoiding deterioration.
Success The patient does not suffer deterioration.
Failure The patient suffers deterioration (see 
Deterioration below).
Fumble The patient loses 1d3 hit points directly from 
current hit points immediately (but no new 
wound number is recorded), and suffers deteri -
oration at the end of the week.
Modifiers to Chirurgery: If unclean or unhealthy con -
ditions (disease, severe filth, freezing cold) predominate in the 
area where a patient is recuperating, apply a –5 modifier to the 
Chirurgery skill. Other modifiers must be determined by the 
Gamemaster.
Deterioration and Aggravation
Injuries or illness may grow worse rather than better. If the 
character is active enough to interfere with healing, or if he is 
not tended properly, then he may take further hit point damage. 
This may be due to blood loss, dehydration, infection, further 
illness, and more. The damage from deterioration and aggrava -
tion indicates the effects of all these factors.
d eterioratio N
Deterioration only affects unhealthy characters who do not 
get a successful Chirurgery roll during the week. 
Deterioration causes the loss of 1d6 hit points per week 
directly to current hit points (no wound is recorded and First 
Aid can’t help). As with natural healing, this damage occurs 
on Sunday at noon. The net result of Natural Healing and 
Deterioration may be a gain in hit points, a loss, or nil.
a ggravatio N
Gamemasters must inevitably face situations where knights 
who should be resting insist on activity, perhaps “to travel just 
for a few hours” or “one good fight.” During a scenario an in -
jured character may choose (or be forced) to leave his sickbed 
for several days, then rest, then ride many miles, then rest again. 
The rules for aggravation take care of this. For simplicity’s sake, 
resting between actions is not tracked, only damage from specif-
ic activities.
These rules are intended to permit freedom of action for 
players, as opposed to simply forbidding injured characters from 
being involved in the game. Players who wish to risk their char -
acter by taking actions while wounded or unhealthy are free 
to do so, with realistic consequences. Sometimes in P aladin , 
action may be more important than life — perhaps a character 
wishes to avenge a brother or deliver a vital message, even if it 
means riding or fighting while injured.
==================== BOOK PAGE 134 ====================
133
Aggravation means making your condition worse by 
activity while ill or injured. Each incident of aggravation 
causes 1 or more points of damage directly to current hit 
points, with no wound recorded. 1 point is the normal loss, 
but up to 3 points may be suffered from extremely violent 
or sustained activities — such as mortal combat with an 
ogre — at the Gamemaster’s option. Aggravation cannot be 
cured by First Aid.
Aggravation damage is inflicted after the activity is com -
plete unless the Gamemaster decides otherwise. The extra dam-
age caused by aggravation may cause a character to become un -
conscious immediately after his rash action.
Current health, extent of injuries, and duration and inten -
sity of activity are all important in determining aggravation; see 
the table above. Aggravation is possible for all injured charac -
ters, healthy or unhealthy, when strenuous activity is undertak -
en. Aggravation may occur for unhealthy characters when even 
moderate activity is undertaken. Note that characters below the 
Unconscious threshold cannot receive aggravation damage be -
cause they can, at best, perform only light activities. Normally 
they will be comatose.
A brief and trivial incident, such as a ten-minute dance 
during a feast, should rarely cause aggravation except to a severe-
ly wounded character, probably one recovering from a major 
wound. The Gamemaster has to judge each incident separately, 
based on the Activity Level definitions below, storytelling con -
cerns, and common sense.
Aggravation can be heaped upon deterioration. Aggravation 
cannot be halted by Chirurgery, or any other die roll.
a ctivity  l evels
No Activity: Just resting, eating and sleeping. No aggrava -
tion. The best way to recover.
Light Activity: W alking short distances slowly, talking, 
writing short letters. No aggravation, even for unhealthy charac-
ters, unless the activity is performed to excess.
Moderate Activity: W alking longer distances, riding a 
horse (even for a few minutes), celebrating, dancing, engaging in 
romance, engaging in an angry argument. Aggravation is possi -
ble for unhealthy characters, at Gamemaster option.
Strenuous Activity: Fighting, running, climbing, traveling 
a significant distance on horse or on foot (or even in a litter, if 
the Gamemaster so rules). Aggravation is certain for unhealthy 
characters, and possible for healthy characters who are injured.
Example of Aggravation: Sir Adalhart enters a rowdy tour-
nament held on a Saturday to celebrate the victorious battle fought 
earlier that year by Lord Isoré’s men.
As the day progresses, Adalhart fights many times and takes 
several light wounds, gaining successful First Aid for most. After 
the third wound, the Gamemaster rules that Adalhart is per -
forming strenuous activity while injured, and tells the player to 
record 1 point of aggravation damage. First Aid may not be used 
on this damage.
At the end of the day, still conscious, Adalhart staggers to his 
tent to rest. On the first evening of his convalescence he is down 13 
points. He is healthy, so deterioration will not occur, nor is chiru -
rgery needed. Conveniently, he will gain points the very next day, 
Sunday, when natural healing is normally applied.
Sir Adalhart rests Sunday morning. His comrades stop in to 
say hello, but do not insist that he join them that day. So his level of 
activity is light. At noon, Adalhart gains his Healing Rate of 3 hit 
points as his natural healing for the week. Further healing occurs 
normally on the following Sunday.
Adalhart decides to rest the requisite number of weeks to re -
turn to full hit points, avoiding any further strenuous activity in 
order to ensure quick recovery. Being healthy, he is able to engage in 
moderate activity freely, such as dancing and romance.
Sources of Damage
Many things cause physical damage. W eapons are foremost, 
but sometimes accidents are not far behind. Magic can inflict 
damage, but this is unusual: the effects of magic are normally 
more subtle.
All damage is figured in the same way: by subtracting dam-
age points from current hit points. W ounds and recovery may 
be handled differently, however, at the Gamemaster’s option. 
Suffocation and disease do not normally cause Major or Mortal 
wounds, but on the down side, cannot be dealt with using First 
Aid unless the Gamemaster wishes it.

134
Battle or Combat Wounds 
The primary type of injury sustained during the game. If 
the character is wearing armor, it absorbs some or all of the dam-
age. Major and Mortal wounds must be checked for. First Aid 
may be applied individually to any number of wounds.
Aggravation
Caused by excess activity while already injured. See page 
132. First Aid is not allowed.
Deterioration 
Suffered by unhealthy characters who do not receive prop -
er care in the form of chirurgery. First Aid may not be used on 
damage from deterioration.
Disease 
Disease should be a rare event in P aladin . Usually dis -
ease results only from incarceration in a deep dungeon, rather 
than from a casual trip to a large city or similar action. The 
Gamemaster has to determine damage and whether the disease 
causes a wound that First Aid can help. Normally no wound is 
inflicted, only general loss of hit points, or possibly Stats (as 
per the Winter Phase). A diseased character is unhealthy, re -
gardless of hit points. Check the “Chirurgery needed” box and 
proceed normally.
Dropped Objects 
Dropped objects do damage depending on their size and 
the height from which they are dropped. Size is determined in 
pounds, with each 10 lbs. = 1 point of damage. Height adds 
damage at the rate of 1 point of damage per 3 feet dropped. 
A 35 lb. rock, dropped from 23 feet, causes size (4 damage 
pts.)+height (8 damage pts.) = 12 points of damage. Armor 
protects against dropped objects, and a wound number is re -
corded, with normal results.
Falling 
Falling from a distance of less than three feet results in no 
damage. Each six feet or fraction thereof adds 1d6 damage. For 
instance, falling off a horse, about a five-foot drop, causes 1d6 
damage. A fall of twelve feet, from a house roof, causes 2d6. A 
fall of 50 feet, from a castle wall or a cliff, causes 8d6. Armor does 
not absorb falling damage. Falling causes a wound, which may be 
treated by First Aid.
Fire and Heat
Normally, fire does 1d6 points of damage per melee round, 
plus a cumulative 1d6 more for each consecutive round of con -
stant contact. Thus, during the second consecutive round of 
contact, a character takes 2d6 points of damage; in the third 
round, 3d6; in the fourth, 4d6; and so on.
A flame or scalding source of heat must be held against an 
immobilized character to deal damage in this way; otherwise, 
such as in the case of a torch used as a weapon in combat, the 
contact is not sufficient to cause cumulative damage, instead 
dealing just 1d6 points with each hit.
Further, this damage assumes an open fire between the size 
of a fresh torch and a bonfire. 
Larger fires, or sources of heat such as red-hot irons or ket -
tles of boiling lead, can be substantially hotter, doing 2d6 cumu-
lative damage per round or even more. The Gamemaster must 
determine heat damage beyond the basic 1d6.
Larger or more intense sources of heat may also do damage 
at a short distance. However, no natural fire causes damage to 
characters over 12 feet away. Fires may also cause suffocation due 
to smoke inhalation (see Suffocation, below).
Armor of any kind (but not clothing) reduces fire dam -
age by 1d6; that is, a character wearing armor takes no dam -
age from a normal fire in the first round, then takes 1d6 in the 
second round of consecutive contact, 2d6 in the third round, 
and so on.
Each wound from fire or heat damage should be based on 
the total damage inflicted by a singular source or application of 
heat, not necessarily on the damage per round. Thus, continuous 
contact results in one wound, while repeated, distinct instances 
of contact (such as being struck repeatedly with a burning torch) 
result in a number of individual wounds.
For example, a character has a hot poker held against his 
flesh for two rounds. He takes 1d6 points of damage (with a 
result of 4 points) in the first round and 2d6 points (result 7 
points) in the second round. He records a single wound of 
4+7 = 11 points, not two separate wounds of 4 and 7 points 
respectively.
First Aid is useful for dealing with most fire and heat 
damage.
Poison
Every poison has a potency, which measures how strong it 
is, expressed in a number of d6 (like weapon damage). When 
poison is ingested or otherwise introduced into the body of its 
victim, roll the damage and subtract the victim’s CON (like ar-
mor). If the final damage taken is greater than his CON by even 
one point, he falls victim to a major poisoning and must check 
the “Chirurgery needed” box. If the final damage exceeds his 
T otal Hit Point value, the poisoning was lethal.
Suffocation 
Damage from inhaling water, smoke, or poisonous vapors 
may be averted by attempting a CON roll. This simulates hold-
ing one’s breath.
The CON roll must be made each round while the 
character is exposed to possible harm from inhalation. If 
he is successful, he takes no damage. Once the CON roll 
is failed, the character takes 1d6 damage per melee round 
thereafter while he remains within the harmful substance 
or area. He may take more than 1d6 damage if the inhaled 
substance is itself toxic.

A night mingled with pain and cries begging for help,
When some died and others groaned in horrible suffering.
O grief and lamentation! The dead are stripped naked,
Vultures, crows and wolves greedily devour their flesh,
They grow stiff, and their corpses lie there, unburied, helpless.

Chapter Eight:  
Mass Combat
K
nights fight many battles where large numbers of knights, 
soldiers, and warriors clash. In such a situation, individ -
ual prowess while still important, is often submerged be-
neath the movement, emotions, and actions of the masses. 
Three types of mass combat are used in P aladin : the large-
scale battle, the smaller skirmish, and the siege. Most common are 
skirmishes. These are smaller, generally with a single unit against 
a single unit (at most, 200 combatants). In both skirmishes and 
battles, special rules are used to resolve what happens, but the 
procedure for a skirmish is much shorter and simpler, so—being 
likely to see use more often in your campaign—it is explained 
first. Sieges are long, drawn out affairs where knights have little 
to do until a breach is made in a wall or gate. 
The following are some important general points on mass 
combat: 
Victory or Defeat: The actual, final resolution of a battle, 
siege or a skirmish is normally up to the Gamemaster. The de -
cision should be based on commanders’ Battle rolls, the Player-
knights’ success or failure, and storytelling factors. 
In a skirmish, the Player-knights can make a big difference. 
If the Player-knights all do well in melee, then as the most im -
portant warriors in the group, their success should affect morale 
and help to drive their unit to victory.
In a large-scale battle or siege, where the Player-knights may 
be minor, or even insignificant, participants, their success or fail-
ure should not usually affect the outcome.
Glory: Glory is gained in skirmishes, battles and sieges.
Skirmish Glory is normally derived only from melee com -
bat (i.e. Glory for defeating enemies, for heroic actions, etc.). 
However, if a Player-knight is a commander or subordinate 
leader in a skirmish, Glory for successful Battle skill use may be 
gained if appropriate. There is no Glory just for participation 
in a skirmish unless the situation is something very special, in 
which case the Gamemaster determines the appropriate Glory 
awarded.
Battle Glory is a different story. This Glory is based on par-
ticipation. Just being there can be worth Glory. Battle Glory is 
determined per battle round. The bigger the battle, the greater 
the Glory.
Siege glory is also based on participation. Siege Glory de -
pends on the length of the siege and the strength of the castle 
being besieged. 
Battle Skill
T
he Battle skill measures an individual’s knowledge and 
use of individual tactics in skirmishes and battles. A high 
Battle skill value means that the character knows how to 
stick with his unit, how to examine a battlefield to recognize 
what is dangerous or advantageous, and how to take advantage 
of what he sees.
In general, the Battle skill has two applications. The first al-
lows leaders to command their group of combatants, and is used 
in both large battles and skirmishes. The second dictates tactical 
decisions that take place when individuals are separated from 
their larger unit; this use of the skill applies only in large battles.
==================== BOOK PAGE 139 ====================
Chapter Eight: Mass Combat
138
Skirmish
A 
skirmish opens with a mass attack by one or both sides. 
One side or the other gains an initial advantage, and 
combat then dissolves into individual melee. Most mass 
combat during scenarios is of this type.
In a skirmish only the commander of the force makes a Battle 
roll. If the commander is not a Player-knight, the Gamemaster 
determines the commander’s skill and makes the roll. This roll is 
always unopposed. The roll is made on T able 8–1: Commander’s 
Battle Roll Results, below.
The modifier from the table is applied to everyone’s com -
bat skills in the first melee round (usually Lance, Sword, and 
Horsemanship, but not DEX or V alorous rolls or any other non-
skill rolls).
t able  8–1: c o MMaNder ’s  b attle  r oll  r esults  
Battle Roll First Round Modifier
Critical +5
Success 0
Failure –5
Fumble –10
After the commander’s roll, combat is joined and resolved 
character by character using normal melee combat rules. Each 
Player-knight should face an enemy knight, unless the enemy 
unit does not have enough knights to go around. After the first 
round of the skirmish the Battle modifier is not used; only nor -
mal combat modifiers apply.
In a skirmish, most of the combatants will be non-player 
characters. The skirmish does not end until the situation re -
garding the non-player characters is resolved. It may be that the 
Player-knights are totally victorious, only to look up and see that 
the rest of their unit has been routed. Or they may be defeat -
ed by their opponents, only to be rescued by their non-player 
companions.
The Franks are often outnumbered in battle: 
“In truth, my lord, their ranks are so enormous,
If all our men were meat well-cooked and salted,
They’d slice us up and eat us in one course, sire!”
— Song of Aspremont , V
Followers’ Fate
T o determine what the rest of the unit has done, one 
or more Followers’ Fate rolls must be made using the table 
below. Usually this roll is delayed for one to five rounds of 
melee while the Gamemaster deals with individual combats. 
Eventually a Player-knight will look around and ask what is 
happening with the rest of the unit, or the Gamemaster feels 
it time to make the roll.
Every character who leads troops (other than their own 
squires) requires a Followers’ Fate roll, including the overall 
commander. In most skirmishes the overall commander has all 
non-player characters under his command, but Player-knights 
such as banneret knights are considered to be subordinate lead -
ers, responsible for their own men.
A Follower’s Fate roll is done using each leader’s Battle skill, 
modified by the commander’s initial result on the Commander’s 
T able above. The result of the Fate roll is checked on the table 
below.
Note: Being a subordinate leader is the only way that a play-
er can make a Battle roll in a skirmish if his character is not the 
overall commander of the unit.
t able  8–2: No N-Player  f ollowers ’ f ates  
Battle Roll  Followers’ Fate
Critical No losses, and 1 enemy of appropriate rank is captured 
per five non-player characters.
Success 10% losses (2% killed, 8% wounded). Survivors are vic-
torious against their opponents.
Failure 50% losses (10% killed, 25% wounded, 15% captured) 
and surviving troops retreat from the field.
Fumble 75% losses (50% killed, 25% captured), surviving 
troops routed from field.
Losses from groups that contain different classes of troops 
require a ruling from the Gamemaster to ascertain who was cap-
tured, wounded, or killed. W ealthy knights with several squires 
are far less likely to be killed than footmen or impoverished 
knights with no squires, but are prime targets for capture.
Each Fate roll determines what happened to that sub-sec -
tion of the unit. The Gamemaster must make sure that each 
non-player character in the skirmish is clearly assigned a leader 
for the purposes of Fate rolls, with no confusing overlaps.
Example of a Skirmish: Duke Thierry, with a band of 
19 other knights and 30 footmen, is riding northward. He per -
sonally leads 10 non-player knights, and the 30 footmen are 
under his direct command. Sir Isoré leads a subordinate troop 
of household knights (three other Player-knights including Sir 
Adalhart, plus five non-player knights). The unit totals 50 
men, including the duke.
The group sights a party of 75 Saxon raiders. Thierry decides 
to smash the invaders, and the knights prepare to make a charge 
with lances.
Thierry, handled by the Gamemaster, is responsible for the 
fate of his 10 non-player knights and 30 non-player footmen, while 
Isoré, a Player-knight, is responsible for five non-player knights. 
The three other Player-knights are responsible for their own fate. 
Duke Thierry begins the skirmish by making his roll as com -
mander, and receives a lucky Battle roll of 18, a critical success. 
Since Thierry is a Gamemaster character, no Glory is recorded 
for the success. Consulting T able 8–1: Commander’s Battle Roll 
Results, the Gamemaster announces that all members of the unit 
have +5 modifiers to their initial Lance attack in melee. The unit 
charges with a cheer, and the Saxons are caught in the flank. Isoré, 
as a subordinate leader, has +5 for his Followers’ Fate roll, as will 
Thierry himself for his Followers’ Fate roll, due to this roll.
==================== BOOK PAGE 140 ====================
139
The success or failure of individual characters in the skirmish 
is determined by normal melee combat. On the first melee round 
of combat, Sir Adalhart’s Lance skill of 10 is modified by Thierry’s 
successful Battle roll to a value of 15. Adalhart rolls a 14 on the 
first round and hits a Saxon. The other Player-knights do well in 
the first round, defeating several more Saxons. Glory is gained for 
each defeated enemy.
In subsequent rounds, the +5 modifier no longer applies, and 
combat is resolved normally. No one performs any unusual or heroic 
acts in the skirmish, so no other Glory is gained by the Player-knights.
Later in the fight, after resolving three rounds of melee for the 
players, the Gamemaster decides to see what has happened to the 
non-player characters. He starts by rolling for the fate of Thierry’s 
knights and footmen.
Thierry has a +5 modifier to Battle from his initial roll as 
commander, and rolls a success in his Follower’s Fate roll. The re-
sults of a successful roll on the Followers’ Fates table are applied to 
all the men directly following the duke with these results: the men 
are victorious, and take 10% losses.
2% killed out of 40 = .8 = one non-player character killed. 
The Gamemaster rules that it is one of the youngest non-player 
knights, who was reckless during the charge and attacked three 
Saxons at once.
8% wounded out of 40 = 3.4 = three non-player characters are 
wounded. The Gamemaster rules that all three are footmen.
Now Isoré rolls for his five non-player knights. He has a +5 
modifier from the commander’s initial roll, but manages to fail his 
Battle roll. His surviving men retreat. Results are as follows.
10% killed out of 5 = .5 = 1 household knight killed.
25% wounded out of 5 = 1.25 = 1 household knight wounded.
15% captured out of 5 = .75 = 1 knight captured.
The Gamemaster rules that the skirmish ends with Thierry’s 
troop of footmen driving off the surviving Saxons. The Player-
knights were all victorious in melee, so no special results obtain. Sir 
Isoré’s non-played followers were driven back, but since his Player-
knight followers did so well, the Gamemaster rules that no loss or 
humiliation occurred as a result of Isoré’s failed Battle roll.
Since Thierry’s force won the fight, Isoré’s knight that was cap-
tured is rescued, and no ransom need be paid.
Battle
B
attles, in which large bodies of men meet to kill each oth-
er, are a constant part of a knight’s life. P aladin  treats 
such long, large-scale engagements in an abstract manner. 
Although a battle is a lengthy series of individual combats, the 
rules abstract this to make it shorter and more playable. If the 
Gamemaster and players enjoy drawn out individual melees, 
they may certainly play them out—but most players tend to find 
them interminable and, after a time, boring.
In each battle round, your knight makes one combat roll that 
represents an entire half-hour of combat and chaos, of shouting 
and riding, advance and withdrawal. Though this seems almost 
too simple and uneventful, a wide variety of other options enlivens 
the process and makes the event memorable and exciting.
The Feudal Army
Feudal battles and armies are not complex, following a tra -
ditional and predictable pattern. Similarly, army tactics rarely 
vary. In fact, military sophistication is at a historical low in the 
Dark Ages. The feudal lords have enough difficulty just getting 
the armies to the field without having to maneuver them too! 
Battlefields are sometimes chosen in advance, though accidental 
meetings also occur—usually because the ability to scout for the 
enemy is poor. 
The P aladin  battle system assumes that the standard feu -
dal battle tactics are always followed. V ariations in military strat-
egy may be introduced by the enterprising Gamemaster, but they 
will be applied to this basic system.
When a feudal lord goes to war, he first summons his vas -
sals. Their obligation is to come fully armed and equipped, with 
provisions for 40 days (service time is of indefinite length if the 
homeland is invaded). For local defense, the lord probably calls 
up the local levy as well, a mob of untrained peasants armed with 
farming tools. Rich lords might also hire mercenaries, especial -
ly specialty troops such as archers, engineers, and spearmen, or 
simply affordable foot soldiers if the campaign is going to last 
more than 40 days.
Battalions
Medieval armies are organized into three sections. Each 
section is called a battaille (or battalion), and has a fixed place 
to march and to set up for battle. Each is labeled by the order in 
which it marches on the road.
 n The V anguard marches first, commanded by the sec-
ond-highest leader of the army. On the battlefield, the 
vanguard lines up on the right side of the battle line. 
 n The Main battalion marches in the center, led by the 
high commander of the army, and assembles for com-
bat in the center of the battle line.
 n The Rearward battalion is commanded by the third 
highest noble, marches at the end of the line, and as -
sembles on the left side of the field of combat.
Battalions are divided into organic elements, called 
Followings, commanded by their noble leaders (thus, a start -
ing Player-knight is in a following led by Duke Thierry). Each 
following may then be further sub-divided into units; a noble 
might name his household officers or trusted veterans as unit 
commanders.
A unit is defined as any group of knights or other warriors 
following a designated unit commander; it generally numbers 
from 6 to 20 knights and their squires, or up to a hundred foot 
troops. A knight banneret is always considered a unit command-
er for his own knights (see Commanders), and may also have oth-
er knights temporarily assigned to him for the battle.
