==================== BOOK PAGE 141 ====================
Chapter Eight: Mass Combat
140
Types Of Troops
Several kinds of troops accompany a lord into battle.
Knights: W ell trained and well outfitted, knights form 
a core of professional mounted warriors. They are the heart 
of the army. 
Sergeants: Professional mounted troops, decently armed 
and trained, but generally lacking the skill, confidence, and first-
rate horses of knights. Don’t confuse them with the modern 
sense of the word “sergeant.” Other fighters, such as poor mer -
cenary knights without a liege, ambitious men-at-arms, or un -
attached squires also count as sergeants for this purpose. Many 
mercenary units are composed of sergeants.
Hobilars: Mounted, unarmored troops usually used as 
scouts and messengers, but never mustered for battle as a unit.
Foot soldiers: Used to garrison castles, assault walls, and 
build camps. They are often armed with shield and spear, but 
sometimes with great spears, long knives, bows, crossbows, or 
axes, depending on their nationality. They usually wear leather 
armor, often augmented by miscellaneous parts of real armor 
plundered from the dead; only kings can afford to outfit their 
foot soldiers with good armor. Knights do not bother with foot-
men if there are enemy knights to fight instead!
Engineers: Men who can build and work the mighty en -
gines and constructions used in siege, such as trebuchets, mang-
onels, and belfreys. They are also responsible for building castles 
able to withstand such engines. Engineers oversee siege works 
and the digging of tunnels (in the latter capacity, they are some-
times referred to as “sappers”). Without engineers, it is hopeless 
to besiege a city or castle.
Camp followers: Other people who commonly accompany 
armies; they are worthless as combat troops. These followers are 
a constant nuisance on the march and are always a great trouble 
to feed, yet few medieval leaders ever forbid them—or have the 
authority to send them away. Spectators, suppliers, well-wishers, 
and hangers-on congregate to trade with or cheat the soldiers. 
Knights bring girlfriends or wives and servants. Virtually every 
army has camp followers.
Heralds: Because of their special status, heralds play an im-
portant part in battle. Heralds are recognized as neutral messen-
gers and may not be attacked. Prior to a battle, they carry messag-
es back and forth between commanders to plan the battlefield’s 
location. Just before the armies engage, heralds from both sides 
withdraw to the same hilltop, steeple, or silo to watch and re -
cord the events of the battle. They help each other to identify 
combatants. The victorious side chooses the name of the bat -
tle. Afterward, heralds may carry further messages between the 
combatants, commonly to make a temporary truce for burying 
the dead and exchanging ransoms.
Commanders
There are three main levels of command, all of which are 
important to the Player-knights in any battle. During the battle, 
these leaders make Battle rolls that affect the individual combat 
of the knights. 
Army commander: The individual who commands every-
one. His Battle roll is important only one time, when the battle 
starts.
Battalion commander: One of three such officers in the 
army. Each battalion commander’s Battle roll is important only 
once, the first time his battalion enters combat. This usually oc-
curs during the First Charge, and is modified by the army com -
mander’s Battle roll result. 
Unit commander: The person to whom a knight reports 
directly in the battle. He is the most important commander to 
a Player-knight because his decisions determine what the unit 
does each round. As well, it is to his banner that an individu -
al knight rallies between rounds. A knight stays as close to his 
unit’s banner as possible, for there his companions and friends 
can aid him if necessary (and vice versa). The knights of this unit 
are those who have agreed to protect him, who know and recog-
nize him, and who depend on him for aid.
Note that an army commander always has his own unit, gen-
erally composed of his own hand-picked bodyguards, and he is 
also the battalion commander for the main battalion. Likewise, a 
battalion commander leads his own unit of bodyguards.
Remember that battlefield leaders are always determined 
by lordly ranks, in order of precedence as noted previously in 
“Battalions.” It does not matter that the lord in question may 
be only 16 years old with a terrible Battle skill. Lordship is 
about commanding the military, and nobles are jealous of their 
prerogatives.
T wo exceptions may exist: First, if a duke is present, he 
outranks all counts in his duchy, since it is precisely his job to 
command the army. If there are several dukes in the army, they 
determine their position in the army through Glory. Secondly, 
although a lord always commands, he may choose to take the 
advice of a trusted companion or officer on the battlefield. 
Nonetheless, the noble must give the orders and the success or 
failure is credited to the lord, not to his advisor.
The rest of the rigid feudal hierarchy defines the command 
structure. A knight is always in the following of his liege lord, 
or of a unit commander assigned by that leader. If a single indi -
vidual commands the entire force and it is too small to be divid-
ed into battalions, then the battle is handled as a skirmish (see 
Skirmish above).
Battle System
T
he remainder of this section outlines a step-by-step pro -
cess for the Gamemaster and players to follow to run a 
P aladin  battle. Players do not need intimate knowledge 
of all of the rules, but portions of this section are written with in-
dividual knights in mind, so that players can keep track of their 
own characters.
==================== BOOK PAGE 142 ====================
141
Before The Battle
Each of the following steps takes place only once before 
the battle: 
1. Determine the enemy.
2. Determine each army’s commanders.
3. Place the Player-knights in units.
4. Determine Scale.
5. Determine Duration.
6. Determine Strategic considerations.
Determine The Enemy
Most likely, the campaign determines what armies are fight-
ing and under what circumstances (or the players might raise an 
army and attack some personal foe). Regardless, the Gamemaster 
determines the precise makeup of the opposing army.
Commanders
Each army has a single high commander. Each of the three 
battalions has a battalion commander. These commanders may 
be Player-knights (if of high enough rank), or lords determined 
by the Gamemaster and the story line. 
If any of the commanders are Player-knights, note their 
Battle skills. If commanders are determined randomly, the Battle 
skill of an army or battalion commander equals 1d6+15. A unit 
commander, if chosen at random, has a Battle skill of 2d6+8. 
Gamemasters should probably go into a little more detail for the 
enemy battalion commander facing the Player-knights, and also 
for an enemy hero or champion (whom the Player-knights may 
encounter on the field). Normally these two would be Notable, 
Famous, or Paladin-level knights: Give each a name and a coat 
of arms, and work up any statistics pertinent to a personal 
encounter. 
Determine the enemy commander’s bodyguards as well, 
each of whom should be at least an Ordinary knight with decent 
combat skills.
Determine Players’ Units
Decide whether all Player-knights are in the same unit, and 
in what battalion. Everyone in a battle must be with some unit, 
which is always assigned to a battalion. For simplicity’s sake, 
assign all players’ units to the same battalion unless compelling 
game reasons require otherwise. Determine the unit leader for 
the Player-knights’ units (it might be one of them). Y ou need 
know only his Battle skill.
ExamplE  BattlE  of  p amplona
Duke Ernaut is commanded to lead an army against the Moors in Northern Spain. Duke Thierry is summoned to the Fields 
of May (the annual muster of Frankland) and brings his men, including Sir Isoré and the other Player-knights. The commanders 
important to the players are: 
Commander Role Battle 
Duke Ernaut Army Commander 19
Duke Thierry V anguard Commander 17
Sir Isoré Player Unit Commander 15
Sir Isoré leads all the Player-knights, and is fighting in Duke Thierry’s battaille. 
The Gamemaster also secretly notes the enemy commanders: 
Commander Role Battle

And special knights in the Moorish forces:

142
Determine Scale
The battle’s size is a function of the number of total par -
ticipants on both sides. Each fighter, whether knight, squire, 
footman, noble, kern, or peasant levy, counts as one participant.
Battle Scale Number of Participants
Small 200 to 2,000
Medium 2,001 to 5000
Large 5,001 to 10,000
Huge 10,001 or more
Determine Duration
Each army commander secretly writes down the determi -
nation of his side to fight to the finish, as indicated by the max -
imum amount of time (in battle rounds) that his army is will -
ing to fight per day. The number assigned by each commander 
should be a number between 0 and 12, with 8 being typical. 
Roughly speaking, the number recorded by each command-
er represents his army’s attitude, morale, and willingness to fight, 
within the following guidelines: 
Attitude Acceptable Duration
Poor (e.g. fearful, outclassed) 0–2 rounds
Uncertain (cautious, doubtful) 3–5 rounds
Normal (determined, willing) 6–10 rounds
Good (zealous, hateful of foe, reckless) 11–12 rounds
Gamemasters must keep careful track of the number of 
Battle rounds fought. The side with the lower number breaks 
off and withdraws from combat when it has fought its number 
of rounds. Gamemasters should keep the final number for the 
opposing side secret from the players; this allows for some uncer-
tainty to keep them guessing about whether to withdraw due to 
wounds, to use inspiration in a given round, and so on. 
Normally an army rises before sunrise, then arms and 
prepares at once, but delays inevitably occur and slow the pro -
cess. Depending on his or her sense of drama and realism, the 
Gamemaster can subtract as much as 1d6 from each command -
er’s chosen duration for the day, representing the time needed 
for the armies to prepare and possibly other variables, such as 
weather.
Note that this modifier could result in a battle actually be -
ing aborted for the day, such as when a commander chooses 4 
rounds, and then the Gamemaster subtracts 4 or more due to the 
die roll. Such is war. 
Also remember to subtract a number of rounds if a battle 
is set to begin late in the day. T o be allowed to fight for the full 
12 rounds, both sides must begin early, even in the long days of 
summer. Other factors to consider may depend on the story. For 
instance, maybe an army tried to escape but was caught, so the 
battle begins at mid-day. Under such circumstances, even a fa -
natical or reckless commander could attempt to fight for only 5 
or 6 rounds.
Note that a battle may end early if one side routs or retreats. 
The First Charge counts as one battle round.
Strategic Considerations
Selection of the battlefield is critical; some sites offer great 
advantages to one army or the other. Many other manageable 
matters can affect the outcome of a battle as well. The specific 
site may have been predetermined by the events or consider -
ations of the campaign, but in game terms, the leader of each 
force is still entitled to make a Battle roll to affect his army’s 
effectiveness.
In battle, as in a skirmish, the primary commander of each 
force makes a Battle roll before any direct hostilities begin. If 
the Player-knights’ commander is not also a Player-knight, the 
Gamemaster determines the commander’s skill and makes an 
unopposed roll on the T actics Modifier table.
A number of modifiers apply to each commander’s Battle 
roll, as follows: 
s ituatio N Modifiers
Commander’s Army Modifier
Outnumbers foe at least 2: 1 +5
Outnumbers foe at least 5: 1 +10
Is outnumbered by at least 2: 1 –5
Is outnumbered by at least 5: 1 –10
Is in commander’s homeland +10
Is in enemy commander’s homeland  –10
t actics  Modifier
Battle Roll Result Battalion Commander Modifier
Critical success +5
Success +0
Failure –5
Fumble –10
The modifier from the T actics Modifier table is now ap -
plied to the Battle rolls of each battalion leader (see Battalion 
Commander Roll) in that commander’s army.
Example Battle of Pamplona, continued: The Frankish 
army has 4,000 men, the Moors 5,000. This is a Large battle. 
Duke Ernaut is determined and his men of good morale. 
The Frankish Duration is 8. The Moors are less determined, their 
Duration is only 6. The Gamemaster decides that there will be no 
delay in the battle, so does not reduce the Duration. The battle will 
last 6 rounds (about 3 hours), unless one side retreats or routs before 
that time is reached.
There are no Strategic Considerations. 
Ernaut rolls his Battle: a success. +0 Modifier.
The W ali of Saragossa rolls a success as well. +0 Modifier.

The First Charge
Follow these steps to resolve the First Charge: 
1. Players determine if they will take part in the First 
Charge.
2. Battalion Commander roll.
3. Determine enemy skill.
4. Determine player skill modifiers.
5. Resolve player attacks
6. Determine followers’ fate.
T o participate in the First Charge, a warrior must have 
a horse and a lance, and he must be in a unit that is going to 
charge. The knight lines up at the front of the battaille. If not 
in the First Charge, a knight begins at the back of the battlaille.
Battalion Commander Roll
The Lance skill of all knights in the First Charge is mod -
ified by their battalion commanders’ Battle roll, as determined 
here. Modify each battalion commander’s Battle skill by his army 
commander’s tactics modifier, if any.
Battalion Commander’s Battle Roll First Charge Modifier
Critical success +5
Success +0
Failure –5
Fumble –10
Unit commanders have no effect on the First Charge. They, 
like every other knight, are merely following their respective bat-
talion commanders’ orders.
First Charge
Each knight taking part in the First Charge fights an op -
posed resolution using his Lance skill, as modified by his bat -
talion commander’s battle roll, against an enemy determined by 
the Gamemaster.
d eterMiNe  e NeM y  s kill
Most likely, the ongoing story has determined the types and 
numbers of foes — such as Saxons, Moors, or other Frankish 
knights — that the players’ army faces. The specific opponent 
can be determined using the  Battle Enemy T ables found at the 
end of this chapter. Their skills and armor vary widely depend -
ing on the type of foe. For ease and speed of play, Gamemasters 
should determine these opponents beforehand.
Y ou can assume that all the opponents in a given enemy unit 
are of the same quality and value. Thus, if the Player-knights’ 
unit is fighting against knights, and the Gamemaster rolls a 3 
on the Battle Enemy — Knights, Early  table, the result is “Poor 
knights.” Each Player-knight faces a knight with a Sword skill of 
11, Lance skill of 10, a damage bonus of 3d6, armor of 6 (plus 
shield), mounted on a rouncy.
The Gamemaster assigns a foe for each Player-knight (or 
rolls on the appropriate Battle Enemy table) to determine the 
results of individual combat. This foe is used in the First Charge; 
then, at the Gamemaster’s discretion, another foe may be as -
signed or checked anew for each subsequent round of melee.
c Harge !
Use the standard Lance combat rules, including +5 if 
charging non-Lance armed foes. If the charge is against enemy 
knights, they go lance against lance. If against footmen, the usual 
+5/–5 reflexive bonus applies unless the foot troops are armed 
with great spears in the front ranks, negating the mounted ad -
vantage of the knights.
The Gamemaster rolls individual combat separately for 
each Player-knight.
Remember to have each player note whether his knight’s 
lance shattered! If so, he may not use it in later battle rounds 
without rearming.
Missile  t roo Ps
Bowmen, crossbowmen, or javelin-armed troops are treated 
differently from melee troops and horsemen. 
When knights attack missile troops, the enemy always 
shoots first, before the knights can close. However, to simulate 
the nervousness of the missile troops, their skill is opposed by 
the knight’s appropriate weapon skill (i.e., usually Lance skill for 
the First Charge), just as if the two were in melee. 
If a missile weapon wins the contest, damage is rolled nor -
mally against the knight. If the damage result is an odd number 
then the knight’s horse is hit; on an even number, the knight is 
struck. If the knight gets a partial success, he may use his shield 
to protect himself or his mount.
Horse damage can be disastrous to the knight. A wounded 
horse immediately tries to stop its charge: It continues only if 
the knight makes a successful Horsemanship roll. On a failure, 
the horse stops; on a fumble, the rider is thrown (and takes 1d6 
damage, as usual). Any horse that takes a Major W ound or is 
rendered unconscious or killed as a result of the missile fire falls 
to the ground immediately; the knight must make a DEX roll 
to land safely, or else he takes 2d6 points of damage from the 
violent fall. Knights without horses begin the next round alone 
and afoot (see Melee).
Follower Results
As in skirmish combat, each unit leader should make a 
Battle roll to determine the fate of his followers. See Followers’ 
Fates, below, for more information.
End Of Round
The first battle round is over. Knights may have intact lances 
which they can use in the following round, or they might need to 
draw swords or other weapons for the subsequent rounds. There 
can be only one First Charge per battle, although it is possible that 
a unit can later make another lance charge if conditions are right.

==================== BOOK PAGE 145 ====================

144
Example Battle of Pamplona, First Charge: Duke Thierry 
rolls his Battle (+0 from Duke Ernaut’s Battle roll): a critical!+5 
to the vanguard’s Lance skill.
The W ali of Burgos rolls a mere success; +0 to his battal -
ion’s skill. 
The Player-knights are all in Sir Isoré’s unit (a total of 10 
knights), and gain +5 to their lance rolls. They are facing a unit of 
foot soldiers armed with swords and shields. The Foot soldiers have 
a skill of 17, 5d6 damage, and 4 points of armor. The knights have 
a +15 bonus to their Lance skill: +5 from the Commander’s roll, 
+5 for lances against non-lances, and +5 for horse against foot. The 
Enemy have a –5 modifier for foot against horse. 
The Player-knights do well, all surviving. Sir Isoré rolls 
his Followers’ Fate and gets a success (10% losses (2% killed, 8% 
wounded). Survivors are victorious against their opponents.
No knights are killed (2% of 10 knights is only .2), and one 
non-Player-knight is wounded. The enemy unit is mauled, and re-
treats from the field. 
The First Charge is over. 
Melee
The remaining battle rounds are handled much like stan -
dard melee combat, so this type of round is likely to be repeated 
several times.
Follow these steps in each round: 
1. Check each knight’s status.
2. Roll for Melee Events.
3. Roll on the Unit Battle tables (T able 8–3 or T able 8–4).
4. Resolve individual melees.
5. Determine Followers’ Fates.
Player-knight Status
Start each melee round by checking each knight’s status. 
Each player needs to know if his or her knight is: 
 n Alone, attached to a unit, or leading a unit.
 n Mounted or on foot.
 n Holding a prisoner.
If a knight was unhorsed last round, he begins this round 
alone and afoot (see below). 
Holding a prisoner prevents a knight from engaging in 
combat, for if the knight becomes engaged the prisoner auto -
matically escapes and no ransom can be collected. A knight with 
a prisoner must escort him to the rear of the battle or call his 
squire (if available) to remove the prisoner for him. If neither of 
those events occur, then the prisoner escapes.
Melee Events
Roll 3d6 and check the following table for each player unit. 
The resulting modifier is applied to the unit commander’s Battle 
and Followers’ Fate rolls for the round, and to all of the unit’s 
weapon rolls for the round. 
If alone, an individual is treated as if he were his own unit 
(he must make his own Melee Events roll) and the result is ap -
plied to the character’s subsequent rolls this round. See the Unit 
Battle Roll section below).
3d6 Result Event/Situation Modifier 
3 Player battalion routs* (see Rout). –15 
4 Player unit retreats (see Retreat). –10 
5 Surge of enemy knights. –10 
6 (triple 2s) Player battalion retreats* (see Retreat). –5 
6 Player battalion outnumbered. –5 
7 Player battalion surrounded. –5 
8 Enemy pushes forward. –5 
9 –12 Could go either way. +0 
13 Enemy is confused. +5 
14 Enemy battalion outnumbered. +5 
15 Enemy pulls away. +5 
15 (triple 5s) Enemy battalion retreats* (see Retreat). +5 
16 Surge of victory. +10 
17 Enemy unit retreats (see Retreat). +10 
18 Enemy battalion routs* (see Rout). +15 
* Ignore these results if they are generated by a lone knight acting as a unit; 
roll again until you get a suitable result.
Note that the Gamemaster does not make Melee Event rolls 
for enemy units.
Unit Battle Roll
This roll determines opportunities of the moment, or the 
lack of them, and whether the unit is involuntarily engaged by 
the enemy or disengaged this round (see below). 
Only unit commanders and knights who are alone make 
Battle rolls in melee, modified by the most recent Melee Events 
result for that unit or knight. 
Knights who are alone always suffer a –10 modifier to their 
Battle skill for this purpose. Always roll first to see if lone knights 
are engaged by an enemy, as explained below. 
Units that are Isolated, because their battalion retreated 
and they did not, suffer a –5 modifier. If the battalion routed 
and they did not, they suffer –10. 
Remember that battalion commanders and even army com-
manders also command their own units directly.

MouNted  vs . f oot , or  f oot  vs . f oot
Battle Roll Result Effect

to the rear, join a unit, or seek a special melee 
event. If mounted, it can make a lance charge.
Success Unit can engage, remain disengaged, or with -
draw to the rear. If it engages, use the appropri-

Failure Unit is engaged by enemy unit.

on weapon rolls.

Otherwise, it is engaged by enemy unit.

* Remember that +5/–5 reflexive modifiers normally apply to combatants 
on foot fighting mounted foes.
Disengaged Units
If a unit (or a lone knight acting as a unit) is disengaged, 
each member of that unit may perform one of the following ac -
tions (or check with the Gamemaster for multiple actions): 
 n Engage the enemy (see below)
 n Withdraw to the back of the battle (see below)
 n Get and/or give several treatments of first aid (see the 
First Aid skill)
 n Check a fallen character for signs of life, give him first 
aid, get him on a horse, and ride him to the back of 
the battle
 n Pillage a corpse or living person of goods and armor
 n Take off or put on armor
 n Attempt to rally troops to himself (see below)
In addition, a lone, disengaged knight may perform one of 
the following actions: 
 n Make a Squire roll to have his squire arrive at his side
 n Look for his unit if he is alone (see page 146)
 n Look for and find a riderless horse (see page 146)
e Ngage  t He  e NeM y
A disengaged unit (or a lone knight acting as a unit) that 
received a success on its Battle roll this round may attack a disen-
gaged enemy unit as normal.
A unit that received a critical success on its Battle roll may 
engage enemy units in any one of the following ways: attack a 
disengaged enemy unit, assist an allied unit, or seek a special me-
lee event.
Attack Disengaged Enemy: Units that are disengaged can 
attack an enemy unit determined by the Gamemaster or by using 
the appropriate Battle Enemy table. 
Assist a Unit: A disengaged unit (or lone knight) can 
choose to help another allied unit (including a lone allied 
knight). The assisting unit enters combat against whatever ene -
my unit is attacking the allied one (the Gamemaster needs to de-
termine the foe), forcing it to divide its combat rolls as described 
in Multiple Opponents in Chapter 7).
In addition, if the assisted unit is a lone knight, he doesn’t 
suffer the –10 modifier to his Battle roll for being alone this 
round.
Seek Special Event: Unit commanders or lone knights 
who got a critical success on their Battle roll this round may roll 
1d20 on the following table: 
t able  8–5: b attle  s Pecial  e veNts
1d20 Result Effect
1–5 Disadvantageous assault: Y ou suffer –3 modifier on 
weapon roll.
6–10 Only normal targets available: Roll again on the ap -
propriate Battle Enemy table.
11–15 Advantageous assault: Y ou gain a +3 modifier on 
weapon roll.
16–18 Enemy hero or noble: Roll at +3 on Battle Enemy ta-
ble to determine bodyguards.
19 Enemy standard: Roll at +5 on Battle Enemy table to 
determine bodyguards.
20 Enemy battalion commander. 
Next, roll on the appropriate Battle Enemy table, but with 
modifiers as noted above. 
Note: If the players manage to defeat an enemy hero, no -
ble, standard bearer, or battalion commander, they have struck 
a major blow against the enemy. Their success modifies the next 
round’s Melee Events roll: they get +5 to the roll for a captured 
or slain hero or noble, +10 for a captured standard, and +15 for 
a captured or slain battalion commander. 
w itHdraw
Unit commanders may order their unit to the rear of the 
battle, out of harm’s way.
r ally  t roo Ps
The unit commander makes a Battle roll, modified by an 
amount equal to his (Glory/1,000)–2d6.

f iNd  y our  u Nit  (l o Ne  k NigHt )
A knight separated from his original unit may make an 
Awareness roll. On a success or a critical success, he sees his for -

f iNd  a  MouNt  (l o Ne  k NigHt )
A dismounted knight may take the time to locate an unhurt 
and riderless horse somewhere on the battlefield, rolling 1d6 to 
find its type: 
1d6 Result Mount Located
1–4 Charger
5 Rouncy
6 Palfrey
He then makes a Horsemanship roll to catch and mount 
the horse. On a success or a critical success, he mounts it in time 
for the next melee round.
Engaged Units
Engaged units and knights on foot who are engaged by 
mounted troops cannot attempt to flee, but must fight. Units 
and knights who are mounted or who are afoot but fighting oth-
ers afoot may choose either to fight or to flee.
Any lone knight engaged by a powerful foe always has the 
option to surrender.
f igHt
This is the standard combat round. In general, everything 
done previously was to reach this moment of knightly purpose.
Find Enemy Skill: Use the Battle Enemy table to find the 
knight’s foe this round (the foe is likely to change every round).
Melee: Battle melee is handled just as a single melee com -
bat round, applying only to Player-knights. Players must keep 
track of their results each round (i.e. critical, success, partial 
success, failure, or fumble). This helps determine Glory at the 
end of the battle. Remember that a player’s combat skill may 
be modified by the most recent Melee Events result and/or by 
Special Event results. 
Player-knights may gain the opportunity to gain Glory in 
battle by capturing an enemy commander, a noble, or the battal-
ion standard. In these events, Gamemasters should momentarily 
switch from the Battle System procedure to normal melee com-
bat. The players can carry on their melee as long as they wish 
and are able (Player-knights may well die trying to attain such 
Glory). The entire fight counts as one battle round. After it is 
over and the players either win or give up, switch back to Battle 
mode and complete the battle.
Glory for these special combats is determined a little differ-
ently from that in normal battle rounds, so players must record 
all the results of their combats, especially enemies killed or de -
feated in battle, so they can collect Glory for it later. The Glory 
for this kind of “special battle round” is equal to the minimum 
for 1 battle round, plus any Glory gained for slaying or incapaci-
tating an enemy, figured as per normal combat.
f lee
While engaged, lone knights and units may choose to flee 
rather than fight against bad odds. Although flight is dangerous, 
it may be the best thing when opposed by great odds. 
Fleeing in battle is much like evading an opponent in me -
lee: to flee, a knight or unit commander must attempt a DEX 
roll (if on foot and fighting other afoot) or a Horsemanship roll 
(if mounted) opposed to the enemy’s weapon skill. Mounted 
characters trying to flee from footmen get a +10 modifier on 
Horsemanship rolls for this purpose.
t able  8–7: f lee
Flee (DEX or  
Horsemanship) Roll Effect
Critical success Escape: Knight is disengaged next round 
and gains +5 modifier on next Battle roll.
Success Escape: Knight is disengaged next round.
Partial success Escape: Knight is disengaged next round, 
but is hit during his flight. T ake normal 
damage, with shield reduction.
Failure Knight is still engaged. T ake normal damage 
from foe, with no shield applied. 
Fumble Knight is still engaged. T ake normal damage 
from foe, with no shield applied. He also 
drops his weapon (if a sword) or breaks it 
(any other weapon).
s urreNder
Knights may surrender if in danger of being slain. It is cus -
tomary for knights to accept surrender from other knights and 
hold them for ransom. Most foreign nobles accept surrender and 
may choose to ransom captured foes afterwards; others may just 
throw them into prison.
However, surrendering may be tantamount to suicide with 
enemies who are not knights or nobles, or to some barbarians 
(like the Saxons).

Followers’ Fates
After each complete battle round, each unit commander 
must roll on the Followers’ Fates table to see what has happened 
to his unit: 
t able  8–8: f ollowers ’ f ates  
Result Effect
Critical success One enemy of appropriate rank is captured per 5 
subordinates. No losses.
Success 10% losses (2% killed, 8% wounded).
Failure 50% losses (10% killed, 25% wounded, 15% 
captured).
Fumble 75% losses (50% killed, 25% captured).
Retreat, Rout, and Pursuit
Some results of the Melee Events table have special results, 
as detailed here.
Retreat
Retreat indicates an orderly withdrawal of forces from the 
battlefield. The armies still fight as they retreat, if necessary, 
possibly with a designated rear guard sacrificing itself to save the 
rest.
Note that battalions can be forced to retreat only on a roll 
of triple 5s or triple 2s on the Melee Events roll. Units are not 
required to retreat when their battalion does, but it is recom -
mended. If they do not, then their subsequent Battle rolls suffer 
a –5 modifier because the unit is isolated. Retreats are treated 
as part of normal battle. Units in retreat are likely to rally again 
and re-enter the battle, probably based on the actions of their 
commanders.
Rout
A rout is a panicked withdrawal from the field. Soldiers, 
even knights and nobles, abandon the field in reckless haste, of -
ten throwing away their encumbering equipment as they go.
Only battalions rout. Knights are not required to rout 
when their battalion does, but it is recommended. If they do not, 
then their subsequent Battle rolls suffer a –10 modifier because 
the unit is isolated.
Knights whose battalions rout have a choice of action de -
pending on whether they are engaged or disengaged: disengaged 
knights may escape, run away, or stand; while engaged knights 
may only run away or stand.
Escape: T o escape, the knight makes a Flee roll (see Ta b l e  
8–7: Flee ), without modifier.
Run Away: The knight is one of the majority who are run-
ning from the field: He makes a Flee roll with a –15 modifier.
Stand: The knight is determined to stand and rally a unit, 
or die in the attempt. Make a Battle roll using this special table: 
Result Effect
Critical success His heroic stand stymies the enemy. The battal -
ion rallies! Gain 100 Glory, and the attention of 
the leaders.
Success He rallies a unit of 2d6+1 men but is surrounded 
by the enemy; they must cut their way free. Each 
knight must fight 1d3 enemies. 
Failure He is surrounded by the enemy, alone, and must 
fight his way free. In addition, he suffers a –5 mod-
ifier to weapon skills, a +5 modifier on the Battle 
Enemy table, and he must face 1d3 foes. 
Fumble He is deeply surrounded by the enemy, alone. He 
suffers a –10 modifier to weapon skills, a +10 
modifier on the Battle Enemy table, and faces 
1d6 foes.
Pursuit
If the enemy has routed, a Player-knight may choose to be 
among those who have broken ranks to run them down. By its 
very nature, pursuit breaks the integrity of a unit, rendering all 
pursuers alone, even if the leader of the unit orders the pursuit.
Only knights who are mounted may pursue a mounted 
foe. Men on foot may pursue dismounted foes. Only two bat -
tle rounds of pursuit are possible during each single instance of 
a rout. Once a knight has pursued a routed enemy (even for 1 
round), he may not rejoin the original battle this day, even if the 
battle continues among other battalions.
f irst  Pursuit  r ouNd
The first round of pursuit is handled like a normal bat -
tle round of melee. This simulates chasing the enemy off the 
battlefield. 
s eco Nd  Pursuit  r ouNd
Once the foe has fled, the pursuit is more like a hunt. In 
fact, this is so much the case that a Hunting roll is used (instead 
of a weapon roll) against the foe’s opposed weapon roll for this 
round, and the results checked against the following special rout 
table. No Battle roll is made this round.
Hunting Roll Result
Critical success Pursuer found enemy camp: Gain 2d6 + £2 of boo-
ty and 2 more rounds’ worth of pursuit Glory.
Success Pursuer killed 1d6 more enemies and adds 1 
round’s worth of pursuit Glory. If he succeeds on a 
Prudent roll, he also captured an enemy. 
Partial success Pursuer killed 1d3 more enemies and adds 1 
round’s worth of pursuit Glory.
Failure Pursuer killed 1 more enemy and adds 1 round’s 
worth of pursuit Glory. However, he was also hurt, 
and takes 3d6 damage (apply armor).
Fumble Pursuer was ambushed! If pursuing knights, take 
6d6 damage from the surprise lance strike; if foot 
forces, take 3d6 twice. Get no pursuit Glory for 
this round.

After The Battle
Once a battle is over, there are still several steps: 
1. Determine who won.
2. T end the Wounded.
3. Determine plunder and ransoms.
4. Calculate Glory.
Determine The Victor
Determine whether the battle was a clear victory, inde -
cisive, or a loss for the players’ side. If the story has not deter -
mined the results, the Gamemaster rolls 1d20 on T able 8–10: 
Battle Results for the results on the player’s army. Modifiers to 
the Battle Results roll are as follows: 
t able  8–9: v ictory
Situation Modifier
A battalion on the players’ side routed  –10
A battalion on the players’ side retreated –5
Enemy battalion retreated +5
Enemy battalion routed +10
t able  8–10: b attle  r esults
1d20 Result Effect Fate Modifier
2 or less Decisive defeat +5
3–18 Indecisive +0
19 or more Decisive victory –5
The total casualties for each army is determined by a final 
Battle roll made by the army commander, with the Fate Modifier 
from the chart above. Compare the result with the Follower’s 
Fates table to find the total numbers lost for the entire army. 
Tend The Wounded
Those who are too hurt to travel may be tended in a field 
hospital nearby. Others are taken by cart or wagon to cities and 
monasteries to be tended until healed. Many characters, includ-
ing Player-knights, may need extensive time to recover after a 
battle. The army may march on to campaign elsewhere, leaving 
them behind.
Burial of the dead is a tremendous task after battle. The los-
ing side usually asks for a truce period to bury their dead, which 
is usually granted. 
Determine Plunder
Only an army that wins a decisive victory obtains plunder, 
as determined by the Gamemaster.
Collect Ransoms
Although the actual collection of a ransom may take months, 
Gamemasters may wish to grant the ransom quickly, since in most 
cases a major battle is the end of the year’s activities anyway.
Calculate Glory
ParticiP aNt  g lory
The procedure here is to find the basic amount of battle 
Glory, and then multiply it by the several other factors shown 
here to find his total Battle Glory.
Size of Battle Basic Glory per Round*
Small 15
Medium 30
Large 45
Huge 60
* This Glory is gained for each battle round during which the character 
took part in the battle. Rounds spent at the back of the battle (i.e., without risk) 
do not count.
Combat Roll (by Round) Multiplier*
Critical success x2
Success, partial success x1
Failure, fumble x.5
Disengaged x.01
* This multiplier is applied to the basic Glory for each battle round, as 
determined in the previous chart, based on the character’s action in that round.
Victory Modifier Multiplier
Decisive victory x2
Indecisive x1
Decisive defeat x.5
Special Modifier * Multiplier
Character’s unit was outnumbered more than 2: 1 x1.5
Character’s unit was outnumbered 5: 1 or more x2
Character’s unit outnumbered enemy more than 2: 1 x.75
Character’s unit outnumbered them 5: 1 or more x.5
* Apply a modifier from this chart only once; if more than one condition 
applies, choose the one that took place for the greatest duration during the battle.
c o MMaNder  g lory
Army commanders receive gain individual Glory as normal. 
They also receive extra Glory equal to the basic battle Glory x 
the total number of battle rounds x the victory modifier. 
Battalion commanders receive extra Glory equal to one 
third of that gained by the army commander. 
Unit commanders do not receive extra Glory. 
Example of Commander Glory: King Charlemagne leads 
an army in a small battle (base 15 Glory per round) that lasts 8 
rounds, and is victorious. His resulting commander Glory = 15 x 
8 x 1 = 120 Glory.
Duke Thierry commanded the vanguard, so he gets 40 Glory.
i Ndividual  g lory
If a character was involved in a melee Special Event, he 
gets Glory for the individual combats of that fight, calculated 
normally. This individual Glory is not affected by battle Glory 
==================== BOOK PAGE 150 ====================
149
multipliers; it includes only the normal combat Glory, which is 
added to the final result of the battle Glory.
Battle Enemy Tables
A
lthough these tables are largely based on historical prec-
edents, we’ve taken some liberties to make them more 
colorful and interesting, such as making some mounted 
troops available where history does not indicate they existed. 
The quality given for a group is the average for its mem -
bers. The leader is always one level of quality higher than the 
common group, so that a unit of Ordinary knights is led by a 
Rich knight, or a group of Saxon warriors is led by a Edhilingui, 
for example. Where two values appear separated by a slash under 
the “W eapon Skill” column, the first value applies to the primary 
weapon and the second applies to the secondary weapon; if no 
secondary weapon is listed for a given chart, assume it is a dagger.
Damage listings already include modifiers for the type of 
weapon used, if applicable. For example, a Basque listed as using 
a javelin for 3d6 damage would have a Damage stat of 5d6 with 
a sword.

Ceorl/Frilingi: An armed peasant or freeman.
Hearthguard/Huscarl: A professional warrior or bodyguard. Any unit listed as Hearthguard/Huscarls has a Edhilingui or a berserker among them as their leader.
Edhilingui: Saxon noble. The noble is the leader of the unit, and their unit members are Hearthguard/Huscarls.
Berserker: Madman, inspired by a pagan passion. They are leader of the unit, and all their unit members are Hearthguard/Huscarls.

Siege
A 
siege is a long, time-consuming event in which disease 
is the most likely outcome for the besiegers. T o avoid 
such a long and potentially tedious part of the game, the 
siege of a castle is made into a single die roll using this skill.
A defender causes Siege to be used whenever he (I) takes 
refuge in a fortification; (II) has any Siege skill; and (III) has 
sufficient garrison.
In any siege, the Defensive V alue (DV) of a fortification is 
applied directly as a bonus to the Siege skill of the defender, so 
a noblewoman with a Siege skill of 3 in a motte-and-bailey (DV 
5/3) has an effective skill of 11.
Six methods exist to take a hostile castle. Three are assaults: 
go over the walls, through the walls, or under the walls, and are 
handled under “ Assault.” The next two, treachery and blockade, 
are handled differently and have their own rules. The last way to 
conquer a castle is personal combat between champions. 
Assault over the wall is expensive because it costs the lives of 
many good men. Not necessarily knights, who are saved for the 
final assault, but footmen and mercenaries (who still have value to 
a liege). An assault requires ladders, grapples, and perhaps a siege 
tower or two. Although expensive, it is the quickest method.
Assault through the walls can be attempted by means of a 
breach. This is the most likely form of attack. A hole must be 
made through which men can enter the castle, by boring, ram -
ming, picking, or battering by siege engines.
Digging under the walls is a third method of assault. It is 
labor-intensive and time-consuming. A tunnel can be dug under 
the wall through which men attack, or sometimes it is dug to 
cause a section of wall to collapse to allow an attack through the 
breach.
Blockade is a long-term, time-consuming method intend -
ed to starve the defender into submission. However, due to the 
unsanitary conditions of medieval times, it is often a race to see 
who will get dysentery first — the attackers or the besieged.
T reachery is the most popular and cheapest means of cas -
tle-taking, for it is often easier to find a dissident defender who 
will leave a postern gate open than it is to order a thousand men 
to their deaths. T reachery within a besieged castle occurs as one of 
the results of failed morale tests. See the Morale rules for details.
Duels between champions are popular in the Gestes, if only 
because it allows the hero of the Geste to show his mettle against 
a giant. It is the absolute cheapest (in terms of 
men and material) way of taking a castle, as it 
relies on a single man (or giant) to get the job 
done. 
Defense Value
Permanent fortifications have a rating 
called DV , or Defense V alue. In essence, the 
DV measures how good a castle or fortress is 
as a defensive structure. This value depends 
on the building materials, types of structures, 
quality of fortifications, and so on. The avail -
able defensive works change and improve tre -
mendously as campaign time progresses. See 
the Castles and Defensive W orks  section of 
Chapter 12 for more information.
Castles usually have a central stronghold 
surrounded by one or more rings of defense. 
These are expressed as single figures with 
slashes, where the first number is the outer 
wall (the first to be assaulted) and the other 
numbers are the successive rings of defense. 
Thus, standard motte-and-bailey, essentially a 
wooden fort that can be thrown up in a week, 
is designated as a DV 5/3. The 5 represents the 
ditch and palisade, and the 3 is the stone tower 
atop a hillock.
In Simple Siege Resolution, a castle’s total 
DV value is used, adding each value to create 
one number. Thus, the standard motte-and-
bailey is treated as a DV 5+3 = 8.
==================== BOOK PAGE 158 ====================
157
Note that sometimes a natural feature provides a permanent 
DV. This is added to the DV of the castle as “#N+, ” which means 
“# Naturally added to.” That is, this value is added to the DV of 
each ring, even after the outer defenses are breached. Castles on 
high hills or rocky crags get this sort of bonus.
Area
Area is a rough measurement of how much space a castle or 
a city takes up. This is important because, naturally, the entire 
thing has to be enclosed by walls. Hence, fortifications for a larg-
er area cost more to build, but still net only the value of the wall, 
and then only if the entire area is surrounded. 1 Area is equal to 
1 acre of land. 
Most castles are simply Area 1.
Villages and T owns are Area 2–9.
Cities are Area 20 or more.

Isolated W atchtower: Square stone keep: DV 10. Cost £30

ditch, moat, curtain wall, gate, gate tower. Stronghold: Square 
keep.
Medium Castle: DV 26/10. Cost £102. Outer works: Double 
ditch, moat, curtain wall, gate, square gate tower, two square tow-
ers. Stronghold: Square keep.
Large Castle: DV 32/19. Cost £147. Outer works: Double 
ditch, moat, curtain wall, gate, square gate tower, two square dou-
ble towers. Stronghold: T all square keep, gate tower.
City W all: Stone curtain wall, with 4 gate towers and 20 
square towers: DV 40, Area 20. Cost £2170, with a Citadel (Small 
castle)integrated with the walls.
W alls of Paris: Double stone curtain wall, 11 gate towers, 4 
strong stone towers where the walls meet the Seine, 77 other tow -
ers: DV 49, Area 640. Cost £16264. A separate large castle (the 
Louvre) outside the walls.
Simple Sieges 
The defender is holed up, and the attacker has an army and 
some siege equipment.
1. Determine DV of the castle.
2. Determine Siege skills of attacker and defender, 
3. Determine amount of siege equipment on each side.
4. Subtract defender’s siege equipment from attacker’s, 
apply result.
5. Roll Siege vs. Siege and compare results to the Siege 
Assault Results table
DV
Combine the DV of all rings of a castle. For example, a 
motte and bailey castle with DV 5/3 is counted as DV 8.
Siege Skill
If the Gamemaster doesn’t already have the Siege skill of 
one party, simply roll 1d20 to determine the skill of a defender, 
or 2d6+5 for an attacker.
Siege Equipment
If the amount of siege equipment isn’t already known, roll a 
double d20 to determine how much is available: 
Critical 1d20+10
Success 1d20
Failure 1d10
Fumble 1d6
Subtract the points of siege equipment of the defender 
from those of the attacker. 
 n If the attacker has any points left, those points are 
subtracted from the DV of the castle. If there are still 
points left, add the remainder to the attacker’s Siege 
skill 
 n If the defender has points left, they are added to the 
DV. 
In all cases, all the committed siege equipment for both 
sides is lost.
Assault
The assault uses an opposed resolution of the attacker’s 
Siege roll against the defender’s Siege Roll. Ignore the possibil -
ity of defenders retreating to inner defenses — this roll is all or 
nothing — if the attacker wins, he takes the castle. 
Example of Simple Siege Resolution: Lady Aude, defending 
a motte-and-bailey castle (DV 5/3) with a Siege skill of 3, has a 
total skill of 11, plus 4 points of siege equipment. Lord W arin, the 
attacker, has a Siege skill of 14 and 25 points of siege equipment. He 
sets up the batteries and archers, sends axe-men against the walls, 
and so on, while the defenders drop rocks and boiling water and 
shoot arrows spending their siege equipment.
W arin has much more siege equipment, so Aude’s 4 merely 
cancels out 4 of his, leaving him with 21. The castle has DV 8, and 
these cancel out 8 more of W arin’s equipment, leaving him with 13. 
He has a +13 bonus to his Siege skill, for a total of 27.
However, even though Lady Aude has an effective skill of only 
3, she rolls a 2, a success. Sir W arin rolls a 9, +7 for the 4 points 
over 20, for a total of 16—also a success. Comparing the results on 
the Siege Assault Results table, we see that, even though her castle 
lies largely in ruins, Lady Aude’s brave men drive off the attackers, 
for the result is both Aude and W arin succeeded: the castle is not 
taken, with light losses to the attacker. All the siege equipment has 
been used up.

Advanced Siege 
Siege can quite easily be made much more complicated. If 
the players are interested in the tactics of sieges, a Gamemaster 
should feel free to make this process as complex as desired. In an 
advanced siege, each ring of defense is assaulted separately and 
each round represents one month.
Example of Siege Determination: Lord Isoré (Siege 15) is 
attacking Chateau D’Erlemont (defended by its Castellan, Siege 
12). He has £16 worth of siege equipment. The chateau has simple 
stone wall, a gate tower, and a stone keep: DV 11/10, and £5 worth 
of siege equipment.
The Siege Turn
Each turn of a siege is a month of game time. Each game 
month: 
1. Roll Siege to determine health.
2. Choose a Tactic (Assault, Blockade, Treachery, Single 
combat).
HealtH
Both attackers and defenders must roll to determine if ill -
ness breaks out in their forces. Each Player-knight rolls their 
Siege skill individually. The commander rolls for the health of 
the rest of the force.

Mo NtHly  t actic
Each month the attacker chooses one tactic and makes the 
appropriate rolls. T actics may change from month to month — 
a commander may decide to assault one month, then blockade 
for two months while waiting for his money-chest and more 
siege equipment, then try treachery, etc. In reality, mining and 
battery take place continuously, as do attempts to seduce the 
garrison, while the besieging army cuts off all help and supplies 
from the castle. 
Assault: The Siege commander and the Castle commander 
roll opposed Siege skills, modified by the castle’s Defensive value 
and each side’s siege equipment. Use T able 8–12: Siege Assault 
Results, above, attacking one ring of defenses at a time. A com -
mander may use some, all, or none of his remaining siege equip-
ment in the assault. 
Blockade: In the case of a blockade, both sides must roll 
Stewardship to see how their supplies are holding out (see Ta b l e  
8–13: Blockade ). 
T reachery: instead of an assault, the Attacker attempts to 
suborn a member of the defenders to open the gates (see Ta b l e  
8–14: T reachery). 
Single Combat: Send a challenge to the defender offering 
single combat to resolve the siege. 
a ssault
The assault is resolved as in the Simple Siege, above. 
Siege Assault Example: The first assault is made against 
the DV 11 outer walls. Lord Isoré decides to use 10 points of siege 
equipment. The castellan decides to use 3. The DV of the outer 
walls is reduced to 4: 11 (DV)+3 (defensive equipment) –10 (of -
fensive equipment). Lord Isoré rolls his unmodified Siege skill; the 
Castellan has a +4 bonus to his Siege, raising it to 16. Lord Isoré 
rolls a success, while The Castellan rolls a failure. The defenders 
take Light losses and are driven from the walls into the keep. The 
attackers take Moderate losses. Both the Castellan and Lord Isoré 
are required to take Morale rolls this month. 
b lockade
Blockade is the attempt by the attackers to simply starve the 
defenders out. No attempt is made to assault the fortifications. 
Each Siege Round the two commanders roll Stewardship 
individually to see how their supplies are holding out.

An attacker may try to bribe a member of the defending 
force to surrender the castle or open the gates for an assault. The 
attacker may also promise lenience (or death) to the defenders. 
Each month, instead of an Assault or Blockade, the attacker 
may try to bribe one or more of the following targets. The at -
tacker may modify his Intrigue by +1 per livre spent on bribes. 
The attacker may choose one group to target: 
Castle Commander: Reduces the commander’s V alorous 
in a Morale test.
A Defending Knight: Reduces the commander’s Standing 
[retinue] in a Morale test.
Common Soldiers, Grooms, or W asherwomen: Reduces 
the commander’s Standing [commoners] in a Morale test.

Morale
Breaking the opponent’s morale leads to successful conclu-
sion far more often than simply killing them. No one who expe-
riences such large scale destruction emerges unaffected. Once a 
siege begins, the mood and action of an army lies under only the 
barest control of its commanders.
Morale becomes a factor when: 
 n A line of Defenses falls. (Defender) 
 n An assault causes Moderate or worse losses. (Attacker 
or Defender)
 n A successful Intrigue roll by the attacker, represent -
ing messages promising gold and glory if the recipient 
will open the gates. (Defender)
 n A failed or fumbled Health roll. (Attacker or 
Defender)
 n A failed or fumbled Stewardship roll (Attacker or 
Defender)
T o make a Morale test, roll, in order, the commander’s 
V alorous, Standing [retinue] and Standing [commoners]. 
V alorous is the commander’s own willingness to fight on, or to 
take a handsome bribe from the besieging army. Standing [reti -
nue] is the chance that other knights in the castle will open a gate 
or otherwise betray the castle. Standing [commoners] represents 
the common fighting man, as well as servants, townsfolk, etc. 
Siege
==================== BOOK PAGE 161 ====================
Chapter Eight: Mass Combat
160
Example Morale:  After Lord Isoré took the outer-works 
of the Chateau, both sides must take Morale tests: Isoré because 
he had Moderate losses, and the Castellan because he lost a line 
of defenses. 
Isoré (V alor 15, Standing [retainers] 14, Standing [common-
ers] 12) rolls successfully for V alorous and Standing [retainers], 
and critical for Standing [commoners]. The siege continues, and 
the common soldiers give a +5 bonus to the next assault. 
The Castellan rolls a success for his V alorous, but fails his 
Standing [retainers] roll. The dissatisfaction of the knights is felt 
by the commoners, giving a –5 to Standing [commoners]. The 
Castellan rolls a failure, meaning that someone betrayed the castle! 
The castle is taken by assault and the siege is over.

The Player-knights should be given things to do during a 
siege, whether it is to pillage the surrounding countryside for food 
and supplies, sortie from the castle to destroy siege equipment or 
take a message to their liege lord, or assault the walls. Y ou can use 
the Battle Enemy T ables, above, to determine their opponents.

The Castle Captured 
Knights and lords in a town or castle that falls are automat-
ically captured by the besiegers (if they didn’t die in the fighting 
or get killed by the peasants). Gamemasters may allow for the 
existence of an escape tunnel if it advances the plot. 
Common soldiers may be killed, enslaved, recruited, or let 
go by the besiegers. T ownsfolk will probably be spared after a 
few days and nights of pillage and rapine. 
Calculate Glory
In addition to Glory gained from individual actions per -
formed during the siege, participants gain Glory for a siege if 
they are on the winning side. Glory per Siege T urn (month) is 
equal to the total DV of the defenses (even those that have al -
ready been overcome).
Additional Glory: Some circumstances modify the Glory 
for a siege: whether the castle fell immediately, or held out for an 
extended period, or if it was even taken at all. These modifiers 
are applied to the total glory for the siege.

so all characters gain an additional 10 Glory, for a total of 31 Glory 
for the Siege of Chateau D’Erlemont.

Siege
==================== BOOK PAGE 163 ====================
[Empty Page or Image Only]
==================== BOOK PAGE 164 ====================
163
M
agic in the Carolingian world is not a thing for 
knights. They often fall prey to it, but almost nev -
er use it. The Gamemaster should generally describe 
the effects seen, saying: “Fire breaks out in a circle around the 
knight” or make simple statements, such as: “Y ou have been 
blessed.” There is no need to describe how magic works, or even 
to state it is being used.
Magic should never dominate the game. Gamemasters 
should feel free to make magic take whatever form they wish, as 
long as it is subtle. Establish a mood with magic: let palaces glow 
from a warm internal light, serve exotic and intoxicating wines 
from Cathay, have a dove deliver a letter from heaven. Magic is 
an essential plot device for Gamemasters. A magical event or 
curse can form the basis for an adventure.
Magic in this game is for purposes of roleplaying, not for 
cartoon violence. P aladin  magic imitates the traditional magi -
cal effects found in the epics, rather than comic-book flash-bang 
spells. Fate and luck are important components of magic, not 
just spells and spell casters. The fundamental laws of society, 
such as loyalty or hospitality, are enforced by the decrees of fate, 
and enter the realm of magic. 
T o make magic work, the Gamemaster simply says that it 
works; there is no way for knights to defend against it. This is 
extremely powerful, and Gamemasters are urged to use it spar -
ingly and not corrupt the game by using magic at every turn. It 
ought to be used as special effects instead of major plot devices. 
Permanent magical effects are more common than magical 
users. W e find swords that are better than usual, horns that can 
only be sounded by a specific person, the water of a fountain 
that inspires feelings of hatred or love, or a castle that revolves 
around its axis. They are sources of awe, wonder and fear for nor-
mal people. 
Society
E
veryone in the world of P aladin  knows that magic ex -
ists, and they fear it. T o knights, the application of magic 
is unknown in every way. Its effects are known through 
story and rumor, but no one knows how it is done. It is extremely 
Chapter Nine: Magic
potent, partially because of its very mystery. P aladin  magic is 
dangerous because it is hidden and subtle. Y our character knows 
that magic is more likely to drive him mad or age him a century 
in a day than to toast him with a lightning bolt. 
People accept the world of magic as a normal part of the 
great unknowable reality. They have heard about, and per -
haps seen, magical objects like the sword Durendal and the 
Oriflamme. But men do not hope to understand it.
Magicians are not to be trusted. Everyone knows some rea-
son for this, though the reasons vary depending on the point of 
view of the observer. Some mistrust them because they can alter 
reality, or talk to the dead, or can tell what the weather is going 
to be and change it if they don’t like it. Other people dislike ma-
gicians because they believe that all occult powers come from the 
Devil. Some don’t like anyone who is strange, and magicians have 
access to the unknowable; what is not known cannot be trusted.
Types of Magic
D
ifferent types of magic are recognized. Knowledge 
and wisdom are two of the best-known application of 
Christian magic. Magical healing can be done by the lay -
ing on of hands and channeling the power of God. Blessings (and 
curses) are common. Spirits and demons can be summoned, ban-
ished or exorcised. 
So, what types of magic play a role in the life of the Franks? 
What are known magic places, persons and items? And what 
use can Player-knights make of supernatural power? For clari -
ty’s sake, we distinguish three categories of magic present in the 
world of P aladin : wondrous Divine magic coming from God, 
arcane Wizardly magic resulting from knowledge, and fantastic 
Faerie magic.
Since all player characters are knights, it follows logically 
that all magic-wielding characters, such as sorcerers, faeries and 
priests, are non-player characters. Their role in the stories is to 
guide or test the knights on their adventures. In the background, 
Frankish magic-using characters may serve powerful lords as as -
trologers or scholars, or hold positions of power themselves, as 
bishops or abbots, for example. Noble pagan ladies often have 
==================== BOOK PAGE 165 ====================
Chapter Nine: Magic
164
minor magical healing or divination skills. There are even the 
rare wizard-knights, like Sir Maugis. 
Below, we discuss these different magical manifestations 
from a Gamemaster perspective, before addressing the issue of 
player access to certain types of divine aid.
Divine Magic
The most important source of magic is divine power from 
Heaven, which the Christian priests try to channel down to 
earth for the sake and salvation of all.
Prayers
Priests, monks and pious hermits all try to attract the good-
will of God and benefit from His infinite power. They do so by 
sacred rituals and prayers. Of course, ordinary folk also pray God 
for aid. Though divine aid is rare, it does sometimes happen. In 
game terms, God may manifest his power through miraculous 
events, such as healings, visions, or the appearance of angels.
After the battle of Roncevaux, God helps his followers by 
stopping the course of the sun: 
See, an angel now comes again and speaks; 
Immediately he commanded the king: 
“Charlemagne, ride on, daylight will not fail you.
You have, as God knows, lost the flower of Frankland; 
You can avenge yourself on this criminal race. ” 
With these words the emperor mounted his horse.
God performed a miracle for Charlemagne,
For the sun remained where it was.
— The Song of Roland , CLXXIX–CLXXX
Whatever its manifestation, divine magic cannot backfire, 
nor can it be countered by faeries, spells or other forms of mag -
ic. Against the maleficent forces of paganism (trolls, woodland 
sprites, sacred groves or enchanted springs) there are no better 
weapons than prayers.
Sacred Places
Chapels, churches, cathedrals, monasteries, abbeys, tombs 
of saints, pilgrimage sites and other sacred grounds are known 
to radiate more or less divine power. Over time, Christian rituals 
have permanently sanctified these places where the pure of heart 
have generously prayed for others, where a great miracle once 
happened, or where one or more relics are kept. This ambient 
holy magic can be felt by devout Christians praying at such plac-
es. Therefore, a Player-knight receives a bonus when praying at 
a sacred place.
Relics and Blessed Objects
Similar to sacred places, some objects, called relics, are spe-
cial to the Christian faith and permanently imbued with Divine 
magic. They are usually stored in a special shrine called reliquary 
and displayed to the pious to be venerated, for their blessing may 
rub off on the truly faithful. 
Another common use of relics is to set them into armor or 
weapons in order to assist Christian knights in their combat with 
the forces of evil. Body parts of saints (bones, tears, blood, or 
hair) or objects they used to perform their blessed deeds (crosses, 
swords, staffs, cloaks, and books) may all qualify as relics. These 
sacred objects may confer a bonus to certain types of prayers. For 
example, a relic of St. Hubertus (the patron of hunters) may give 
+3 to prayers concerning a hunt or hunters. Or a knight wearing 
a small necklace containing a drop of St. Martin’s blood may add 
+1 to his score in Generous. For more examples and information 
on relics, see Chapter 12: W ealth and T reasure.
Wizardly Magic
Wizardly magic is opposed to the sacred supernatural. 
Ordinary people see it as “black magic, ” used by wicked sorcer -
ers who use it to gain power and manipulate their victims. They 
obtain their magic from arcane books, their knowledge of astrol-
ogy, and magic objects which they use to cast powerful spells. 
Their magic sometimes produces effects that resemble God’s 
miracles, and because of this confusion, all magicians are deeply 
mistrusted.
Wizards and Sorcerers
Player-knights should never be magicians, since they would 
have a disruptive effect on the game. Wizards can be present in 
the game as NPCs, though. Chapter 16: Non-Player Characters 
presents magicians who play a role in the legends. Other exam -
ples of magic-wielding characters are court astrologers, who can 
read the stars and (sometimes) predict the future. Whether their 
skills are really magic, or reliable, is up to the Gamemaster.
Spells and Player-Knights
Most magicians and sorceresses cast their magic spells with 
the help of an enchanted object. Some use a spell book describ -
ing intricate rituals, others use the arcane knowledge of magic 
herbs.
Some spells cast on Player-knights have a force equal to the 
result of 1d20, which is rolled when the spell is cast. Great magi-
cians like Atlantes or Maugis may add +5, or even +10, to their 
roll. 
For example, if a magician casts a Sleep spell on a Player-
knight, the Gamemaster rolls a1d20 and compares the result to 
the victim’s resistance (if any); usually an attribute, trait, or pas-
sion. For a Sleep spell, the Gamemaster might allow the victim 
to roll against Energetic or CON as resistance.
Examples of magic rituals that can be found in the epics 
include curing illness or wounds, understanding animal speech, 
opening locks, long distance travel, invisibility, casting curses, 
creating food and drink, finding objects or thieves, restoring sex-
ual potency, inducing love or hate, conjuring demons, creating 
mists or fogs, and finding plants.
==================== BOOK PAGE 166 ====================
165
They charge through the gate, eager to fight.
Then a mist begins to fall from the sky
so that none of the men can see to ride.
The Franks have no way to defend their lives
while the enemies attack the tents with fierce cries.
They lead off the horses and kill the squires,
they carry off the food from the kitchen fires
and kill the chief steward before they retire.
— The Coronation of Louis , LVII
Conjuring Demons
A frequently-used alternative to casting spells is to conjure 
a demon, which is bound to the magician’s will and commanded 
to produce magic for them. Conjuring demons is generally seen 
as black magic, though Christian wizards and good faeries are 
known to practice this type of magic, showing that it is possible 
to harness evil in the service of good.
Demons can produce any sort of magical effect, but to 
command them a wizard needs a few things: he must perform a 
specific ritual, call the demon by his exact name (like Astaroth, 
Beliel, Draginazz or Marax) and, finally, bind it to his will. 
Conjuring demons from hell is not without risks, because if 
the last step fails, the demon attacks his would-be master. For 
more information on demons, see Chapter 18: Opponents and 
Creatures.
Atlantes conjured demons to build his invisible fortress: 
Hell’s demons by enchantment built
The whole thing in a single day.
— Orlando Innamorato , 2.3.XXVII
Enchanted Items
Items possessing magical qualities are called enchanted. For 
some specific examples, see Chapter 12: W ealth and T reasure.
Faerie Magic
The third sort of magic is a more natural one, by which such 
otherworldly creatures as faeries exist. While the Church claims 
all faeries to be evil, some of them are rather helpful and benign 
spirits, though unpredictable.
Faeries
Most faeries (like Morgan, Falerina or Alcina) have a few 
things in common: they are beautiful, lustful, deceitful, vengeful 
and selfish. These harmful faeries try to take advantage of a good 
knight’s passions and the codes of honor and chivalry. One of 
the prime objectives of these wicked fays is to capture knights 
by providing a pleasant prison of abundance and lust. Although 
these faeries are not exactly evil, they nevertheless represent the 
corrupting force of wealth that tempts all noble heroes. By prey-
ing on character flaws (like a knight’s Indulgent, Lustful, Lazy or 
Selfish traits), these enchantresses of oblivion lure their victims 
into their lairs, where they may indulge in games, food, clothing, 
music, dancing, singing, hawking, hunting and seduction. As fa-
eries frequently turn the codes of Honor and Chivalry against 
knights, the use of magic is allowed in fighting them without 
losing Honor. Faeries themselves do not need special items or 
spell books, since magic comes naturally to them. 
Strictly speaking, a lady like Angelica is not really a faerie, 
but rather an enchanted princess, with such supreme beauty 
(APP 30) that it induces extreme Love passions in the hearts of 
many knights, with the same effect as some faerie enchantments.
Not all faeries are harmful. Some bless heroes at their birth, 
as happens with Ogier. Some act like guardian angels, such as 
the black and white faeries protecting the brothers Aquilant and 
Grifo. Lady Logistilla and King Oberon (see Chapter 16: Non-
player Characters) are kind and helpful, although their aid rarely 
comes for free.
Faerie Castles
Magical sites exist which do not derive their power from 
God. The most frequent sort of magic places are the hidden fa -
erie realms and enchanted castles that Player-knights occasion -
ally find. They are usually encountered in inaccessible spots like 
high mountain tops, isolated forests (deep in the Ardennes or 
Broceliande), far-away isles, or at the bottom of lakes or rivers. 
These mysterious enchanted realms are not only inhabited by 
faerie ladies, they are often the homes of unusual monsters and 
other supernatural phenomena — like an accelerated time or the 
absence of shadows.
Magic for Player-knights
God favors the brave!
T
he Franks fight with God on their side. Even though they 
might not possess the spiritual purity of monks or priests, 
God assists his Frankish champions every now and again. 
Indeed, Player-knights may benefit from divine help in five dif -
ferent situations: (1) praying or being prayed for, (2) in a judicial 
combat, (3) when converting a pagan adversary, (4) by receiving 
dreams or omens, and (5) when foreign ladies fall in love with 
the knight. Each of these will be briefly discussed and their spe -
cific rules and mechanisms explained.
Prayers and Miracles
Player-knights may pray to God to ask for a specific thing 
to happen, such as healing a child, blessing a marriage, or victory 
in battle. When praying, a player should clearly state what his 
character is asking from God. Obviously, casual or selfish prayers 
should not be granted!
In P aladin , Charlemagne is God’s representative on earth. 
God only helps Charlemagne and those who are loyal to him. 
Charlemagne’s pagan or Christian enemies, all enchanters (even 
Christian ones), excommunicated knights, magic users, or 
knights living in defiance of Charlemagne’s power can’t benefit 
from prayers.
Magic for Player-knights
==================== BOOK PAGE 167 ====================
Chapter Nine: Magic
166
For a knight to receive divine aid by prayer, the player must 
roll Love [Charlemagne]. This passion, and not Love [God], in-
dicates a knight’s base chance that his prayers will be heard.
Other characters may pray for a Player-knight. At the 
Gamemaster’s discretion, Player-knights may be the passive ben-
eficiary of an NPC’s prayer. T o benefit from their prayers, a play-
er must roll Love [God]. If successful, divine interventions take 
the form of bonuses to die rolls or other forms of aid, just like 
when Player-knights themselves pray for help.
In short: Love [Charlemagne] allows a knight to call on 
God himself. Love [God] allows a knight to benefit from the 
prayers of others.
Knights even have a special short prayer they recite in combat 
or in other moments of extreme danger. This is the so-called 
“Prayer of Mortal Peril”: 
“ As this is true, God in majesty great,
defend my body, that I may escape
death at the hand of this giant, evil incarnate.
He is so huge, so hideous. So, in haste,
holy Mary, please come to my aid,
let me not be the cause of a dreadful disgrace
through cowardice on all of my race. ” 
He stood up and made the cross in his face.
— The Coronation of Louis , XXII
Whatever the nature of the prayer, the following modifiers 
always apply:

Groups: If necessary, a Gamemaster must decide how many 
of the persons present at a ceremony are sincerely participating. 
Peasants praying for a disliked lord, for example, most likely only 
participate if they succeed their standing roll.
