Naturally, divine aid from prayers can take on many oth -
er forms. A normal success may grant an exceptional bonus to 
certain die rolls, or even directly to one or more statistics or 
game-related scores. Indeed, wondrous magic can do wonderful 
things: 
Huon was lying on the field, severely injured and in a 
pitiable state. He almost fainted, but the power of God 
in Heaven and the words the abbot spoke gave him back 
his bodily vigor. Huon no longer felt injuries nor pain. 
— Huon of Bordeaux , XVII
A Gamemaster could take example on Huon of Bordeaux  
and rule that a Player-knight, benefiting from an abbot’s prayers 
after a successful Love [God] roll, no longer suffers from the 
adverse effects due to lost Hit Points (normal success), or even 
regains his full Hit Points (critical).
A critical success provokes a genuine miracle: wounds in -
stantly heal, the enemy’s fortification collapses or catches fire, 
etc. The exact nature of miracles is left up to the Gamemaster. 
For example, celestial knights may appear and fight along the 
Frankish side: 
==================== BOOK PAGE 168 ====================
167
Astride their stallions, three white knights come
Down from the ridge of lofty Aspremont
And one by one pass each battalion; 
They do not speak and are addressed by none; 
They ride in haste until they reach the front.
— Song of Aspremont , IX
Judicial Combats and Ordeals
In a trial by combat God helps justice to prevail: 
Huon advances to take the sword, because he is convinced 
his opponent surrenders. The noble young sir stretches 
out his hand, but the traitor lashes out and strikes him 
on the arm with such force that nigh three hundred rings 
of his mail burst: his arm would have been cut off, but 
God protected his champion.
— Huon of Bordeaux , XVII
At some point in your campaign, a Player-knight is likely 
to fight a trial by combat. Whether he acts as the champion for 
his lord or to defend the honor of a lady, the knight must defeat 
an opponent in a duel to prove who has God on their side. God 
protects the Just; therefore, if the Player-knight is defending the 
right (as determined by the Gamemaster), he may roll Just; on a 
success, add half the value of the roll to his armor (add his entire 
Just score on a critical). This bonus is cumulative with any oth -
ers, and Inspiration may still be attempted.
Example of a Trial by Combat: Sir W aldemar fights to re-
fute the slanderous accusation that his sister committed adultery. 
Sir W aldemar rolls against his Just score. He gets a successful 
roll of 7. Assuming his sister is actually innocent (which only the 
Gamemaster knows for sure), Sir W aldemar’s armor score may be 
raised by 4 points (3½ rounded up) for the duration of the judicial 
combat. Whatever the outcome of the Just roll, he may also roll his 
Love [ family] for Inspiration. 
The same rules may be applied for Player-knights submit -
ting to an Ordeal (See T rial by Ordeal on page 222). Success at 
Just during a trial of Boiling W ater or Hot Iron means that the 
character automatically passes the V alorous roll, and gains twice 
the benefit of healing. In a Proof of the Cross, the character’s 
STR roll is doubled to determine how long the character held 
up the weights. 
Converting Pagans
The Frankish people are second to none in strength; they 
conquer through God’s love, and they prevail through 
faith. 
— Ermold the Black , III
Instead of fighting a pagan opponent, a Player-knight may 
try to convince him to convert to Christianity and become a 
friend rather than an enemy. 
Three steps or rolls are needed for the conversion of a 
pagan knight: Eloquence, Religion and opposed Honor. The 
knight may try to Inspire himself with Love [God], but this is 
not obligatory.
1. The Player-knight must be in a position to talk. In 
order to engage his opponent in a verbal duel, the 
knight must be able to make himself understood. 
This means that for most pagans, apart from the 
Saxons and the Danes, a successful Languages roll 
is required. Once communication is established, the 
Player-knight must issue his challenge by rolling 
against his Eloquence. If successful, go to step 2. On 
a critical success, the knight gains +5 to any further 
rolls.
2. T o convince his opponent to consider converting, 
a Player-knight must expound arguments which 
demonstrate how the Christian faith is superior to all 
others, and to his opponent’s worship of pagan deities 
in particular. Roll Religion. If successful, go to step 3, 
with a critical success again adding +5 to the follow -
ing roll.
Duke Naymo describes the T rue Faith in an attempt to con-
vert a Saracen knight: 
“When our Lord God came down out of the skies
For our salvation Himself he so despised
That in the Virgin’s womb did he reside
And took on the flesh and blood of humankind
Until the time that He was born a child; 
Three years and thirty He lived His earthly life; 
By John in Jordan Lord Jesus was baptized,
That by baptism man may have new life
And live as blessed eternally with Christ: 
The Jews took Jesus, I’ll not tell you a lie; 
Upon the Cross He suffered pain and spite; 
They pierced His heart with a great cutting pike; 
And His last breath that left Him as He die
Drew down to Hell straightaway, and once inside
Restored the Faithful whom the Flood had denied: 
Abraham, Noah, Jacob, Joseph — all alive!
On the third day He rose as God on high; 
These things are as they are, and thus transpired; 
The Truth of them consider now a while,
For by His death was Hell sealed for all time; 
The Devil, ever since, losing his might. ” 
— Song of Aspremont , I
3. Once the Player-knight has caught his enemy’s atten-
tion (step 1) and expressed his religious appeal (step 
2), he must invite his opponent to join the True Faith. 
The two make an opposed Honor roll.
Magic for Player-knights
==================== BOOK PAGE 169 ====================
Chapter Nine: Magic
168
If they want to preserve their Honor, Saracens willing to con-
vert need a good occasion to do so: 
“If God promotes the plans that I have made,
In Him I’ll trust and Christian law obey; 
But for the length of this ferocious fray,
While in the battle, I cannot change my faith. ” 
— Song of Aspremont , III
For the conversion attempt to be successful, both the knight 
and his pagan opponent must succeed at their Honor roll. If the 
knight wins the contest the pagan surrenders and agrees to be 
converted. The convert automatically acquires scores in Love 
[God] and Love [Charlemagne] equal to the Frankish knight’s 
successful Honor roll. At his baptism, his Christian godfather 
must choose a new name for him. T raditionally, the convert is 
then married to a Christian lady.
If the pagan is victorious with a partial success for the 
Player-knight, he declines the offer to convert, but recognizes 
his opponent as a worthy and respected comrade. If the Player-
knight fails the roll, the pagan sneers and insults him. If the 
Player-knight fumbles his Honor, the pagan instantly develops a 
Hatred against him equal to his own Honor score.
If the pagan fails his Honor roll, he insults the knight and 
challenges him to a fight (or continues the fight already in prog-
ress). If he fumbles the roll, he pretends to be willing to be bap -
tized, but will break this promise at the first occasion and attack 
or betray the knight.
Honorable converted pagans completely renounce their 
former beliefs and usually become excellent Christians. They 
may immediately benefit from prayers and other forms of di -
vine aid.
The miraculous conversion of Otuel, a valiant pagan knight: 
That instant a dove, white as snow, passed before Ro -
land’s eyes and those of the entire army. It was the Holy 
Ghost in the shape of a dove. The bird spoke tender words 
at Otuel: “Go to Roland and do as he tells you. ” Otuel 
replied: “I don’t know what my eyes behold, but my spirit 
and senses are transformed; what I loathed before now 
seems true to me. I will abandon my sword of my own free 
will and not out of fear. I shall no longer fight you, and for 
your sake shall I rally Charlemagne and his companions, 
even though I never imagined doing such a thing. The 
gods I worshiped now seem all worthless, as are those who 
serve them. From now on shall I call on Virgin Mary with 
all my heart, mother of Christ, to help and assist me.
Roland heard these words and replied, smilingly: “ Are 
these strong resolutions? Do they come from your heart?” 
Otuel replied: “Yes, it is nothing but the truth. ” Then they 
embraced. 
— Karlamagnus Saga , 6.VI

Dreams and Omens
The day turns into night: 
The sky becomes black and cloudy.
Gods sends a sign to warn both sides: 
The sky opens up and flames fall down,
Burning both the battle standards,
The one of Girart, the one of Charles Martel.
The most valiant warriors tremble with fear,
As they feel the earth shake under their feet.
“This is the end of the world, ” they say.
Count Girart is petrified, just as Charles.
The two armies separate and retire; 
All fighting ceases abruptly.
— Girart of Roussillon , CLXXII
==================== BOOK PAGE 170 ====================
169
Whenever God wishes to communicate with his champi -
ons, he sends an angel, a prophetic dream, or some other sort 
of sign. It could be a command, a warning, or some useful piece 
of information. A dream may reveal something hidden — like 
an opponent’s weakness, the hiding place of a treasure, a key, or 
an entrance to a faerie castle. Dreams and omens can be a subtle 
way for the Gamemaster to guide the players and give them a bit 
of advice or knowledge about future events, or they can simply 
whet the player’s appetite about upcoming adventures.
T o benefit from divine messages, a character first needs to 
succeed with a Love [God] roll to receive it. A critical success 
gives him +5 to the subsequent Religion roll. 
In most cases the message is obscure and ambiguous, so it 
needs to be interpreted carefully, preferably by a man of learning 
like a priest or a bishop. Hints about the correct meaning of the 
omen are only given to players if they or their advisor succeed 
with a Religion roll.
The miracle of the sprouting lances: 
And then, some of the Christians were preparing their 
arms, the night before the battle, and they fixed their 
lances straight up in the ground, in the meadow by the 
bank of the river. And the following morning they found 
them with branches grown on them, and having bark 
and roots, namely, the lances of those who were about to 
receive the palm of victory and martyrdom for the faith 
of Christ, in the first line of battle. 
— Red Book of Hergest, ‘  
 History of Charlemagne , X
Note that the practice of oneiromancy (the interpretation 
of dreams) is officially forbidden for people outside the Church; 
but it is still widely practiced by hermits, witches, and astrologers 
at foreign courts. Apart from the obvious problems of interpreta-
tion, the Church’s interdict is based on the knowledge that many 
false prophets use fake omens, visions and prophecies to rally the 
credulous and uneducated to their sinful causes. Knights should 
beware of dead people speaking to them in dreams, for demons 
and sorcerers send false dreams and prophecies to trick heroes.
Charlemagne receives prophetic dreams: 
He dreamed he was at the main pass of Cize; 
In his hands he was holding his lance of ash.
Count Ganelon seized it from his grasp; 
He broke it and brandished it with such violence
That the splinters flew up to the sky.
Charlemagne sleeps on without being roused.
After this dream he had another vision: 
That he was in Frankland in his chapel at Aachen; 
In his right hand he is bitten by a vicious boar.
From the direction of the Ardennes he saw a leopard 
coming; 
It attacks his body with great ferocity.
From within the hall a hunting-dog came down,
Bounding and leaping towards Charlemagne.
It tore the right ear of the first boar; 
Angrily it wrestles with the leopard.
The Franks say there is a mighty battle; 
They do not know which of them will win it.
Charles sleeps on without being roused.
— Song of Roland , LVI–LVII
Example of an Omen: The Saxons will soon raid a Player-
knight’s poorly-defended manor. In order to give the knight a 
chance to prepare himself, the Gamemaster asks the player to roll 
Love [God]. On a success, the knight wakes up to discover a gloomy 
and foreshadowing event: a wily fox has eaten all the ducks and 
chickens in the hen house, and all their eggs have turned into stones. 
Only the rooster survived, but it is bleeding badly and has lost most 
of its feathers.
If the player wonders about the meaning of the strange omen, 
he should ask his chaplain or bishop, or roll Religion himself. On a 
successful roll, the Gamemaster should give a clarifying hint, like 
saying: “Y our watch dog is old and deaf, and your rickety fence was 
obviously not enough to keep the fox out. Now, what do you think 
would happen if your manor was to suffer an attack?”
Winning the Heart  
of Pagan Ladies
Duke Naymo captures a Breton princess, who is worried 
about what the Frank may do to her.
“Lady, you are taken, he cries
I will take you to the king of the Franks
Where you will receive Christian baptism. ” 
“I cannot resist you, she laments,
But I am a queen, King Aquin’s wife,
So please save me from dishonor!”
“Have no fear, ” replies the duke.
— Aquin of Brittany , v.2924–2940
Noble pagan ladies often fall in love with Frankish heroes. 
The theme of falling in love is as present as military prowess. 
In the literature, the love of pagan women for Christian heroes 
often leads to their baptism and marriage, which in turn offsets 
and complements the slaughter of pagan armies.
A Saracen lady lusting after a Frankish knight: 
“Mahomet, lord, if your great power could ever
Bring him and me much closer both together,
Say side by side in a good bed well nestled,
In pleasure’s terms it would be worth an empire!”
— Song of Aspremont , III

Chapter Nine: Magic
170
An enamored pagan lady will gladly help her Frankish lover 
and his friends, and generates an Amor [knight] passion (see be-
low). This includes helping them to escape from her father’s pris-
on, betraying a pagan fortification to the Franks, or fleeing from 
home taking a relic or a treasure with her. In return, the lady 
expects to be baptized and married to her lover as soon as possi-
ble. Should a Frankish lover betray her and not comply with her 
demands afterwards, the lady generates a Hate [knight] equal 
to her previous Amor score, and the Player-knight immediately 
loses 5 points of Honor and Love [God]. See the adventure The 
Pagan Lady (page 419) for an example of such a disgraceful ploy.
There are two possible ways for a pagan lady to fall in love 
with a knight, depending on the knight’s action (or inaction): 
passive infatuation and deliberate amor.
Passive Infatuation
The Saracen lady Orable longs for “her” William: 
“No sea, nor gate, nor lock shall prevent
my love to go to William; 
My passion devours me!
He is so valiant, courteous and wise.
No squire ever accomplished glorious deeds
like him. He made Aquilant his messenger
and sent me a trained falcon. ” 
— William’s Youth , XLIV
For a virtuous pagan lady to spontaneous -
ly fall madly in love with a Player-knight, the 
Gamemaster may roll the knight’s APP against 
the lady’s Chaste. If the knight wins, the lady 
is smitten. The lady develops an Amor for the 
knight (see Amor, page 76 ), who may not know 
that this has happened until she shows up at his 
cell door pledging her love for him.
See the adventure The Pagan Prison  (page 
420) for Passive Infatuation in action. 
Deliberate Amor
For the love of Sir Guy of Burgundy, a young 
Saracen princess helps the paladins escape her 
father’s prison: 
“Be not ye the Duke of Burgoyne, Sir Gye,
Nevewe unto the Kinge, Charles so fre?”
“Noe, certes, lady, it is not I; 
It is yondir knight, that ye may see. ” 
“ A, him I have loved many a day
And yet knowe I him noght
For his love I do alle that maye
To chere you with dede and thought.
For his love wille I christende be
And lefe Mohoundes laye.
Spekith to him nowe for me,
As I you truste maye.
And but he wole graunte me his love,
Of you askape shalle none here.
By him that is almyghty above,
Ye shalle abye it ellis full dere. ” 
Tho wente Duke Neymes to Sir Gye
And saide, “This layde loveth the; 
For thy love she maketh us alle merye
And baptizede wole she be.
Ye shalle hir take to your wedded wife,
For alle us she may save. ” 
— The Sultan of Babylon

It is possible for knight to actively court a pagan noblewom-
an, but the lady may offer a fiercer resistance. For a knight to suc-
cessfully seduce a desired damsel, the player’s APP roll must beat 
the lady’s Honor. T ypical Honor scores for noble ladies range 
from 10 (a lady-in-waiting), to 15 (a noble lady) to even as high 
as 20 (a genuine princess). If successful, the knight and the lady 
create Amor passions for each other (see Amor, page 76).

==================== BOOK PAGE 174 ====================
173
W
inter is the time for rest, recuperation, and character 
growth. During this time, knights engage in training, 
amorous pursuits, and gossip. In game terms, the play -
ers perform character updating.
Although most military activity stops for the winter, peo -
ple still are active. They gather for feasts and holy days. These 
meetings are of major importance. A lord often calls his vassals 
to feast at his castle, or travels through the snow to visit each of 
them at their own holdings.
Most of the wintertime is spent in social, non-scenario action. 
Gamemasters may wish to run scenarios that take place during the 
winter, and if so, will have to rule as to whether or not the normal 
opportunities for training are available during that same year, or 
are lost due to the characters being in action instead.
The Winter Phase is formalized into these 10 steps, which 
should be handled in the order listed: 
1. Solo Scenario (if applicable)
2. Aging (if applicable)
3. Economic Circumstances
4. Survival
5. Personal Event
6. Family
7. Experience (if applicable)
8. Training and Practice
9. Compute Glory
10. Glory Bonus
1. Solo Scenario
Y
our character may benefit from one or more solo adven -
tures (see Chapter 19: Adventures ). This is particularly 
important if you were absent for a play session and your 
knight missed the last game year of play. These are brief, one-on-
one sessions with the Gamemaster to provide missed experience 
or to catch up with the storyline. Check with the Gamemaster.
Chapter Ten:  
The Winter Phase
The Courtly Year
Gamemasters may wish to use the following structure to 
shape their campaign, and give plenty of opportunity to use 
courtly as well as combat skills. 
Easter Feast/Spring Court: As the flowers bloom and 
the crops peep forth, knights report to their lord’s court to see 
how everyone fared over the winter, and to see what duties 
they have for the upcoming season. 
King’s Day: Every Pentecost (50 days after Easter 
Sunday) King Charlemagne holds a great court, where he de -
livers justice to all (theoretically). Charlemagne expects the 
paladins to report their deeds of the last year. Many people 
flock to court to witness this. It’s a natural time to visit and 
hobnob with the great lords and knights of the king’s court.
Field of May: A grand court and assembly held for several 
weeks in early May. Charlemagne calls on some of his Counts to 
provide knights for the army, and announces the campaign sea-
son. Justice is meted out, laws are proclaimed, and every man 
who does not answer the summons is fined his heribannum. 
Summer Adventure Season: Summer sees the knights 
performing their main duties. If not called for the army at 
the May Field, Player-knights may perform normal duties for 
their lord, but they are also likely to go adventuring. They may 
visit the courts of other lords, perhaps even foreign lords. 
Harvest time: Knights retire to their lord’s castle, or if 
vassal knights, to their own manors to oversee the harvest. 
This is the time to calculate manor income. They then retire 
for the cold season with their families.
Christmas Feast and Winter Phase: At midwinter 
knights visit their lord’s castle for festivities. Bachelor knights 
receive their annual stipend. Visitors are likely to be there. It is 
opportunity to socialize with the lord, his family and the court.
Players wishing to perform a solo other than Y our Manor, 
V assal Service, Romance, or Madness must succeed with a 
Standing [lord] roll to get permission. The player is then free to 
choose a solo of his liking. Please consider that the list isn’t ex -
haustive, and other solos are quite possible on a player’s request. 
The Gamemaster judges whatever rolls and checks are appropri-
ate and describes the outcome of the solo.
==================== BOOK PAGE 175 ====================
Chapter Ten: The Winter Phase
174
2. Aging
I
ncrease the character’s age by one year at this time. All ag-
ing is done during the Winter Phase, regardless of actual 
birth date. Y our primary squire ages at this point, and is 
replaced with a new 14-year-old squire if he reaches age 18.
Aging eventually takes a character out of play, usually 
around age 40 or later. When any statistic reaches a value of 3 or 
less, even APP, the character is bedridden and may no longer par-
ticipate in active play. A bedridden character no longer receives 
Glory unless circumstances are truly unusual, but continues to 
age. He may still give orders (which will probably be ignored), 
write a will, tell tales of his adventures, retire to a monastery, or 
take other actions that the Gamemaster permits. When any at -
tribute reaches 0, the character dies.
Aging Procedure
Every character of 30 years of age or more must roll on the 
Aging table every Winter Phase. The result of aging may be a 
random reduction in one or more of the character’s statistics. For 
example, a character might lose a point of STR and a point of 
CON, or three points of SIZ, or nothing, in a given year.
T o determine aging, roll 1d20 and consult T able 10–1: 
Aging to find the number of attributes affected by aging this 
year. This is given in terms of the number of rolls on T able 10–2: 
Attributes Lost. For example, if the Aging table roll is a 4, this 
calls for 3 rolls on the Attributes Lost table.
A character may die as a result of a fatal Attributes Lost roll 
if the result brings any attribute to 0.
t able  10–1: a giNg  
1d20 Result Attributes Affected 
1 5
2–3 4
4–6 3
7–10 2
11–15 1
16–20 None
t able  10–2: a ttributes  l ost

Besides aging, T able 10–2 is used when a character receives 
a major or mortal wound, suffers Shock from a failed passion 
result, or perhaps suffers from some magical effect.
When characters lose attribute points from the aging tables, 
whether as a result of aging or from some other source, various 
natural forces such as disease or inimical magic are often blamed. 
Thus, for example, characters who suffer a loss of SIZ or STR 
may be said to have been “withered by the Evil Eye” or “afflicted 
with elf stroke; ” loss of APP is commonly ascribed to the “pox.” 
A loss of DEX is commonly referred to as “the palsy, ” while a loss 
of CON is likely attributed to “consumption.” 
3. Economic Circumstances
A
sk the Gamemaster if anything important has hap -
pened to your character’s lands and economic needs, 
keeping notes as needed. Raids, unscheduled visits by 
a lord, or faerie curses might reduce a knight’s economic cir -
cumstances, forcing a reduction in his grade of maintenance, 
while a very good harvest might let a knight increase his grade 
of maintenance.
Fief management
If a character owns land, he must determine his income for 
the year. A standard manor provides an average of £6 per year 
(and an equal amount of Glory). Normal expenses are as fol -
lows: £2 for the knight and his squire, £2 for the family and £2 
for horses. An unmarried landed knight spends only £4 plus a 
probable £1 to hire a steward, which leaves him with an average 
annual £1 profit per year.
Accumulated wealth, called treasure, is usually in the form 
of surplus goods which can be bartered, such as clothing, fur -
niture, or jewelry. Since money is not heroic, the P aladin  fief 
management rules are not meant to make Player-knights rich. 
The goal is to spend money in order to get Glory through con -
spicuous consumption (1 Glory per £ spent). Indeed, savings 
are rare. Lords are supposed to give their wealth to those who 
depend on him and whose loyalty he needs to assure: his vassals 
and followers.
Not all years are average, and harvests and income may vary 
from year to year. The exact economic situation of a manor may 
be determined by a modified Stewardship roll:

Maintenance
Every character must determine his situation as regards 
food, money, clothing, and shelter, expressed as overall grade of 
Maintenance (e.g. Impoverished, Ordinary, Rich). This is how 
much money a knight spends on clothes, food, armor, horses, and 
comforts for himself, his squire, and his family. Independently 
of this, damaged weapons and armor should be replaced or re -
paired as best they can be. Y our grade of Maintenance influences 
many other steps during the Winter Phase.
Gifts given by the lord to the knight should be determined 
now, and any plunder or other valuable items gained during 
the last year evaluated for worth. This may be enough that the 
knight may increase his grade of maintenance. Buying and sell -
ing may have to be done in order to convert goods into cash: see 
Chapter 12: W ealth and T reasure.
Whenever a knight’s treasury allows him to raise his 
Maintenance level, but the player doesn’t want to, he should roll 
T emperate. On a failure, he must roll Indulgent. If he succeeds, he 
must increase his Maintenance. On the other hand, if a knight with 
sufficient money wishes to lower his Maintenance level, he must 
first fail an Indulgent and then succeed with a T emperate roll.
Economic Effects
Knights incur various modifiers to rolls on subsequent ta -
bles in this segment of the Winter Phase (as always, it’s best to 
keep track of these modifiers on a piece of scrap paper). 
Grades of Maintenance
Impoverished Knight (less than £3): This is a disas -
trous economic state to be in. No knight should suffer such 
a state if any alternative is available and consistent with the 
dictates of loyalty and honor.
The knight’s armor suffers a permanent 1-point loss of 
protection value every winter due to severe rust and neglect. 
Finally, he must make a CON roll or lose 1 point of CON, as 
if through aging, due to the hardships of having insufficient 
shelter.
However, even with all this to suffer, the knight loses 
no Glory, nor do his traits and passions change. The state of 
a knight’s reputation is independent of such petty issues as 
money.
Poor Knight (£3–£5): This grade of maintenance is un-
fortunate but not disastrous. Many knights occasionally suf -
fer this state for a year or two during their careers. Household 
knights have little control over their state of maintenance, and 
may be kept at this level if they are greatly disfavored by their 
lord or if their lord has himself become poor.
Ordinary knight (£6–£8): Household knights are al -
most always maintained at this level, unless they are in ex -
treme favor or disfavor with their lord, or their lord is poor 
himself. In general, vassal knights (most Player-knights) are 
capable only of maintaining themselves at this grade, unless 
they have gained wealth from plunder or ransoms.
Rich Knight (£9–£12): Lords are usually expected to 
maintain themselves at this grade. They normally have the re-
sources to do so unless disaster or war strikes their lands. In 
rare instances, household knights whose lords are exception -
ally wealthy may be kept at this grade.
Superlative knight (£13 or more): Only the richest 
knights and greatest lords can maintain themselves at this 
grade.

4. Survival
E
ach year, a player must roll on T able 10–7 to check if any 
of his relatives, members of his retinue, or children die. 
He must also roll for any special mounts, including 
extraordinary horses, coursers, destriers, and monsters like hip -
pogriffs (he must check all his mounts if he is a knight errant 
with no lord or manor herd to replace his losses). His lord or 
his manor herd will automatically replace chargers, rouncies and 
sumpters.

5. Personal Event
F
or players who do not perform a solo adventure, random 
events are a quick and fun way to spice up the Winter 
Phase and test the Player-knight’s personality.
Players roll for a random event on the tables below. This 
offers the possibility to introduce uncommon events into the 
Player-knight’s life. The indicated result should be interpreted 
and/or transformed into a small story. Describe and vary them as 
you like. Liven things up, instead of simply reading out the table 
result. The Gamemaster may use these minor events as a hook 
for his next scenario.
6. Family
Y
our character’s family already exists, but children are desir-
able, especially for a vassal knight. The ideal way to gain 
children is through marriage. Once married, legitimate 
children are possible. The wife’s Glory and dowry are also useful.
If your character is unmarried and wishes to remain so this 
winter, you may skip the marriage and childbirth portions of 
this step (if you wish to roll on the Childbirth table but not the 
Marriage table, you may do that as well).
Marriage
When a character wishes to marry, the player should inform 
the Gamemaster, who decides whether this honor is possible for 
the character. A marriage may require some game play to find 
the wife, or T able 10–10: Random Marriage for V assal Knights 
can be used. 
Most marriages are one of two types: below your class and 
within your class. T o marry above your class, into a count’s or a 
duke’s holding, requires game play, not random die rolling.
Marriage Below Character’s Class: Use this course of ac-
tion whenever your character wants to have a wife, no matter 
what or who. She is probably a handmaid of your lord’s wife, a 
serving woman, an assistant seamstress to some rich merchant 
woman, or the younger sister of a knight. Household knights 
usually marry this way.
The Gamemaster should almost always permit this form of 
marriage, unless the character has been absent from home for 
years, has been shamed, or if marriage would not fit in well with 
the scenarios he intends to run.
The procedure is simple. Get permission from the charac -
ter’s lord with a roll of Standing [lord]. If successful, the lord 
grants the boon to wed. If your knight has not already chosen 
an available woman, the lord selects an appropriate match. Once 
successful, the character is married to an ordinary woman who 
has a dowry of £1d6 and 10 Glory.
If the standing roll fails then the lord refuses permission to 
wed. The knight may ask again next winter. A fumble might in-
dicate that the lord was offended somehow, or that something 
humorous happened, but a fumble should not indicate marriage 
to an unsuitable woman.
Marriage Within Character’s Class: For a vassal knight or 
higher noble it may take time to find a likely candidate for mar-
riage. The Gamemaster should personalize potential wives who 
hold great dowries and have great Glory.
T o find an ordinary wife within the character’s class, roll his 
Courtesy each winter. If successful, you may either roll on the 
Random Marriage table below, or wait a year. If you choose to put 
it off, just note “met candidate for marriage, still waiting” in your 
character’s history. Y ou can wait for years if you want, if there is no

be in love. Raise your Chaste score by 1 point.
Success: Despite their best efforts, you do not fall prey to the wiles of the serving wenches. Check Chaste.
Failure: Unknown to anyone, you conceived a bastard child. Roll on the Father Class table to know the rank of the lady. For 
her age, roll 1d20+14. The child will be born next year.
Fumble: Y ou got caught in flagrante delicto with a damsel (roll class and age as per failure) and must either marry her, pay 
compensation to her father (equal to a sixth of his heribannum), or lose 1 point of Honor.
2: Energetic Critical: Y our zealous commitment to fulfill your daily tasks is an example to others. Y ou earn two extra skill training points.
Success: Y our work hard all winter and earn an extra training point.
Failure: Instead of training your skills, you spend your time feasting and womanizing. Check Lazy.

Forgiving.

feud.

Failure: Y ou claimed too much Glory for yourself at court. Check Selfish.
Fumble: Y our disgraceful selfishness towards all those who depend on you for their livelihood costs you 1 point of Standings 
[retinue, Church, and commoners].
5: Honest Critical: Y ou refused to give a false testimony against one of your rivals. Gain one point in Honest.
Success: Y ou openly stated and maintained your opinion about the lord’s imprudent behavior with a serving wench during a 
recent feast. Check Honest.
Failure: Y ou were caught lying. A random standing is lowered by 1.
Fumble: Y our repetitive lies lowered your credibility. Y ou lose 1 point of Honor.
6: Just Critical: Y ou subtly confronted your bishop on one of his unlawful practices of taxing. Gain one point in Just.
Success: Y our lord intervened on your behalf in a legal dispute, but you refused his aid. Check Just.
Failure: Y ou earn £1 in bribes, but Standing [commoners] is lowered by 1.
Fumble: Y ou were publicly reprimanded by a bishop for your iniquitous judgment or counsel. Lose 1 point of Honor.
7: Merciful Critical: Someone falsely accused you of a crime. When you were proven innocent, you forgave the accuser and accepted his 
humble apologies. Gain one point in Merciful.
Success: A rival neighbor trespassed on your rights, but you showed yourself magnanimous and settled the dispute by a com-
mon accord. Check Merciful.
Failure: Y ou condemned a father for the crimes of his daughter and reduced the family to servitude. Check Cruel.
Fumble: Y ou refused to hear the pleas of the needy. Standing [Church] and Standing [commoners] are lowered by 1.
8: Modest Critical: Someone achieved success by “borrowing” one of your good ideas. When he boasted at court about “his” exploit, 
you congratulated him. Gain one point in Modest.
Success: Y ou allow your peers to sit closer to the lord’s table and agree to be served first. Check Modest.
Failure: Y ou hire the services of a minstrel to spread your renown. Pay £1 and gain double Glory for your deeds in the fol -
lowing year.
Fumble: Y our exuberant boasting inadvertently gravely offended another knight, who demands you pay him a reparation fee 
of £1.

9: Prudent Critical: Y ou carefully planned and prepared the winter provisions with the steward, which averted a local food shortage this 
season. Gain one point in Prudent.
Success: During a hunt with the lord, most companions tried to follow the master of the hunt by jumping a precipice, whereby 
some lost their steeds. Y ou didn’t, and earn a Prudent check.
Failure: Y ou took the risk to go out on a cold, wet and stormy night. Only a successful CON roll can avoid an Aging roll.

Failure: Y ou accuse someone of your lord’s court of a random rumor. Y our lord rolls Just/Arbitrary to see if he agrees with you 
(Arbitrary) or not ( Just); raise or lower his standing by 1, accordingly.
Fumble: Y our wild accusations anger your lord. His standing is lowered by 1 point.
12: V alorous Critical: Y ou single-handedly saved the life of your lord when he was attacked by a wild boar during a hunt. Gain a point of 
V alorous and 50 Glory.
Success: Y ou rescued somebody of your lord’s household from a fire. Check V alorous and gain 10 Glory. 
Failure: During a nightly raid into Saxony, you pretended to be ill and stayed with the squires. Check Cowardly.
Fumble: Y ou disgraced yourself by running away from a wolf. Y ou lose one point of Honor.
13: Love 
[Charlemagne] 
Critical: God honors your dedication to Charlemagne with a small miracle: you may reroll any one die roll during your next 
adventure.
Success: An anonymous missus dominicus overheard you when you praised your sovereign. Y ou gain a check in Love 
[Charlemagne].
Failure: When some drunken knights at your table made doubtful, even dishonorable, jokes about the king, instead of telling 
them off, you laughed (nothing else happens).
Fumble: Hearing about one of the king’s glorious feats, you openly doubted its truthfulness — and he heard about it. Y our 
Standing [Charlemagne] decreases by one point.
14: Honor Critical: If you are unmarried, your lord offers you a bride, whose dowry consists of two manors and £2d6. If you are already 
married, he gives you the best available horse for the phase. In any case, you gain a check in Honor.
Success: Heralds praise your honorable conduct. Y ou gain 20 Glory and a check in Honor.
Failure: Y ou are accused of a breach of hospitality. T o settle the affair, your lord orders you to organize a feast, which costs you £1.
Fumble: People speak ill of your disgraceful and untrustworthy behavior. Y ou lose 2 points in a random standing.
15: Love [family] Critical: Y ou fought a duel to defend the family honor. Suffer a 3d6 wound (without armor) and gain checks in V alorous, 
Love [family], and Standing [family].
Success: Y ou offered yourself as a guarantee for an accused family member. Gain a check in Standing [family].
Failure: Y ou publicly criticized or slandered a family member (roll on the Family Member table). Lower your Love [family] by 1.
Fumble: Y ou refused to come to the aid of a family member in distress (roll on the Family Member table to see who). Lower 
your Love [family] by 2.
16: Love [God] Critical: Y ou went on a pilgrimage to a local sacred place. It costs you £1, but you gain a point in both Love [God] and 
Standing [Church]. However, you have no more time for yourself and must skip Step 8: T raining and Practice.
Success: The chaplain cited your exemplary conduct as an example of piousness in one of his sermons. Check Love [God].
Failure: Y ou swore and cursed abundantly. Y our Standing[Church] is lowered by 1 point.
Fumble: Y our failed to regularly attend mass. Y our lack of faith is shocking. Y ou lose 1 point in Love [God].

Childbirth
Characters normally make a 1d20 roll on T able 10–11: 
Childbirth once per year whether they are married or not, 
whether rolling for a wife, concubine, lover, camp follower, or 
simply a some woman they have encountered during play within 
the previous year.
If the Gamemaster permits it, a childbirth roll may be at -
tempted per wife, lover, or concubine, if sufficient opportunity 
was fulfilled during play to possibly allow conception.
Table  10–11: Childbir Th  
1d20 Result Mother and Child Result
1–10 No birth.
11 Mother and child die at childbirth.
12 Mother dies in childbirth, but the child lives*.
13–19 A healthy child is born*.
20 T wins are born*.
* Roll 1d6 for each child born: odd number = female, even = male.
Any knight may keep non-wives, but they each require an 
upkeep of £1/2. per year, subtracted from the treasury before the 
childbirth roll is made. If payment can’t be made then the knight 
was unable to maintain the woman’s interest or health and she 
has departed; childbirth is not rolled for that character. Wives 
do not require extra payment; their costs are figured into the 
knight’s Maintenance.

Family Event
This section determines whether any important or unusu -
al events occur in your extended family. If the characters are 
members of a single clan, remember that one character’s mother 
might be another’s aunt or cousin.
Each character rolls once per winter on the Family Events 
table. Results marked “Family” apply equally to all members of 
the extended family, so players may benefit from several differ -
ent events each year.

7. Experience
C
haracters spend some of the winter reflecting over the year 
that has gone by. This process is simulated using an “experi-
ence roll.” Throughout game play during the game’s summer 
season, potential improvements are recorded as a check on a skill, pas-
sion, standing or trait value (see Gaining Experience in Chapter 6).
Each skill gets only one check per year, but any number of 
skills may be checked. The number of successes achieved during 
play is not relevant: only one check is needed, and more gain no 
bonus. However, all initial scores of 20 or more get an automatic 
free check each Winter Phase.
Roll 1d20 for each experience check. If the number rolled is equal 
or greater than the current value (or 20 for values over 20), then the 
character learned from experience and adds one point to that score.
Sometimes a roll result grants a “+1” or “–1” to a value (for 
example, some passion rolls). These are applied after rolling for 
experience. If you acquire two or more +1’s to the same statistic, 
they are all applied.
Retinue
Y our knight’s body squire and all your specialist personnel 
automatically receive checks for each of their listed skills each 
year. Roll for each skill to see if they gain from experience. 
8. Training and Practice
F
igure any training or practice done over the winter. This 
step includes the natural growth of young Player-knights, 
and deliberate changes to reputation and behavior affect-
ing traits, passions and standings. Every year, you may choose 
one option out of three: 
1. Change an Attribute, T rait, Passion or Standing: One 
score may be changed by one point, up or down, though in the 
case of traits this alters the value of the opposite trait as well. 
T raits, passions and standings cannot be increased over 15, and 
attributes cannot be raised over 20 by this method. Important age 
restrictions apply on attribute increase: SIZ may not be increased 
after age 21, and no attributes may be increased after age 30.
2. T rain Skills Up to 15: Y ou may raise four skills: one or-
dinary skill, one courtly skill, one combat skill, and one freely 
chosen skill, by 1 point. General skills with an initial value of 0 
cannot be increased, nor can any skill be brought over a value 
of 15. Y our freely-chosen skill may not be one of the other skills 
you’ve improved by this method this year. 
3. T rain a Skill Up to 20: Y ou may increase one skill with 
a value greater than 15 by one point, to a maximum value of 20.
9. Compute Glory
D
uring the winter, all Glory gained during the previous year’s 
play is computed. The total is added to the current number 
in the main “Glory” box on the character sheet. This stage 
is one of the high points of the game for many players, as they see 
their character’s fame and prestige increase through Glory.
Check with the Gamemaster to be sure you’ve recorded all 
the various Glory awards correctly.
Glory from Play: This is the primary source of Glory. If you 
participated in a scenario last game year, the Glory gained by your 
character should already be written in the “Glory this Game” box 
on the character sheet, and ideally recorded in detail on the back 
of the sheet with a date attached. For example, if you killed a bear 
while hunting, you might have recorded an award of 10 Glory.
Glory from Solo Scenarios: Y our character may have gone 
through a Solo Scenario. With Gamemaster approval, Glory 
may have been gained thereby. For example, the Y our Manor solo 
may have gained you special Glory equal to your Just trait.
Glory from One-time Honors: If any special honor or ambi-
tion was achieved last year, such as marriage to an heiress, this honor 
should have been recorded on the back of the character sheet.
Annual Glory: Many sources of Glory are annual. For ex -
ample, holding a manor gains 6 Glory per year, and being an ide-
al knight (Chivalrous, Romantic or Religious) gains 100 Glory. 
Passive Glory for statistics over 15 should be calculated. See the 
Passive Glory section in Chapter 4 for more information. 
10. Glory Bonus
T
he last thing done in the Winter Phase is to add any 
Glory bonus points to the character’s scores.
Bonus points are gained whenever a character’s Glory 
total passes a 1,000-point threshold (for example, a knight’s Glory 
might increase from 1,800 to 3,010 in an extraordinary year, yield-
ing two points). One or more bonus points become available and 
must be spent immediately to raise one score (attribute, trait, pas-
sion, standing, or skill) by one point. No restrictions apply to the 
increase. Refigure derived statistics now if you increase an attribute. 
If a character now qualifies for a Chivalrous, Pious or 
Romantic bonus, the bonus itself is gained immediately and may 
be used during the year’s scenarios, but you don’t get Glory for it 
until next year’s Winter Phase.

Chapter Eleven:  
Ambitions and Ideals
E
very player should start his or her first game as a squire, 
with the aim to advance from there. Ambitions are a se -
ries of “career options” open to player characters. They are 
more mundane then Ideals, for they correspond to social positions 
characters can strive to attain. The requirements given below ap-
ply to player characters only. Non-player characters may have dif-
ferent skills or personality and still qualify for a given job.
The first ambition of any player character is to become a 
knight. Once knighted, he can pursue his life in the various di -
rections described below.
Knighthood
K
nighthood is the unifying factor among members of the 
noble class in the Middle Ages. It sets a man apart from 
the common folk and defines the aristocracy of society. 
All knights, despite any personal differences between them, have 
more in common with each other than they have with the peas-
ant class. The richest knights hobnob with the poorest and ac -
knowledge each other as brothers of the military class.
Differences between knights do exist, though. Primarily 
this is measured by titles and Glory, but behavior, rank, and 
wealth also have a strong effect. Who would say that a humble 
household knight is equal in importance to Sir Roland, the pre-
eminent paladin of the land, Count of the Breton March, with 
hundreds of knights at his beck and call? 
As brothers in arms, knights may stand together on the bat-
tlefield or cross lances in a tournament, but if they reach a door-
way together, there is little argument to be made about who has 
the right to go first. 
Other differences between P aladin  knights are measured 
by several factors. First, the quality of a knight is determined to 
some extent by the amount of money spent in his yearly mainte-
nance (see Grades of Maintenance in Chapter 10). A knight may 
earn titles and gain honor and glory as befits his new rank.
Mercenary or Household Knight 
Knights without a lord are the lowest class of knights. 
They are called Knights Mercenary because they must seek to 
sustain themselves through work for money. Since knights are 
fighting men, they generally make their living by seeking mer -
cenary soldier employment, and differ from ordinary merce -
nary cavalry (sergeants) only in that they have taken the oath 
of knighthood.
Household knights derive their income directly from their 
lord, either through direct maintenance or by cash payments. 
They live in their lord’s manor or castle, not on their own land. 
They are his bodyguard and standing army, and travel wherever 
he takes them. Their loyalty is crucial to the lord’s success, per -
haps even to his survival, so they are usually treated well and re -
ceive great honor.
Household knights are also known as “bachelor” knights, 
from bas chevalier , or “low knight.” Bachelor has come to be 
associated with unmarried men because bachelor knights were 
generally not rich enough to support a wife.
A household knight may bear a pennocelle (a small pen -
nant) upon his lance to distinguish his rank from mercenary 
knights and sergeants, who wear no decoration.
Requirements
A character must have the following scores to qualify for 
knighthood: 
 n Traits: V alorous 13
 n Passions: Honor 10
 n Standings: Standing [lord] 10
 n General Skills: First Aid 10, Horsemanship 10,  
two courtly skills 10
 n Combat Skills: Battle 10, two melee weapon  
skills 13, Lance 10 (in Phase 1 and later)
Duties
A knight must serve and protect his lord in war and in peace.
Benefits
Standard of Living: A household knight is normally 
maintained at Ordinary level by his lord. This means he pro -
vides the knight with sufficient lodging, food and clothing, and 
==================== BOOK PAGE 185 ====================
Chapter Eleven: Ambitions and Ideals
184
equipment (a charger, armor and helmet, weapons and shield).
He pays for the repair of equipment, and will replace a horse 
should the knight lose it while serving him.
A knight mercenary is usually maintained at an Ordinary 
level — when he has an employer. However, he may wander as 
a knight errant — homeless and lordless, looking for adventure 
(or a job), living on the charity of others. He may become a Poor, 
or even Impoverished knight. 
Glory: When he gets dubbed, a knight earns a 1,000 Glory 
point bonus.
Retirement
A household knight needs the permission of his lord (roll 
standing) to retire as a hermit or a monk.
Companion
In the Ardennes Forest, two chivalrous knights meet: 
Raimbaud asked him what he was called. Hamon re -
plied: “I am someone you may not easily subdue with half 
a lance!” Raimbaud then said that he wouldn’t continue 
his way unless they meet each other in a joust to see who 
of them was most worthy and strongest. So Hamon asked 
Raimbaud to let his men stand back and they would 
clear the field to meet in single combat. “Let us joust and 
see who of us wins the day. That he who shall conquer the 
other be his master. ” 
Raimbaud went back the distance of two bow shots. 
Both knights were duly armed: they spurred their steeds 
and rushed forward to each other. Both hit their oppo -
nent’s shield and their wooden lances broke. They both 
drew their swords and started striking each other on the 
head in such a way that their swords got stuck in the oth-
er man’s helmet.
Seeing how his adversary was such a fine knight, 
Raimbaud felt a true friendship for him; he withdrew 
forty paces and asked him his name. The other answers: 
“I am Hamon of Galicia, and I would like to cross the 
river; I wish to become your friend, as do my knights, for 
we are on our way to Charlemagne’s court at Aachen. ” 
Raimbaud replies: “That is also were I am heading. ” 
Hamon proposes that they sacredly swear to become com-
panions. And this they did; they laid down their arms, 
sat down and talked in friendship.
— Karlamagnus Saga , 1.XVIII
Even though many heroes are solitary adventurers, they 
sometimes swear eternal friendship with another knight. Roland 
and Oliver are the most famous pair of brothers-in-arms, but 
many others exist, like the ill-fated friends Ami and Amile.
T o cure his friend Ami from leprosy, God has ordered Amile 
to wash his friend in the blood of his own children.
Count Amile was distraught and bewildered.
He dropped to the floor in a faint,
And with him dropped the goblet
And the bare steel sword.
When he recovers his senses, he said: 
“ Amile, wretch that you are,
Born to behead your own children!
Yet what does it matter,
If my deed will restore a man
Who is scorned by the world
And regarded as dead?
Now he will be brought back to life!”
— Ami and Amile
Requirements
T wo knights, scarae, or paladins, not related by birth, but 
who may have received their training at the same household, 
may freely pledge to each other loyal comradeship and brother -
hood in arms.
Sealing the solemn bonds of companionship is a serious 
affair, and an oath must be sworn at the royal court or in the 
presence of a paladin or a missus dominicus.
Duties
Lords, come an listen to this poem
About Ugon of Berri and the good Duke Orson.
Ugon and Orson sealed a companionship: 
By their faith they pledged to hold all in common
And never to forsake one another for anything in the 
world.
— Orson of Beauvais , II
Companions owe each other mutual support, much like 
the duties a vassal owes to his lord. Their oath obliges them to 
protect each other, to pay their companion’s ransom or seek to 
liberate him should he be imprisoned by pagans.
Benefits
When two Player-knights contract a companionship, they 
both receive the following advantages: 
Glory: Upon vowing companionship, both knight gain 
1/10 of the other’s Glory.
Honor: They both gain a point of Honor, and thereafter 
they must both use the lower Honor score of the two.
Heritage: They have common heirs and have the right to 
marry the other’s wife should she be widowed (unless the usu -
al rules for consanguinity apply). A new character may choose 
to inherit a score from his own father or from his father’s 
companion. 
Retirement
A knight may not retire from active play without the 
consent of his companion. The only way to dissolve a the 
==================== BOOK PAGE 186 ====================
185
companionship, other than by the death of a companion, is by 
mutual agreement, again in presence of either a missus domini -
cus or paladin, or at the royal court.
Officer
Some estates are so large that they require the attention of 
more than one knight and his wife. These estates have officers, 
as listed below. Smaller estates have fewer officers, who perform 
multiple functions. These offices exist at all levels of the king -
dom, from the poorest manor up to counties, and the kingdom 
itself.
When a person is appointed to be an officer the job is nei -
ther a gift nor a grant, but is held at the pleasure of the liege 
lord, who may remove the holder for any — or no — reason 
whatsoever.
Castellan: A castellan is an officer who is in charge of a cas-
tle for a lord. He is responsible for the maintenance and defense 
of that castle. Unlike most of the other offices, this office is often 
granted as a grant or a gift. 
Constable: The person responsible for the order and com-
fort of the court, performing tasks such as arranging lodgings, 
commanding messengers, watchmen, musicians, sergeants, 
huntsmen, and horn blowers.
Marshal: Commander of field forces during war, and re -
sponsible for the protection of the household.
Seneschal or Steward: This is the man in charge of feeding 
the court, especially the part of the process between the storage 
cellars and the plate. He is the lord over all the cooks, servers, etc. 
He is in charge of any Pages. 
Vicar: The person in charge of making sure that justice is 
properly delivered in a county. He is one of the two judges of the 
mallus (see Justice in Chapter 13).
Requirements
Officers are appointed by their lord. T o qualify as an officer 
a character must have: 
Standings: Standing [lord] 16, Standing [retinue] 16
Traits and Skills: 
 n Castellan: Prudent 16, Siege 16, Stewardship 13
 n Constable: Energetic 16, Courtesy 16, 
Stewardship 13
 n Marshal: V alorous 16, Battle 16, Siege 13
 n Seneschal: Energetic 16, Stewardship 16, 
Courtesy 13
 n Vicar: Just 16, Intrigue 16, Folk Lore 13
Duties
An officer serves his lord’s household day and night, all year 
long. He is usually obliged to stay at the castle, or follow his lord 
around in his retinue.
Benefits
Standard of Living: Officers are maintained at an Ordinary 
level at least. They are allowed to use the common horses from 
the lord’s stable in addition to their own.
Glory: A knight who is appointed as an Officer gains a 
one-time Glory bonus. The value is determined by the rank of 
the lord who appointed him: 
Lord Glory
Banneret Knight 75
Count 150
King 300
Retirement
The attention required by these positions generally 
does not really allow much time to go adventuring, except 
on the lord’s most important business. A Player-knight may 
retire into one of these honorable positions when he stops 
active play.
Scarae
Scarae are  young knights who serve as Charlemagne’s elite 
troops. Normally they live with the royal court, but they are reg-
ularly sent out on difficult missions. After a few years of loyal 
service, a scara is usually promoted and becomes a count, a lay 
abbot or bishop, or even a paladin.
Requirements
A scara is appointed by the palace count, a paladin, or 
Charlemagne himself. T o qualify as a scara, a knight must be less 
than 30 years old and have the following scores: 
 n Traits: V alorous 16
 n Passions: Love [Charlemagne] 13, Honor 13
 n Standings: Standing [Charlemagne] 13
 n General Skills: First Aid 13, Horsemanship 13, any 2 
other ordinary skills 13, any 2 courtly skills 13
 n Combat Skills: Battle 13, Lance 13, any 2 melee 
weapon skills 16
 n Glory: 4,000+
 n Bonus: Chivalrous, Religious or Romantic bonus
Duties
Scarae serve and protect King Charlemagne and follow him 
around wherever he goes. They may be attached to the retinue 
of a paladin for some specific missions. On campaign the scarae 
are regularly charged with dangerous missions deep into enemy 
territory. Other duties include the protection of important peo-
ple through dangerous terrain, or the liberation of those held in 
foreign prisons.
Knighthood
==================== BOOK PAGE 187 ====================
Chapter Eleven: Ambitions and Ideals
186
Benefits
Standard of Living: A scara is maintained at the royal 
court as an Ordinary knight.
Glory: A scara gains a one-time 500 Glory bonus when he 
is appointed.
Equipment: Charlemagne always provides his scarae with 
the best outfit as soon as it becomes available.
Standings: The palatine troops are respected by all and re-
ceive +1 to all their standings.
Retirement
A scara cannot retire unless he obtains a special permission 
from King Charlemagne himself (roll standing at –10).
Vassal Knight
Knights may obtain special honors from their lord. Simple 
gifts are common, usually as horses, better armor, and so on. The 
most prestigious honor is to receive a fief: a manor to be held 
by the vassal (either as a gift or a grant), which raises a bachelor 
knight to the status of vassal knight.
The land received by a vassal knight may be either a gift 
or a grant (see Feudalism in Chapter 13). Player-knights hold 
their land as a grant unless the Gamemaster chooses to have it 
otherwise.
A vassal knight holds at least one manor and its land. This 
gives him enough income to provide his own food and equip -
ment, and supports him as an Ordinary knight in terms of eco -
nomic quality. He usually lives on his land, which has a nice (but 
not extravagant) hall, when he is not serving his share of castle 
garrison duty, summer active duty time, or visiting court to offer 
his advice.
Requirements
T o be a vassal knight the character must inherit the land, 
have it granted or gifted by a lord, or conquer it.
Duties
The duties of a vassal knight are the same as those of any 
knight: to serve and protect his lord. As a knight given a special 
honor by his lord, it is expected that a vassal knight perform his 
duties with consummate skill and energy.
Benefits
Standard of Living: A vassal knight’s manor provides 
enough annual revenue for him to maintain an appropriate (usu-
ally Ordinary) life style. 
Glory: A vassal knight receives 50 Glory upon receiving his 
first manor. He receives annual Glory for his land equal to the 
income generated by the fief, usually 6 points, but perhaps more. 
He may have enough wealth to maintain himself at a higher 
standard of living (i.e., Rich or Superlative). If he does so during 
any given year he receives annual Glory based on the grade main-
tained that year.
Standings: A vassal knight receives +1 in Standing [lord].
Marriage: A vassal knight usually receives a wife (if he does 
not already have one) soon after gaining his holdings, complete-
ly at the will of his lord. The woman may be the heiress of other 
lands, or simply be provided to maintain stewardship. 
Precedence: His land gives him rank and prestige, and rais-
es him in the hierarchy of knighthood over all landless knights. 
Safety: A vassal knight has a higher price for ransom than bach-
elor knights, and is more likely to be spared in a fight (see Chapter 
12: W ealth and T reasure for more information on ransoms).
Retirement
A vassal knight needs the permission of his lord (roll stand-
ing) to retire as a hermit or a monk.
Banneret Knight
A banneret knight is the lowest rank of lord. He personally 
holds several manors and estates, but he must have at least three 
vassal knights holding land from him as well. He should be able 
to afford household knights of his own. 
Requirements
T o be a banneret knight the character must inherit the land, 
be granted or gifted it by a lord, or conquer new lands.
Duties
The duties of a banneret knight are the same as those of 
any knight: to serve and protect his liege. But because he has 
enfeoffed other knights, his responsibility as a leader is greater. 
When summoned to battle, the banneret must bring his vassal 
knights as well as his household knights.
Benefits
A banneret knight has all the benefits of a vassal knight, 
with the following additions or addendums: 
Standard of Living: A banneret is wealthy enough to 
maintain himself at the quality of a Rich knight, as long as fam -
ine or raids do not interfere. His hall is much nicer than that of a 
vassal knight, but usually not a fortified estate or castle.
Glory: A banneret receives 100 Glory when first titled. 
The banneret gets annual Glory points thereafter equal to the 
income value of his estate, as long as he holds it. This is at least 30 
points, often much more, plus the Glory from his maintenance.
Standings: As an important and respected vassal, a banner-
et gains +1 to Standings [lord] and [retinue].
Marriage: As a great lady, the Gamemaster should ensure 
that a banneret’s wife is more individualized and wealthier than 
the wives of mere vassal knights.
Army: A banneret knight has a private army pledged to 
obey him, composed of household knights and sergeants, plus 3 
or more vassal knights and their own followers.
Precedence: A lord knight has increased rank and prestige, 
and is raised in the hierarchy of knighthood over all landless 
==================== BOOK PAGE 188 ====================
187
and vassal knights. He is always seated higher at table, enters the 
room later, and is served before lesser knights.
Retirement
A banneret knight needs the permission of his lord (roll 
standing) to retire as a hermit or a monk.
Count
The ruler of an entire county, a count controls about 75 
knights on the average. He has about 100 manors. Counties al -
ways have at least one medium castle and at least 3 small ones.
Requirements
A count is always appointed by Charlemagne himself. T o 
qualify as a count, a character must have: 
 n Traits: Honest 13, Just 13, V alorous 16
 n Passions: Love [Charlemagne] 16, Honor 16
 n Standings: Standing [Charlemagne] 16
 n General Skills: Intrigue 13
 n Combat Skills: Battle 13, any 2 melee weapons 16
 n Glory: 6,000+
Duties
Counts must support themselves, their families, and retain-
ers in the proper style and manner, costing at least £84 annually. 
The count himself must live as a Superlative knight. 
A count must provide military aid and counsel to his lord in 
matters both personal and social. He must provide for the well -
being of his vassals and household knights, and must maintain 
the health and prosperity of his county. He is (at least putatively) 
responsible for making sure his vassals meet their own responsi-
bilities as landlords.
A player count must hold a hunt and a feast when requested 
by his Gamemaster, and sponsor a local tournament. He must 
make command decisions, and play that character in role when 
required by the campaign.
Benefits
A count has all the benefits of a banneret knight, with the 
following additions or addendums: 
Glory: Counts receive a one-time award of 350 Glory, and 
yearly Glory equal to their holding, up to 100 points annually. 
Standings: A count is a powerful and respected lord, and as 
such he gains +2 to Standing [retinue] and [family].
Army: A count has at least 50 vassal and banneret knights 
under his command, plus their own followers.
Precedence: A Count ranks higher than all banneret 
knights. Precedence between counts is determined by the Glory 
generated by their holdings, with some ancient privileges ac -
knowledged as pre-eminent, such as the Count of Paris.
Retirement
Unless the Gamemaster allows a player to continue, possibly 
as a missus dominicus, a count is obliged to retire from active play.
Duke
A duke is the military leader of a collection of counties, 
known as a Duchy. He is a count as well. He leads the troops of 
his duchy in battle, either as the Army commander or a Battalion 
commander. He must attend the May Fields with his troops if 
summoned by the king, and attends the Winter Court to advise 
the king on the next year’s campaigns. 
Requirements
A duke is appointed by Charlemagne himself. A duke must 
already be a count (or be given a county along with being ap -
pointed duke). In addition, he must have: 
 n Traits: Energetic 15, V alorous 16
 n Passions: Love [Charlemagne] 18, Honor 16
 n Standings: Standing [Charlemagne] 18
 n General Skills: Intrigue 16
 n Combat Skills: Battle 16, Siege 16
 n Glory: 7,000+
Benefits
A duke has all the benefits of a count, with the following 
additions or addendums: 
Glory: Dukes receive a one-time award of 750 Glory, and 
yearly Glory equal to their holding, up to 150 points annually. 
Standings: A duke is a powerful and respected lord, and as 
such he gains +3 to Standing [retinue] and [family] (only +1 if 
he was already a count). 
Army: A duke has the knights of his own county under his 
personal command. In addition he commands the armies of sev-
eral counties. 
Precedence: A duke ranks higher than all counts. 
Precedence between dukes is determined by the Glory generated 
by their respective duchies.
Retirement
Unless the Gamemaster allows a player to continue, possibly 
as a missus dominicus, a duke is obliged to retire from active play.
Lay Abbot
An abbot is the head of a cloistered community of monks. 
He commands the monks and rules over the abbey’s lands as its 
rightful lord. Depending on the size the community, an abbot 
commands 5 to 20 knights who assure the abbey’s protection. A 
lay abbot has not taken religious vows.
Knighthood

188
Requirements
A lay abbot may be appointed by the previous abbot of the 
monastery, the local bishop, a count, or by Charlemagne himself. 
T o qualify as a lay abbot, a knight character must have: 
 n Passions: Love [Charlemagne] 16, Honor 16
 n Standings: Standing [Church] 16
 n General Skills: Languages 10, Religion 10, Reading 
& Writing 10
 n Glory: 5,000+
 n Bonus: Religious bonus
Duties
Although not a priest nor a monk himself, a lay abbot is 
nevertheless expected to uphold the values of the Church, which 
means he is expected to lead a chaste life of austerity and prayer.
Every year, an abbot must send a certain number of his 
knights to the royal banner (he is generally allowed to keep two 
knights back from the muster). He may be asked to perform spe-
cial tasks, such as securing hostages, stocking military equipment 
or raising horses for the royal army.
Benefits
Standard of Living: An abbey may well control enough 
manors to qualify as a banneret’s holding, if not several such 
holdings. The abbot is nominally wealthy enough to maintain 
himself at the quality of a Rich knight, though he is not ex -
pected to. 
Glory: An abbot receives a one-time award of 50 Glory for 
being named Abbot. He receives 50 annual Glory points for ad-
ministering the abbey’s possessions.
Standings: As an influential member of the Church hierar-
chy, an abbot gains +2 to Standing [Church].
Piety: The life among spiritual monks who dedicate their 
life to God rubs off on the lay abbot, who gains +2 Love [God].
Retirement
Unless the Gamemaster allows a player to continue, possi -
bly as a missus dominicus, a lay abbot is obliged to retire from 
active play.
Lay Bishop
A bishop is the spiritual leader of a large ecclesiastical com-
munity, centered around a city’s cathedral. A lay bishop has not 
taken religious vows. 
Requirements
A lay bishop is always appointed by Charlemagne himself. 
T o qualify as a lay bishop, a knight character must have: 
 n Passions: Love [Charlemagne] 16, Honor 16
 n Standings: Standing [Charlemagne] 16
 n General Skills: Intrigue 13, Languages 10, Religion 
10, Reading & Writing 10
 n Glory: 6,000+
 n Bonus: Religious bonus
Duties
A bishop is responsible for the spiritual well-being of all 
Christians living in his bishopric. He oversees the priests. A bish-
op assists the local count with his administration, the course of 
justice and other important affairs.
As major royal councilors, all bishops must come to the 
king’s court for the autumn assembly.
Benefits
Standard of Living: A bishopric owns many manors 
— possibly as many as a small county. The bishop is nomi -
nally wealthy enough to maintain himself at the quality of a 
Superlative knight, though he is not expected to. 
Glory: Lay bishops receive a one-time 350 Glory award, 
and 100 annual Glory.
Standing: As an influential member of the Church hierar-
chy, a bishop gains +3 to Standing [Church].
Retirement
Unless the Gamemaster allows a player to continue, possi -
bly as a missus dominicus, a lay bishop is obliged to retire from 
active play.
Missus Dominicus
A missus dominicus is a special envoy of the king, holding 
his full delegated power. He is sent to maintain royal standards 
in the counties, with the power to investigate county matters, 
and even arrest the count if his administration proves corrupt!
Requirements
T o be appointed as a missus by Charlemagne, Player-knights 
must already be either a count, abbot or bishop. T o qualify, he 
must also have: 
 n Traits: Energetic 16, Just 16
 n Passions: Honor 16
 n General Skills: Awareness 16, Folk Lore 10, 
Eloquence 10, Intrigue 10
Duties
Missi dominici are usually sent to a region to control and ad-
monish the local count(s), to restore law and order, and to judge 
serious crimes. A missus reports to Charlemagne personally.
Benefits
Glory: +500 one-time award
Standings: +1 to all standings
==================== BOOK PAGE 190 ====================
189
Retirement
A missus may retire from active play at any time — though 
not during a mission, of course.
Paladin
The ultimate knightly ambition is to become one of the 
twelve paladins. They are a body of handpicked knights who 
serve at Charlemagne’s personal command. They are chosen be-
cause they personify the virtues that the king wishes to bring to 
his land: knighthood, chivalry, and romance.
Requirements
A candidate for paladinhood must be sponsored by anoth -
er paladin. There must be a vacancy, which only occurs when a 
member dies, retires into a monastery or renounces an active role 
at the royal court and leaves for the Holy Land.
Paladins must have the following scores: 
 n Traits: V alorous 20
 n Passions: Love [Charlemagne] 16, Honor 20
 n Standings: Standing [Charlemagne] 16
 n General Skills: Horsemanship 16, any 2 other gener-
al skills 16, any 2 courtly skills 16
 n Combat Skills: Battle 16, Lance 16, any 2 melee 
weapon skills 20
 n Glory: 8,000+
 n Ideals: Chivalrous bonus
Duties
A paladin must strive to attend Charlemagne’s great 
Pentecostal feasts, there to relate the deeds of the year gone by 
and to renew his oath of Paladinhood: “I swear, by the honor done 
me by my lord Charlemagne and his peers, to never commit violence 
without good purpose, to shun all murder and treason, and to give 
mercy where it is asked. I swear, upon pain of death, to always protect 
ladies, gentlewomen, damsels, and widows. I promise to never fight 
for an unjust cause, and to never fight for personal gain. ” 
Paladins act as leaders in war, especially as officers over oth-
er knights, war councilors, and the scarae, and as an elite battle 
unit.
Paladins act as leaders in peace. Their duty is to deliver the 
king’s justice across the land, and to act as models of behavior 
for all to emulate. They carry the king’s messages, conduct his 
business, and protect his roads, people, and property.
Benefits
A Paladin wears a purple and golden sash as a mark of his 
office. He possesses the greatest honor in the land, and is always 
respected by those who revere peace, wisdom, and the king’s jus-
tice. Paladins are held in esteem by men, admired by women, and 
always welcome anywhere in the realm.
Upon his acceptance as a Paladin, a character gains: 
Glory: +1,000, one time
Standings: +3 to all
Divine Aid: Paladins receive a reward of +5 to CON
Precedence: Most paladins hold another office, such as 
Count, as well. They rank above the other lords of the same 
rank. Precedence between paladins is based on their personal 
Glory.
Retirement
Paladinhood is normally a function for life. A paladin’s only 
possibility to retire from play — which needs no approval from 
Charlemagne — is to become a monk or a hermit.
Black Knight
The Black Knights are not intended as an ambition for 
Player-knights — they are evil, after all. However, they are 
a hidden force in the game that the knights may encounter. 
Unfortunately for Player-knights, the Black Knights have influ-
ence at the highest levels in the land….
When his father installs his permanent court at Aachen 
in 793, Prince Charlot creates a secret knightly order for his 
exclusive personal service. The Black Knights’ motto is “The 
end justifies the means. ”  Their missions include intimidation, 
blackmail, the usurpation of lands and titles, and even political 
assassination.
Nobody, outside of the band and their victims, knows of the 
Black Knight’s existence; and the members do not know each 
other’s identity. As its name indicates, they dress completely in 
black — blackened armor, black surcoats, and shields of plain 
black. Black Knights do this in order operate anonymously. T o 
enable them to recognize other members, Prince Charlot gives 
them a token, such as a colored stone or a dagger, marked with 
the sign of the order. Black Knights receive their verbal orders 
exclusively from Prince Charlot.
Notable Members: Macharias of Lausanne, Amauri of 
the Riviers T ower, Gibouart of Sicily, and most of Ganelon’s 
descendants.
Prince Charlot’s knights often operate during the night: 
Charlot agrees with the plan. The vile traitors leave to 
prepare themselves; they don their armor, they buckle 
their helmet, they put their sword on the left side, they 
mount their vigorous mounts, they seize their robust 
shield and pointy lance. In total, more than a hundred 
knight are ready to do battle. O, God! Charlemagne 
knows nothing of all this. If the lord does not intervene, 
innocent young people will die this night! 
— Huon of Bordeaux , IV
Requirements
As the ideals and objectives of this order are rather against 
the spirit of the game, joining the Black Knights is only possible 
with explicit Gamemaster approval.
Knighthood
==================== BOOK PAGE 191 ====================
