190
T o qualify as a Black Knight, a character knight must have 
the following scores: 
 n Traits: V engeful 16, Deceitful 16, Arbitrary 16
 n Passions: Love [Charlot] 16, Love [Charlemagne] < 5
 n General Skills: Any 2 general skills 16, any 2 courtly 
skills 16
 n Combat Skills: Any 2 melee weapons 16, Lance 16
Duties
Black Knights must obey and protect Prince Charlot in all 
circumstances, even if his orders are in opposition to the knight’s 
other obligations.
Benefits
Glory: As it is an anonymous order, joining the Black 
Knights gains a character no Glory.
Courtly Skills: Black Knights regularly receive inside in -
formation about their rivals at court and elsewhere, so they get 
+5 to their Intrigue score.
Combat Skills: As they do not shun the use of unknightly 
weapons, Black Knight receive +3 in Bow and Crossbow.
Retirement
Once a Black Knight, always a Black Knight. Retirement 
is no option. Renegade Black Knights are relentlessly hunted 
down by those loyal to their prince.
Ideals
I
dealism and faith can help make a man a hero. By ascrib -
ing to and behaving in accordance with certain values—
that is, by maintaining a minimum value in a number of 
selected traits—a character gains certain rewards and benefits 
beyond those of lesser knights. Some of these gifts are of a mag -
ical nature.
A knight may take up the ambition of living in accordance 
with an ideal of behavior. Some famous knights maintain two 
ideals at once, though this is a great challenge.
Chivalrous Knight
Chivalry supports the protection of the weak by the strong. 
Before its acceptance, most knights live by the attitude that 
“might makes right.” Chivalry seeks to turn that attitude into 
one of noblesse oblige, the belief that nobility obligates one to 
behave honorably and benevolently. This definition of chivalry 
places its emphasis upon refining the knights’ duty. 
Requirements
Chivalrous knights must have a total of 90 or more points 
in Energetic, Generous, Just, Merciful, Modest and V alorous, 
and they must have at least 16 Honor.
Any knight may strive to become Chivalrous, independent-
ly of his culture or homeland.
Duties
A Chivalrous knight must respect the code of knighthood 
under all circumstances, using his skills to protect the weak, 
spread culture and civilization, and uphold the law of the land. 
As part of being a Chivalrous knight, he swears to uphold the 
following oath: “T o protect the widow, the orphan, the poor; not 
to slay a vanquished and defenseless adversary; not to take part 
in a false judgment or treason, or to withdraw if it cannot be pre -
vented; to never give evil counsel to a lady; to help, if possible, a 
fellow being in distress. ” 
Benefits
Inspiration: A chivalric knight who is Inspired for Honor 
receives a double skill bonus: +10 for normal success and +20 
for a critical.
Divine Aid: Whether naked or fully armored, chivalric 
knights receive 3 points of invisible protection against all physi-
cal damage, drowning or other such unpleasant effects.
Glory: Chivalrous knights get 100 Glory points per year if 
they maintain their status.
Religious Knight
All people know that Christianity is the One T rue Faith, 
but Pious knights go one step further and work hard to promote 
their religion through their exemplary lifestyle.
Only a Christian knight (including a convert to 
Christianity) may strive to become Religious, no matter his cul-
ture or homeland.
Requirements
Christianity is the ultimate moral force in the world of 
Charlemagne. T o qualify as an exemplary Christian, knights 
must have a total of 90 or more points in Chaste, Forgiving, 
Merciful, Modest, T emperate and T rusting, and must have a 
Love [God] of at least 16.
Duties
Religious knights are the embodiment of the Christian vir-
tues. They must uphold the values of the Church, regularly at -
tend mass, lead a virtuous life, protect the weak and the Church, 
and respect the Peace of God.
Benefits
Divine Aid: A Religious knight has a +5 bonus to all his 
Prayer rolls, (see Magic for Player-Knights in Chapter 9).
Glory: Religious knights get 100 Glory points per year if 
they maintain their status.
==================== BOOK PAGE 192 ====================
191
Romantic Knight
A knight of Romance honors all women and must do ev-
erything in his nature and ability to protect women, to deliver 
them justice, to respect them, and to do their bidding. He must 
honor every lady as if she were his own lover. 
Requirements
A Romantic character must have an Amor [lady] or Love 
[Amor] of at least 16. He must have a total of 90 or more points 
in Forgiving, Generous, Honest, Just, Prudent, and T rusting, 
and have Romance and 4 other courtly skills (chosen by his lov-
er) at 10.
Any knight may strive to become romantic, no matter what 
his culture or homeland is.
Ideals
Duties
A knight must prove his passion for his lady at least once 
per year by doing her bidding, even under duress. This activity 
must be played out, or done solitaire using the Romance solo (see 
Chapter 19).
A knight must entertain his Amor with presents equaling at 
least £1 per year. A lover must be true to his love; infidelity is the 
worst offense a lover can commit.
Benefits
Inspiration: A Romantic knight who is Inspired for his 
Amor receives a double skill bonus: +10 instead of +5 for nor -
mal success and +20 instead of +10 for a critical.
Divine Aid: Players of Romantic knights are allowed to re-
roll the die or dice once per adventure.
Glory: Romantic knights get 100 Glory points per year if 
they maintain their status.

193
M
oney and wealth should be a means of self-expression 
for a Player-knight, never an end in itself. Each player 
must choose how much he wants to become involved 
in economics. It is possible to ignore economics completely. A 
vassal knight normally receives enough income from his land to 
easily maintain the standard lifestyle for his station.
Extravagance, however, requires more attention to detail. 
In this section the basic economic aspects of your character’s 
world are discussed. Part of the enjoyment of P aladin  comes 
from spending money freely and lavishly in the game. Y our char-
acter starts the game well off, and may become rich. And there 
are many things to spend money on….
Understanding Wealth
Coinage and Value
d: Denier. A small silver coin. 
s: Sol. A currency of account (i.e., it only exists for accounting 
purposes). 1s = 12d. 
£: Livre. A currency of account equal to one pound of silver. £1 
= 20s = 240d.
L
ivres and deniers are the basic units to measure value. V alue 
is important because wealth is measured as standard of liv-
ing and property, not necessarily cash on hand. Household 
knights do not get cash every Christmas, instead the lord spends 
that much to keep him up to standards: the knight gets his 
clothes patched by the castle ladies for free, gets his food at the 
common meal, and has his armor repaired by the lord’s black -
smith. None of this business need be recorded or role-played, it 
is just normal.
The only Frankish coin in use is the silver denier, and most 
values are figured in livres and deniers. Gold coins are known 
only in the lands of the Byzantines, Saracens and Persians. Many 
Jewish and some Frankish merchants use gold when trading in 
luxury goods with these cultures.
Chapter Twelve:  
Wealth and Treasure
The Manor
A manor is an economic unit and not one of area or popu -
lation, so a manor can vary widely from the standards given here. 
A vassal knight’s manor must generate at least £6 to maintain 
him in his station. 
Wealth Levels
In P aladin  the economic difference between knights is 
quantified by the annual income allotted to them each Winter 
Phase. Five categories of knights exist: Impoverished, Poor, 
Ordinary, Rich, and Superlative. The game effects of these cate-
gories are listed in Chapter 10: The Winter Phase.
The daily measure of wealth is that which can be seen and 
partaken of: clothing and daily food. Rich people wear fancier 
clothes and eat better, more varied foods. Knights, being mem -
bers of the noble class, wear very nice clothing and eat well, 
whether fed at their lord’s board or on their own manor.
A knight’s basic standard of living is set at £2. This means 
that it takes about £2 worth of food and goods annually to main-
tain a knight and his squire at Ordinary, expected standards. 
This standard includes meat and good wine at every meal, and 
nice clothing.
Horses are the greatest cost to a knight. T o maintain all his 
horses (at least one healthy oat-fed charger, and several rouncies 
and sumpters) costs another £2. Rouncies and sumpters can be 
replaced for free from the knight or lord’s stables, but chargers 
are an extraordinary expense. A successful Standing [lord] roll is 
needed for a lord to replace a household knight’s charger.
A family needs to be cared for. Family expenses at a knight’s 
level add another £2. This expense covers the costs of housing, 
clothing, and feeding a wife of good station and children in suit-
able fashion. The full annual expense of a vassal knight, main -
tained at an Ordinary level of quality, is £6 per year. He receives 
this amount in income from his manor.
It is useful to compare knights’ standards with those of a 
peasant family of two adults and three children. The common 
peasant exists in a self-sufficient world where money is unknown, 
==================== BOOK PAGE 195 ====================
Chapter Twelve: Wealth and Treasure
194
and the family makes, grows, or barters for everything they need. 
In money-value, annual expense and income for a peasant family 
is £1 per year.
Impoverished Knight
A knight with an income of less than £3 per year is 
Impoverished. His is a miserable lot. He appears ragged, lean 
and sickly; his armor dented and rusty. He has no squire and 
lacks a charger. As a result, an impoverished knight usually rides 
with the sergeants in battle, even though he may be far more 
skilled or valorous.
Legally, impoverishment means trouble. An impoverished 
bachelor knight, who normally lives at the hall of his lord, is not 
required to remain loyal to his lord since under the feudal oath 
his lord promised him sustenance. He may leave freely, or may be 
sent away on a quest by the helpless lord.
If a vassal knight neglects his land to the point of impover -
ishment, his lord has the right to take the land back. The knight 
has clearly failed to maintain the land and uphold his end of the 
feudal bargain.
Poor Knight
“My sons, by God, of high birth are you both,
Of a proud line and noble, knightly folk—
Much of their deeds and often I’ve been told; 
But tell me this, which truthfully I’d know: 
Have you as yet been dubbed as knights or no?”
“My lord, we’ve not, through poverty alone; 
Dire poverty has made us leave the home
Of the dear sire whose blood flows in our own. ” 
 — Gerard of Vienne , XII
Poor knights receive too little economic support to main -
tain themselves in the manner in which they ought. A knight 
who receives between £3–£5 per year is considered Poor. In 
general a Poor knight has no squire; rides a mangy, sway-backed 
charger; is hungry, lean and ragged; and wears dented armor.
Ordinary Knight
Regular, or Ordinary, knights are the knights most often 
discussed in P aladin . Whenever the word knight is used, un -
modified by adjective, it refers to this type of person. Ordinary 
knights comprise the majority of chivalry.
An Ordinary knight spends £6 to £8 per year which keeps 
himself, his family, a single squire, and his horses healthy and 
robust, and his equipment in good repair. This may be provided 
by his lord, or by a normal manor.
Rich Knight
Rich knights spend between £9 and £12 per year on their 
maintenance, significantly above normal standards.
Rich knights wear clothing of rich fabrics and furs in the lat-
est style, use silver-decorated tack for their glossy-coated horses, 
and enjoy rich feasts. They have two squires in attendance, both 
well-mounted and attentive to their lord’s needs. Their families 
live well, off the fat of the land.
Knights who live above their expected means (£6 per year) 
gain yearly Glory equal to the number of livre spent on maintain-
ing their station (typically 10 Glory/year for Rich knights). If a 
lord supplies their maintenance, the lord also gains this Glory.
Superlative Knights
Superlative knights are the most extravagant and impressive 
of all, requiring more than £12 per year for upkeep. Their armor 
shines brightly, their clothing is sumptuous with intricate stitch-
ing, extensive use of gold thread, jewels, imported feathers, and 
furs from fantastic beasts. Three squires, each proud in match -
ing livery, attend their lord’s needs. Superlative knights benefit 
from their superior health and the aid given in battle by well-
equipped, loyal squires.
As above, knights who live above their expected means gain 
additional yearly Glory, averaging 15 Glory per year. 
Prerogatives of Wealth
T
he wealthy have many privileges that are natural and 
right. Here are the most important which are common 
to everyone. 
The Lord’s Progress
While most lords have a main residence, they usually travel 
about their lands with their households in an official “progress.” 
This is because it is easier for them to go where the food is, rath-
er than move the food to where they are. So the lord goes from 
place to place within his own demesne, stopping in to visit vas -
sals, moving with his family and household. 
This process has the added advantages of checking to see 
how the host manor or castle is doing economically and po -
litically, testing the loyalties of the vassals and servants, and 
conducting whatever business is beyond the capability or re -
sponsibility of the resident overseer. If a vassal is reluctant or un-
trustworthy, a lord may choose to stay a long time, eating up the 
knight’s wealth and thereby restricting future activity.
Usually when a lord arrives the chief resident gives up his 
own quarters—which are of course the best—to his lord. The 
chief then takes the second best, while the resident of the second 
best bumps the third best, and so on, until someone ends up stay-
ing with the household knights for the duration of the stay. Only 
in special cases of extreme luxury do vassals have special rooms 
for visiting lords. 
Heralds
Announcements are often made at court. A banneret prob-
ably just has a sergeant-at-arms bawl out proclamations, but 
higher-ranking nobles employ professional heralds for that duty. 
Heralds have several duties.
==================== BOOK PAGE 196 ====================
195
In court, heralds announce visitors by name and distinctive 
titles when they enter. On judgment days, they proclaim all the 
cases to be discussed, state the judgments rendered, and record 
all the pertinent facts for later reference. While this can add fla-
vor to the game, Gamemasters should beware of boring the play-
ers with too much idle verbiage.
Heralds can read and write. They read public proclama -
tions from scrolls, and they record the attendance of every no -
bleman and knight at a feast or tournament. Scrolls are made 
of parchment and, in royal circles, are calligraphed, illuminated, 
and decorated with gold or silver leaf and glued-on jewels.
Fanfares blown on long trumpets announce the entrance 
and exit of important persons. The definition of important is 
left up to the nobleman (here is an opportunity to insult some -
one, intentionally or not). Fanfares are also used to announce 
major decrees and to punctuate ceremonial functions such as 
homage swearing, naming of officers, and granting knighthood.
Retinues
All rich people are continually accompanied by a retinue, 
whose size depends only upon the wealth of the noble. Even the 
traveling entourage of a fully accoutered knight is quite some -
thing to behold. 
T o start, many horses are present. Every ordinary knight has 
his warhorse for combat. But no one would ride such a magnif -
icent steed for ordinary travel; the creature would be too tired 
when the fight comes up. Also, due to the complex military sad-
dle born by the warhorse, it is most comfortable to ride a sim -
ple riding horse, such as a rouncy or palfrey, and switch to the 
charger for battle. Nor, in most cases, does a knight ride around 
the countryside all armed and armored, due to the discomfort 
caused by weight and heat, so a pack horse is needed to carry his 
armor. A warhorse cannot be used as a packhorse and vice ver -
sa: the equipment and saddles are so different that either steed 
would soon have serious saddle sores if used for the other pur -
pose. More importantly, a warhorse is a noble beast, and it is just 
wrong to burden it with baggage.
An ordinary knight’s full field entourage would, then, nor-
mally include a total of 3 people and 6 steeds: 
1 knight, 1 squire, 1 page, 2 riding horses (for knight and 
squire), 1 warhorse, 2 sumpters (one for armor and one for tents 
and food),1 pony for the page.
Add the knight’s wife and the entourage grows greatly. She 
has two maidservants and her own page, all of whom would ride. 
At least another packhorse is needed for their supplies, plus a 
groom to care for the horses (the squire performs this duty for 
the knight); her party totals at least 5 people and 6 horses.
Thus an ordinary knight, traveling with his wife, would 
have a party of 8 people and 12 horses and ponies.
There may be additional sergeants, foot soldiers, messen -
gers, children and their nurse, cooks, heralds, and musicians, 
with assistants of their own.
For further illustration, let us assemble the retinue of a 
banneret knight who travels to a tournament with his whole 
entourage: 1 banneret knight, with his 2 squires and 2 pages: 9 
steeds required (2 warhorses, 4 riding horses — 1 spare, 2 ponies, 
1 sumpter); 3 ordinary knights, with their 3 squires: 12 steeds 
required (3 chargers, 6 riding horses, 3 sumpters); 4 servants 
(herald, messenger, cook, assistant cook): 6 steeds required (4 
riding horses, 2 packhorses); his wife with 3 maidservants and 
4 pages: 9 steeds required (5 palfreys — 1 spare, 4 ponies); 4 
wife’s servants (2 butlers, 2 grooms): 6 steeds required (4 riding 
horses, 2 packhorses); 2 children with 1 nurse: 3 steeds required 
(1 palfrey, 2 ponies). T otal: 30 people and 45 horses and ponies.
Note that there are 4 knights, with 26 additional people. A 
tournament for 100 knights adds a total of about 750 people, 
including the knights themselves, to the local population. This 
might create an encampment equal to the entire population of 
the sponsoring castle and nearby town. Imagine the multitude at 
a tournament five times the size!
Getting Money
N
oblemen are notoriously short of money. Being import -
ant is expensive! But in addition to his own expenses, a 
noblemen’s virtue is reflected by his largesse (generosi -
ty), and if he wants to be famous within his social circle then 
he will have to reward favorable behavior with an open hand. 
Charlemagne leads everyone in the largesse that he bestows.
Noblemen have many methods of deriving income from 
their holdings and vassals. The most important are the agricul -
tural and material goods which a knight or lord regularly collects 
to maintain himself, his family, and his household, but every feu-
dal lord has a few other methods as well.
Land
Land is the basis for measuring the economic success of a 
knight. Land provides the basics of life, the people to provide the 
basics, and the raw materials that are turned into profit.
The manor is the basic source of economic measure for 
knights. Other sources may be granted or gifted to individuals 
at the Gamemaster’s whim. The more land a character gains, the 
stronger his economic position becomes. 
Noblemen should maintain effective stewards of their 
properties to encourage efficiency and profit. Wives are tradi -
tionally good at this, or the younger brother of someone import-
ant may suffice.
Ransom
Ransoming captives from battle is the single most lucrative 
(and dangerous) way to get money. Everyone captured alive in 
battle is worth money to his captor. Prisoners are, by the rules of 
war, owned by their captors. By further rules of war, it is gentle-
manly to return the captive alive, for a ransom. It is very gener -
ous, though foolhardy, to release enemy captives for free.
Getting Money
==================== BOOK PAGE 197 ====================
Chapter Twelve: Wealth and Treasure
196
Landed knights and other lords may have to pawn their 
possessions and lands to raise the ransom, but will do so if need-
ed. They’ll have to repay whoever lent them the money. 
Ransoms may be gained during tournaments and from 
combat in general, if appropriate. The primary restriction is that 
the combat must be serious, not “for love.” 
The minimum values of ransoms are established by custom 
and law and are not usually open to bargaining or debate, except 
in the case of very important or famous people. Proud knights 
naturally pay more for their release, but Modest ones are satis -
fied to offer the ransoms below to their captors.
The Heribannum is a fine assessed if a man does not show 
up for military service. Ransom is a price paid for your release 
when captured by an honorable enemy. We r g i l d is the price paid 
by a killer to his victim’s family to prevent a feud. All three fines 
are equivalent, and are considered the “worth” of the man.

Ransoming Vassals
As explained below, vassals have the obligation to ransom 
their lord. But what about knights with no vassals, particularly 
poor, errant knights without a lord and household knights?
Poor knights without a lord, alas, have no hope. They might 
be captured while questing, while serving as a mercenary, or per-
haps while engaged in a bit of knightly procurement. They can 
beg for help from their family, but most families of poor knights 
are themselves too poor to afford ransom. A friend may lend aid, 
or the poor knight can pledge himself to some task for his cap -
tor. But in general his fate is glum: to rot away in captivity.
Household knights can expect to be ransomed by their 
lord. They are not strangers to their lord but his everyday com -
panions. They are the chosen few who reside close to the lord, 
and who have presumably exhibited valor and loyalty to prove 
their worth. The feudal bond guarantees that a lord will provide 
for the safety and wellbeing of his vassals. A landed vassal has his 
wellbeing guaranteed by his property. The household knights 
get theirs from their lord.
If captured, a Player-knight may roll Standing [lord]. 
Success means that his lord is motivated by his vow and does 
everything to try and fulfill it. A failed roll indicates that he does 
not. 
T o a player lord, failure to fulfill this basic obligation of ran-
som means that he automatically loses 1 point each of Honor 
and Standing [retinue] per knight abandoned. Furthermore, the 
captured knight is released from his oath of vassalage (if he sur -
vives and returns home).
Ransoming Family
Families may provide a knight’s ransom on a successful 
Standing [family] roll. Each member of the extended family will 
contribute something to the ransom, spreading the cost among 
many. Note that the Player-knights may be called upon to help 
ransom a distant cousin. Usually the portion requested is not too 
much — only one livre or so. It helps to have a large family!
Four Universal Aids
Four universal feudal customs reward lords with special in-
come. These four taxes give the lord the right to collect income 
from his holding whether the vassals like it or not. These are 
called aids.
Each of these four methods may be imposed only once by 
a lord, with exceptions noted below. When imposed, each vassal 
pays an amount equal the average (normal) yearly income of his 
holding.
Knighting of the Eldest Son
Creating a new knight is expensive — £8 minimum. This 
money is raised to outfit a man with the equipment listed for an 
ordinary knight.
The tax may not be enough to outfit the candidate. A father 
has choices to make here: he can squeeze his people (and incur 
their hate), he can pay from his own treasury, or he can just outfit 
his son as a poor knight. If outfitted improperly the young man 
might not be accepted for the office and not receive the honors 
of knighthood.
On the other hand the collection may make much more 
than the £8 necessary. All or part may be used to upgrade the 
new knight’s outfit. Or the father can just keep the extra money, 
though he gets a Selfish check for doing so. Usually it is all spent 
in a lavish feast, perhaps even a tournament, to mark the event.
Glory gained for the expenditure of money at a knighting 
goes to both the new knight and the father; a nobleman who 
spends more than £8 on his son’s knighting ceremony gets addi-
tional Glory for both himself and his son, at the usual rate of 1 
point of Glory per £1 spent (up to 100 Glory maximum).
Sometimes a ranking nobleman knights many other knights 
along with his son. This is a rare opportunity for qualified, but 
impoverished, individuals to become knights at the nobleman’s 
expense. Each of the extra new knights gets his 1,000 Glory, as 
==================== BOOK PAGE 198 ====================
197
usual, and all extra Glory for expenses over £8 is accrued by the 
knights and their new lord.
Wedding of the Eldest Daughter 
The family of the bride usually pays for the wedding. A 
nobleman whose eldest daughter is married receives Glory for 
the money spent on the event. T o cover the expenses, a tax is 
collected. Part of it may be spent on the festivities, and part may 
be included in the dowry that every wife is expected to provide.
Ransom the Lord 
The ransom of the lord is the duty of the vassals. This tax 
provides the money needed. In all cases the noble vassals receive 
at least their usual year’s income from the tax, although some -
times they must squeeze their peasants to raise so much. The rest 
of the necessary sum is collected from the citizens of the towns 
of the holding, unless the vassals volunteer to pay more, earning 
Honor and Generous checks if the additional amount they grant 
is great.
If a lord is captured a second time the vassals are not obliged 
to pay his ransom again. T aking money from them to pay an -
other ransom is done as an Impost or as V oluntary T allage (see 
below).
To Support a Crusade
Fighting the infidel is a venture financed by all the vassals, 
so that they can share in the Glory and expense.
Tallage
A fifth method of taxation, called tallage, is invoked when-
ever a lord tries to raise extra money. The purpose might be any-
thing: to raise an army to invade an enemy, to defend the realm, 
to build ships, to finance a tournament, to build a town, or to 
outfit an expedition. The lord calls together his court (knights 
and lords who hold land rights) and the leading citizens of his 
towns (commoners who control the mercantile wealth). The 
lord pleads his case before them, either as one group or sepa -
rately. The vassals and citizenry discuss the situation, and return 
their decisions to the lord.
Results of the request can vary widely. The court might 
agree to make the payments, and if inspired (perhaps by the 
threat of an oncoming army) might even volunteer more money 
or additional labor. They might pay part of the request because 
they like their lord. They might pay half, but only if the lord 
makes some sort of concessions in return. They might not pay 
anything because they don’t like him, or because they, too, are 
broke, or because they disagree with the intent. Many factors af-
fect their decision. Standing checks should be liberally applied. 
Impost
An impost is something that is imposed upon the com -
moners. It can be used to describe a legitimate fine, perhaps im -
posed for violating the lord’s law; or an arbitrary fee, as is all too 
common.
Imposts are simply declared, and then collected from the 
commoners with whatever force is necessary. The payment is 
usually collected (remember that each peasant family has a year-
ly income in goods of about £1 equivalent), and the only cost is 
the undying hatred of the people who supply your daily bread: 
–2 Standing [commoners] per impost.
Service at Court
Noblemen holding royal offices get income for holding 
down an extra job or two. Key offices are lucrative. They include 
the offices of Seneschal (the person in charge of feeding the 
court) and Marshal (the commander of field forces, and respon-
sible for the protection of the household). Other minor offices 
include collecting taxes on roads and bridges, collecting import 
tariffs at borders, and keeping the forests.
Tournaments
T ournaments can be expensive for losers, who forfeit hors-
es, equipment and perhaps even ransoms. Some noblemen make 
a profession of conquering opponents at tournaments just for 
the money. 
War and Raid
W ar sometimes yields great profits. Successful raiding of 
other knights’ lands can net many herds of cattle and sheep. 
T aking cities by storm yields rich plunder, and surrendering 
towns pay a fee for their liberation. Castles are a lord’s treasure 
house and usually hold many riches. Ransoming his prisoners 
can make a knight rich. 
The dangers of this are evident as well, for defeat causes all 
the money raised to have been wasted, costs extra for ransoms 
and possibly even be needed for reparations to the victorious 
party for damages done to his lands. 
Loans
Loans are possible, but should be avoided at all costs, since 
they put the fate of nobles in the hands of merchants or the cler-
gy. City dwellers, bishops, and abbots are the only people likely 
to have money to lend. At the start of the campaign the towns 
are all poor, however, and unlikely places to ask for income. 
During Charlemagne’s reign it becomes increasingly possible. 
Merchants always charge interest on loans, equal to the 
Phase number times 10% a year! Alternately, they may extract 
promises odious to lords. For instance, freedom from future tax-
es, or even the right to marry the daughters of a lord, thereby 
insuring that their children become nobles! 
Normally, a knight may borrow money up to his own herib-
annum value, for a good or Christian purpose only.
Marry Well
W ar leaves many widows, and lords may have only mar -
riageable daughters for heirs. A marriage can earn a wise knight 
Getting Money
==================== BOOK PAGE 199 ====================
Chapter Twelve: Wealth and Treasure
198
a great deal of money through a generous dowry, as well as lands 
and titles. 
Selling Goods
If knights have excess treasure which they wish to sell they 
can do so at any market. However, they only collect half of the 
amount that it would cost them to purchase the same item. It 
may be better to simply store it away at the manor. 
Deposits
Some knights may wish to bring their excess money to a safe 
place, such as the lord’s castle or a great abbey, to prevent it from 
being stolen by a band of robber knights or by raiding enemies.
Depositing money at an abbey may be done only by knights 
personally known to the abbot or of very good reputation. 
Knights with an Honor score of 16 or more, or those succeed -
ing an Standing [Church] roll, may deposit their money for free. 
Others have to pay a small fee of usually no more than 5% to 
10% per year.
Major Investments
K
nights may have the opportunity to make major invest -
ments. Most of the time this isn’t necessary — it is suffi -
cient to just say “I’m spending £10 on my manor, ” for in-
stance. But sometimes the details are important, or just fun, and 
so the price list offers prices for some really large items, if desired.
Special Expenses
There are many situations when a knight can spend his 
money to maintain or enhance his status. He should provide 
sumptuous feasts for christenings, weddings and knightings. 
When his daughters marry, he has to give them a dowry. On the 
death of a close relative he organizes a solemn Christian funeral. 
Finally, if his lord is captured, a knight is obliged to help pay the 
ransom.
Knights frequently spend money on things which are not 
strictly necessary, but which allow them to raise their standing 
with certain people or groups. Building a small chapel on one’s 
domain may attract the goodwill of the Church. Organizing 
a feast at the end of the harvest pleases the commoners. 
Distributing extra cash to one’s entourage at the end of a success-
ful adventure makes them happy and more loyal. See Standings 
in Chapter 4 for more details.
Occasional Spending
C
onspicuous consumption is the rule among knights, if 
not needed for normal maintenance. No one saves mon-
ey, except perhaps to put aside a few £ for future expens-
es. T raveling to the nearby city to buy new equipment, gifts for 
lovers, or other items is a pleasant event after the slaughter and 
trouble of an adventure. 
Note that prices for items change as campaign time passes, 
and the types of goods available increases. The Standard Price 
List is specifically for the early part of the game.
The Market
E
very city has a permanent market. This economic nu -
cleus, in fact, defines what a city is: a place where you 
can buy anything on the Standard Price List (see below). 
T owns and manors do not usually have a market beyond a week-
ly meeting of itinerant peddlers.
Most of the cities in P aladin  have about 1,000–2,000 in -
habitants. Paris and Metz are the only Frankish cities which have 
5,000 permanent residents. Rome is at least twice that size, while 
the city of Constantinople is bigger still. Whatever the size of 
the city, the rules for buying and selling are always the same, al -
though the range of available items may vary.
Buying
Items can be purchased at a market for Standard Price List 
costs. Given prices depend strongly on place and on the econom-
ic situation: shortage may temporarily raise prices, while an over-
abundance temporarily lowers them.
Selling
Knights can sell goods at the market as well. This is done 
by going to a merchant and negotiating with him for the price. 
Selling goods at market nets the buyer half the price shown on 
the price list. This half-price is a law of marketing, and one of 
those damnable things which commoners delight in because it 
pesters the gentry so much.
Trade
Knights find it most advantageous to trade goods with their 
own lord. In such trades the lord usually grants the full price as 
shown on the Standard Price List. It is better to try to trade with 
your lord than to sell treasure at a market.
However, trading armor to lords other than your own is 
not generally possible. Instead they send the knight to the black-
smith, who grants a value in trade of only half the listed value. 
(These commoners must have a conspiracy or something!) 
Lords or very rich knights often hire the service of a per -
sonal merchant who trades their goods for them. Such a man 
receives a salary, and his expertise guarantees better prices at the 
market; especially when selling.
Standard Price List
All prices given in the Standard Price List are the mini -
mum charged. Occasionally, some items may only be purchased 
at higher costs or not available at all, at the Gamemaster’s 
discretion.
==================== BOOK PAGE 200 ====================
199
When a price is stated as “per knight, ” it includes his squire.
These are prices to be found in any city, and are non-nego -
tiable (it is beneath a knight to bicker with a merchant, anyway). 
However, the Gamemaster may adjust prices as needed, based 
on common sense. For example, if a knight wished to buy armor 
decorated with silver, the price might be triple the given amount.
Items marked with 2, 3, or 4, only become available in the 
corresponding Phase and cannot be bought at the beginning 
of the game in the year 767. Some items from foreign cultures 
(marked with a n) cannot be acquired at markets at all, but are 
given for comparison only.
Food & Drink
All food prices are doubled during years of famine (see 
Chapter 15: The Future), or on a roll of 1 on a d6. Subsequent 
famines continue to double food prices (x4, x8, etc.).

before making any purchases on the tables below.

Specialist Personnel
T o complete his retinue, a knight may hire specialists. When 
such a person accompanies him in his retinue, the knight is al -
lowed to use the specialist’s skills instead of his own. Retinues 
provide Glory equal to their cost, which is usually £1 per spe -
cialist per year. Some specialists do not become available before 
a certain Phase. Note that firing a specialist always costs an extra 
year’s pay (or a point of Honor if the knight can’t pay).
Once you’ve enrolled a specialist, roll up his or her age as 
1d20+20 (or 15 for squires) and starting skills (see the Specialist 
Personnel table below). Apart from for magicians, the person -
nel’s skills are automatically checked during the Winter Phase.
Since they are part of the knight’s entourage, use Standing 
[retinue] for specialists when needed.
Chaplain: An educated cleric providing spiritual guidance 
to his lord’s house, for whom he performs all Christian rites. He 
manages the archives, writes official documents and important 
letters.
Engineer: A master in the art of building wooden or stone 
bridges, buildings and fortifications, and of destroying them by 
using siege engines or digging tunnels. 
Healer: Knows how to prepare cures for illnesses and infec-
tions and how to treat wounds and other injuries.
Herald: T ravels around the domain to make important 
public announcements and acts as a judge at tournaments. Once 
per year, on a successful Eloquence roll, a herald may double his 
master’s Glory for a single feat, action or event.
Horse groom: T akes extraordinary care for all horses of the 
herd. On a successful skill roll, all the lord’s horses have +1 to 
their Winter Phase survival roll.
Magician: Magicians (or “astrologers” , as they prefer to call 
themselves) may perform acts of divination. Since other types of 
magic are condemned by the Church, a lord who asks his court 
magician to dabble in necromancy or other such things will al -
most certainly get checks in negative traits and/or provoke the 
magician’s departure or the Church’s displeasure. Unlike other 
specialists, a magician (or, rather, his lord) must spend his skill 
value in livres on books, research, and exotic ingredients in order 
to get the annual skill checks.
Master smith: A master smith may attempt to create a 
sword of excellent quality once per year. Fumble: flawed sword 
(–1). Failure: normal sword. Success: sword with a +1 skill bo -
nus, but breaks on a tie. Critical: sword with +1 skill bonus, 
unbreakable.
Merchant: His strength consists in buying or selling a lord’s 
goods at more advantageous prices. On a successful T rade roll, 
he manages to sell off surplus goods at 75% of the Standard Price 
List value, 100% on a critical success, 50% on a failure and 25% 
on a fumbled roll. When used for buying, the merchant acquires 
new goods at 50% of the normal price on a critical, 75% on a 
normal success, 100% on a failure and 150% on a fumble.
Minstrel: A singer, storyteller and musician who entertains 
his audience at markets and noble courts. A minstrel may assist 
his master in wooing a noble lady and make a skill roll in his 
stead once per year.
Spy: A spy discreetly informs his master about what’s going 
on in a certain city, region or court. A lord can give a spy up to

==================== BOOK PAGE 206 ====================
205
three specific tasks per year, such as determining the ambitions 
or standings of a certain person, the military situation at a castle, 
or the fidelity of a wife. Difficult tasks may have a negative mod-
ifier on the spy’s skill rolls. A successful skill roll gives the player 
a +5 bonus to certain future rolls (Gamemaster’s discretion). A 
fumble indicates the spy has been unmasked and caught. On a 
subsequent failed standing roll the spy turns his coat and gives 
information about his lord to his intended victim.
Squire: Some lords prefer to have more than one squire, 
not only as a status enhancement, but also to increase the chanc-
es of getting a fresh horse in battle. After the aging roll of each 
Winter Phase, there is a 1 on a d6 chance that the squire leaves 
his lord’s service (no extra money need be paid).
Steward: Usually it’s the lord’s wife who does the manage-
ment of the estate. Unmarried knights may hire a steward to su-
pervise the harvesting, repair roads and bridges, collect taxes, sell 
agricultural surplus, and buy what’s needed.
Slaves
The cost of slaves is not given, since it is assumed Player-
knights will never buy them. Still, if a character wishes to buy a 
slave in order to set him or her free, the typical cost is somewhere 
between 120d and £1½, but may reach as much as £10 for very 
special slaves.
Enchanted Items
H
eroes often possess an enchanted or magic sword or ride 
a fantastic horse. Such wondrous items and steeds cannot 
be bought at a market, or anywhere else.
Enchanted or Magic?
Apart from ordinary valuable objects like bracelets, rings, 
medallions, crosses, buckles, chalices, and reliquaries, the heroes 
of legends often acquire rare objects, some of which are said to 
be “enchanted” or “magic.” These objects pass from hand to 
hand, either won in combat or by a series of (magical) trials.
Unlike genuine magic items, most enchanted ones have no 
precise supernatural power. They are simply of extraordinary 
quality, without having any strange magical properties. They are 
desired as much for the prestige derived from their history and 
former owners as for their inherent quality, giving passive Glory 
to their owners.
Genuinely magic weapons grant another bonus in addition 
to passive Glory. The qualities of magic weapons often depend 
directly on the knight’s chivalric or other moral qualities, like 
Hector’s Armor or the Sword of Justice and V alor. Many magical 
items provide a set bonus or effect.
Is Magic Dishonorable?
Is it immoral or dishonorable for a knight to use magic 
items in combat, since they give an unfair advantage over the 
opponent? Y es. The use of magic weapons is usually seen as 
subversive of chivalry, because they nullify the effects of person-
al valor. However…
The use of magic by an honorable character is permitted to 
fight evil or dishonorable opponents.
Characters may be unaware of an item’s magical properties, 
as it is often camouflaged by the wielder’s own skill.
Armor never protects people entirely. Some wearers of very 
powerful magic armor are killed, like Argalia, Mandricard and 
Gradasso. Nor do magic weapons guarantee success in combat 
(with the notable exception of the Golden Lance). Even the 
wielders of such powerful items as Durendal and the Blinding 
Shield can be vanquished.
Now, consider the example of the noble Rogero, who won-
ders if his use of his magic shield to vanquish his adversaries is 
chivalrous. Later he throws the “unchivalrous” shield into a lake.
“What can I do to make up for such a disgrace?” he 
mused. “If I won, people will say it was by virtue of mag-
ic, not by my own valor. ” 
— Orlando Furioso , XXII.90
So, the use of magic weapons or items brings unfair advan -
tages to a knight and must therefore be confined to non-chival -
ric contexts. As a rule, a Chivalrous Player-knight who know -
ingly makes use of magic in chivalrous combat no longer gets 
the natural armor (one of the Chivalrous bonus benefits) for the 
rest of the year. There is one notable exception to this rule: items 
whose bonus depends on the wielder’s personality (like Hector’s 
Armor), may be used by Chivalrous knights without penalty.
Miscellaneous Magic Objects
Agolant’s Ring: This golden ring is a present from 
Agolant’s wife to Duke Naymo. It protects the wearer from 
evil magic and poison (oppose 1d20 to the magic or poison), 
safeguards treasure (delude robbers, roll 1d20 opposed to their 
Awareness), and warns of treachery (the ring changes color in 
the presence of traitors).
Amulet with a Holy Relic: Grants a Hit Point bonus equal 
to the wearer’s points over 15 in a specific Christian trait.
Astolf ’s Horn: When blown, this horn causes all who hear 
to make an immediate roll of V alorous –10. After Sir Astolf, Sir 
Huon receives it as a gift from Oberon and uses it on his quest.
Aumon’s T ent: Four carbuncles magically illuminate its 
surroundings during the night, and a glass mirror on top of 
the tent allows those within to see the surroundings as with a 
telescope.
Book of Sorcery: This book is bound in scorched and 
tanned human skin, and set with an iron claw as a lock. It con -
tains the forbidden knowledge of demons. Just opening the 
book makes one shiver with cold. A Reckless roll is needed to 
dare opening it. T o receive its full effects, the book must be read 
alone at full moon in a place where somebody died (roll Faerie 
Lore to know this). If these conditions are not met, no benefit is

gained from reading it, but negative effects still apply. The book 
may only ever be read once, whether successfully or not.
Magicians who read it and succeed at both Reading & 
W riting and the appropriate Language gain a +1d6 bonus to 
their Magic and Faerie Lore skills. On a failed roll, important 
parts of the book remain a mystery forever (no second readings), 
but no permanent adverse effects occur (apart from the chills). 
Finally, a successful Love [God] roll is needed to avoid perma -
nent Madness after reading. Or, alternatively, the reader may 
gain five points (ten on a fumble) in random negative traits.
Books of Knowledge: How-to books that guide knights. 
A non-magic book that simply gives the reader +1 on a specific 
knowledge skill. For example, a Roman book on strategy could 
give a permanent +1 to Battle and/or Siege on a single successful 
Reading roll.
Books of Magic: Books used to cast spells or conjure up 
demons, of the sort owned by Maugis, Atlantes and Melissa. 
Bound demons (conjured with the aid of books or otherwise) 
provide information or even foreknowledge, transportation, and 
illusions. Christian and pagan magicians alike use these demons. 
Byzantine Cup: Drinking from this golden goblet takes 
away fever and cures most common diseases. It induces mild 
hallucinations, followed by a day of extreme fatigue (treat as 
if Unconscious).
Cassandra’s Canopy: W oven by Cassandra of T roy 
(Hector’s sister) a long time ago, Bradamant and Rogero use it 
as their wedding pavilion. It has moving tapestries and acts as a 
permanent blessing on loving, married couples.
Charlemagne’s Scepter: Charlemagne’s golden staff gives 
its wielder +3 to all standings. Only the King of the Franks may 
hold it.
Charlemagne’s T alisman: This small necklace reli -
quary contains hairs of the Holy Virgin and the royal seal 
(Charlemagne uses a ring with a smaller seal for court decisions). 
It was given to him by his mother, Bertrada. He wears it around 
his neck at all times. It protects him from magic and gives him 
+1 to all Christian traits.
Conjuring Powder: Maugis uses this powder to conjure up 
demons bound to his will, in the fashion of Arab genies.
Crown of the Lombards: The royal Lombard crown is a 
plain iron circlet containing a nail of the T rue Cross. It gives the 
wearer +3 to APP and Love [God].
Cumaean Pavilion: A wedding gift from the pagan Emir 
Dolistone to his daughter Flordelis. Inside the tent hangs a pro-
phetic tapestry which shows a significant future event whenever 
a honorable and chaste person enters the pavilion alone (i.e. it 
functions only for a person succeeding rolls in both Honor and 
Chaste).
Cuckold’s Cup: Made by Morgan, this goblet reveals a hus-
band’s or spouse’s infidelity.
Drink from this cup and you shall witness a notable effect: 
if you belong to the order of cuckoldry, the wine will spill 
all over your chest, and not a drop will reach your lips; but 
if your wife is faithful, you shall drink it straight off.
— Orlando Furioso , XLII.103
Ermengard’s Healing T ent: Once per year, if a knight 
spends an entire night asleep in this small tent, he may roll 
Forgiving and be healed a number of Hit Points equal to his suc-
cessful roll.
Faerie Crystal: This pure white crystal is accompanied by 
a small riddle sung to the knight when a good faerie gives it to a 
worthy knight: “The thing we receive without saying thanks/which 
we enjoy without knowing how/which we give to others when we’ve 
lost ourselves in one another/and which we lose without knowing. ” 
The answer is “Life.” On receiving a critical or mortal injury the 
crystal breaks and saves the knight’s life. W orks only once!
Faerie Fruit: Eating otherworldly fruit makes you lose your 
memories of the previous day(s). This fruit is the reason why 
people who accidentally visit a Faerie Court often do not re -
member it afterwards. Faerie fruit brought to the normal world 
rots in a single day.
Faerie Healing Stone: When pressed against an open 
wound (instead of using First Aid), the stone heals the victim’s 
wound completely and transfers the damage to the owner of

the stone. Humans can benefit from the Faerie Stone only once 
per lifetime.
Fastrada’s Ring: Queen Fastrada finds this ring at the site 
of the future Palace of Aachen. She gives it to King Charlemagne 
at their wedding in 783. The ring carries an enchantment of 
Love (2d6+6).
Flask of Infinite W ater: A thirsty person may pour as 
much water as he can drink out of this flask. At its bottom, the 
following inscription can be read: “When my heart is hard and I 
cannot cry,/Or when I hide your goods under my white cloak./My 
cruelty isn’t boundless, surely,/Love makes my heart melt,/And my 
hard face opens up. ” 
Fountain of Love and Fountain of Hate: These two iden-
tical fountains appear magically in the Ardennes Forest near a 
waterfall. Drinking its water has the effect of a 2d6+6 Love or 
Hate passion for the next encountered person of the opposite 
sex. A previously existing Love or Hate is transformed into a 
Hate or Love of equal power.
This fount is formed entirely
Of alabaster, white and polished,
That’s so ornately decked with gold
It bathed the flowered green in light.
(…)
The nature of this fountain was
That any knight in love
Who drank it would fall out of love

Frisian Banner: This battle standard was hidden by Saint 
Willibrord, only to be found by Sir Magnus the Strong who then 
used it to liberate Rome anno 800. In addition to conferring a 
+3 bonus to Battle, the red flaming banner protects its wielder 
from storms and lightning.
Healing Balm: The ointment which was used to embalm 
the dead body of Christ was later made into a powerful heal -
ing balm. It instantly heals one wound completely. The Saracen 
Ferragut steals it from the Pope’s treasury when plundering 
Rome (767).
But at my saddle holds a vessel

God was anointed.

Logastilla’s Book: This books informs the reader of all 
sorts of magic spells, traps and illusions, and provides the infor -
mation to undo them. Logistilla gives it to Astolf to help him 
in his quest. A successful roll in Read & W rite is necessary to 
correctly interpret the text.
Lovers’ Rings of Premonition: This pair of golden rings 
automatically tarnishes whenever the knight’s lover (who is 
wearing the other ring) is in danger.
Maugis’ Spellbook: As a child, Maugis got this book from 
the faerie lady Oriande, at whose court he grew up. He now uses 
the spellbook to learn the names and weaknesses of any demon 
servants he may wish to conjure by means of his Conjuring 
Powder (q.v.).
Melissa’s Mirror: When looking into this mirror, one 
doesn’t see the reflection of what is in front of it, but what was 
there in the past.
Ogier’s Ring: This ring restores the wearer’s vigor and 
youthfulness. A character wearing this ring is effectively protect-
ed from all natural aging rolls.
Oliphant: Roland’s great signaling horn, carved in ivory, 
bound in gold, and studded with gems. When sounded it can be 
heard for 10 miles around and temporarily confers +5 V alorous 
to all friendly knights, while imparting –5 V alorous to enemies. 
However, it gives the owner +5 to Reckless, and it does a direct 
1d6 damage (without armor) to the knight whenever he blows it.
Oriflamme: A gift to Charlemagne from the Pope. When 
held by a Christian it blazes with white flame and blinds all pa -
gans looking at it. The royal war banner of the Franks, it is usu -
ally kept at the abbey of St. Denis, as it was formerly the banner 
of St. Dionysus, the apostle of the city of Paris. It is a blue banner 
with six red roses. If carried by a Christian knight on a battle -
field, it gives him +5 to Battle.
Potion of Sleep: The glass bottle is marked with the 
phrase: “Both are heavier than lead/When you close them./It 
is not for sale,/For buyers are rare. ” The bottle doesn’t have an 
unusual weight. Its content is a tasteless gray fluid. It cannot be 
preserved outside the bottle. When drunk, the imbiber falls into 
a magical slumber for eight hours.
Powder of Sleep: Both Count Basin and Maugis possess 
a potent powder which sends persons or animals who inhale it 
immediately to sleep for a few hours.
Powder of Sneezing: Maugis uses this powder to make 
people involuntarily sneeze and lose concentration for a few 
minutes. A sneezing person is at –5 for all actions for 3d6 melee 
rounds.
Ring of Protection: Such a silver ring protects a wearer 
from steel, fire or drowning. The owner of a ring of protection 
sustains only half damage from one specific damage source.
Ring of Reason: Princess Angelica of Cathay wears this 
powerful golden ring, which negates all enchantments and illu -
sions, and confers invisibility when placed in one’s mouth.
T assilo Chalice: This magnificent golden ornamented 
chalice belongs to the Agilolfing dukes of Bavaria. The owner 
receives +5 to both Eloquence and Proud.

Almace: Archbishop T urpin’s sword gives a Christian

Courtain: Sir Ogier’s magical long sword, of which the 
point is broken (hence its name: “curt”). The wielder adds a 
damage bonus equal to his Just –10.
Dagger of Fate: The blade of this dagger takes on the color 
of the wielder’s destiny if she concentrates on her future while 
brandishing it. For example: it turns blood red if she is about to 
face a bloody ambush. This allows Gamemasters to give (sub -
tle) hints to their players. A sense of mystery is guaranteed: “red” 
may be interpreted in different ways by different players in dif -
ferent situations.
Durendal: Roland wins this famous magical sword from 
the Saracen Aumon in 767. Its golden hilt is set with four rel -

Golden Lance: This unbreakable lance has a golden tip and

wearer gets armor points equal to his Honor score. The appear -
ance of the armor is automatically altered to reflect this score, i.e. 
for someone with Honor 10 it looks like chainmail, for someone 
with Honor 6 it’s leather, and for a knight with Honor 16 it’s 
full plate.
Joyeuse: King Charlemagne’s sword. It is inlaid with sev -
eral relics, notably a piece of the Holy Lance. “Who holds this 
sword need never be afraid/of any mortal wound, poison or pain. ” 
Its wielder is completely protected from poison, never suffers the 
extra effects of Major W ounds, nor the –5 or –10 penalties due 
to lost Hit Points.
Mambrino’s Helmet: A golden helmet which Renaud 
captures from the Moorish Prince Mambrino. It confers +1 to 
armor.
